In one case where the parties had agreed upon all of the substantial terms of the contract, the Court went so far as to hold that "the understanding that the contract should be formally drawn up and put in writing did not leave the transaction incomplete and without binding force, in the absence of a positive agreement that it should not be binding until so reduced to writing and formally executed." 

Disken v. Herter, supra. The more general statement of the rule, however, is that, if it appears that the parties did not intend to be bound until the execution of a formal con- 38

OFFER AND ACCEPTANCE §§ 35-37 

tract, neither party is bound until that is done. Donnelly v. 

Currie Hardware Co., 66 N. J. L. 388, 49 Atl. 428.

It is frequently said that the fact that the parties intended to execute a formal instrument, although not conclusive, is strong evidence that they did not intend to be bound until that was done. Sullivan v. Happy Hour Amusement Co., 177 App. Div. 232, 236, 163 N. Y. S. 715; Potomac 

Insurance Co. v. Kelly, 173 App. Div. 791, 160 N. Y. S. 161.

REVOCATION § 36. 

Revocation of Offer.

An unaccepted offer creates no right and, therefore, may be withdrawn at any time before acceptance. For example, an order for goods which has not yet been accepted by the firm to which it was sent may be canceled by the proposed buyer, even though he has expressly stipulated that the order could not be countermanded. Cary v. Appo, 84 

N. Y. S. 569; Howe Scale Co. v. Wolfshaut, 170 N. Y. S. 943. 

Bids at auction sales are mere offers which may be withdrawn at any time before the fall of the hammer; and, except where the sale has been announced to be without reserve, the auctioneer may, before the contract is complete, withdraw the goods from sale. Uniform Sales Act, sec. 21, subd. 2; Personal Property Law, sec. 102, subd. 2.

Where an offer has been modified by the offerer, in the course of negotiations between the parties, the original offer has thereby been revoked and is no longer open for acceptance. Schenectady Stove Co. v. Holbrook, 101 N. Y. 

45, 4 N. E. 4. 

§ 37. Agreement To Hold Offer Open. 

Even though the party making the offer agrees to hold it open for acceptance for a definite time, in the absence of a consideration for his promise, he may, upon giving notice to the offeree, withdraw his offer before the time has expired. Dickinson v. Dodds, 2 Ch. Div. (Eng.) 463; Cole- 39

§§ 37  

man v. Applegarth, 68 Md. 21, 11 Atl. 284, 6 Am. St. Rep. 

417. But unless revoked, such a proposal remains in force as a continuing offer until the expiration of the specified period, capable of ripening into a contract upon acceptance at any time during that interval. Boston & Maine R. R. 

Co. v. Bartlett, 3 Cush. (Mass.) 224.

Where a valid consideration has been given by the offeree in exchange for the offerer's promise to hold the offer open for a stated length of time, the offerer is not at liberty to withdraw his offer before the expiration of that period. 

This constitutes an independent contract between the parties, known as an option. Coleman v. Applegarth, supra. 

§ 38. Revocation of Offer for Unilateral Contract. 

Since no unilateral contract is created until the act for which the offer was made has been fully performed, the offerer may withdraw his offer at any time before the completion of the act, even though the offeree has begun performance, and has expended time or money, or both, relying upon the continuance of the offer. Wherever possible in such cases, courts construe the offer as contemplating a bilateral contract and spell out an acceptance from the part performance. Where this is impossible, various solutions of the difficulty have been suggested and occasionally adopted in order to satisfy the equities of a particular case, but, as a general rule, the courts have applied to these cases the strict rule of law which gives to an offerer the absolute right to revoke his offer at any time before acceptance, provided he gives notice of revocation to the offeree. Petterson v. Pattberg, 248 N. Y. 86, 161 N. E. 428; see authorities cited in Williston on Contracts, Rev. Ed., Vol. I, secs. 

60-60A; Wormser, "The True Conception of Unilateral Con tracts," 26 Yale Law Journal 136; Finn, "The Forging of 

Good Unilaterals out of Bad Bilaterals," 3 Brooklyn Law 

Review 6. See section 478, infra. 

§ 39. 

Communication of Revocation.

A revocation is not effective until it has been communi- 40

OFFER AND ACCEPTANCE §§ 39-41 

cated to the offeree. Therefore a revocation sent by mail or telegraph is not operative until it has been received by the offeree. It follows that, where an offer is sent by mail, a receipt by the offeree of a letter revoking the offer subsequent to the mailing of the letter of acceptance would be ineffective, since the acceptance was completed before the offeree had knowledge that the offerer desired to revoke the offer. Patrick v. Bowman, 149 U. S. 411, 424, 37 Law. 

Ed. 790, 13 Sup. Ct. Rep. 811, 866; Kempner v. Cohn, 47 

Ark. 519, 1 S. W. 869, 58 Am. Rep. 775. Knowledge by the offeree that the property offered to him has been sold to another is sufficient notice of revocation. Dickinson v. Dodds, 2 Ch. Div. (Eng.) 463; Petterson v. Pattberg, 248 N. Y. 

86, 161 N. E. 428. 

§ 40. 

Revocation of Public Offer. 

An offer made by advertisement to the public generally may be revoked by the same means or agency through which the offer was made known. When the same publicity has been given to the revocation as was given to the offer, the revocation is effective even as against one who acted on the offer without notice of its withdrawal. Shuey v. United 

States, 92 U. S. 73, 23 Law. Ed. 697. 

§ 41. 

Revocation of Offer by Death or Insanity. 

An offer which has not been accepted is automatically revoked by the death or the insanity of either party. Thus, where A makes an offer to B and A dies before acceptance, B cannot bind A's estate by thereafter accepting the offer, even though he attempts to do so within the time permitted by the offer. But where an offer of sale of property was sent by mail, the death of one of the parties subsequent to the mailing of an acceptance and prior to its actual receipt was held to be of no effect, since the contract was consummated prior to the death. Mactier's Administrators v. Frith, 6 Wend. (N.Y.) 103, 21 Am. Dec. 262; Beach v. 

First Methodist Episcopal Church, 96 Ill. 177; Herrlich v. 

Hyman, 61 Misc. 606, 113 N. Y. S. 971; Restatement, Contracts, sec. 48. 41

CHAPTER III REALITY OF CONSENT-MISTAKE § 42. 

Mistake Preventing Consent. 

A mistake affecting offer and acceptance arises where the parties make a serious error as to the substance of the contract, persons, or subject matter thereof; under such circumstances no contractual obligation arises because there is no mutuality of assent and, therefore, no contract. Not every mistake, however, will prevent consent. Embry v. 

Hargadine-McKittrick Dry Goods Co., 127 Mo. App. 383, 105 S. W. 777. Numberless mistakes may, and often do, occur which have no bearing upon the consent of the parties. 

For instance, if a party misjudges the value or quality of the subject matter of the contract, there is a mistake, but it is not one which prevents consent. It is only in limited and exceptional cases that a mistake operates to prevent consent and, therefore, to render the contract void. Such a mistake, frequently designated as an operative mistake, is illustrated by the following situations: 

1. Where the parties do not mean the same thing. Thus,

A obtains a policy of marine insurance on the vessel "Empress." Two vessels by that name are listed in Lloyd's 

Register, and the vessel which the insurance company intends to cover is not the one which the policy holder intends to insure. No contract results, for the minds of the parties have not met. Hughes v. Mercantile Mutual Insurance Co., 55 N. Y. 265, 14 Am. Rep. 254; Raffles v. Wichelhaus, 2 

Hurl. & C. (Eng.) 906. 

2. Where the parties mean the same thing, but they both have formed untrue conclusions as to the subject matter in some important particular which involves the very essence of the agreement. To illustrate: Both A and B believe that a certain identified case contains apples, and they negotiate for its sale as such. In reality, however, the case contains potatoes. Under the influence of their expressed belief, the parties enter into an agreement for the 42

REALITY OF CONSENT-MISTAKE 

$$ 42-44 sale and purchase of that particular case as a case of apples. 

The contract is, of course, void. The parties meant the same thing, but there was a mutual mistake as to the nature of the specific subject matter which affected the very essence of the agreement and which prevented a meeting of the minds. 

A offers to purchase a case of apples from B. B accepts the offer and, pursuant to its terms, ships what he believes to be a case of apples. In reality, the case contains potatoes. Is the mistake an operative mistake; i. e., one which prevents consent? Clearly, the mistake is merely one in performance of the contract; it has no effect whatever upon the formation of the contract.

Operative mistake is one of five elements which relate to consent. The remaining four-misrepresentation, fraud, duress, and undue influence will be considered in succeeding chapters. 

§ 43. Mistake Preventing Consent Distinguished from Mis take in Expressing Agreement. 

A mistake preventing consent is a mistake of intention, and not of expression. For instance, both parties may intend to contract for the sale of "Blackacre," but, by mistake, the written agreement may describe "Shoreacre."

Such an error does not amount to a mistake which prevents the formation of a contract. It is merely an error in the expression of the agreement, in which case a court of equity may reform it to make the written contract, in its altered form, express the true agreement of the parties. However, since even a court of equity cannot make a contract for the parties, in order for reformation to be decreed the mistake must be mutual. Baird v. Erie R. R. Co., 210 N. Y. 225, 104 N. E. 614; Curtis v. Albee, 167 N. Y. 360, 60 N. E. 660. $ 44. Mistake Preventing Consent Distinguished from 

Mistake in Interpretation. 

A mistake preventing consent relates to the formation of the contract and not to its interpretation. One who has 43

§§ 44  

entered into a plain and unambiguous contract cannot avoid his obligation by showing that he erred in his understanding of its terms. Hayward v. Wemple, 152 App. Div. 195, 136 

N. Y. S. 625, aff'd 206 N. Y. 692, 99 N. E. 1108. On this principle, a mistake on the part of one of the parties as to the legal effect of a contract, the terms of which he knows, is generally no ground for relief. Moran v. Wellington, 101 

Misc. 594, 167 N. Y. S. 465. However, where an instrument does not express the intention of the parties because of a mutual mistake as to the legal effect of the words used in the writing, reformation of the instrument to conform it to the true intention of the parties may be allowed. Maher v. 

Hibernia Insurance Co., 67 N. Y. 283; Williston on Contracts, Rev. Ed., Vol. V, sec. 1585; cf. Restatement, Contracts, sec. 504. 

$ 45. When Mistake Is Operative. 

The cases in which mistake prevents the formation of contracts are the rare exceptions to the rule that a man is bound by agreements to which he has clearly expressed his assent. In these exceptional cases, a mistake renders the agreement void and not merely voidable; its operation produces such effect in the following instances: 1. 

Mistake as to the nature of the instrument.

2. 

Mistake as to the person with whom the agreement is made. 

3. Mistake as to the subject matter of the agreement. 

Mistake as to its existence; a. b. Mistake as to its identity; e. f. C. d. Mistake as to its quantity; 

Mistake as to its price; 

Mistake as to its ownership.

Mistake as to its nature and quality; $ 46. 

Mistake as to Nature of Instrument.

A mistake as to the nature of the transaction occurs in cases where a person has been induced by fraud to execute a written instrument of a tenor different from that which he 44

REALITY OF CONSENT-MISTAKE §§ 46, 47 

intended to execute. As a general proposition, a party cannot avoid his obligation by saying that the legal effect of the instrument is different from what he thought it to be. 

But under certain conditions, such an excuse is acceptable. 

For instance, where one who is blind or illiterate signs a contract that is misread or misrepresented to him, the mistake induced by deceit renders the agreement void. In such cases, the mind of the signer does not accompany the signature. Muller v. Rosenblath, 157 App. Div. 513, 142 

N. Y. S. 602. 

§ 47. 

out Reading.

Mistake as to Nature of Instrument-Signing with- It does not necessarily follow that one who has attached his signature to a contract has given his assent thereto, and, therefore, is bound. Where one reads a contract before signing, the question of consent can scarcely arise, but where one signs without first reading, and it later develops that the instrument is of an entirely different nature than the signer supposed it to be, the question as to his negligence in not reading the instrument is an important factor in determining his liability. 

As a general rule, one who signs a contract without reading it is guilty of negligence. Where, however, his failure to read the instrument was induced by the other party's fraudulent representations as to the nature or conditions of the contract, the signer's negligence in not reading it is not a bar to relief. If not thus induced, he is bound by the terms of the contract because his misunderstanding is due to his own negligence, for which the other party is in no way responsible. Therefore, the rule is thoroughly settled. that where parties are dealing upon an equal footingthere being no fraud, undue influence, or relation of confidence or trust-one who signs a contract without reading it is bound by its terms. Metzger v. Aetna Insurance Co., 227 N. Y. 411, 125 N. E. 814. He may not complain that the instrument does not represent the contract, because his misunderstanding is due to his own negligence. Under 45

§  

such circumstances, neither courts of law nor courts of equity will relieve the signer from the consequences of his folly. The same rule has been applied to a party who was unable to read the instrument himself because he did not have his glasses with him and who gave no excuse for not having it read to him. Golle v. State Bank, 52 Wash. 437, 100 Pac. 984. 

But if the failure to read the instrument or, where the signer could not read, the failure to have another read the instrument to him was induced by fraudulent representations as to its terms or nature, the agreement is void for mistake induced by fraud. Wilcox v. American Telephone & 

Telegraph Co., 176 N. Y. 115, 68 N. E. 153; Whipple v. 

Brown Bros. Co., 225 N. Y. 237, 121 N. E. 748; Pimpinello v. 

Swift & Co., 253 N. Y. 159, 170 N. E. 530. To illustrate: 

In Wilcox v. American Telephone & Telegraph Co., supra, the defense to an action in ejectment was based upon an instrument under seal, executed by the plaintiff, which gave to the defendant the right to operate its lines over the plaintiff's land. The plaintiff, who did not have his spectacles with him at the time, had signed in reliance on the statement of the defendant's agent that the paper was a receipt for one dollar which the agent had paid to the plaintiff for a slight injury to one of the latter's trees. 

It was held that the plaintiff was not precluded by his negligence from attacking the validity of the instrument. In Pimpinello v. Swift & Co., supra, an illiterate signed a general release in reliance upon his own attorney's fraudulent statement that the instrument was a mere receipt. Holding that the signer was not negligent and that the release was void, the Court of Appeals said, at p. 163: "If the signer is illiterate, or blind, or ignorant of the alien language of the writing, and the contents thereof are misread or misrepresented to him by the other party, or even by a stranger, unless the signer be negligent, the writing is void." 

A conflict of authority is found only in cases where the signer was able to read and gave no valid excuse for signing without reading other than that he relied on the ■ I 46

REALITY OF CONSENT-MISTAKE §§ 47, 48 

fraudulent representation of the other party with respect to the nature and contents of the instrument. In New 

York and by the weight of authority in other jurisdictions, it is held, in actions between the original parties to the transaction, that one who has been fraudulently misled as to the contents of a paper is not estopped by his own negligence from asserting the invalidity of the contract on the ground of mistake induced by fraud. Outcault Advertising 

Co. v. Stratton, 178 App. Div. 353, 164 N. Y. S. 792; Bixler v. Wright, 116 Me. 133, 100 Atl. 467, L. R. A. 1917F 633. 

§ 48. Rule Applied to Negotiable Instruments. 

Even though one is induced by means of deceit and misrepresentation to sign a negotiable instrument without reading it, although not liable to the payee who practiced the fraud inducing the mistake, he, nevertheless, will be liable to a holder in due course unless he can clearly show that he was not guilty of negligence. The theory is that one who, by his carelessness or misplaced confidence, has enabled another to obtain the money of an innocent third party should answer for the loss. Any other rule would seriously impair the value of commercial paper. Chapman v. Rose, 56 N. Y. 137, 15 Am. Rep. 401; Munnich v. Jaffe, 164 App. 

Div. 30, 149 N. Y. S. 338. For example, in Chapman v.

Rose, supra, it appeared that a farmer who had just signed an order for a hay fork and two pulleys, at a total cost of nine dollars, upon being asked to sign a duplicate of that order, affixed his signature to a second paper, which was, in fact, a promissory note for $270, and which subsequently came into the hands of a holder in due course. The farmer was held liable on the note. But where a person is without negligence in signing a paper, the nature of which he is ignorant, the instrument is deemed void from its inception and cannot become operative even in the hands of an innocent purchaser for value. Foster v. Mackinnon, 4 Com. 

Pleas (Eng.) 704 (leading case); Walker v. Ebert, 29 Wis. 

194, 9 Am. Rep. 548. In all cases coming under this rule, the courts dwell very strongly on the question of negligence. 47

48, 49 

In Chapman v. Rose, supra, and Walker v. Ebert, supra, the plaintiffs were holders in due course of negotiable instruments and the defendants were the makers thereof who were induced to sign through fraudulent misrepresentations as to the nature of the instruments. In the former case, the defendant was held liable because he signed the instrument without reading it and, therefore, was guilty of negligence; in the latter case, the defendant could not read because he was illiterate and, therefore, was not negligent in signing the note in reliance upon the false representations. 

§ 49. Mistake as to Person with Whom Agreement Is Made. 

A mistake as to the party with whom an agreement is made may prevent the formation of a contract, because one of the parties was never in the mind of the other, who, therefore, never consented to contract with him. School Sisters of Notre Dame v. Kusnitt, 125 Md. 323, 93 Atl. 928, L. R. A. 

1916D 792. Such a mistake occurs most frequently in cases where one person falsely impersonates another or falsely represents that he is acting as agent for another. The rules applicable to these cases will be discussed in the sections following. Another instance of a mistake as to the person with whom one is dealing is a case where a stranger to the contract substitutes himself for one of the parties, without the knowledge or consent of the other party. The leading case in this country is Boston Ice Co. v. Potter, 123 Mass. 

28, 25 Am. Rep. 9. This was an action for ice sold and delivered. The defendant had previously taken ice from the plaintiff but, on becoming dissatisfied with the manner of supply, had terminated the arrangement and made a contract with the Citizen's Ice Company. The plaintiff thereafter bought the business of the Citizen's Ice Company and delivered ice to the defendant for one year without notifying him of the change. In affirming a judgment for the defendant, the Court said: "A party has a right to select and determine with whom he will contract, and cannot have another person thrust upon him without his consent. It 48

REALITY OF CONSENT-MISTAKE §§ 49, 50 

may be of importance to him who performs the contract, as when he contracts with another to paint a picture, or write a book, or furnish articles of a particular kind, or when he relies upon the character or qualities of an individual, or has, as in this case, reasons why he does not wish to deal with a particular party. In all these cases, as he may contract with whom he pleases, the sufficiency of his reasons for so doing cannot be inquired into.” This is an extreme case, however, and borders closely on cases involving the question of the right of either party to assign an executory contract, which is very different from the question of the effect of a mistake as to the identity of one of the parties to the original agreement. See Arkansas Valley 

Smelting Co. v. Belden Mining Co., 127 U. S. 379, 32 Law.

Ed. 246, 8 Sup. Ct. Rep. 1308. The grounds upon which the 

Court based its decision in Boston Ice Co. v. Potter, supra, are ably criticized in an article on "The Doctrine of Boston 

Ice Co. v. Potter" by George P. Costigan, Jr., in 7 Columbia 

Law Review 32.

Where a buyer receives and retains goods after notice of the substitution of a new seller, he thereby assents to the change and is liable. If goods ordered of A are furnished by B and the buyer becomes aware of this fact at any time. before he has used the goods, he must pay for them. Cin cinnati Siemens-Lungren Gas Illuminating Co. v. Western 

Siemens-Lungren Co., 152 U. S. 200, 202, 38 Law. Ed. 411, 14 Sup. Ct. Rep. 523; Barnes v. Shoemaker, 112 Ind. 512, 14 N. E. 367. 

§ 50. Contract Made with Person Falsely Impersonating 

Another.

A serious controversy has been waged concerning the effect of a mistake as to the party with whom one is dealing where one person fraudulently impersonates another. The question mooted is whether the contract is void on the ground of mistake, or voidable on the theory of fraud. The distinction is important, because if the contract involves the sale of personal property and is void, bona fide pur- 49

§ 

chasers do not acquire title from the person causing the mistake; if only voidable, they acquire title.

It is the general rule in New York that if the parties are dealing face to face, the contract is voidable; while if the negotiations are carried on by correspondence, the agreement is void. In the former, consent is given; but in the latter, there is a total want of consent. This rule is supported by the weight of authority in other jurisdictions. 

Thus, although a sale to an impostor dealing face to face with the vendor is voidable for fraud as between the parties, yet the impostor may pass title to an innocent purchaser for value. Phelps v. McQuade, 220 N. Y. 232, 115 N. E. 

441, L. R. A. 1918B 973; Edmunds v. Merchants' Despatch 

Transportation Co., 135 Mass. 283. But where the impostor is carrying on his impersonation of another by correspondence, and not face to face, the agreement is void for want of consent, and no title or right can be acquired from the impostor by innocent third parties. Cundy v. Lindsay, 3 

App. Cas. (Eng.) 459 (leading case); Mercantile National 

Bank v. Silverman, 148 App. Div. 1, 132 N. Y. S. 1017, aff'd 210 N. Y. 567, 104 N. E. 1134; Morgan Munitions 

Supply Co. v. Studebaker Corp. of America, 226 N. Y. 94, 123 N. E. 146. The distinction between Phelps v. McQuade, supra, and Mercantile National Bank v. Silverman, supra, rests upon the theory that, where the parties are dealing face to face, the vendor intends to deal with the person present and identified by sight and hearing, and that, therefore, the minds of the parties meet, resulting in a contract and transfer of title to property; but where the parties are not dealing face to face, it is the intention of the vendor to deal not with the impostor, but with the party whom the impostor represents himself to be, and, therefore, there is no meeting of the minds and, hence, no contract and no passing of title to the property to the impostor. The title under such circumstances remains in the vendor. In the latter case there is a mistake as to the person with whom the contract is made.

As pointed out by the Court in Phelps v. McQuade, supra, 50

REALITY OF CONSENT MISTAKE 

§ 50 the question whether the parties are dealing face to face or by letter is material only as bearing upon the question of intent; in either case, additional facts may be shown that would alter the rule. This is illustrated by the later case of 

Cohen v. Lincoln Savings Bank, 275 N. Y. 399, 10 N. E. (2d) 457. In this case, the plaintiff delivered to H, her husband, a check drawn to her order with instructions to use it to purchase for her an assignment of one-half of a condemnation award. H had carried on negotiations for this purchase with one A, an attorney, as a result of which a day was set for the closing of title. At A's office on that day, K, an attorney who claimed to represent W, the owner of the award, introduced an impostor as the owner. The check was thereupon indorsed to the order of W and was delivered to the impostor in exchange for an "assignment" of one-half of the award. Neither the plaintiff nor her husband had had any dealings with the impostor prior to the closing; and the negotiations on the closing were limited to the indorsement and transfer of the check in exchange for the "assignment." After the impostor had received the check, he indorsed it in the name of W to one J, who received it in good faith and for value. J was paid by the bank on which the check was drawn. The Court of Appeals, two judges dissenting, held that although at the time of the closing, the parties had dealt face to face, under the particular facts of this case, the impostor was not the person who was intended to be the indorsee and, therefore, his indorsement passed no title to the check. The Court emphasized the fact that in this case there were no prior negotiations with the impostor, saying that in all prior cases in which, as a result of a face to face transaction, the victim was deemed to have intended to pass title to the impostor there was an antecedent fraud by which the impostor induced the victim to deal with him by masquerading as another. Here, the Court concluded, there had been no dealings with the impostor prior to his receipt of the check at the closing and, consequently, no inference could be drawn that the victim intended to pass title to the impostor. "The absence of all 51

§§ 50  

prior dealings differentiates this case from every prior case" in which title was held to pass. 

§ 51. 

Contract Made with Person Falsely Representing 

Authority from Another. 

Where one falsely represents himself to be the agent of another and thereby obtains money or credit, there is a mistake as to the person with whom the agreement is made, and, therefore, the contract is void. This is an application of the rule treated in the preceding section under different circumstances. In such cases, the injured party intends to contract not with the person before him but with the principal who is either non-existent or who has not authorized the impostor to represent him. Hentz v. Miller, 94 N. Y. 

64; United Cigar Stores Co. v. American Raw Silk Co., 184 

App. Div. 217, 171 N. Y. S. 480, aff'd 229 N. Y. 532, 129 

N. E. 904; Strang v. Westchester County National Bank, 235 N. Y. 68, 138 N. E. 739. But where an agent enters into a contract in his own name for the benefit of an undisclosed principal, the contract is not void for mistake, for the reason that the agent is himself liable on the contract to the same extent as if he were not acting for another. There is no mistake because the other party is contracting with the very person with whom he intended to contract, viz., the agent. Kelly Asphalt Block Co. v. Barber Asphalt Paving 

Co., 211 N. Y. 68, 105 N. E. 88, L. R. A. 1915C 256. 

§ 52. Mistake as to Subject Matter-Existence. 

If, unknown to either party, the subject matter has ceased to exist at the time the contract is made, the agreement is void. Thus, in the sale of a ship or a cargo which has been lost at sea, or of a horse which is dead, no rights are conferred on either party. Duncan v. New York Mutual 

Insurance Co., 138 N. Y. 88, 33 N. E. 730, 20 L. R. A. 386; 

Allen v. Hammond, 11 Pet. (U. S.) 63, 9 Law. Ed. 633; 

Gibson v. Pelkie, 37 Mich. 380; Riegel v. American Life 

Insurance Co., 153 Pa. 134, 25 Atl. 1070. In the case last cited, a creditor had a policy for $6,000 on the life of his 52

REALITY OF CONSENT-MISTAKE §§ 52, 53 

debtor. After thirteen years, during which the whereabouts of the debtor was unknown, the payments of the premiums became burdensome and the creditor took a paidup policy for $2,500 in lieu of the $6,000 policy. In fact, unknown to either party, the insured had died about ten days before the old policy was canceled and the new one issued. The creditor brought an action to reinstate the old policy, and the Court held that the cancelation was void because there was a mutual mistake of a material fact, that both parties proceeded upon an assumption that the insured was still living, and that the element of doubt as to whether he was living or dead did not enter into the transaction. But if the doubt or risk is made an element of the contract, there is no mistake. Sears v. Grand Lodge, Ancient Order of United Workmen, 163 N. Y. 374, 57 N. E. 

618, 50 L. R. A. 204; Mowatt v. Wright, 1 Wend. (N. Y.) 

355, 19 Am. Dec. 508.

The common law rule as to an attempted sale of nonexistent goods has been incorporated in the Uniform Sales Act, which provides that: "When the parties purport to sell specific goods, and the goods without the knowledge of the seller have wholly perished at the time when the agreement is made, the agreement is void." Uniform Sales Act, sec. 7; Personal Property Law, sec. 88. 

§ 53. 

Mistake as to Subject Matter-Identity. 

A mistake as to the identity of the subject matter occurs where one party is thinking of one thing and the other of another thing, both things having the same name or answering to the same description. Such a mistake prevents the formation of a contract, because the minds of the parties have never met upon the subject matter of the agreement. 

Hughes v. Mercantile Mutual Insurance Co., 55 N. Y. 265, 14 Rep. 254; Kyle v. Kavanagh, 103 Mass. 356, 4 Am.

Rep. 560. For example, a quitclaim deed conveying certain land "on Prospect Street" in Waltham was held void where it appeared that there were two Prospect Streets and the 53

§§ 53,  

land the defendant intended to buy was not the land the plaintiff intended to sell. Kyle v. Kavanagh, supra. $ 54. Mistake as to Subject Matter-Nature and Essential 

Qualities. 

A mistake as to the nature and essential qualities of the subject matter will prevent the formation of a contract. 

This does not relate merely to a mistake in the quality, where one of the parties does not get all that he expected, but to a mistake as to the nature of the subject matter. 

When a buyer makes a mistake as to the qualities of a specific article, he is without remedy unless there has been warranty or fraud. But where there is a mutual mistake as to the fundamental qualities of the subject matter which goes to the whole substance of the agreement and renders the thing contracted for essentially different in kind, the contract is void. This must be distinguished from the performance of a contract for the sale of an article where the article delivered is supposed to answer the description of the one bought, but, in fact, does not. In such a case, there is only a failure of performance. Failure of performance implies the existence of a contract; while a mistake prevents its formation.

The leading case in this country is Sherwood v. Walker, 66 Mich. 568, 33 N. W. 919. In this case, previous to the sale of a cow, the owner and the buyer believed that she was barren, and, therefore, worth only a small sum, but before she was delivered it was discovered that she was with calf, and therefore worth a large sum for breeding purposes. It was held that the mistake avoided the sale. The 

Court said: "I know that this is a close question and the dividing line between the adjudicated cases is not easily discerned. But it must be considered as well settled that a party who has given an apparent consent to a contract of sale may refuse to execute it, or he may avoid it after it has been completed, if the assent was founded, or the contract made, upon the mistake of a material fact, such as the subject-matter of the sale, the price, or some collateral fact 54

REALITY OF CONSENT-MISTAKE 

$ 54 materially inducing the agreement; and this can be done when the mistake is mutual. * * *If there is a difference or misapprehension as to the substance of the thing bargained for, if the thing actually delivered or received is different in substance from the thing bargained for and intended to be sold, then there is no contract; but if it be only a difference in some quality or accident, even though the mistake may have been the actuating motive to the purchaser or seller, or both of them, yet the contract remains binding. The difficulty in every case is to determine whether the mistake or misapprehension is as to the substance of the whole contract, going, as it were, to the root of the matter, or only to some point, even though a material point, an error as to which does not affect the substance of the whole consideration. It has been held, in accordance with the principles above stated, that where a horse is bought under the belief that he is sound, and both vendor and vendee honestly believe him to be sound, the purchaser must stand by his bargain, and pay the full price, unless there was a warranty. It seems to me, however, in the case made by this record, that the mistake or misapprehension of the parties went to the whole substance of the agreement. If the cow was a breeder, she was worth at least $750; if barren, she was worth not over $80. The parties would not have made the contract of sale except upon the understanding and belief that she was incapable of breeding and of no use as a cow. It is true she is now the identical animal that they thought her to be when the contract was made; there is no mistake as to the identity of the creature. Yet the mistake was not of the mere quality of the animal, but went to the very nature of the thing. barren cow is substantially a different creature than a breeding one. There is as much difference between them for all purposes of use as there is between an ox and a cow that is capable of breeding and giving milk. If the mutual mistake had simply related to the fact whether she was with calf or not for one season, then it might have been a good sale; but the mistake affected the character of the animal for all time, 55

§ 

and for her present and ultimate use. She was not in fact the animal, or the kind of animal, the defendants intended to sell or the plaintiff to buy. She was not a barren cow, and, if this fact had been known, there would have been no contract. The mistake affected the substance of the whole consideration, and it must be considered that there was no contract to sell or sale of the cow as she actually was. thing sold and bought had in fact no existence." 

The 

If parties are simply ignorant of the real nature or character of the subject matter and do not affirmatively attribute to it certain qualities, the contract stands. Wood v. Boynton, 64 Wis. 265, 25 N. W. 42, 54 Am. Rep. 610. For example, in the case last cited, the plaintiff sued to recover possession of a small stone which she had found lying on the ground and had sold for one dollar, in ignorance of the fact that it was a rough diamond of the value of about $700. 

Since no fraud on the part of the buyer was shown, it was held that the seller could not repudiate the sale merely because she afterwards learned that she had made a bad bargain. In Massachusetts, this rule was applied to the sale of a promissory note in a case where it was afterwards ascertained that, two hours before the sale, the makers of the note had made a voluntary assignment of all their assets for the benefit of creditors. Hecht v. Batcheller, 147 Mass. 335, 17 N. E. 651, 9 Am. St. Rep. 708. A contrary view was expressed by the New York Court of Appeals, in Roberts v. Fisher, 43 N. Y. 159, 3 Am. Rep. 680, where a worthless note of a third party had been given and accepted in good faith in payment of an account for goods sold and delivered. 

In holding that the party that gave the worthless note should bear the loss, the Court said, at page 162: "If A sells to

B a horse on a farm, and gets his pay by note, the horse being considered as delivered there, and it turns out contrary to the expectation of both, that the horse was accidentally killed on the day before the sale, it would scarcely be pretended that A could recover upon the note; yet it is difficult to distinguish the cases in principle." It should be noted, however, that the case differs from Hecht v. Batcheller, 56

REALITY OF CONSENT—MISTAKE §§ 54, 55 

supra, in that the note in question was not itself the subject of a sale but was given in payment of an antecedent debt. 

In the absence of a warranty, fraud, or mutual mistake as to the fundamental nature or character of the subject matter, the value of a thing sold, as compared with the price paid, is immaterial. Stettheimer v. Killip, 75 N. Y. 282. 

§ 55. Mistake as to Subject Matter-Quantity. 

By analogy with similar cases involving mistake as to price, it is clear that if one party intends to buy a certain quantity and the other to sell a different quantity, there is no contract. See Rupley v. Daggett, 74 Ill. 351. The rule that a contract is void when made upon an assumed state of facts concerning which there is a mutual mistake may also be applied in the case of a mistake as to quantity. For example, where a piece of land was found to contain approximately only half as many acres as the contracting parties had assumed, it was held that the buyer was entitled to be relieved from the contract on the ground of a mutual mistake as to quantity. Belknap v. Sealey, 14 N. Y. 143, 67 

Am. Dec. 120. Where the sale of land is by the acre, or where the price of a parcel has been fixed with reference to the number of acres contained therein and the acreage is found to be materially less than the parties had supposed, a proportionate abatement of the purchase price may be decreed. This, however, is in the nature of reformation of a contract to conform to the actual agreement of the parties, rather than an avoidance of the contract on the ground of a mistake preventing consent. Paine v. Upton, 87 N. Y. 327, 41 Am. Rep. 371; Gallup v. Bernd, 132 N. Y. 370, 30 N. E. 

743; Mills v. Kampfe, 202 N. Y. 46, 94 N. E. 1072. Similar relief has been granted in the case of goods sold in bulk. 

See authorities cited in 31 A. L. R. 384, note. For example, where a pile of oats was sold in bulk upon a rough estimate of quantity based upon comparison with a measured pile and it was afterwards discovered that a mistake had been made in regard to the quantity measured, the buyer was al- 57

§§ 55,  

lowed to recover the money paid for the quantity which he did not receive. Wheadon v. Olds, 20 Wend. (N. Y.) 174. 

Under the Uniform Sales Act, a present sale of a definite number, weight, or measure of a specific mass of fungible goods, e.g., oil, wheat, and other cereal grains, operates to make the buyer an owner in common of such share of the mass as the number, weight, or measure bought bears to the entire mass. "If the mass contains less than the number, weight or measure bought, the buyer becomes the owner of the whole mass and the seller is bound to make good the deficiency from similar goods unless a contrary intent appears." Uniform Sales Act, sec. 6; Personal Property Law, sec. 87; Kimberly v. Patchin, 19 N. Y. 330, 75 Am. Dec. 334. 

§ 56. 

Mistake as to Subject Matter-Price. 

There is no meeting of the minds when the seller bona fide thinks he is selling at one price, and the vendee thinks he is buying at another. Fullerton v. Dalton, 58 Barb. 

236, aff'd 49 N. Y. 659. A supposed he sold a horse to

B for $165, and B was equally honest in his belief that he was buying it for $65 and took the horse with him. It was held that A was entitled to maintain replevin, because there was no sale. Rupley v. Daggett, 74 Ill. 351. But where the offerer makes a mistake in stating the price or in estimating the amount which he ought to charge, he must bear the loss caused by his own negligence, unless the offeree has knowingly taken advantage of his error. A asks B $60 for an article. B accepts. Thereafter A discovers that he should have asked $160. A is bound on his $60 offer. On this principle, a vendor's mistake in computing the total price of a list of articles which he is offering for a lump sum, or a mathematical error made by a contractor or his employee in preparing an estimate is no ground for avoidance of the contract where the other party acted in good faith. Tatum v. Coast Lumber Co., 16 Idaho 471, 101 Pac. 

957, 23 L. R. A. (N. S.) 1109; Steinmeyer v. Schroeppel, 226 Ill. 9, 80 N. E. 564, 10 L. R. A. (N. S.) 114. But where 58

REALITY OF CONSENT-MISTAKE 

$$ 56, 57 the offerer's mistake was known to the other party at the time of accepting the offer, or was so patent that it ought to have been apparent to him, the vendor or contractor is not bound by the offer. Tyra v. Cheney, 129 Minn. 428, 152 

N. W. 385; Davis v. Reisinger, 120 App. Div. 766, 105 

N. Y. S. 603; City of New York v. Dowd Lumber Co., 140 

App. Div. 358, 125 N. Y. S. 394; Curtis v. Albee, 167 N. Y. 

360, 60 N. E. 660. And in some cases, where the equities were strongly in favor of the party making the error, and the parties could easily be put in status quo, courts of equity have granted rescission upon some other ground. See 

Harper v. City of Newburgh, 159 App. Div. 695, 145 

N. Y. S. 59. These cases, however, are not intended to impair the well-established general rule that a party cannot avoid his contract obligation by showing that he said one thing and meant another or that he would not have made the offer which he actually did make if he had not made an error in calculating his cost and profit. Hayward v. Wemple, 152 App. Div. 195, 136 N. Y. S. 625, aff'd 

206 N. Y. 692, 99 N. E. 1108. 

§ 57. Mistake as to Subject Matter-Ownership. 

An agreement for the sale of property or some right or interest therein based on the erroneous assumption that the seller is the owner thereof is void for mistake. Martin v. 

McCormick, 8 N. Y. 331. This must be distinguished from a case where the sale is merely of such interest as the seller may have, as in the case of a quitclaim deed. A mistake as to title or ownership occurs only when the contract is made on the assumption of both parties that the vendor has title to that which he is attempting to sell. In practice, this rule is seldom invoked, for the reason that a seller of personal property impliedly warrants his title and a seller of real property either gives a deed with express covenants or merely a quitclaim deed. A buyer's usual remedy, if he has any, is, therefore, an action for breach of warranty or covenant. Williston on Contracts, Rev. Ed., Vol. V, secs. 

1565 and 1566. See Thomas v. Bartow, 48 N. Y. 193. 59

§  § 58. 

Remedies for Operative Mistake. 

The remedies afforded for operative mistake are intended to, and do, place the parties in status quo; i.e., the position the parties were in before the agreement was made. These remedies may be classified as follows: 

1. At Law. As the effect of a mistake preventing consent is to render the contract void, it may be relied upon as a complete defense to an action based thereon, and if the contract is executed in whole or in part, an action may be maintained to recover what has been paid. City of New 

York v. Dowd Lumber Co., 140 App. Div. 358, 125 N. Y. S. 

394; Wheadon v. Olds, 20 Wend. (N. Y.) 174. But no action · is maintainable for damages for breach of such a contract. 

2. In Equity. In equity a suit for specific performance may be resisted, or suit may be brought to have the con tract declared void. Duncan v. New York Mutual Insurance 

Co., 138 N. Y. 88, 33 N. E. 730, 20 L. R. A. 386. 

3. Concurrent. In certain cases, the legal and equitable remedies for operative mistake are concurrent. Thus, where plaintiff, through mistake induced by fraud, has signed a release of a claim, he may, at his option, sue in equity to cancel the release, or he may disregard the release as a void instrument, sue at law on his original claim, and defeat the defense of release, if raised, by showing that the instrument was executed by mistake. Boxberger v. New York, New 

Haven & Hartford R. R. Co., 237 N. Y. 75, 142 N. E. 357; 

Pimpinello v. Swift & Co., 253 N. Y. 159, 170 N. E. 530. 

See, also, Editorial, New York Law Journal, May 22, 1934. 60

CHAPTER IV REALITY OF CONSENT-MISREPRESENTATION § 59. Misrepresentation Defined. 

A misrepresentation, as the term is generally understood in the law of contracts, is an innocent misstatement of a material fact. Canadian Agency v. Assets Realization Co., 

165 App. Div. 96, 150 N. Y. S. 758. Although in its broadest sense misrepresentation includes an intentional false statement, in this chapter the term will be used exclusively to denote a mere misstatement of a material fact, unaccompanied by fraud. 

§ 60. Effect of Misrepresentation. 

A misrepresentation of a material fact renders the contract voidable, not void, at the option of the party thereby misled. It is immaterial that there may have been no actual intention to defraud. Seneca Wire & Manufacturing 

Co. v. A. B. Leach & Co., 247 N. Y. 1, 159 N. E. 700. For example, a contract for the sale of a large block of stock in the United States Worsted Company was rescinded on the ground that the seller had misrepresented the financial condition and past earnings of the said company, although it was not claimed that such misrepresentations were made fraudulently. Canadian Agency v. Assets Realization Co., 165 App. Div. 96, 150 N. Y. S. 758. 

§ 61. Misrepresentation and Operative Mistake Dis tinguished. 

A mistake which prevents consent renders a contract void, and not, as in the case of misrepresentation, voidable.

In operative mistake, the parties may apparently consent, while in reality there is no consent; in misrepresentation, the parties have actually consented to all of the terms of the contract, but the consent of one of the parties has been induced by a misstatement of the other. An operative mistake may be induced by a misrepresentation, in which case 61

$$ 61 

the contract is void; but, since operative mistake prevents consent, a misrepresentation cannot be predicated upon an operative mistake. 

§ 62. Misrepresentation and Fraud Distinguished. 

The distinction between misrepresentation and fraud lies in the fact that, in the case of fraud, the guilty party has made a false representation with knowledge of its falsity; whereas a misrepresentation lacks this element of knowledge. Furthermore, fraud is a tort, giving rise to an action for damages for deceit. In the case of a misrepresentation, no tort is committed, and, therefore, no action for damages for the misrepresentation will lie. 

§ 63. Misrepresentation Distinguished from Condition or Warranty. 

A misrepresentation differs from both a condition and a warranty in that a misrepresentation is a misstatement of a material fact which is an inducement to the making of a contract; whereas a condition or a warranty is a term of the contract. A condition is a term of a contract which is regarded as of the essence of the agreement, a breach of which discharges the other party from all liability under the contract. A warranty, in its strict sense, is a collateral promise or undertaking, a breach of which gives to the injured party a right to sue for damages, even though he has accepted the benefits of the contract with knowledge of the breach. Fairbank Canning Co. v. Metzger, 118 N. Y. 

260, 23 N. E. 372; Condict v. Onward Construction Co., 210 

N. Y. 88, 103 N. E. 886. However, the terms warranty and condition are frequently used interchangeably by the courts. 

Davison v. Von Lingen, 113 U. S. 40, 28 Law. Ed. 885, 5 Sup.

Ct. Rep. 346. This is true particularly in insurance and maritime law, where it is a settled practice to use the term warranty to denote a condition of the contract. Norrington v. Wright, 115 U. S. 188, 29 Law. Ed. 366, 6 Sup. Ct. Rep. 

12; Cady, Law of Insurance, 3d Ed., sec. 90. The distinction between a condition and a warranty has been lessened, 62

REALITY OF CONSENT-MISREPRESENTATION §§ 63, 64 

also, by the Uniform Sales Act, which provides that where there is a breach of warranty by the seller, the buyer, at his option, may rescind the contract or sue for damages. Uniform Sales Act, sec. 69; Personal Property Law, sec. 150.

The same statement, therefore, may be a misrepresentation, a condition, or a warranty. If the statement is not made part of the contract but is merely an inducement to the execution of the contract, it constitutes, if false, a misrepresentation. When the representation, however, is made a part of the contract, it is either a condition or a warranty. If it is of the essence of the agreement, it is a condition; if not, a warranty.

In a contract with a printer for business circulars intended for general distribution, a stipulation which provides for delivery at a specified hour ordinarily would be regarded as a subsidiary undertaking. A delay of two or three hours in making delivery would not be serious. 

But if the contract was for printing programs for a theatrical performance, a late delivery of a few hours would render the programs useless. Under such circumstances, the stipulation as to time is of the very essence of the contract. In the first instance, a failure to perform at the time agreed upon would be a breach of a warranty; in the latter, a breach of a condition. In one case a breach of the stipulation as to time does not warrant repudiation of the contract; while in the other it is ground for rescission. 

But, by their contract, parties may make material what otherwise would be immaterial or make immaterial what otherwise would be material. Davison v. Von Lingen, supra. 

§ 64. Remedies for Misrepresentation. 

A misrepresentation is sufficient to avoid a contract both at law and in equity. Where equitable relief is required, such as where a reconveyance of real property is sought, the action to rescind must be in equity. If, however, the party misled desires only the return of his money paid under the contract, the action should be at law. Seneca Wire & Manufacturing Co. v. A. B. Leach & Co., 247 N. Y. 1, 63

§  

159 N. E. 700. Since the contract is binding until avoided and may be ratified after the discovery of the falsity of the representations, the failure of the injured party to rescind promptly after full knowledge of the facts may deprive him of the right to avoid. Saratoga & Schenectady · 

R. R. Co. v. Row, 24 Wend. (N. Y.) 74, 35 Am. Dec. 598;

Susquehanna Mutual Fire Insurance Co. v. Oberholtzer, 

172 Pa. 223, 32 Atl. 1105. 64

CHAPTER V  

$ 65. Nature of Fraud.

The general signification of the term fraud is quite well understood. Yet it is difficult to give any satisfactory definition of it, as the courts have refused to lay down any rule beyond which they will not go. Equity refuses to limit its jurisdiction in cases of fraud. In 1745 Lord Chancellor

Hardwicke said: "The court very wisely hath never laid down any general rule beyond which it will not go, lest other means for avoiding the equity of the courts should be found out." So, also, in Du Flon v. Powers, 14 Abb. Pr. (N. S.) 391, the Court said that: "The devices resorted to, to qualify the appearance of fraud, like those intended to cover up usury, are often so painstaking and subtile as to betray the conscious guilt of the wrongdoer. As long ago as when Hovenden wrote it was declared to be dangerous to give a definition of fraud so inclusive and exclusive as to fix the limit, since the ingenuity of the unscrupulous would discover a means of evasion. Late writers have respected that view (1 Story Eq. J., sec. 186; 2 Pars. Cont., 5 ed., p. 

769), and the most respectable attempts to give such a definition have met with criticism. On the whole our courts have considered it most safe and wise to leave each case to be determined according to its own peculiar facts and circumstances, and the application of well settled principles." 

What is fraud depends upon the facts in each case. The rule of perfect morality which prohibits all cunning, craft, or astuteness practiced for one's own benefit is not enforced in any code of human laws. A person, in his dealings with his fellow men, may procure some advantages to which he has no moral right, but to which, nevertheless, he may succeed in establishing a legal right. To what extent, then, may this cunning and craft be carried? To this no specific answer can be given. One Court has said that: "It is impossible to frame a definition of fraud which will accurately 65

§§ 65,  

define it in all of its multitudinous forms." Marsh v. Cook, 32 N. J. Eq. 262. However, the general theory of the law is that there must be full and free consent in order to create a contractual obligation. Although the law will not generally inquire into men's acts and contracts to determine whether they are wise and prudent, yet it will not suffer persons to be entrapped by fraudulent contrivances, or cunning, or the deceitful conduct of those who purposely mislead them. 

§ 66. Fraud Distinguished from Other Elements Affecting 

Consent.

Of the five elements which relate to, and affect, consentoperative mistake, misrepresentation, fraud, duress, and undue influence-fraud is the only element which gives rise not alone to a right of rescission but also to an independent cause of action in tort for damages. Rescission and an action for damages, however, are inconsistent remedies, and the defrauded party must elect to pursue one or the other. He cannot avail himself of both. See section 74, infra. In this connection, it is well to note that operative mistake is the only element which renders a contract void; the remaining four elements, including fraud, render the contract merely voidable at the option of the injured party. 

Fraud, as discussed in this Chapter, has the effect of inducing a party to enter into a contract which he otherwise would not have done. The defrauded party consents to enter into the precise contract he intended to execute, but his consent is fraudulently obtained. 

Fraud resulting in operative mistake, considered in Chapter III, supra, is clearly distinguishable. Here no consent is given, for the mistake induced by the fraud prevents consent from being given to the contract. The following illustrations will serve to clarify this distinction: 

1. A vendor falsely represents that a particular stone is a diamond. The vendee, relying on the representation, agrees to purchase the "diamond," and executes and delivers to the vendor a promissory note for the purchase price. This 66

§§ 66, 

is a case of fraud inducing consent, for the vendee did execute the precise instrument he intended to execute, but his consent was induced by fraud. 

2. A swindler falsely represents that a particular instrument is a receipt. It is in fact a promissory note. Relying on the false representation and without negligence on his part, the victim signs the note in the mistaken belief that it is a receipt. The victim obviously did not consent to execute the note. This is, therefore, a case of fraud inducing a mistake which prevents consent and renders the contract void. See Whipple v. Brown Bros. Co., 225 N. Y. 237, 121 

N. E. 748; Gilbert v. Rothschild, 280 N. Y. 66, 19 N. E. (2d) 785. 

§ 67. Elements of Fraud. 

Although no definition has been formulated which covers fraud in all of its aspects, the courts have laid down definite requirements with respect to the elements which must be proved in order to establish fraud as an actionable wrong. 

Fraud which gives rise to an action for deceit may be defined as "a material false representation of fact, made with knowledge of its falsehood, or recklessly, without belief in its truth, with the intention that it should be acted upon by the complaining party, and actually inducing him to act upon it to his injury." Huffcut's Anson on Contracts, 2d 

Ed., sec. 220; Clark on Contracts, 3d Ed., sec. 139; Arthur v. Griswold, 55 N. Y. 400; Brackett v. Griswold, 112 N. Y. 

454, 467, 20 N. E. 376.

This definition resolves itself into the following essential features: 

1. Representation must be material. 

2. Representation must be false. 

3. Representation must be of a fact and not a mere expression of opinion or a promise. 

4. Representation must be made with knowledge of its falsehood, or with reckless disregard as to whether it be true or false. 67

§§ 67,  

5. 

Representation must be made with the intention that it be acted upon by the injured party. 

6. Representation must cause the other party to act in reliance upon it to his injury.

Some authorities have tabulated the elements of fraud a little differently, but all agree that every one of the six requisites given above must be clearly and decisively proved in order to maintain an action for fraud. See Southern Development Co. v. Silva, 125 U. S. 247, 31 Law. Ed. 678, 8

Sup. Ct. Rep. 881. The New York Court of Appeals has given its sanction to the division of fraud into five essential elements, tersely stated as "representation, falsity, scienter, deception and injury." Arthur v. Griswold, supra, at p. 410; Brackett v. Griswold, supra, at p. 467; Reno v. 

Bull, 226 N. Y. 546, 550, 124 N. E. 144. An analysis of the cases shows, however, that this division has been made by combining the first and third of the six elements given above. Wherever these six elements are present, fraud is established, which may be either the basis of an action. for damages or ground for rescission of a contract. Adams v. Gillig, 199 N. Y. 314, 92 N. E. 670, 32 L. R. A. (N. S.) 

127. There are many cases in which a party is entitled to rescind an agreement on the ground that his consent was obtained by the fraud of the other party, although one or more of these six elements are missing, but, in such cases as a general rule, relief is granted either on the ground of mistake induced by fraud or on the ground of misrepresentation not amounting to fraud. These subjects have been discussed in the two preceding chapters. In this chapter, therefore, we shall consider the six elements of fraud which must be proved in order to maintain an action for deceit and which are, also, ground for rescinding a contract and avoiding all liability under it. 

§ 68. 

Representation Must Be Material. 

The false representation must be material, and, as a rule, it is material if the contract would not have been made but for the false representation. In Smith v. Countryman, 68

REALITY OF CONSENT—FRAUD 

§ 68 30 N. Y. 655, the Court quotes with approval from 2 Parsons on Contracts 267, as follows: "If the fraud be such that had it not been practised, the contract would not have been made or the transaction completed, then it is material to it." It must be a substantial inducement. Trifling and immaterial representations will not induce a court to interfere. Dawe v. Morris, 149 Mass. 188, 21 N. E. 313, 4 L. R. A. 158. A case illustrating materiality of a false representation is Gordon v. Street, 2 Q. B. Div. (Eng.) 641 

(1899). In that case the plaintiff, a money lender, advertised under a fictitious name, and the defendant borrowed money and gave a promissory note to secure the sum borrowed and interest. In an action on the promissory note, the jury found that the plaintiff intentionally concealed his identity to induce the defendant to borrow money of him as if from another, and that the defendant was so induced; that the plaintiff acted fraudulently; that the defendant entered into the contract believing that he was doing so with a person of the name given by the plaintiff; and that the defendant repudiated the contract within a reasonable time after he discovered that the plaintiff was the person with whom he had contracted. 

It was argued for the plaintiff that these findings were immaterial because the fraud proved was not material to the contract sued on, since whether the defendant contracted with plaintiff or with any other lender of money to take a loan of £100 and pay £50 for it, it was the same thing to the defendant, for when the day of payment arrived, the defendant by law would have to pay money contracted to be paid to whomsoever he had contracted to pay it, and it mattered not to him who that person was. The Court held: "The first point which arises is not whether the fraud was material to the contract entered into, but whether the fraud was material to the inducement which brought about the contract; and, if so, the jury having found the fact of fraud, I cannot doubt that the fraudulent concealing of the plaintiff's name was that which induced the defendant to enter into the contract upon which he is now sued, and was therefore material to 69

68, 69 

the inducement. On what ground is it to be said that a defendant who has been induced by fraud into signing a contract cannot, when sued upon it, set up the well-known defense that he was induced by the fraud of the plaintiff to enter into it? If the fraud be material to the inducement, it appears to me plain that he can. And, further, I will say that to enter into a contract for a loan with a creditor such as Isaac Gordon * * * so that, when the day for payment arrives, the borrower can have no possible chance of a day's or even an hour's grace, but on the contrary has the certainty of being pestered with writs and threats of writs and bailiffs and bankruptcy notices, *** whereby life is rendered unbearable, and health is often injured, is by no means, in my opinion, the same thing as entering into a contract for a loan with a man who, when the day of payment arrives, does none of these things, but, on the contrary, deals in a fair and nonoppressive manner; and to contract with the oppressive class is very much to the detriment of the borrower. I am by no means prepared to say that the fraud in this case was not material to the contract itself." 

Representation Must Be False. 

§ 69. 

A false representation may consist of: 

1. An actual false statement; 2. 

Active or artful concealment; or 

3. An incomplete disclosure amounting to a suppression of the truth.

These three types of false representation will be discussed separately. 

1. Actual False Statement. 

The general rule is that one who has made an actual false statement of a fact which is known to him and unknown to the other party cannot disclaim liability for fraud on the ground that the other party should not have relied upon such statement. The weight of authority, both English and 

American, supports the doctrine that, under ordinary circumstances, one to whom a statement of fact is made is 70

§ 

entitled to rely absolutely on its truth, and, in the absence of any knowledge on his part or any facts which should arouse suspicion, is not bound to make an independent examination to verify it. A party asserting facts which are peculiarly within his own knowledge cannot complain that the other party has taken him at his word. Loverin v. Kuhne, 94 Conn. 219, 108 Atl. 554, 33 A. L. R. 848; 

Lewis v. Jewell, 151 Mass. 345, 24 N. E. 52; Mead v. Bunn, 32 N. Y. 275. In the case of a sale, for instance, where the purchaser either makes no examination of the property whatever or takes a mere cursory view of it, relying on the representation of the vendor as to its nature and qualities, it is no defense to the vendor, when his representations are discovered to have been false, to say that the vendee was negligent in relying on such statements instead of making an independent examination. Woodruff v. Day, 278 

Ill. 199, 115 N. E. 885; Schumaker v. Mather, 133 N. Y. 

590, 30 N. E. 755. The law does not, however, go so far as to hold that one to whom representations are made may shut his eyes to the truth which is staring him in the face. Where by the use of ordinary intelligence and the faculty of sight, the party claiming to have been deceived would have been enabled to detect the falsity of the statements made to him, he cannot claim that he was entitled to rely absolutely on such statements. For example, where the owner of a farm stated to a prospective purchaser that there was but one small plot of quack grass on the farm and it appeared that the purchaser, who was himself an experienced farmer familiar with quack grass, had walked all over the farm and had inspected fields in which the weed flourished in plain sight, it was held that the purchaser, by the exercise of ordinary diligence, could have discovered the falsity of the representation and, therefore, was not entitled to maintain an action for fraud. Long v. Warren, 68 N. Y. 426. The decision in this case is in harmony with well-recognized rules of law and has never been questioned, but the language of the opinion has been criticized by the same Court as too broad a statement of the 71

§ 

law applicable to the case. See Schumaker v. Mather, supra, at p. 596.

There is some conflict of authority as to the effect of a false statement of a fact which is a matter of public record, but the weight of authority now clearly supports the rule adopted by the New York Court of Appeals, in Mead v. 

Bunn, supra, that a contracting party has an absolute right to rely on the express statement of a fact which is known to the party making the statement and unknown to the former, and he is under no duty to verify such statement even though the fact stated be a matter of public record. For an able review of the authorities on this subject, see annotation following Loverin v. Kuhne, supra, reported in 33 A. L. R. 848, at pp. 917-941. 2. 

Active or Artful Concealment. 

A mere non-disclosure or silence ordinarily does not amount to fraud. Where there is no relation of trust or confidence, one is not bound to prevent the other party's self-deception; but if there be silence or active or artful concealment for the purpose of defrauding another, it is as effective in accomplishing the purpose as a false statement of fact. In an action for the purchase price of a span of mules, the buyer's defense was that he had rescinded the contract because of the fraud and deceit of the seller. The trial court found that the plaintiff, when he sold the mules, refused specifically either to warrant their soundness or to make any statement as to their condition, but told the defendant to examine them for himself. The defendant's examination failed to reveal that one of the mules. was suffering from a disease which the trial court deemed a latent and material defect. At the time of the sale, the seller knew of the existence of this disease. Immediately upon discovery of the disease, the buyer offered to return the mules to the seller. The Court held that the seller was not entitled to recover the purchase price. "Deceit can consist in silence, when honesty requires speech, as much as in speech itself." Salmonson v. Horswill, 39 S. D. 402, 164 72

§ 

N. W. 973; Grigsby v. Stapleton, 94 Mo. 423, 7 S. W. 421.

Where the manufacturer of a land roller concealed defects in the tongue of the machine by the use of putty and paint and thereby succeeded in selling it as a sound and safe machine, he was held guilty of deceit. Kuelling v. Roderick 

Lean Manufacturing Co., 183 N. Y. 78, 75 N. E. 1098, 2 

L. R. A. (N. S.) 303; Bench v. Sheldon, 14 Barb. 66. However, in the case of sales on credit, the mere failure of the buyer to disclose the fact that he is insolvent is not generally regarded as fraudulent. He may intend to pay when the bill matures even though he is insolvent at the time of purchase. While the purchase of goods on credit with no intention of paying for them is fraudulent, the mere fact of insolvency alone does not negative an intent to pay. German 

National Bank v. Princeton State Bank, 128 Wis. 60, 107 

N. W. 454, 6 L. R. A. (N. S.) 556; Hotchkin v. Third National Bank, 127 N. Y. 329, 27 N. E. 1050; Sinnott v. German-American Bank, 164 N. Y. 386, 58 N. E. 286. However, the fact that the buyer knew that he was hopelessly insolvent and that there was no probability that he would be able to pay permits a jury to infer that the buyer did not intend to pay. Whitten v. Fitzwater, 129 N. Y. 626, 29 N. E. 

298; Baldwin v. Childs, 249 N. Y. 212, 163 N. E. 737; Williston on Contracts, Rev. Ed., Vol. V, sec. 1521. 

3. An Incomplete Disclosure Amounting to a Suppression of the Truth.

The limit of the application of the third class of what may constitute fraud is not clearly defined, and there is much conflict in its application. But it is readily conceivable that a partial statement of fact may be of such character as to create a false impression, notwithstanding the truthfulness of the facts stated. As one Court has said, "Everybody knows that sometimes half a truth is no better than a downright falsehood." Rex v. Kylsant, 1 K. B. Div. (Eng.) 442 (1932). Thus, it is fraudulent suppression for a merchant, in preparing a financial statement of his business, to list accurately all his assets but to omit a part 73

§§ 69,  

of his liabilities, thereby showing his solvency, when, if all his liabilities had been included, the statement would have shown his insolvency. Stewart v. Wyoming Cattle 

Ranche Co., 128 U. S. 383, 32 Law. Ed. 439, 9 Sup. Ct. Rep. 

101; Brown v. Montgomery, 20 N. Y. 287, 75 Am. Dec. 404.

The dividing line as to when silence, or the suppression of the truth, amounts to fraud is much controverted, but it is quite comprehensively stated as follows: "It is true that silence as to a material fact is not necessarily, as matter of law, equivalent to a false representation. But mere silence is quite different from concealment; a suppression of the truth may amount to a suggestion of falsehood; and if, with intent to deceive, either party to a contract of sale conceals or suppresses a material fact, which he is in good faith bound to disclose, this is evidence of and equivalent to a false representation, because the concealment or suppression is in effect a representation that what is disclosed is the whole truth. The gist of the action is fraudulently producing a false impression upon the mind of the other party; and if this result is accomplished, it is unimportant whether the means of accomplishing it are words or acts of the defendant, or his concealment or suppression of material facts not equally within the knowledge or reach of the plaintiff." Stewart v. Wyoming Cattle 

Ranche Co., supra.

§ 70. Representation Must Be of a Fact and Not of Opinion or Promise. 

In order to constitute fraud, a representation must be a statement of a past or present fact and not a mere expression of opinion, expectation, prediction, belief, or promise. 

Even though it be false and known to be such, a representation is not actionable unless it is a representation of a past or present fact. Belka v. Allen, 82 Vt. 456, 74 Atl. 

91. "The reason is, that it is apparent from the subject matter, that what is thus stated as knowledge must be considered and understood, by the party to whom it is addressed, as an expression of strong belief only, because it 74

§ is a subject of which knowledge, in its strict sense, cannot be had." Page v. Bent, 2 Metc. (Mass.) 371, and authorities there cited. Thus, representations by a seller of corporate bonds that the same are good, or by a sophomore in college that he expects to graduate within a year, or by a seller of law books that portions of the same will be written by prominent members of the Bar are mere expressions of opinion insufficient to constitute fraud. Oberlander v. 

Spiess, 45 N. Y. 175; Podolsky v. Sandler, 161 N. Y. S. 

363; Powell Co. v. Bernstein, 164 N. Y. S. 795. Similarly, expressed belief as to how much wheat land will produce, or what it will cost to build a house, or flourishing statements of an auctioneer that land is fertile do not amount to fraud.

A good illustration of this principle is afforded by what is known as "dealers' talk." As a rule, expressions of opinion by a seller to the buyer, even though exaggerated, are not sufficient to constitute fraud. Arnold v. Norfolk & New 

Brunswick Hosiery Co., 148 N. Y. 392, 42 N. E. 980. It has been said that: "The law does not exact good faith from a seller in those vague commendations of his wares which manifestly are open to difference of opinion, which do not imply untrue assertions concerning matters of direct observation, and as to which it always has been 'understood,' the world over, that such statements are to be distrusted." 

Deming v. Darling, 148 Mass. 504, 20 N. E. 107, 2 L. R. A. 

743 and authorities cited. In this connection, a distinction is made between a representation as to the value of an article and a statement with respect to an extrinsic fact affecting the value. Thus, an assertion that property is worth so much, though untrue and known to be so by the vendor, is regarded as a mere expression of opinion and will not sustain an action of fraud, even though the purchaser relies upon the statement, unless some trick or artifice is employed to induce the vendee to forego independent inquiry. Chrysler v. Canaday, 90 N. Y. 272, 43 Am. Rep. 

166; Meritas Realty Co. v. Farley, 166 App. Div. 420, 151 

N. Y. S. 1052, aff'd 224 N. Y. 671, 121 N. E. 879. But a 75

§ 

statement in regard to any extrinsic fact affecting the value may be the basis of an action for fraud; e. g., a false assertion that the property cost a certain amount or that the vendor had been offered a certain sum for it. Fairchild v. 

McMahon, 139 N. Y. 290, 34 N. E. 779; Kohl v. Taylor, 62 Wash. 678, 114 Pac. 874, 35 L. R. A. (N.S.) 174. Similarly, it was held, in Southern Development Co. v. Silva, 125 U. S. 247, 31 Law. Ed. 678, 8 Sup. Ct. Rep. 881, that a statement with respect to the quantity of ore "in sight" in a mine was no more than an expression of opinion which, like the vendor's statements with respect to the value of the mine, constituted mere trade talk, and gave the buyer no right to rescind a contract for the sale of the mine on the ground of fraud. In Deming v. Darling, supra, it was held that representations that a bond "was of the very best and safest and was an A No. 1 bond," and that "the railroad mortgaged was good security for the bonds," though false and made in bad faith, afforded no ground for an action. A representation that a building is "fireproof" has been held to be a representation of fact and not a mere matter of opinion. 

Hickey v. Morrell, 102 N. Y. 454, 7 N. E. 321. Likewise, representations that the climate of a certain city is "very healthy" and that a large percentage of the inhabitants are wealthy have been held to be not mere expressions of opinion but representations of existing facts. Marshall v. Seelig, 49 

App. Div. 433, 63 N. Y. S. 355. The tendency of the courts is to restrain rather than to increase the liberty of "dealer's talk" or "puffing" in the sale of property. In Foote v. 

Wilson, 104 Kan. 191, 178 Pac. 430, the Court characterized such talk as an unwarrantable perversion of the truth. So, too, in a criminal prosecution for dishonest advertising through the mails, the Supreme Court of the United States held that even though the sellers give purchasers value received, they are guilty of fraud if by exaggerated advertising propaganda they have led the purchasers to expect more. The Court said: "Mere puffing, indeed, might not be within its meaning (of this, however, no opinion need be expressed), that is, the mere exaggeration of 76

$ the qualities which the article has; but when a proposed seller goes beyond that, assigns to the article qualities which it does not possess, does not simply magnify in opinion the advantages which it has but invents advantages and falsely asserts their existence, he transcends the limits of 'puffing' and engages in false representations and pretenses."

United States v. New South Farm & Home Co., 241 U. S. 

64, 60 Law. Ed. 890, 36 Sup. Ct. Rep. 505.

A representation as to the law applicable to a given transaction is, in its nature, generally a representation merely as to the opinion of the person and is therefore not sufficient to constitute fraud. Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Phinney, 

178 U. S. 327, 44 Law. Ed. 1088, 20 Sup. Ct. Rep. 906;

Bilafsky v. Conveyances' Title Insurance Co., 192 Mass. 

504, 78 N. E. 534. For example, a representation as to the legal effect of a written instrument is not a representation of an existing fact upon which an action for fraud can be based. However, it has been held that if one of the parties to a contract is ignorant of a matter of law involved therein, and the other knows that he is and takes advantage of the circumstance, the latter is guilty of fraud. Cooke v. 

Nathan, 16 Barb. 342; Ryon v. John Wanamaker, New 

York, Inc., 116 Misc. 91, 190 N. Y. S. 250, aff'd 235 N. Y. 

545, 139 N. E. 728. Moreover, the act of an agent of an insurance company in inducing a policy holder to surrender his rights by means of a false representation as to the law on the subject has been held to constitute fraud, where it appeared that the plaintiff had been induced to rely upon the superior knowledge possessed by the agent of the defendant company. Berry v. American Central Insurance Co. of St. Louis, 132 N. Y. 49, 30 N. E. 254. The rule that a misrepresentation of law is not sufficient to support an action for fraud is confined to pure questions of domestic law. 

If there is a mixed representation of law and fact or, since a representation as to foreign law is deemed one of fact, a representation as to the law of another state or country, relief may be granted. Van Slochem v. Villard, 207 N. Y. 

587, 101 N. E. 467. 77

§ 

In view of the requirement that a representation relate to an existing fact in order to constitute fraud, a statement as to what one intends to do in the future is not sufficient to constitute fraud, even though the party fails to do what he has stated he would do. Cerny v. Paxton & Gallagher Co., 78 Neb. 134, 110 N. W. 882, 10 L. R. A. (N.S.) 

640; Miller v. Sutliff, 241 Ill. 521, 89 N. E. 651, 24 L. R. A. (N.S.) 735; Bigelow v. Barnes, 121 Minn. 148, 140 N. W. 

1032, 45 L. R. A. (N.S.) 203.
