66; Anderson v. Hicks, 150 App. Div. 289, 134 N. Y. S. 1018. 

§ 124. Ratification. 

The general rule is that a contract, voidable by reason of the intoxication of one of the parties, may be ratified by him when he becomes sober. Johnson v. Harmon, 94 

U. S. 371, 24 Law. Ed. 271. He is allowed a reasonable time after he understands the nature and effect of the transaction in which to disaffirm it; but, if he takes no steps to disaffirm it within a reasonable time after he has such knowledge, he is deemed to have ratified it. Carpenter v. Rodgers, 61 

Mich. 384, 28 N. W. 156; Matz v. Martinson, 127 Minn. 262, 149 N. W. 370, L. R. A. 1915B 1121. 

§ 125. 

Validity of Contracts for Necessaries. 

A completely intoxicated person is generally placed on the same footing as an insane person in reference to liability 119

$$ 125 

for necessaries furnished him, and he is therefore liable on an implied contract in law for such necessaries. The implied contract arises by operation of law and is in no sense a true contract, but is quasi-contractual in nature. The obligation to pay for necessaries is based on humanity and necessity. Personal Property Law, sec. 83; McCrillis v. 

Bartlett, 8 N. H. 569. 

§ 126. Adjudication of Incompetency. 

By statute in New York, the Supreme Court is given jurisdiction of the custody of the person and the care of the property of a person incompetent to manage himself or his affairs in consequence of habitual drunkenness. Provision is made for the appointment of a committee for such a person. Civil Practice Act, sec. 1356, et seq. Similar statutory provisions exist in other states. During the existence of such commission, contracts entered into by the person so adjudged incompetent are void. Carter v. Beckwith, 128 

N. Y. 312, 28 N. E. 582; McCarthy v. Bowling Green Storage & Van Co., 182 App. Div. 18, 169 N. Y. S. 463.

SPENDTHRIFTS § 127. In General. 

The common law laid no restraint on the power of a spendthrift to contract. The same is true of the law of New 

York and of most states to-day. In several states, however, such persons may be placed under guardianship, in which event, they lose capacity to make contracts on their own behalf. See Sullivan v. Lloyd, 221 Mass. 108, 108 N. E. 

923; Sturgis v. Sturgis, 51 Ore. 10, 93 Pac. 696, 15 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1034.

MARRIED WOMEN § 128. In General. 

The common law disabilities of coverture, as to the capacity of a married woman to contract, have been almost 120

§§ 128 

entirely removed by statute. A married woman now has the same rights in this respect as a femme sole. See Domes tic Relations Law, section 51. 

ALIENS § 129. Rights of Aliens. 

An alien is one who is the subject of, and owes allegiance to, a foreign government. McGregor v. McGregor, 33 How.

Pr. (N. Y.) 456. He has the same power to contract that a citizen has, and he may sue and be sued. Bradstreet v. 

Oneida County Supervisors, 13 Wend. (N. Y.) 546. The former rule that an alien woman who marries a citizen becomes a citizen and that an American woman who marries an alien is expatriated is no longer the law since September 22, 1922, the change having been made by the "Cable" 

Act, 42 United States Statutes, sections 1021 and 1022; 8

United States Code Annotated, sections 367 to 370. Under the statute, an alien woman does not become a citizen by marriage to an American citizen nor does an American woman lose her American citizenship by marriage to an alien. Another provision of the Act is to the effect that naturalization of a husband no longer naturalizes his wife, but merely his minor children resident in the United States at the time of his naturalization or coming to the United States before reaching their majority with the intention of making the United States their permanent residence. $ 130. Contracts Involving Trade with Persons in Enemy Territory.

Following the common law rule, the United States, as soon as it declares war against another nation, prohibits all trade by persons resident in the United States with persons in enemy territory. The object is to cut off all communication between individuals of the belligerent states. The Rapid, 8 

Cranch (U. S.) 155; Scott, Cases on International Law, 2d

Ed., Part III, Chap. VII. While the subject is complex, in general it may be said that, between such persons, war sus- 121

§§ 130  

pends action under executed contracts, suspends or dissolves executory contracts, and terminates the capacity to make contracts. Scholefield v. Eichelberger, 7 Pet. (U. S.) 586; 

Sutherland v. Mayer, 271 U. S. 272, 70 Law. Ed. 943, 46 

Sup. Ct. Rep. 538; Wilson on International Law, 2d Ed., sec. 113. The tendency of the courts is to preserve and not to destroy contracts made before war. Therefore, contracts made before war will be merely suspended during the war, unless from their nature they are incapable of suspension, in which case, they will not revive at the close of the war. Watts, Watts & Co. v. Unione Austriaca di 

Navigazione, 248 U. S. 9, 63 Law. Ed. 100, 39 Sup. Ct. Rep. 1. 

§ 131. 

Alien Enemies as Plaintiffs.

Alien enemies are nationals of countries at war with the United States. The rights of alien enemies resident in the 

United States may be seriously circumscribed if such restriction is deemed advisable for our national security. Alien enemies may be subject to internment. Their property may be made subject to confiscation. Brown v. United States, 8 Cranch (U. S.) 110; Briggs, The Law of Nations, 800.

They may be refused the right to bring action as plaintiffs, on the theory that thereby they might strengthen the hands of the enemy. Hungarian General Creditbank v. Titus, 182 App. Div. 826, 169 N. Y. S. 926. The modern tendency, however, is to grant alien enemies access to the courts. "Objection to it in these days goes only so far as it would give aid and comfort to the other side." Birge-Forbes Co. v. Heye, 251 U. S. 317, 323, 64 Law. Ed. 286, 40 Sup. Ct. 

Rep. 160. Posselt v. D'Espard, 87 N. J. Eq. 571, 100 Atl. 893. § 132. 

Alien Enemies as Defendants.

An alien enemy may be sued on a contract entered into before the beginning of the war. The reason which denies him the right to sue can have no application where he is sued. As plaintiff, the courts are called upon to render him assistance which might aid his country; as defendant, the 122

§§ 132 

action is to his disadvantage. Compagnie Universelle de 

Telegraphie et de Telephonie sans Fil v. United States Service Corp., 84 N. J. Eq. 604, 95 Atl. 187, aff'd 85 N. J. Eq. 

601, 96 Atl. 292; Watts, Watts & Co. v. Unione Austriaca di 

Navigazione, 248 U. S. 9, 63 Law. Ed. 100, 39 Sup. Ct.

Rep. 1. 

CONVICTS 

Capacity To Contract. 

A convict undergoing a sentence of imprisonment, even for life, or awaiting execution is not thereby rendered incapable of making valid contracts. In New York, it is provided by statute that a person sentenced to imprisonment for life shall be deemed thereafter civilly dead. Penal Law, sec. 511. Similar provisions exist in other states. Such a person may dispose of his property by will or by deed and may make contracts, for the breach of which his estate. would be liable, although, on his part, the contract would be unenforceable. This is because the prisoner's civil rights are suspended; while the rights of creditors are not suspended. Stephani v. Lent, 30 Misc. 346, 63 N. Y. S. 471; 

Avery v. Everett, 110 N. Y. 317, 18 N. E. 148, 1 L. R. A. 

264; Byers v. Sun Savings Bank, 41 Okla. 728, 139 Pac. 

948, 52 L. R. A. (N.S.) 320.

As to the care of the estate of a person sentenced to life imprisonment, provision is made by statute for the appointment of a committee for such period of imprisonment. See 

Correction Law, section 320.

CORPORATIONS AND SOVEREIGNS § 134. 

Corporations.

A corporation has contractual capacity within its chartered powers. Contracts not within the corporation's chartered powers are said to be ultra vires. However, in New 

York, when the contract has been fully performed on the part of either party, the plea of ultra vires cannot be sus- 123

§§ 134, 135  

tained. Whitney Arms Co. v. Barlow, 63 N. Y. 62, 20 Am. 

Rep. 504; Holmes & Griggs Manufacturing Co. v. Holmes & 

Wessell Metal Co., 127 N. Y. 252, 27 N. E. 831. No attempt is here made to treat fully the contractual capacity of corporations, because the question properly belongs to corporation law. 

§ 135. 

Federal and State Governments. 

The capacity of the government of the United States and of each of the states to contract is co-extensive with the duties and powers of government. Therefore, for the purpose of government, they have full contractual capacity. 

This is incidental to the general rights of sovereignty. 

Wilson v. Shaw, 204 U. S. 24, 51 Law. Ed. 351, 27 Sup. Ct. 

Rep. 233; Danolds v. State of New York, 89 N. Y. 36, 42 

Am. Rep. 277. However, an action cannot be brought against the federal government, or against any other sovereign government without its consent. See Chisholm v. Georgia, 2 Dall. (U.S.) 419, 1 Law. Ed. 440. Furthermore, by virtue of the Eleventh Amendment to the Constitution of the United States, no action can be brought by a citizen of another state, or by a citizen or subject of any foreign state, against any state of the United States. Gables v. Girard, 46 Fed. 500; Hans v. Louisiana, 134 U. S. 1, 33 Law. Ed. 

842, 10 Sup. Ct. Rep. 504. The United States Supreme 

Court, however, has jurisdiction over actions between states of the United States. Federal Constitutio 

Federal Constitution, Art. III. The federal government has provided the Court of Claims for the adjustment of claims against the United States. The jurisdiction and procedure of the Federal Court of Claims are regulated by Acts of Congress. See 5 Federal Statutes 

Annotated, 2d Ed., sections 136-187; 28 United States Code

Annotated, sections 241-293. The states, by statutory provision, in many instances provide courts for the hearing and determination of claims against themselves. The organization and jurisdiction of the Court of Claims of New York are regulated by statute. See Court of Claims Act, sections 

2 and 12. 124

§ 

§ 136. Foreign States and Sovereigns. 

Foreign states and sovereigns may make contracts and sue thereon, but can be sued only when they voluntarily submit to the jurisdiction of the court. See Honduras v. 

Soto, 112 N. Y. 310, 19 N. E. 845; French Republic v.

Inland Navigation Co., 263 Fed. 410. Foreign ambassadors, ministers, and other diplomatic agents, as well as their officials and servants, are immune from criminal and civil prosecutions in our courts. See In re Baiz, 135 U. S. 403, 34 Law. Ed. 222, 10 Sup. Ct. Rep. 854. Severe penalties are imposed upon those who sue out any writ or process against such persons. 3 Federal Statutes Annotated, 2d 

Ed., sec. 4064; 22 United States Code Annotated, sec. 253.

This does not mean, however, that a nation would be obliged to tolerate the continued presence of a diplomatic representative who flouted its laws; the representative is supposed to respect the laws, and if a situation were to arise where the representative should show disregard for them, the nation could ask that he be recalled. The civil and criminal exemption, however, does not extend to consuls who are commercial, rather than diplomatic, agents. Bors v. Preston, 111 

U. S. 252, 28 Law. Ed. 419, 4 Sup. Ct. Rep. 407. 125

CHAPTER IX  § 137. Necessity of Legal Subject Matter. 

An agreement, to result in a contract, must create an obligation; if, therefore, the purpose or object of the contract is the performance or the doing of an illegal act, there can be no contract, because there is no obligation which the courts will enforce. A contract, though based on consent and contractual capacity, derives its obligatory force from the sanction of the law. It is therefore a fundamental principle of the law of contracts that a contract must have a lawful purpose. Buckley v. Humason, 50 Minn. 195, 52 

N. W. 385, 16 L. R. A. 423 and note. 

§ 138. 

Classification of Illegal Agreements. 

For convenience, illegal agreements may be classified as follows: 

1. Agreements in violation of a prohibitive statute; 

2. Agreements in violation of express rules of common law; 

3. Agreements in violation of public policy. 

Agreements in violation of common law and public policy are, however, so closely related as to be indistinguishable in many instances. Williston on Contracts, Rev. Ed., Vol.

V, sec. 1628. Agreements against public policy are illegal because they are in violation of common law. The term "public policy," as applied to this subject, is comprehensive and covers a wide range. It is evidenced by the trend of legislation, judicial decisions, and the principles of the common law. It embraces all acts or contracts which "tend clearly to injure the public health, the 'public morals, the public confidence in the purity of the administration of the law or which tend to undermine that sense of security for individual rights, whether of personal liberty or of private property, which any citizen ought to feel." Holland v. 

Sheehan, 108 Minn. 362, 122 N. W. 1, 23 L. R. A. (N. S.) 126

§§ 138, 139 

510; Johnston v. Fargo, 184 N. Y. 379, 77 N. E. 388, 7

L. R. A. (N.S.) 537. 

§ 139. Agreements in Violation of Statutes. 

A contract is illegal if it violates a constitutional statute or if it cannot be performed without a violation of such a statute. Thus, where a valid city ordinance, duly authorized and enacted, makes a particular act or business unlawful, or makes it unlawful for an unlicensed person to engage in a designated business, any contract made in violation thereof is void. Johnston v. Dahlgren, 166 N. Y. 354, 59 

N. E. 987; Sirkin v. Fourteenth Street Store, 124 App. 

Div. 384, 108 N. Y. S. 830; Fifth Avenue Coach Co. v. New 

York, 194 N. Y. 19, 86 N. E. 824, 21 L. R. A. (N. S.) 744.

In certain instances, however, where licenses are required by law, the courts permit recovery on contracts entered into by unlicensed persons, on the ground that the legislative intent was not to deprive offenders of their right to recover. 

Rosasco Creameries, Inc. v. Cohen, 276 N. Y. 274, 11 N. E. (2d) 908; Sajor v. Ampol, 275 N. Y. 125, 9 N. E. (2d) 803; 

Fosdick v. Investors Syndicate, Inc., 266 N. Y. 130, 194 

N. E. 58. See, also, sections 142 and 143, infra.

It is, however, only a violation of a constitutional statute which will render a contract illegal; and the mere fact that an act of the legislature is on the statute books is not conclusive of its constitutionality. The statute may be in contravention of the provisions of the federal or state constitution, or it may constitute an unlawful exercise of the police power of the state. The police power is not unlimited in scope; it may be exercised only for the protection of the public welfare, health, safety, and morality. To be valid the enactment must tend in some appreciable way to accomplish the purposes for which the power may be exercised. Under the guise of protecting public interests, the legislature cannot arbitrarily infringe upon the property rights or liberties of individuals, or interfere with private business, or impose unnecessary restrictions upon lawful occupations. Therefore, if the legislature has exceeded its 127

§§ 139  

police powers in the enactment of a statute, a contract in violation thereof would not be illegal. People ex rel. Wineburgh Advertising Co. v. Murphy, 195 N. Y. 126, 88 N. E. 

17, 21 L. R. A. (N.S.) 735; Lochner v. New York, 198 

U. S. 45, 49 Law. Ed. 937, 25 Sup. Ct. Rep. 539. In determining whether a particular statute is a proper exercise of the police power, the court will consider the social and economic circumstances which prompted its enactment. "Although an emergency may not call into life a power which has never lived, nevertheless emergency may afford a reason for the exertion of a living power already enjoyed." Wilson v. New, 243 U. S. 332, 61 Law. Ed. 755, 37 

Sup. Ct. Rep. 298; Home Building & Loan Assn. v. Blaisdell, 290 U. S. 398, 78 Law. Ed. 413, 54 Sup. Ct. Rep. 231. See, also, Nebbia v. New York, 291 U. S. 502, 78 Law. Ed. 940, 54 Sup. Ct. Rep. 505. 

§ 140. Status of Contract Violative of Constitutional 

Statute.

While a contract violative of a constitutional statute is illegal, it is not necessarily void. Contracts may be illegal for certain purposes, and yet enforceable in other respects. 

They are void only: 

1. Where the statute expressly declares them to be void; 

2. Where the purpose of the statute would be frustrated by the enforcement of the contract in question. 

§ 141. 

Contract Expressly Prohibited by Statute. 

When contracts are prohibited, the prohibition may be brought about by express words or by implication; and whether they are impliedly prohibited is a question of construction. Where a statute expressly prohibits an act, or expressly declares that certain kinds of contracts shall be void, there is no room for construction, because there is no doubt of the legislature's intention. Thus, in New York usurious and gambling contracts are void because they are expressly declared to be so by statute. General Business 

Law, sec. 373; Penal Law, secs. 991 and 992; Oppikofer 128

§§ 141, 142 

v. Murphy, 146 App. Div. 581, 131 N. Y. S. 168. So, also, where a statute declares void all contracts whereby an employee agrees to accept in advance of his employment something other than money, a contract in violation thereof is invalid. Hancock v. Yaden, 121 Ind. 366, 23 N. E. 253, 6 

L. R. A. 576; Levison v. Boas, 150 Cal. 185, 88 Pac. 825, 12 

L. R. A. (N.S.) 575, 583 and note. By statute in New York, a provision in a lease, exempting a landlord from all liability for his negligence or the negligence of his employees in the operation or maintenance of the premises, is void as against public policy, and wholly unenforceable. Real Property 

Law, sec. 234; Greenspan v. East 33d Street Realty Corp., 169 Misc. 353, 7 N. Y. S. (2d) 727, aff'd 256 App. Div. 565, 10 N. Y. S. (2d) 688; Hanfeld v. Broido, Inc., 167 Misc. 85, 3 N. Y. S. (2d) 463. Similarly, a contract to procure rebates from railroad companies for a shipper was held void where the statute "prohibited and declared to be unlawful" such contracts. It is not, however, necessary that a statute, in express words, declare an act void in order to render a contract violating it a nullity. Parks v. Jacob Dold Packing 

Co., 6 Misc. 570, 27 N. Y. S. 289. 

§ 142. Contract Prohibited by Implication. 

Frequently a statute imposes a penalty for the doing of an act, without either expressly prohibiting it or expressly declaring it illegal or void. Some courts hold that a penalty implies a prohibition; others hold that it is thus implied only when the statute is for the protection of the public welfare or for the prevention of fraud. The latter view is adopted in New York and in most jurisdictions. The question is largely one of legislative intention and interpretation. The courts will always consider the: 

1. Language of the statute; 

2. Subject matter; 

3. Wrong or evil sought to be remedied; 

4. Purpose to be accomplished.

If from all these it appears that it was the intention of the legislature to render the prohibited act void, the 129

§ 

courts will so hold. Pangborn v. Westlake, 36 Iowa 546; 

Burck v. Taylor, 152 U. S. 634, 38 Law. Ed. 578, 14 Sup.

Ct. Rep. 696. Thus, a contract for the payment of wages of employees otherwise than semi-monthly and in cash is void where the statute makes such companies liable to criminal prosecution for failure to pay at such time and in such manner. New York Central & Hudson River R. R. 

Co. v. Williams, 199 N. Y. 108, 92 N. E. 404, 35 L. R. A. (N.S.) 549. So, also, the imposition, by statute, of a penalty for a violation of an excise law which is enacted for public protection, renders a contract in violation thereof void by implication. Griffith v. Wells, 3 Denio (N. Y.) 

226. Likewise, where the charter of a bank forbids the taking of more than six per centum as interest, but does not expressly declare a contract for more to be void, such a contract may nevertheless be declared void as contravening the statute. Bank of United States v. Owens, 2 Pet. 

Where, however, a statute imposes a penalty merely for the purpose of revenue and not for public protection, as in the case where a tax or license fee is required to be paid before engaging in business or where a revenue stamp is required to be affixed to an instrument, a contract, though in violation of the statute, is valid. The reason advanced is that the doing of the act penalized by statute is permitted upon payment of the penalty; that the penalty is a species of license money exacted for the privilege of doing the act; and that the act is not otherwise unlawful unless expressly so declared. Hughes v. Snell, 28 Okla. 828, 115 

Pac. 1105, 34 L. R. A. (N. S.) 1133. But where the statute expressly declares the act unlawful, there can be no recovery whatsoever for the illegal services performed. Plumber's case, Johnston v. Dahlgren, 166 N. Y. 354, 59 N. E. 987. 

In Uhlmann v. Kin Daw, 97 Ore. 681, 193 Pac. 435, the 

Court said: "Of course, if a statute expressly declares that an agreement made in contravention of it is void, then the inquiry is at an end; but, in the absence of such a declara- 130

§§ 142, 143 

tion, the court may take the statute by its four corners and carefully consider the terms of the statute, its object, the evil it was enacted to remedy, and the effect of holding agreements in violation of it void, for the purpose of ascertaining whether it was the legislative intent to make such agreements void; and if from all these considerations it is manifest that the lawmakers had no such intention, the agreements should be held to be legal contracts and enforceable as such." 

§ 143. Statutes Requiring License To Engage in Profes sion, Trade, or Business.

Where a statute or an ordinance makes the practice of a particular profession or the carrying on of a particular business unlawful for unlicensed persons, any contract made in such profession or business by one not licensed is generally void. The license is required for public protection and not for revenue purposes only. It protects in that it prevents improper and unqualified persons from acting in a particular capacity. Thus, a person acting without license as an attorney-at-law cannot recover for his services. Penal Law, secs. 270 and 271; Kaplan v. Berman, 37 Misc. 502, 75 

N. Y. S. 1002; Buxton v. Lietz, 139 N. Y. S. 46; Matter of 

Co-operative Law Co., 198 N. Y. 479, 92 N. E. 15, 32

L. R. A. (N.S.) 55. The same doctrine applies to unlicensed physicians, dentists, and school teachers. Accetta v. Zupa, 54 App. Div. 33, 66 N. Y. S. 303; O'Beirne v. 

Carey, 150 N. Y. S. 666; O'Connor v. Francis, 42 App. 

Div. 375, 59 N. Y. S. 28.

The same principle has been applied where, for the protection of the public, a license is required in order to carry on a particular trade or business, such as that of pawnbroker, plumber, undertaker, or the like. Thus, the contracts of a pawnbroker who fails to take out a license or to comply with the requirements of the statutes and ordinances as to recording the contracts which he makes with his customers, are void, and he can claim no lien upon the pledges which he has received. Levison v. Boas, 150 Cal. 131

§§ 143, 144  

185, 88 Pac. 825, 12 L. R. A. (N.S.) 575. In Johnston v. 

Dahlgren, 166 N. Y. 354, 59 N. E. 987, the Court held that a failure to comply with the statute providing for the registration of plumbers precluded a recovery for work performed by a plumber. Undertaker's case, People v. Ringe, 197 N. Y. 143, 90 N. E. 451, 27 L. R. A. (N. S.) 528. The provisions of the Real Property Law requiring real estate brokers to obtain licenses are constitutional, and an unlicensed broker cannot recover in an action for services rendered. Real Property Law, sec. 440-a.

However, in certain instances, the courts will uphold the contractual rights of persons who fail to procure a license as prescribed by statute. Lack of legislative intent to make such contracts void is the basis of such decisions. Thus, where a milk dealer failed to procure a license as provided by statute, a recovery of the agreed and reasonable value of milk sold and delivered was permitted. Rosasco Creameries, Inc. v. Cohen, 276 N. Y. 274, 11 N. E. (2d) 908. See, also, Fosdick v. Investors Syndicate, Inc., 266 N. Y. 130, 194 

N. E. 58.

The legislature has not the power to impose a license or other restriction on a person as a prerequisite to engaging in a certain business where the occupation to which it relates is of such a character that the public interest and welfare do not require its regulation. If the legislature has thus attempted to exercise its power by statutory regulation, the statute is unconstitutional. Fisher Co. v. Woods, 187 N. Y. 

90, 79 N. E. 836, 12 L. R. A. (N.S.) 707; Lincoln Trust Co. v. Williams Building Corp., 229 N. Y. 313, 128 N. E. 209. 

§ 144. Statutes Regulating Dealings in Articles of Com merce.

Statutes regulating dealings in articles of commerce have been held to render void sales which contravene their provisions; e.g., statutes requiring weights and measures to be approved and sealed by the proper officer, or requiring goods to be inspected, branded, labeled, tagged, and weighed. These statutes are for the protection of the 132

§§ 144, 145 

public against fraud and imposition from sales of spurious articles. No recovery can be had for articles sold in violation of the statute. Gaulding Fertilizer Co. v. Driver, 99 

Ga. 623, 25 S. E. 922; Bisbee v. McAllen, 39 Minn. 143, 39 

N. W. 299. For a list of properties, the use of which has been controlled by statute, see Lincoln Trust Co. v. Williams 

Building Corp., 229 N. Y. 313, 317, 128 N. E. 209. 

§ 145. Contracts in Violation of Sunday Laws. 

The common law does not prohibit the making of contracts on Sunday. Therefore, in the absence of a statute, a contract made on Sunday is as valid as if made on any other day. The statute is the only guide for determining the validity of Sunday contracts. Due to the want of uniformity in the phraseology of the statutes, there is much conflict of authority in the decisions in different states as to the validity of a contract which is in violation of Sunday laws. The main provisions of the New York law are found in the following sections of the Penal Law:

Sunday labor. Section 2143. All labor on Sunday is prohibited, except the works of necessity or charity. In works of necessity or charity is included whatever is needful during the day for the good order, health, or comfort of the community. 

The Penal Law, section 2146, further provides that all trades, manufactures, agricultural or mechanical employment upon the first day of the week are prohibited, except when they constitute works of necessity, in which case they may be performed on that day in their usual and orderly manner, so as not to interfere with the repose and religious liberty of the community. 

Other provisions to be noted in the Penal Law are as follows: Section 2144 protects one who labors on Sunday in a quiet manner, if he observes another day of the week as holy time. Section 2145 prohibits public sports on Sunday, but permits baseball games after two o'clock in cities. which permit the same. Section 2147 prohibits, with limitations, the sale of food and refreshments on Sunday. Sec- 133

$ 

tions 2152 and 2154 prohibit theatrical and motion picture exhibitions and the like on Sunday, except that cities may permit motion picture exhibitions after two o'clock. Section 2152-a authorizes, within certain limitations, the performance of concerts and recital dances after two o'clock on Sunday, provided the legislative body of the city, town, or village permits such concerts and dances. Section 2153 prohibits barbering on Sunday throughout the state. 

These sections do not condemn or render void a contract made on Sunday. A deed delivered on Sunday is valid. 

McCormick v. Hazard, 77 Misc. 190, 136 N. Y. S. 91. Private sales, as distinguished from public sales, are legal, for a private sale, where the property is not "exposed to sale," is not within the provisions of the statute. Penal 

Law, sec. 2147; Eberle v. Mehrbach, 55 N. Y. 682; Batsford v. Every, 44 Barb. 618. Selling or publicly offering real property for sale on Sunday is not prohibited, because such acts were lawful at common law and are not condemned by statute. The rule is that anything not prohibited by statute may lawfully be done on Sunday. People v. Dunford, 207 N. Y. 17, 100 N. E. 433.

Since all labor on Sunday, except works of necessity and charity, is prohibited, a contract which requires prohibited services to be performed on Sunday is void. Thus, one who teaches music or instructs in the art of photography on Sunday cannot recover for services rendered. Bilordeaux v. H. Bencke Lithographing Co., 30 N. Y. St. Rep. 656, 9 

N. Y. S. 507. So, also, a contract by which an opera singer agrees to perform on a Sunday is void because the service is labor within the meaning of the statute. Hallen v. Thompson, 48 Misc. 642, 96 N. Y. S. 142. But in Key System 

Institute v. Weissman, 117 Misc. 295, 191 N. Y. S. 320, the

Court permitted recovery on a contract to give instruction in the rudiments of education, though instruction on Sunday was contemplated. Paying a debt, making a will, and settling litigation or disputes are not condemned because the acts are not regarded as labor within the meaning of the 134

§§ 145 

Sunday laws. Rapp v. Reehling, 124 Ind. 36, 23 N. E. 777, 7 L. R. A. 498; Shank v. Shoemaker, 18 N. Y. 489.

As a rule, whatever must be done in order to preserve life, health, or property, and which must be done on Sunday if done at all, is a work of necessity or charity as thus defined. But if the work is only to prevent a loss on a secular day, as where a mill or wheel is cleaned on Sunday because to do so on another day would close the mill, it is not a work of necessity. McGrath v. Merwin, 112 Mass. 467, 17 Am. 

Rep. 119.

There is no liability to pay for services rendered on Sunday during prohibited hours. The Sunday act has been held constitutional in New York and in most jurisdictions, but unconstitutional in Illinois and certain other states. People v. Havnor, 149 N. Y. 195, 43 N. E. 541; Eden v. People, 161 Ill. 296, 43 N. E. 1108, 32 L. R. A. 659; State v. Murray, 104 Neb. 51, 175 N. W. 666, 8 A. L. R. 563. 

§ 146. Sunday Newspapers. 

Contracts for advertising in Sunday newspapers are expressly declared to be valid by statute. General Business 

Law, sec. 333. Aside from such statutory provision, it has been held that publishing a newspaper on Sunday is a work of necessity. The opinion is based on the great service the press renders to humanity on Sunday as well as on every other day of the week, and the view that its beneficence is more potent on Sunday than on any other day because of the leisure afforded for reading. Pulitzer Publishing Co. v. 

McNichols, 181 S. W. (Mo.) 1, L. R. A. 1916C 1148. 

§ 147. Holidays Other Than Sunday. 

All contracts and business transactions made and performed on a legal holiday other than Sunday are valid. Negotiable instruments maturing on a legal holiday become payable on the next succeeding business day. However, public offices of the state and of the counties must be closed on legal holidays. Page v. Shainwald, 169 N. Y. 246, 62 

N. E. 356, 57 L. R. A. 173. 135

§§ 147, 148  

The following days are declared holidays by the General 

Construction Law, section 24: New Year's Day; Lincoln's and Washington's birthdays; Memorial Day; the Fourth of July; Labor Day; Columbus Day; Armistice Day; Christmas Day; and if any of such days falls on Sunday, the next day thereafter is deemed a holiday. General election day and Thanksgiving Day are also holidays under the statute.

In accordance with the provisions of the General Construction Law, section 24-a, which deals solely with banking organizations, Saturdays from June thirtieth to Labor Day may be made holidays. The term half-holiday is applied to each Saturday from noon until midnight if it is not otherwise a holiday. The provision in the General Construction 

Law, section 25, which allows "the payment of money or the performance of a condition" which falls on a holiday to be performed on the next succeeding business day does not apply to a Saturday half-holiday, which for this purpose is not regarded as a holiday. Andes Co-operative Dairy Co. v.

Commercial Casualty Insurance Co., 207 App. Div. 102, 201 

N. Y. S. 664, aff'd 237 N. Y. 622, 143 N. E. 767.

GAMBLING OR WAGERING CONTRACTS § 148. General Nature of Gambling and Wagering Con tracts.

A gambling contract arises between parties who agree to play by cards, dice, or other contrivance whereby one shall lose and the other gain. "Illegal gambling implies gain and loss between the parties by betting, such as would excite a spirit of cupidity." Harris v. White, 81 N. Y. 

532, 539.

A wager is a promise to give something upon the determination or ascertainment of an uncertain event in which the parties have no interest except that which they create by the wager. Jordan v. Kent, 44 How. Pr. (N. Y.) 206. 

See, also, Cooney v. Hauck, 112 Kan. 562, 211 Pac. 617, 29 

A. L. R. 427. 136

§§ 149, 150 

§ 149. Legal at Common Law. 

Wagering and gambling contracts were valid at common law. Da Costa v. Jones, 2 Cowper (K. B. Div.) 729, 98 

Eng. Rep. 1331; Thomson v. Hayes, 59 Misc. 425, 111 

N. Y. S. 495; Campbell v. Richardson, 10 Johns. (N. Y.) 

406. But public gaming houses were held to be nuisances because they bred idleness and brought disorderly persons together in great numbers. Ordinary betting is not a crime, but keeping a gambling house is punishable as such. Penal 

Law, sec. 973; People ex rel. Lichtenstein v. Langan, 196 

N. Y. 260, 89 N. E. 921; Thomson v. Hayes, supra. 

§ 150. Illegal by Statute. 

Statutes in New York and in most states now expressly declare gaming and wagering contracts to be void. The

State Constitution in New York, Article I, section 9, now provides, inter alia: "No lottery or the sale of lottery tickets, pool-selling, book-making, or any other kind of gambling, except pari-mutuel betting on horse races as may be prescribed by the legislature and from which the state shall derive a reasonable revenue for the support of government, shall hereafter be authorized or allowed within this state." 

Pari-mutuel as defined by the New Century Dictionary is "A form of betting, as on horseraces, in which those who bet on the winners divide the bets or stakes, less a small percentage for the managers."

Pursuant to the constitutional provision, the legislature enacted a statute, effective March 1, 1940, prescribing conditions under which pari-mutuel betting shall be lawful, "notwithstanding the provisions of any other law, general, special or local." Laws of 1940, Chap. 254, Art. I, secs. 

1-25; Art. II, secs. 35-55; Art. III, secs. 56-62. 

The Penal Law, section 992, provides that all contracts for, or on account of, any money or property wagered, bet, or staked shall be void. And the Penal Law, section 993, provides that securities of whatsoever nature given for money lost at gaming, "shall be utterly void." Therefore, notes, checks, mortgages, conveyances, etc., given in pay- 137

S$ 150, 151 

ment of gambling losses are void. Hollingsworth v. Moulton, 53 Hun 91, 6 N. Y. S. 362, aff'd 119 N. Y. 612, 23 N. E. 

1143; Larschen v. Lantzes, 115 Misc. 616, 189 N. Y. S. 137.

The gambling statute also imposes a penalty for exacting payment of money or property won at cards, faro, etc. 

Penal Law, sec. 989. 

Property staked or paid under a bet or wager may be recovered of the stakeholder or winner. Penal Law, sec. 994; 

Kohler v. Rosenthal, 135 App. Div. 438, 120 N. Y. S. 325.

But where, under this section, plaintiff, a casual better, sued defendant, a professional gambler, to recover money paid for bets, the defendant was not permitted to set off his losses against the plaintiff's claim, on the ground that the legislature intended to discourage gambling and, hence, could not have intended to confer the benefits of the section upon a professional gambler. Watts v. Malatesta, 262 

N. Y. 80, 186 N. E. 210, 88 A. L. R. 1072.

Losers of certain sums at a gaming table may recover their losses. Penal Law, sec. 995. 

§ 151. Wager Contracts Disguised as Future Sales. 

A form of wager often difficult to detect exists where an apparent contract of sale is made for future delivery, in which the parties do not intend delivery of the thing contracted for, but expect to settle the contract by paying and receiving the difference between the market price and the price agreed upon. Such a contract is in reality a bet or a wager on the market price at the time fixed. But if the parties intend, in good faith, a performance of the contract by actual delivery of the property and payment therefor according to the terms of the contract, it will be valid. The actual intention of the parties may be difficult to prove or disprove; but when once the intention is established, there is no difficulty in applying the law. West v. Wright, 86 Hun 

436, 33 N. Y. S. 898; Weld v. Postal Telegraph-Cable Co., 199 N. Y. 88, 92 N. E. 415; Mohr v. Miesen, 47 Minn. 228, 49 N. W. 862. 138

$$ 152, 153 

§ 152. 

actions.

Construction of Option Contracts and Stock Trans- 

Option contracts which can be interpreted as legal in their intent will not be readily condemned. The courts will not presume an illegal intent on the part of the parties. In 

Story v. Salomon, 71 N. Y. 420, 422, the Court said: "We may guess that the parties were speculating upon the fluctuations in the price of the stock, and that the defendant was not to be required to take or deliver any stock in any case, but simply to pay the difference. But a contract which can have a legal interpretation and effect should not be condemned, without any proof, in that way." Taylor & Company's Estate, 192 Pa. 304, 43 Atl. 973; Hurd v. Taylor, 181 

N. Y. 231, 73 N. E. 977. To make a future sale a wager, the intention to settle the differences must be entertained by both parties to the transaction; such an intention by one party alone will not invalidate the contract. Springs v. 

James, 137 App. Div. 110, 121 N. Y. S. 1054, aff'd 202 N. Y. 

603, 96 N. E. 1131. 

§ 153. 

Lotteries. 

"A lottery' is a scheme for the distribution of property by chance, among persons who have paid or agreed to pay a valuable consideration for the chance, whether called a lottery, raffle, or gift enterprise or by some other name." Penal

Law, sec. 1370. When a theater patron buys a ticket which entitles him to witness a motion picture and at the same time gives him a chance to win a money prize, the transaction falls within the provisions of the law, since payment for the ticket constitutes a valuable consideration for the chance. People v. Miller, 271 N. Y. 44, 2 N. E. (2d) 38. For other "Bank 

Night" cases, see 23 Minnesota Law Review 101. So, too, a scheme providing for the distribution of money and cigars among purchasers of certain brands of cigars, such articles to be awarded to those who should most closely estimate the number of cigars of all brands upon which the government would collect taxes during a month named, has been held to be embraced within the statutory prohibition against a lot- 139

§§ 153  tery. People ex rel. Ellison v. Lavin, 179 N. Y. 164, 71 N. E. 

753, 66 L. R. A. 601. "A lottery is unlawful and a public nuisance." Penal Law, sec. 1371. The Penal Law, section 

1383, provides that money paid for lottery tickets may be recovered; section 1385 declares null and void every conveyance of property in pursuance of a lottery. 

USURIOUS CONTRACTS § 154. At Common Law. 

At common law, any rate of interest agreed upon was enforceable, but to protect necessitous borrowers against unconscionable exactions, usury laws, prescribing a legal rate of interest, have been enacted in most states. General Business Law, sec. 370, et seq. To constitute usury, there must be an agreement to pay more than the legal rate of interest. 

Bill v. Fish, 40 Hun 639, 1 N. Y. St. Rep. 473. In the great majority of usurious transactions a subterfuge of one kind or another is resorted to for the purpose of giving the transaction a legal appearance. Such appearance, however, never deters the court from pronouncing the transaction usurious, as soon as that fact appears. Schanz v. 

Sotscheck, 167 App. Div. 202, 152 N. Y. S. 851; Diehl v. 

Becker, 227 N. Y. 318, 125 N. E. 533. But clear and cogent proof is necessary to establish the defense of usury. 

Coast Finance Corp. v. Ira F. Powers Furniture Co., 105 

Ore. 339, 209 Pac. 614, 24 A. L. R. 855. 

§ 155. By Statute. 

The New York statute provides that, with certain specific exceptions, usury renders void all bonds, bills, notes, and other contracts or securities. There is no right to recover for money loaned where the contract is tainted with usury. 

General Business Law, sec. 373; Oppikofer v. Murphy, 146 

App. Div. 581, 131 N. Y. S. 168. But the usury laws are not restricted to cases of actual loans of money; they apply to agreements for the "forbearance of any money" as well. 

General Business Law, sec. 370. Hence, a usurious guarantee of a mortgage and an extension agreement, both executed 140

§§ 155, 155a as part of one transaction, are void, even though preceded by no loan of money. London v. Toney, 263 N. Y. 439, 189 N. E. 485. 

If a promissory note be void in its inception for usury, it remains void even though it subsequently comes into the hands of a bona fide purchaser for value. Sabine v. Paine, 223 N. Y. 401, 119 N. E. 849, 5 A. L. R. 1444. The same rule, however, is not applicable where a bank, in good faith, for full value, and without notice of any defects, discounts a note. In such case, the transaction is purged of usury and the note is a valid and enforceable obligation. Weaver 

Hardware Co. v. Solomovitz, 235 N. Y. 321, 139 N. E. 353; 

Schlesinger v. Lehmaier, 191 N. Y. 69, 83 N. E. 657. Special statutes protect banking institutions against complete loss even where there is a violation of the usury law with knowledge by the bank. Instead of declaring the usurious transaction void, the law imposes penalties; namely, the forfeiture of the entire interest which the usurious instrument carries or which has been agreed upon; if a greater rate of interest has been paid, recovery of twice the entire amount of the interest thus paid is permitted. United States Revised Statutes, secs. 5197 and 5198; 12 United States Code 

Annotated, secs. 85 and 86 (national banks); Banking Law, sec. 108, (state banks and trust companies); sec. 173, (private bankers); sec. 293-a, (industrial banks); sec. 510-a, (investment companies). See, also, City National Bank v. 

Lake Construction Co., 227 App. Div. 85, 237 N. Y. S. 58. 

§ 155a. 

Interest Rates Permitted by Statute. 

In New York, the rate of interest upon loans and forbearances "of any money, goods, or things in action, except as otherwise provided by law" is six per centum per annum. 

General Business Law, sec. 370. By statute, higher rates of interest on specified types of transactions are legal. Thus, state banks and trust companies, by complying with certain provisions, may charge interest of twelve per centum per annum on personal loans of limited maximum amounts. 

Banking Law, sec. 108, subd. 2. 141

§§ 155a, 156  

Corporations and joint stock companies are prohibited from interposing usury as a defense. General Business 

Law, sec. 374; Hubbard v. Tod, 171 U. S. 474, 43 Law. Ed. 

246, 19 Sup. Ct. Rep. 14. So, also, indorsers and guarantors or sureties of corporate obligations cannot interpose usury as a defense. Salvin v. Myles Realty Co., 227 N. Y. 

51, 124 N. E. 94, 6 A. L. R. 581. See Jenkins v. Moyse, 254 

N. Y. 319, 172 N. E. 521, 74 A. L. R. 205.

A lender, by agreement with the borrower, has a right to deduct from the amount loaned, the actual expenses incurred in making the loan and such sums as will meet future expenses in connection therewith, reasonably and honestly to be anticipated. Brown v. Robinson, 224 N. Y. 301, 120 N. E. 

694, 21 A. L. R. 777. Moreover, an agreement to pay an amount which may be more or less than the legal rate of interest, depending upon a reasonable contingency, is not ipso facto usurious. The question in each case is whether there has been good faith or an intent to evade the usury laws. Hartley v. Eagle Insurance Co. of London, England, 

222 N. Y. 178, 118 N. E. 622.

In the case of demand loans of not less than $5,000 secured by specified types of collateral, it is lawful to receive or to contract for any rate of interest which the parties may agree upon in writing. General Business Law, sec. 379; 

Banking Law, sec. 108, subd. 3.

For provisions as to licensed lenders of loans up to $300, see the Banking Law, section 352. For loans of savings and loan associations to members, see the Banking w, section 380. As to pawnbrokers, see the General Business 

Law, section 46.

For any excess paid for a loan or forbearance over the amount specified by statute, an action for recovery against the person who has received the usurious payment is permitted. General Business Law, sec. 372. 

§ 156. Compound Interest. 

Agreements from time to time to pay interest on interest which has already become due is not usurious. However, an 142

§§ 156 

agreement in advance for the payment of interest upon interest, as the same shall accrue, is invalid, not because it is usurious but because it is against public policy, as it might be made oppressive to the debtor. Stewart v. Petree, 55 

N. Y. 621, 14 Am. Rep. 352; Reusens v. Arkenburgh, 135 

App. Div. 75, 119 N. Y. S. 821. See, also, Editorial, New 

York Law Journal, July 31, 1940. 

§ 157. Usury as a Crime. 

The offense of taking certain property as security for usurious loans is a misdemeanor. Penal Law, sec. 2400.

AGREEMENTS AGAINST PUBLIC POLICY § 158. Agreement To Commit Crime. 

An agreement to commit a crime or a promise to give a reward for the commission of a crime is clearly void. But in addition to agreements to commit acts expressly made criminal or illegal by statute, there is a large class of contracts said to be void as against public policy. 

§ 159. Public Policy Defined. 

There is no precise definition of public policy, and consequently no absolute rule by which a contract can be measured or tested to determine whether or not it is contrary to public policy. Each case, as it arises, must be judged and determined according to its own circumstances. Veazey v. 

Allen, 173 N. Y. 359, 368, 66 N. E. 103, 62 L. R. A. 362.

The public policy of the state or of the nation is to be found in its constitution, statutes, and judicial decisions. United 

States v. Trans-Missouri Freight Assn., 166 U. S. 290, 

41 Law. Ed. 1007, 17 Sup. Ct. Rep. 540. Under the common law, public policy will not permit individuals to obligate themselves by a contract either to do or not to do anything, when the thing to be done or omitted is, in any degree, clearly injurious to the public. The test is the evil tendency of the contract and not its actual injury to the public in a particular instance. In Messersmith v. American Fidelity Co., 232 N. Y. 161, 133 N. E. 432, 19 A. L. R. 143

§§ 159, 159a  

* * 

876, the Court of Appeals, Cardozo, J., declared: "The public policy of this state when the legislature acts is what the legislature says it shall be. * 

Courts are slow to substitute their own varying views of policy for those which have found embodiment in settled institutions, in every day beliefs and practices, which have taken root and flourished. * * The field of discretion is still narrower when there has been statutory sanction, tacit, if not express, of callings and forms of conduct which it would have been easy to condemn." Steen v. Modern Woodmen of America, 296 Ill. 104, 129 N. E. 546, 17 A. L. R. 406.

The following are instances of agreements against public policy: An agreement to influence legislation is void, although the legislation sought may be clearly beneficial. An agreement to influence an appointment to office is void, although the intent may be to secure the best qualified person. So, also, agreements whose tendency is to establish a monopoly are void, although, in the particular case, they may not destroy competition or enhance prices. The law looks to the general tendency of such agreements, and it closes the door to temptation by refusing them recognition in any of its courts. Providence Tool Co. v. Norris, 2 Wall. (U. S.) 45, 17 Law. Ed. 868. An agreement made at the inception of a liability to the effect that the statute of limitations shall not be pleaded as a defense thereto is void in New York. There is authority to the contrary in other jurisdictions. Shapley v. Abbott, 42 N. Y. 443; Mutual

Life Insurance Co. v. United States Hotel Co., 82 Misc. 632, 

144 N. Y. S. 476. 

§ 159a. Breach of Contract To Marry. 

In giving recognition to criticisms of actions for money damages for breach of a contract to marry, New York in 1935 abrogated the right to such actions, thereby making effective a new public policy. See 5 Brooklyn Law Review 

196. Rights of action to recover money damages for alienation of affections, criminal conversation, and seduction were also abolished. Civil Practice Act, sec. 61-b. The statute 

144

§§ 159a 

has been held constitutional and the legislature has been held to be acting within its constitutional authority in dealing with the subject of marriage. Acting within its authority, it "has determined as a matter of public policy that marriages should not be entered into because of the threat or danger of an action to recover money damages and the embarrassment and humiliation growing out of such an action." Fearon v. Treanor 272 N. Y. 268, 5 N. E. (2d) 

815, appeal dismissed 301 U. S. 667, 81 Law. Ed. 1332, 57 

Sup. Ct. Rep. 933. 

§ 160. 

Agreements To Commit a Tort. 

An agreement to commit a tort is illegal and void as against public policy. "A court will not lend aid to a party who has committed a tort to enable him to recover from another the price agreed to be paid for his wrongful act. 

The defense is allowed not as a protection to a defendant, but as a disability to the plaintiff." Reiner v. North American Newspaper Alliance, 259 N. Y. 250, 256, 181 N. E. 561, 83 A. L. R. 23. An agreement to publish a libelous book or article is void. Atkins v. Johnson, 43 Vt. 78, 5 Am. Rep. 

260. But an agreement to accept full responsibility for damages arising from the publication of a book to be written, which would not necessarily include libelous matter was held valid. Jewett Publishing Co. v. Butler, 159 Mass. 517, 34 N. E. 1087, 22 L. R. A. 253. 

§ 161. Agreements To Deceive the Public or Third 

Persons.

An agreement made with the intent that it shall operate as a fraud upon the private rights of third persons is void as against public policy. Thus, in Church v. Proctor, 66 

Fed. 240, it was held a good defense to an action for the breach of a contract for the sale of menhadden that the buyer intended to pack and sell them as mackerel. In this case, however, there was participation in the illegal act by the seller. See, also, Materne v. Horwitz, 101 N. Y. 469, 5 N. E. 331. An agreement for the use of the name 145

§  

of a music director by a band with which he was not connected was held to be void, as its purpose was to deceive the public. Blakely v. Sousa, 197 Pa. 305, 47 Atl. 286. For cases involving paid testimonials used in advertising, see

Editorial, New York Law Journal, June 6, 1935. Like wise, a contract for the sale of the editorial influence of a newspaper is illegal and void, such editorials being understood by the public to express the true and unbought views of the publishers. King v. Raleigh & Pamlico Sound 

R. R. Co., 147 N. C. 263, 60 S. E. 1133.

Agreements in fraud of creditors are void. Thus, a composition agreement between a debtor and his creditors is void where, in order to procure the consent of some particular creditor, the debtor secretly promises to pay him an additional sum over the other creditors. But in New York it is held that only the secret agreement with the one creditor is void and that otherwise the composition is valid. Hanover National Bank v. Blake, 142 N. Y. 404, 37 N. E. 519, 27 L. R. A. 33.

So, too, agreements which have for their object the fraudulent raising or lowering of prices at auction sales by the employment of "puffers" to make fictitious bids or by attempts to refrain from bidding and thus stifle competition, are void. Hence, a "puffer" is without remedy to recover his compensation because his contract is illegal, and the purchaser may avoid the contract of purchase on the ground of fraud. Bowman v. McClenahan, 20 App. Div. 346, 46

N. Y. S. 945. However, not every agreement which to some extent, may lessen competition among bidders is illegal. 

For example, an agreement of several persons to unite in making a joint bid at an auction sale may be actuated by a proper motive, in which event it is valid. Stack v. Roth 

Bros. Co., 162 Wis. 281, 156 N. W. 148.

A and B enter into a contract. Thereafter, A and C, who knows of the existence of the contract between A and B, enter into a new contract, the performance of which will, to the knowledge of both parties, necessarily involve the breach of the earlier contract. If A subsequently sues 146

§§ 161 

C to recover damages for the breach of the second contract, can C successfully plead the defense of illegality? 

Budd v. Morning Telegraph, Inc., 241 App. Div. 142, 271 

N. Y. S. 538, aff'd 265 N. Y. 639, 193 N. E. 423. 

§ 162. Agreements Relieving Tort-Feasors from Liability. 

The rule, not without qualification, has often been asserted that a person cannot by contract obtain relief from liability for a tort which he may commit in the future. 

Appleton & Co. v. Warbasse, 92 Misc. 42, 155 N. Y. S. 987; 

Shohoney v. Quincy, Omaha & Kansas City R. R. Co., 231

Mo. 131, 132 S. W. 1059. At all events, however, contracts exempting a tort-feasor from liability will be strictly construed against such tort-feasor. The weight of authority sustains the view that an employer cannot release himself by contract in advance from liability for injury to his employee caused by the employer's negligence. The reason for this holding is the interest the state has "in the conservation of the lives and of the healthful vigor of its citizens, and if employers could contract away their responsibility at common law, it would tend to encourage on their part laxity of conduct in, if not an indifference to, the maintenance of proper and reasonable safeguards to human life and limb." 

The highest incentive to the exercise of due care rests in a consciousness that a failure in this respect will fix liability to make full compensation for the injury. Johnston v. 

Fargo, 184 N. Y. 379, 77 N. E. 388, 7 L. R. A. (N. S.) 537. 

§ 163. 

Contracts Waiving Statutory Rights. 

Statutes are often passed to protect persons against certain types of contracts. The very purpose of such statutes would be defeated if their provisions could be waived by contract. For example, an instalment sale contract which gave to the vendor the right to retake possession upon default of payments by the vendee and to sell the property either at public or private sale and which further provided that the vendee waived all his statutory rights relating thereto was held void in so far as such waiver was con- 147

§§ 163, 164  

cerned, since the purpose of the statute is the protection of the poor or necessitous, and this is accomplished by preserving some rights to the vendee by statute which the courts will not allow him to waive by contract. Crowe v. 

Liquid Carbonic Co., 208 N. Y. 396, 102 N. E. 573. The 

Uniform Conditional Sales Act to-day expressly forbids a waiver by the conditional sales vendee of the statutory protection afforded him. Uniform Conditional Sales Act, sec. 26; Personal Property Law, sec. 80-f. But a new agreement made upon new consideration after the vendee has become in default is valid. Adler v. Weis & 

Fisher Co., 218 N. Y. 295, 112 N. E. 1049. In accordance with the same general principle, under statutes providing that wages must be paid in lawful money, a clause in a contract waiving such right is invalid. Hancock v. 

Yaden, 121 Ind. 366, 23 N. E. 253, 6 L. R. A. 576. So, too, that portion of a contract whereby the promisor waives in advance his statutory rights of exemption of his property from levy is void in most jurisdictions, on the theory that the statute is enacted for the protection of necessitous debtors, and to allow them to contract away their rights in advance would be to defeat the purpose of the statute. 

Kneettle v. Newcomb, 22 N. Y. 249, 78 Am. Dec. 186. 

§ 164. 

Agreements Contrary to Good Morals. 

The most important aspect of immorality with which courts of law have dealt on the ground of public policy is sexual immorality. But in rare instances, other forms of immorality have been held to render a contract void. Thus, where two children sought to divide up the family inheritance by a secret agreement between them irrespective of the will of their parent, the agreement was declared illegal because it encouraged disrespect towards parents. Mercier v. Mercier, 50 Ga. 546, 15 Am. Rep. 694. A contract may be valid though moral turpitude may be involved in its performance. While it is true that a contract may be void when it involves immoral conduct, it is equally true that it is not void simply because there is something immoral in its 148

164, 165 

surroundings or connections. To have this effect, the direct object of the contract must be the promotion of immorality. 

Moore v. Remington, 34 Barb. 427; Baumeister v. Markham, 101 Ky. 122, 39 S. W. 844; Anheuser-Busch Brewing 

Assn. v. Mason, 44 Minn. 318, 46 N. W. 558, 9 L. R. A. 506.

But contracts may be illegal because of immorality even though they do not involve sexual intercourse between the parties to the contract. Thus, a contract for the sale of indecent publications may be void. See St. Hubert Guild v. Quinn, 64 Misc. 336, 118 N. Y. S. 582. So, also, where the lessor knows and intends that premises will be used for immoral purposes, the lease is void, and the obligation of the lessee to pay rent will not be enforced. Ernst v. 

Crosby, 140 N. Y. 364, 35 N. E. 603.

An agreement in consideration of future illicit cohabitation is void. Steinfeld v. Levy, 16 Abb. Pr. (N. S.) 26. In 

Trovinger v. M'Burney, 5 Cowen (N. Y.) 253, an agreement to board an illegitimate child and its mother, the father to be allowed to continue the illicit relations, was held void. See, also, Tyrrell v. York, 57 Hun 292, 10 N. Y.

S. 611. Earlier cases hold that a promise made in consideration of past illicit cohabitation is not illegal but is regarded as a promise in reparation of wrongdoing. Bum v. Winthrop, 1 Johns. Ch. (N. Y.) 329; Todd v. Weber, 95 N. Y. 

181, 47 Am. Rep. 20. So, also, a note given in consideration of past cohabitation has been held not to be illegal. 

People v. Hayes, 140 N. Y. 484, 35 N. E. 951, 23 L. R. A. 

830. This doctrine, however, has apparently been overruled by the recent decision in Locke v. Pembroke, 280 

N. Y. 430, 21 N. E. (2d) 495. 

§ 165. Agreements Tending To Injure Public Service or 

Good Government.

The efficiency of public service is a matter of vital concern to the public, and, therefore, all agreements tending to injure public service or good government are void as being contrary to public policy. Thus, agreements to control the business operations of the government, or the appointment 149

§ 

to public office, or the ordinary course of legislation are invalid, and this, too, without reference to the parties' motives, whether corrupt or otherwise, or whether improper means are contemplated or used in their execution. Trist v. Child, 21 Wall. (U. S.) 441, 22 Law. Ed. 623. 

A combination to obtain contracts from the government is not in itself illegal, and contracts to combine for this purpose will be enforced by the courts. Hegness v. Chilberg, 224 Fed. 28. Thus, an agreement between A and B, whereby B agrees to give A a certain percentage of the profits of a contract that A may obtain from the government for B, is valid. Beck v. Bauman, 187 App. Div. 774, 175 N. Y. S. 

881; Dunham v. Hastings Pavement Co., 118 App. Div. 127, 103 N. Y. S. 480, aff'd 189 N. Y. 500, 81 N. E. 1163. But it is otherwise when the contract provides for the use of improper means and influence which tend to affect the public injuriously. For example, an agreement for services in securing contracts by favoritism from state officials, by reason of social and political relations with such officials, is void. Drake v. Lauer, 93 App. Div. 86, 86 N. Y. S. 986, aff'd 182 N. Y. 533, 75 N. E. 1129.

The federal decisions, holding that contracts for compensation in consideration of procuring contracts with the government to furnish it supplies are void, are directly contrary to the New York decisions. Hazelton v. Sheckells, 202 U. S. 71, 50 Law. Ed. 939, 26 Sup. Ct. Rep. 567; Southard v. Boyd, 51 N. Y. 177; Beck v. Bauman, supra. In order to invalidate such a contract in New York, it must appear that the agreement itself contemplated the illegal act, and it is not rendered illegal merely because condemnable acts were done thereunder if they were not contemplated by the agreement. Drake v. Lauer, supra. 

Agreements to secure legislative action are valid when they contemplate the use of lawful means. For instance, an agreement for professional services in drafting petitions to the legislative body, in collecting evidence, preparing arguments and submitting them, either orally or in writing, to legislative committees, and the like, is as free from the taint 150

S$ 165 of illegality as a contract for the retaining of professional services in a court of law. Chesebrough v. Conover, 140 

N. Y. 382, 35 N. E. 633. But if an agreement contemplates the use of unlawful means in procuring favorable legislation, or if legislation is sought to further an unlawful purpose, it is void. Veazey v. Allen, 173 N. Y. 359, 66 N. E. 

103, 62 L. R. A. 362.

The constitutional right to petition those invested with the powers of government cannot be surrendered by a citizen. Hence, the by-law of a mutual benefit society providing for the expulsion of members who petition the legislature to repeal a statute which has the sanction of the governing body of the society is void, as an infringement of the constitutional right of petition. Spayd v. Ringing Rock 

Lodge No. 665, 270 Pa. 67, 113 Atl. 70, 14 A. L. R. 1443.

Agreements Tending To Pervert the Course of 

$166. Justice.

Agreements which tend to obstruct or interfere with the administration of justice are void. They may be classified as follows: 

1. Agreements compounding crimes; 

2. Agreements ousting courts of jurisdiction; 

3. Agreements tending to encourage litigation-champerty and maintenance. 

§ 167. Agreements Compounding Crimes. 

Criminal prosecutions should be instituted or dismissed solely for the good of the state and not for private gain. 

Therefore, agreements to stifle criminal prosecutions are void because they tend to obstruct public justice. Maxfield v. Hoecker, 49 Hun 605, 2 N. Y. S. 77. But a contract to make reparation is valid, provided it does not stipulate that the prosecution for the offense shall be suppressed or stayed. Barrett v. Weber, 125 N. Y. 18, 25 N. E. 1068; 

Board of Education v. Angel, 75 W. Va. 747, 84 S. E. 747,

L. R. A. 1915E 139. An agreement to suppress the evidence of a crime alleged to have been committed is illegal 151

§§ 167  

regardless of the fact that the person charged is innocent. 

Union Exchange National Bank v. Joseph, 231 N. Y. 250, 131 N. E. 905, 17 A. L. R. 323. A promise not to prosecute is not illegal, where it is made not for the sake of gain but from motives of kindness or on account of relationship. 

Hatch v. Collins, 34 Hun 314; Dodson v. Swan, 2 W. Va. 

511, 98 Am. Dec. 787. See 74 New York Law Review 42 (January, 1940).

The act of one who takes money under an agreement to compound or conceal a crime is itself made criminal in New 

York. Penal Law, sec. 570. 

§ 168. 

Offenses Which May Be Compromised. 

The New York Code of Criminal Procedure provides for the compromise of misdemeanors where the wrongdoer is both civilly and criminally liable, except in certain enumerated cases. Code of Criminal Procedure, secs. 663 and 

664; Cleveland v. Cromwell, 110 App. Div. 82, 96 N. Y. S. 

475. In the absence of such a statute, the compounding of a misdemeanor is illegal. Aycock v. Gill, 183 N. C. 271, 111 

S. E. 342, 24 A. L. R. 1449. 

§ 169. Agreements Ousting Courts of Jurisdiction at Com mon Law.

Under the common law rule, the parties cannot, by contract, oust the courts of their jurisdiction. Therefore, an agreement requiring all differences and disputes arising under such agreement to be settled by arbitration to the exclusion of the courts is void. Although it is permissible to submit to arbitration pending lawsuits or existing differences, it is not permissible for persons to stipulate in advance that in the event of differences arising in the future they will deny themselves the right to resort to the courts. 

Blodgett Co. v. Bebe Co., 190 Cal. 665, 214 Pac. 38, 26 A.

L. R. 1070. The reason for the common law rule is that, as the state has an interest in all its inhabitants, and it is to the state's interest that the rights of all shall be pro- 152

§§ 169, 170 

tected and enforced and that its courts shall be always open for the redress of wrongs, no person should be permitted by contract to deprive himself in advance of the right to appeal to them. But even under the common law rule the parties may agree in advance to leave to arbitration the determination of questions of fact, such as the amount of loss or damage or any other matters that do not leave to the arbitrators the sole question of liability for a breach of the contract. Hamilton v. Liverpool, London & Globe 

Insurance Co., 136 U. S. 242, 34 Law. Ed. 419, 10 Sup. Ct.

Rep. 945; National Contracting Co. v. Hudson River Water 

Power Co., 170 N. Y. 439, 63 N. E. 450, same case, 192 N. Y. 

209, 84 N. E. 965.

Agreements determining the jurisdiction in which actions shall be brought are void. An important case in New 

York on this point is Meacham v. Jamestown, Franklin & 

Clearfield R. R. Co., 211 N. Y. 346, 105 N. E. 653. See, also, Nashua River Paper Co. v. Hammermill Paper Co., 223 

Mass. 8, 111 N. E. 678, L. R. A. 1916D 691; Parker v. Krauss 

Co., 157 Misc. 667, 284 N. Y. S. 478, aff'd 249 App. Div. 718, 292 N. Y. S. 955. 

§ 170. 

Arbitration Agreements. 

It is now provided by statute in New York that a written contract to settle by arbitration a controversy arising between parties to a contract shall be valid, enforceable, and irrevocable, save upon such grounds as exist for the revocation of any contract. Civil Practice Act, sec. 1448; Manufacturers Chemical Co., Inc. v. Caswell Strauss & Co., Inc., 259 App. Div. 321, 19 N. Y. S. (2d) 171. Similar statutes have been enacted in other states. The New York statute has been held to be constitutional. Berkovitz v. Arbib & 

Houlberg, 230 N. Y. 261, 130 N. E. 288; Webster v. Van 

Allen, 217 App. Div. 219, 216 N. Y. S. 552. For statutory provisions on arbitration, see the Civil Practice Act, sections 1448 to 1469. 153

§ 

 § 171. Agreement Tending To Encourage Litigation— Champerty and Maintenance. 

"Champerty" is a bargain between a person and the plaintiff to divide the matter sued for. "Maintenance" is the officious interference in a lawsuit by one who has no interest therein. For a history of the law in relation to champerty and maintenance, see Thallhimer v. Brinckerhoff, 3 Cowen (N. Y.) 623, 15 Am. Dec. 308. At common law, champertous agreements were unlawful because they encouraged litigation. Sedgwick v. Stanton, 14 N. Y. 289.

However, the common law relating to champerty and maintenance no longer obtains in New York except as preserved by statute. The Penal Law, section 274, prohibits an attorney from buying a claim for the purpose of bringing an action thereon. Bundy v. Newton, 65 Hun 619, 19 

N. Y. S. 734, aff'd 138 N. Y. 643, 34 N. E. 513; Browning v. Marvin, 100 N. Y. 144, 2 N. E. 635. It also prohibits an attorney from promising or giving to any person a valuable consideration as an inducement to place in his hands a demand for the purpose of having an action brought thereon. 

But this provision does not expressly prohibit an attorney from taking a case on a contingent fee, nor does it deny him the right to take a case under an agreement with his client whereby the attorney agrees to pay all necessary disbursements, provided such agreement in no way induced the litigation. Ransom v. Cutting, 188 N. Y. 447, 81 N. E. 324;

McCoy v. Gas Engine & Power Co., 152 App. Div. 642, 

137 N. Y. S. 591, aff'd without opinion, 208 N. Y. 631, 102 

N. E. 1106; Fowler v. Callan, 102 N. Y. 395, 7 N. E. 169.

By the weight of authority in this country, a contract for a contingent fee by which an attorney agrees to save his client harmless from the costs and expenses of the proposed litigation is void. Stark County v. Mischel, 42 N. D. 332, 173 

N. W. 817, 6 A. L. R. 174 and note. A contingent fee of fifty per centum is not necessarily unconscionable. In re Fitzsimons, 174 N. Y. 15, 66 N. E. 554; In re Meng, 227 N. Y. 

264, 125 N. E. 508.

Agreements between attorneys for a division of fees is not 154

§§ 171, 172 

prohibited. Penal Law, sec. 274. But agreements between an attorney and a layman or a corporation for "splitting fees" as an inducement or reward for the procuring of business are void. An attorney making such an agreement is guilty of a misdemeanor. Penal Law, sec. 274; In re Newman, 172 App. Div. 173, 158 N. Y. S. 375; In re Clark, 184 

N. Y. 222, 77 N. E. 1. At one time no liability was incurred by laymen who solicited business for lawyers. In 1935, however, statutory provisions were adopted making it a misdemeanor for any person to engage in such activities; or to enter a hospital for the purpose of negotiating a settlement of personal injury claims except under specified circumstances; or for hospital, police department, prison, or court employees to assist an attorney in the solicitation of legal business; or for an attorney to employ a solicitor. Penal 

Law, secs. 270-a to d, and 272. Section 276 makes it a misdemeanor for any person, partnership, corporation, or association to divide or agree to divide with an attorney any part of such attorney's fees; or to receive consideration for placing a claim in an attorney's hands. The Judiciary Law, section 474, contains provisions as to compensation of attorneys. For a discussion of the relationship of attorney and client, see Martin v. Camp, 219 N. Y. 170, 114 N. E. 46,

L. R. A. 1917F 402. 

§ 172. Lien of Attorney-Secret Settlement between 

Quite commonly in this country, the attorney for the plaintiff is given by statute a lien for his services on the judgment or reward recovered. See Nichols v. Orr, 63 

Colo. 333, 166 Pac. 561, 2 A. L. R. 449. The statutory lien of an attorney for his services is created in New York by the Judiciary Law, section 475. Such lien cannot be defeated by a secret settlement between the parties. Peri v. New York Central & Hudson River R. R. Co., 152 N. Y. 

521, 46 N. E. 849. However, a stipulation in the contract between the attorney and client prohibiting the client from settling the litigation without the consent of the attorney 155

§§ 172, 173  

is void as against public policy, though it does not prevent the lien of the attorney from attaching to the judgment. 

In re Snyder, 190 N. Y. 66, 82 N. E. 742, 14 L. R. A. (N.S.) 

1101; Beagles v. Robertson, 135 Mo. App. 306, 115 S. W. 

1042; Martin v. Camp, 219 N. Y. 170, 114 N. E. 46, L. R. A. 1917F 402. 

§ 173. 

Agreements in Restraint of Marriage. 

It is the policy of the law to encourage marriage and to discourage any interference therewith. Therefore, agreements creating general restrictions on marriage are contrary to public policy and are void. Robinson v. Martin, 200 N. Y. 159, 93 N. E. 488; Matter of Seaman, 218 N. Y. 

77, 112 N. E. 576. But a reasonable restraint, designed to prevent hasty or imprudent marriages, is permissible. 

Sterling v. Sinnickson, 5 N. J. L. 756; Lewis v. Johnson, 212 Mo. App. 19, 251 S. W. 136; Barnes v. Hobson, 250

S. W. (Tex.) 238. Thus, an agreement among four sisters to bequeath their property to each other, to the exclusion as far as legally possible of any future husband, has been upheld. Kloberg v. Teller, 103 Misc. 641, 171 N. Y. S. 947.

But a promise by a man to an unmarried woman that, if she continued in his employ and cared for his wants until his death and did not marry until after his death, his executor would then pay her a specified sum has been held void as an agreement in general restraint of marriage. 

Lowe v. Doremus, 84 N. J. L. 658, 87 Atl. 459, 49 L. R. A. (N.S.) 632. Contra, King v. King, 63 Ohio St. 363, 59 

N. E. 111, 52 L. R. A. 157. So, too, an agreement to settle an action for breach of promise of marriage and to pay the plaintiff a specified amount three years after date if she is then alive and unmarried is void. McCoy v. Flynn, 169 Iowa 622, 151 N. W. 465, L. R. A. 1915D 1064.

It is not contrary to public policy, however, for a person having a parental interest in another to restrict marriage along certain lines, such as infancy and relationship. So, also, agreements not to marry a person under a certain age, or in a certain degree of relationship, or into a certain 156

§§ 173, 174 

'family, or not to marry a second time have been held valid on the ground that the restraint is reasonable. Hogan v. Curtin, 88 N. Y. 162, 42 Am. Rep. 244.

The rule that renders void contracts which are in general restraint of marriage has no force when applied to second marriages. Contracts in restraint of a second marriage are uniformly affirmed, for the reason that: "Neither the conservation of morals nor public policy furnishes a basis for the rule as applied to the right of a husband or wife to withhold his or her estate from passing to the support of a second husband or wife, as the case might be."

For a general discussion of this question and the authorities, see Appleby v. Appleby, 100 Minn. 408, 111 N. W. 

305, 10 L. R. A. (N.S.) 590. 

§ 174. Separation Agreements. 

On the ground of public policy, contracts between husband and wife which provide for their future separation are void, as in derogation of the marriage relation, the living apart of husband and wife being considered violative of public policy. Winter v. Winter, 191 N. Y. 462, 84 N. E. 382, 16 L. R. A. (N.S.) 710; Moore v. Moore, 255

Fed. 497. But where the spouses have separated, or where an immediate separation is the object of the contract whereby provision is made for the separate support of the wife, the contract is valid. Winter v. Winter, supra; Tirrell v. Tirrell, 232 N. Y. 224, 133 N. E. 569; La Montagne v. 

La Montagne, 239 App. Div. 352, 267 N. Y. S. 148, aff'd 264 

N. Y. 552, 191 N. E. 560. A subsequent reconciliation terminates the separation agreement. Zimmer v. Settle, 124 N. Y. 

37, 26 N. E. 341.

Agreements to restore marital relationship upon a money consideration are not invalid as opposed to public policy in New York. Rodgers v. Rodgers, 229 N. Y. 255, 128 N. 

E. 117, 11 A. L. R. 274. A contrary rule is applied in some jurisdictions. Thus, in Merrill v. Peaslee, 146 Mass. 460, 16 N. E. 271, the Court said: "It is as much against public policy to restore interrupted conjugal relations for money, 157

§§ 174  

as it is to continue them without interruption for the same consideration." In New York, the contract is upheld on the ground that it is in the interest of public policy to restore peace and harmony between husband and wife and renew their conjugal relations. The New York view is sustained by a decided weight of authority. Darcey v. 

Darcey, 29 R. I. 384, 71 Atl. 595, 23 L. R. A. (N.S.) 886. 

§ 175. Marriage Brokerage Contracts. 

Agreements to pay for bringing about a marriage or to pay to assist a person in getting married by means of introductions to persons of the opposite sex are void, both at law and in equity, as being opposed to morality and public policy. The courts stress the fact that the broker's self interest leads to the temptation to exercise undue and pernicious influence by holding out false and seductive hopes to the parties and thus tends to bring about a marriage without regard to their welfare or happiness. Duval v. Wellman, 124 N. Y. 156, 26 N. E. 343; Antcliff v. June, 81 Mich. 477, 45 N. W. 1019, 10 L. R. A. 621. 

§ 176. Agreements in Restraint of Trade. 

Under the common law, agreements in restraint of trade, when unreasonable, are void, but contracts in reasonable restraint of trade are valid. Quincy Oil Co. v. Sylvester, 238 Mass. 95, 130 N. E. 217; Restatement, Contracts, sec. 

514. This rule is uniformly applied in covenants imposing restraints on trades or occupations. Williston on Contracts, Rev. Ed., Vol. V, sec. 1636; Restatement, Contracts, secs. 

515 and 516. If the protection to a successor in a business or occupation is greater than is necessary to protect his rights, the restraint is unreasonable. What would be reasonable in one case, however, might be most unreasonable in another. Thus, restrictions as to space which might be reasonable when applied to a wholesale dealer, such as denying him the right to again engage in the same business in certain states, would be wholly unreasonable when applied to a local retail dealer. Whatever restraint is greater than is neces- 158

§ sary for the protection of the party can be of no benefit to either. It can be only oppressive, and if oppressive, in legal contemplation, it is unreasonable. Samuel Stores, Inc. v. Abrams, 94 Conn. 248, 108 Atl. 541, 9 A. L. R. 1450; 

Gibbs v. Consolidated Gas Co. of Baltimore, 130 U. S. 396, 32 Law. Ed. 979, 9 Sup. Ct. Rep. 553. Under modern decisions, the restraint may in some cases be practically unlimited as to both time and space. Diamond Match Co. v. 

Roeber, 106 N. Y. 473, 13 N. E. 419; Camors-McConnell Co. v. McConnell, 140 Fed. 412, aff'd p. 987; Central New York 

Telephone & Telegraph Co. v. Averill, 199 N. Y. 128, 92 

N. E. 206, 32 L. R. A. (N.S.) 494. "Covenants in partial restraint of trade are generally upheld as valid when they are agreements (1) by the seller of property or business not to compete with the buyer in such a way as to derogate from the value of the property or business sold [Diamond Match Co. v. Roeber, supra]; (2) by a retiring partner not to compete with the firm [Curtis et al. v. Gokey, 68 N. Y. 300]; (3) by a partner pending the partnership not to do anything to interfere, by competition or otherwise, with the business of the firm; (4) by the buyer of property not to use the same in competition with the business retained by the seller [Hodge v. Sloan, 107
