N. Y. 244, 17 N. E. 335]; and (5) by an assistant, servant, or agent not to compete with his master or employer after the expiration of his time of service." Addyston Pipe & 

Steel Co. v. United States, 85 Fed. 271, aff'd 175 U. S. 211, 44 Law. Ed. 136, 20 Sup. Ct. Rep. 96. In explanation of the above summary of permissible restraints, Taft, J., said: "Before such agreements are upheld, however, the court must find that the restraints attempted thereby are reasonably necessary to the enjoyment by the buyer of the property, good will, or interest in the partnership bought; or to the legitimate ends of the existing partnership; or to the prevention of possible injury to the business of the seller from use by the buyer of the thing sold; or to protection from the danger of loss to the employer's business * "It would be stating it too strongly to say that these five 159

§§ 176, 177  

classes of covenants in restraint of trade include all of those upheld as valid at the common law; but it would certainly seem to follow from the tests laid down for determining the validity of such an agreement that no conventional restraint of trade can be enforced unless the covenant embodying it is merely ancillary to the main purpose of a lawful contract, and necessary to protect the covenantee."

A valid restrictive covenant by a vendor is deemed an incident of the business to which it relates, and it will pass to, and may be enforced by, a subsequent purchaser of the business. Diamond Match Co. v. Roeber, supra; American 

Ice Co. v. Meckel, 109 App. Div. 93, 95 N. Y. S. 1060. 

§ 177. Restriction in Contract of Employment. 

Agreements by employees, ancillary to contracts of employment, restricting their right to labor along the same lines, either for themselves or others, upon the termination of their present employment have given rise to much controversy. In some jurisdictions, it is held that such agreements violate public policy, because the covenantors' means of procuring a livelihood for themselves and their families are thereby diminished, and where general and unlimited as to scope of territory are void. Taylor Iron & Steel Co. v. Nichols, 73 N. J. Eq. 684, 69 Atl. 186, 24 

L. R. A. (N.S.) 933; Kinney v. Scarborough Co., 138 Ga. 

77, 74 S. E. 772, 40 L. R. A. (N.S.) 473 and note. In other jurisdictions, the test of the validity of such contracts is based not so much on the question whether they are prejudicial to public interest, but whether they are reasonably necessary for the fair protection of the employer's business rights. Eureka Laundry Co. v. Long, 146 Wis. 205, 131 N. W. 412, 35 L. R. A. (N.S.) 119. The latter view is adopted in New York. McCall Co. v. Wright, 198 N. Y. 

143, 91 N. E. 516, 31 L. R. A. (N.S.) 249. See, also, Kaumagraph Co. v. Stampagraph Co., 235 N. Y. 1, 138 N. E. 

485. The principle upon which the validity of such agreements is based is protection from the danger of loss to the employer's business caused by the unjust use on the part 160

§§ 177 

of the employee of the confidential knowledge acquired in such business. Thus, where, under contract as a deliveryman, an employee agreed not to have competing relations with his employer's customers, the agreement will be construed to apply only to those customers of the employer whom the employee served or whose names were learned by him during the course of his employment. Interstate Tea 

Co. v. Alt, 271 N. Y. 76, 2 N. E. (2d) 51.

The general principles applicable in determining the validity of contracts in restraint of trade are also applicable in determining the validity of agreements which place restrictions on an employee as a condition of his employment. 

McCall Co. v. Wright, supra. The authorities are collected and reviewed in 9 A. L. R. 1450-1496. 

§ 178. Agreements Not To Disclose Trade Secrets. 

Agreements not to disclose secrets communicated in the course of confidential employment, whether they be of trade or of title, or other secrets of the party important to his business, will be enforced. The most frequent application of this rule is found in contracts involving some secret process or formula of manufacture. Tode v. Gross, 127 

N. Y. 480, 28 N. E. 469, 13 L. R. A. 652; Kaumagraph Co. v. Stampagraph Co., 235 N. Y. 1, 138 N. E. 485. But the rule of law sustaining agreements to keep inviolate trade secrets is not limited to manufacturing processes. It includes such matters as lists of customers and information of any kind pertaining to the employer's business. Witkop & 

Holmes Co. v. Boyce, 61 Misc. 126, 112 N. Y. S. 874, aff'd 

131 App. Div. 922, 115 N. Y. S. 1150. 

§ 179. 

Monopoly Contracts. 

The word "monopoly" embraces any combination, the tendency of which is to prevent competition in its broad sense or to control prices to the detriment of the public. 

Love v. Kozy Theatre Co., 193 Ky. 336, 236 S. W. 243, 26

A. L. R. 364. Therefore, agreements or combinations in restraint of trade, commerce, or competition are void, and 161

$ 

courts will not aid parties seeking to enforce such agreements. Arnot v. Pittston & Elmira Coal Co., 68 N. Y. 558, 23 Am. Rep. 190; Judd v. Harrington, 139 N. Y. 105, 34 

N. E. 790; Strauss v. American Publishers' Assn., 231 U. S. 

222, 58 Law. Ed. 192, 34 Sup. Ct. Rep. 84. See the Penal

Law, section 580, subdivision 6, wherein it is made a crime for two or more persons to conspire to do any act injurious to trade or commerce. However, contracts giving a person the exclusive right to handle goods in a certain locality are not condemned. Walter A. Wood Mowing & Reaping 

Machine Co. v. Greenwood Hardware Co., 75 S. C. 378, 55 S. E. 973, 9 L. R. A. (N.S.) 501. As between members of an unlawful combination or association, there is no remedy for the enforcement of their agreements; but contracts between members of the illegal combination, or the illegal combination itself, and persons who are in no way connected with the combination, are enforceable, provided the contracts in such instances are wholly collateral to the unlawful purpose of the combination. Connolly v. 

Union Sewer Pipe Co., 184 U. S. 540, 46 Law. Ed. 679, 22 

Sup. Ct. Rep. 431. See, also, A. B. Small Co. v. Lamborn & 

Co., 267 U. S. 248, 69 Law. Ed. 597, 45 Sup. Ct. Rep. 300.

The leading case denying the right to enforce contracts between members of an unlawful monopoly is Continental 

Wall Paper Co. v. Louis Voight & Sons Co., 212 U. S. 227, 53 Law. Ed. 486, 29 Sup. Ct. Rep. 280. See, also, International Harvester Co. v. Judge Smith, 163 Mich. 55, 127 

N. W. 695, 30 L. R. A. (N.S.) 580 and note.

The success of business methods alone does not show illegality, although such success may render insuperable the difficulties which rivals must face. Federal Trade Com mission v. Curtis Publishing Co., 260 U. S. 568, 67 Law. 

Ed. 408, 43 Sup. Ct. Rep. 210.

Common law principles in restraint of trade have been supplemented by federal statutes which, in many instances, make acts committed in violation thereof criminal. Such, for instance, are the Sherman Act and the Clayton Act. 162

§ $ 180. Price-Fixing Contracts.

Repeated attempts have been made within recent years to control prices of articles of commerce by a system of contracts fixing the prices between manufacturers and wholesale and retail merchants, thus eliminating all competition and fixing the amount which the consumer shall pay.

Recent legislation has legalized resale price-fixing and thus permits limited restraint of trade under statutes generally known as Fair Trade Acts. In New York, the General Business Law declares legal a contract which provides that a buyer of a commodity bearing the label, trade-mark, brand, or name of the producer will not resell such commodity except at the price stipulated by the vendor. The commodity must be one which is in fair and open competition with commodities in the same general class produced by others. General Business Law, sec. 369-a, subd. (a). 

Bourjois Sales Corp. v. Dorfman, 273 N. Y. 167, 7 N. E. (2d) 30, 110 A. L. R. 1411. It should be noted that the statute specifically limits its application to contracts for trade-marked or branded articles sold by producers or owners. It is made inapplicable to price-fixing agreements between one producer and another or between one wholesaler and another or between one retailer and another. As to such transactions, the common law principles are still applicable and price-fixing agreements, unless modified by other statutes, will be held void.

The statute further provides that contracts shall not be violative of law although such contracts contain stipulations that purchasers from a vendee or producer will not resell such commodity except at the price stipulated. General 

Business Law, secs. 369-a and 369-b. Moreover, the statute provides that "wilfully and knowingly advertising, offering for sale or selling any commodity at less than the price stipulated in any contract entered into pursuant to the provision of section three hundred sixty-nine-a, whether the person so advertising, offering for sale or selling is or is not a party to such a contract, is unfair competition and is action- 163

§ 

able at the suit of any person damaged thereby." General 

Business Law, sec. 369-b. This provision binds persons who are not parties to a contract to the restrictions set up by the law.

As to the right of a retailer to bring action against a competitor who violates the provisions of the statute, see Port 

Chester Wine & Liquor Shop v. Miller Bros. Fruiterers, 281 

N. Y. 101, 22 N. E. (2d) 253. As to the application of the act, see Lentheric, Inc. v. Grant Co., 282 N. Y. 638, 25 

N. E. (2d) 982. 

Note that price-fixing agreements are not binding: "(a)

In closing out the owner's stock for the purpose of discontinuing delivering any such commodity. (b) When the goods are damaged or deteriorated in quality, and notice is given to the public thereof. (c) By any officer acting under the orders of any court." General Business Law, sec. 369-a, subd. 2.

Other recent statutory provisions exempt certain types of contracts from the application of common law principles. 

Such, for instance, are various forms of labor law, contracts of cooperative agricultural marketing associations, and the like. See Williston on Contracts, Rev. Ed., Vol. V, sections 

1654-1656 A.

In the absence of statutory provisions to the contrary, price-fixing agreements are held invalid in the following four main classes: 

1. Cases wherein the original vendor of an article attempts to control the price at which the article shall be sold by a third person who later purchases the same from the vendee of the original vendor. Motion Picture Patents 

Co. v. Universal Film Manufacturing Co., 243 U. S. 502, 

61 Law. Ed. 871, 37 Sup. Ct. Rep. 416. 

2. Cases wherein the vendee makes a contract with the vendor whereby the vendee agrees that he, the vendee, will resell the article in question only at a fixed price. Note that in both this paragraph and in 1, above, the rules stated are still applicable as to articles not in market competition with similar articles and to articles not trade-marked or 164

§ otherwise branded, or from which the trade-mark or name has been deleted. Old Dearborn Distributing Co. v. Seagram-Distillers Corp., 299 U. S. 183, 81 Law. Ed. 109, 57

Sup. Ct. Rep. 139. 

3. Cases wherein the vendee, who has violated the void contract to maintain a fixed price, seeks relief by reason of the fact that the vendor has refused to make any further sales to such vendee merely because the vendee has violated such prior agreement. In such cases, the vendee is without relief. The vendor has the legal right to refuse to make any additional sales, and his motive is immaterial. 

United States v. Colgate & Co., 250 U. S. 300, 63 Law. Ed. 

992, 39 Sup. Ct. Rep. 465. The Court in Federal Trade 

Commission v. Raymond Bros.-Clark-Co., 263 U. S. 565, 572, 68 Law. Ed. 448, 44 Sup. Ct. Rep. 162, said: "If real competition is to continue, the right of the individual to exercise reasonable discretion in respect of his own business methods, must be preserved." 

4. Cases wherein the vendor does not sell the goods to the dealer to whom he transfers possession but merely makes such dealer the vendor's agent to sell and requires such agent to agree not to sell such goods for less than a fixed price. Such contracts are valid. Whitney v. Biggs, 92 Misc. 424, 156 N. Y. S. 1107. Such agent, like other agents, must obey orders given to him by his principal. 

For example, a manufacturer of automobiles may refuse to permit his agent to attach to his cars a device made by a third person.

McMaster v. Ford Motor Co., 122 S. C. 

244, 115 S. E. 244, 29 A. L. R. 230. 165

CHAPTER X EFFECT OF ILLEGALITY § 181. In General. 

Illegality may affect a part or the whole of a contract. 

The illegal parts may or may not be capable of separation. 

If a contract is bad only in part, and that which is bad can be separated from that which is good, the courts will enforce the part which is good. It should be observed that in the following discussion of the rights of the parties to an illegal contract, the plaintiff is the promisee and furnishes the consideration; the defendant, the promisor. Every illegal contract will assume one of four forms: 

1. One promise for one consideration; 2. 

One promise for several considerations; 

3. Several promises for one consideration; 

4. Several promises for several considerations. 

§ 182. One Promise for One Consideration. 

Where one promise is given for one consideration, the illegality of the consideration vitiates the promise, and the promisee cannot maintain an action based on the contract. 

Arnot v. Pittston & Elmira Coal Co., 68 N. Y. 558, 23 Am. 

Rep. 190; Brent v. Gay, 149 Ky. 615, 149 S. W. 915, 41 

L. R. A. (N.S.) 1034.

The same is equally true where the promise is illegal. 

In such cases, the agreement is void, even though the con sideration is legal. Sayres v. Decker Automobile Co., 239 

N. Y. 73, 145 N. E. 744. § 183. One Promise for Several Considerations.

If there are several considerations for one legal promise, some of which considerations are legal and others illegal, the promise is wholly void, as it is impossible to say whether it was the legal or the illegal consideration that induced the promise. Thus, if the consideration for a 166

EFFECT OF ILLEGALITY §§ 183-185 

promissory note is the sale of goods, some of which it is legal and some of which it is illegal to sell, the note cannot be enforced. Sirkin v. Fourteenth Street Store, 124 

App. Div. 384, 108 N. Y. S. 830; In re Snyder, 190 N. Y. 

66, 82 N. E. 742, 14 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1101; Santa Clara 

Valley Mill & Lumber Co. v. Hayes, 76 Cal. 387, 18 Pac. 391. 

§ 184. 

Several Promises for One Consideration.

Where an agreement consists of several promises based upon one consideration, the illegality of the consideration vitiates all the promises. But if the consideration is legal and the promises are severable, the illegality of one of the promises does not invalidate the others. Such contracts are said to be divisible. Erie R. R. Co. v. Union Locomotive & 

Express Co., 35 N. J. L. 240; Central New York Telephone & Telegraph Co. v. Averill, 199 N. Y. 128, 92 N. E. 206, 32 L. R. A. (N.S.) 494.

The result of the rules that partial illegality of consideration vitiates a promise founded thereon and that the illegality of one of several separable promises based upon the same consideration does not affect the validity of the others is to make the validity of an agreement consisting of mutual promises depend upon the party who seeks to enforce it. Thus, if A promises B $100 in consideration of B's promise to do two acts, one of which is lawful and the other unlawful, A may enforce B's lawful promise. B, however, cannot enforce A's promise, because it is founded on a consideration which is in part illegal. Sullivan v. 

Horgan, 17 R. I. 1109, 20 Atl. 232; Bishop v. Palmer, 146 

Mass. 469, 16 N. E. 299. 

§ 185. 

Several Promises for Several Considerations,

Where an agreement consists of several promises based upon several considerations, the illegality of one or more of the considerations will not avoid all the promises if those which were made upon lawful considerations are severable from the others. Leavitt v. Palmer, 3 N. Y. 19, 51 Am. Dec. 333; Osgood v. Bauder, 75 Iowa 550, 39 N. W. 167

§§ 185, 186  

887, 1 L. R. A. 655. Thus, a contract whereby A agrees, for compensation, to make an illegal bid on certain realty for B and to manage such realty at a certain rate of wages per month is enforceable as to the latter but not as to the former promise. McVicker v. McKenzie, 136 Cal. 656, 69 

Pac. 495. 

§ 186. Object Unlawful but Intention Innocent. 

If the thing to be done under the agreement is unlawful, even though the intention is innocent, the agreement is void. Want of guilty knowledge will not save the contract. Ignorance or mistake of law is no excuse. Saratoga 

County Bank v. King, 44 N. Y. 87.

An interesting case in this connection is Medoff v. Fisher, 257 Pa. 126, 101 Atl. 471, wherein an architect sued upon a contract with the defendants for services in the preparation of plans and specifications for a building. The building was to contain a motion picture theatre and also dwellings, bathhouse in the cellar, and stores. A statute provided that no portion of any building thereafter erected, and used for the exhibition of motion pictures "shall be occupied or used as a dwelling or tenement house, apartment house, hotel or department store." It was held that the architect was a party to an agreement to do an unlawful act and hence could not recover.

The Court said: "The principle that, since one may change his mind before the actual perpetration of a forbidden act, the mere intention to commit a wrong is no offense has no proper application under the circumstances at bar; for even though, after an erection of this building, the defendants might not have put it to any forbidden use, yet that fact does not change the status of the case so far as the plaintiff is concerned. The latter's position, therefore, is simply this: All men are supposed to know the law, and, further, one holding himself out as an architect is particularly charged with knowledge of the statutory regulations and restrictions governing the erection and use of buildings; therefore, we must assume both the plaintiff 168

EFFECT OF ILLEGALITY §§ 186, 187 

and defendants knew that the uses to which the latter contemplated putting the proposed structure were forbidden under a criminal penalty by the statutes of Pennsylvania." 

If the drawing of the plans is to be regarded as a participation in the intent or as aiding or abetting the ultimate illegal act, the decision is sound; otherwise it is not. 

§ 187. 

Object Innocent but Intention Unlawful.

In many jurisdictions it is held that where the object of the agreement is innocent in itself, but the intentions of the parties are unlawful, the agreement is void. But, by the weight of authority, knowledge by one of the parties of the unlawful intention of the other does not affect the contract, unless the party does some act to help carry out the unlawful intention and act of the other. Thus, it has been held that, if liquor is sold for the express purpose of enabling the buyer to retail it in violation of law, the sale is void. Kohn v. Melcher, 43 Fed. 641, 10 L. R. A. 439.

And it would seem that where goods are sold for the purpose of enabling the buyer to accomplish an unlawful act, the sale should be held illegal on the same theory. In such a case, the illegal intent is not only known to the seller but is encouraged by the sale. Graves v. Johnson, 156 Mass. 

211, 30 N. E. 818, 15 L. R. A. 834. These cases come very close to the English rule, which is that, if the direct object of the contract is innocent in itself but one of the parties has an unlawful intention or purpose which is known to the other, the agreement is void. Thus, in Pearce v. Brooks, 1 Exch. (Eng.) 213, a coach-builder sued a prostitute for money due for the hire of a brougham, let to her with the knowledge that it was to be used by her in the furtherance of her immoral trade. It was held the coach-builder could not recover.

In New York, however, and in the United States generally, mere knowledge of the unlawful intention and purpose is held insufficient to taint the contract with illegality; there must be an intent to aid, or an act in aid of the accomplishment of the illegal purpose. Anheuser-Busch 169

$ 

Brewing Assn. v. Mason, 44 Minn. 318, 46 N. W. 558, 9 L. 

R. A. 506; Fineman v. Faulkner, 174 N. C. 13, 93 S. E. 

384, L. R. A. 1918A 337. 

For example, in furtherance of an attempt to form a monopoly, B leased a distillery from A. A knew B's motive and intention but did not become a party to the conspiracy. 

The Court held that A could recover his rent. Brooklyn Distilling Co. v. Standard Distilling & Distributing Co., 193 N. 

Y. 551, 86 N. E. 564; Tracy v. Talmage, 14 N. Y. 162, 67 

Am. Dec. 132. In Hull v. Ruggles, 56 N. Y. 424, the action was for goods sold and delivered. The defense was that the goods, which consisted of what is known as prize candy packages with some articles of silverware, were intended to be used as a lottery, of which plaintiff had notice, and that he prepared the goods for that purpose. The Court said: "The plaintiff cites, as an authority in his favor, Tracy v. Talmage, 14 N. Y. 162. That case does hold, that mere knowledge by the vendor, that the purchaser intends to make an unlawful use of the property, is not a defense to an action for its price. That is perhaps all that was necessary to decide in that case, for the determination of the questions there involved. But it is also said there, that if the vendor, with knowledge of the intent of the purchaser, do anything beyond making the sale, to aid or further the unlawful design, he cannot recover for the property." The Court held that the plaintiff could not recover because he aided in carrying out the unlawful design. 

Clearly, if a party aids in the accomplishment of the illegal purpose, the contract is illegal. Materne v. Horwitz, 101 

N. Y. 469, 5 N. E. 331.

If, however, the seller knows or has reason to believe that the goods are to be used for a felonious or treasonable purpose, he cannot recover, the English rule, stated above, being applied in such grave cases. Hanauer v. Doane, 12 

Wall. (U.S.) 342, 20 Law. Ed. 439; Fuller v. Hunt, 182 

Mass. 299, 65 N. E. 390.

Merely lending money with the knowledge that it will be used for gambling purposes does not make the contract 170

EFFECT OF ILLEGALITY §§ 187-189 

void. But the rule is otherwise if there was an express agreement that it should be used for such purposes. Tyler v. Carlisle, 79 Me. 210, 9 Atl. 356. Thus, in Pennsylvania 

R. R. Co. v. Rosenfeld, 249 Fed. 964, it was held that one who lends money to the loser in an illegal transaction, such as gaming or betting, can recover the loan, notwithstanding he knows the loser is going to pay his indebtedness with it. It is obvious that the loan is disconnected from the illegal act and that the cause of action is founded on a distinct and collateral consideration. 

§ 188. 

Relief of Party in Pari Delicto. 

As between parties in pari delicto, the courts as a rule will not enforce an illegal contract or any supposed rights growing out of it. The rule is often expressed that in such cases the law will leave the parties where it finds them. Haynes v. Rudd, 102 N. Y. 372, 7 N. E. 287; Hunter v. Wheate, 289 Fed. 604, 31 A. L. R. 980. Neither will the courts give relief to the plaintiff where his action arises out of his own moral turpitude or out of his violation of the general law enacted to effectuate the public policy of a state or a nation. Levy v. Kansas City, 168 Fed. 524, 22 L. R. A. (N.S.) 862; Platt v. Elias, 186 N. Y. 374, 79

N. E. 1, 11 L. R. A. (N.S.) 554. 

§ 189. 

Relief of Party Not in Pari Delicto. 

Where the parties are not in pari delicto, the courts will afford relief to the innocent party, notwithstanding the illegality of the contract and the fact that it is executed. The rule governing cases in which the parties are not in pari delicto is most frequently applied where the transaction is in violation of a law made for the protection of one party against the acts of the other. As they are not both at fault, the party protected may recover. Duval v. Wellman, 124 

N. Y. 156, 26 N. E. 343; Mason v. McLeod, 57 Kan. 105, 45 

Pac. 76, 41 L. R. A. 548; see Restatement, Contracts, sections 

601, 603, and 604. For example, a statute may declare a contract to be void, and yet only one of the parties may be 171-

§§ 189, 190  

guilty of its violation. Enactments of this character are often for the purpose of protecting one class of persons from the oppression and imposition of another class. Such is the case with all laws which declare usurious contracts void. 

The lender is not allowed to take advantage of the statute, because he is the guilty party, but the borrower may do so, because he is not particeps criminis. He is regarded as the victim of the usurer. The principle applies to every contract declared to be void by statute, in the making of which one of the parties has acted illegally. Tracy v. Talmage, 14 

N. Y. 162, 67 Am. Dec. 132. The general principle has been asserted that if a plaintiff, though culpable, has not been guilty of moral turpitude and the loss he will suffer by denial of relief is wholly out of proportion to the requirements either of public policy or of appropriate individual punishment, he may be allowed to recover. Rosasco Creameries, Inc. v. Cohen, 276 N. Y. 274, 11 N. E. (2d) 908. So, too, where a woman in a nervous and broken condition agreed to pay an attorney an exorbitant contingent fee for the procurement of a divorce, it was held that the parties were not in pari delicto so as to prevent a recovery by the woman from the attorney. In re Sylvester's Estate, 195 Iowa 1329, 192 N. W. 442, 30 A. L. R. 180. 

§ 190. 

Locus Poenitentiae. 

The phrase locus poenitentiae (place for repentance) refers to the opportunity to repent and withdraw from the projected contract before it has become fully executed. 

Thus, if money is paid or goods are delivered for an illegal purpose, they may be recovered before the purpose is carried out. Congress & Empire Spring Co. v. Knowlton, 103 U. S. 49, 26 Law. Ed. 347; Bernard v. Taylor, 23 Ore. 

416, 31 Pac. 968, 18 L. R. A. 859. In these cases, the recovery is based not on the illegal contract but on the repudiation thereof. A recovery is allowed because it has the effect of discouraging the making of illegal contracts. Falkenberg v. Allen, 18 Okla. 210, 90 Pac. 415, 10 L. R. A. (N.S.) 494. As to the right of recovery of money paid on 172

EFFECT OF ILLEGALITY §§ 190-191a an illegal executory contract respecting a newspaper prize contest, see Greenberg v. Evening Post Assn., 91 Conn. 

371, 99 Atl. 1037. $191.

Conflict of Laws-Validity of Contract. 

If a plaintiff sues in New York for breach of a contract which had been made in New York and which by its terms was to be performed in New York, the validity of the contract is clearly to be determined by the New York law. But if the contract sued upon in New York was made in a second state, such as Maine, and was to be performed in a third state, such as Vermont, the highly perplexing problem arises as to precisely what law should be applied to determine the validity of the contract. While the authorities are not harmonious, the tendency of the New York courts in conflict of law cases involving contracts is to apply the law of the place where the contract was made. Swift & Co. v. Bankers 

Trust Co., 280 N. Y. 135, 19 N. E. (2d) 992. See, also, Greene, "New York Rules as to the Law Governing Validity of Contracts," 12 Cornell University Law Quarterly 286. 

The Restatement is to the same effect. Restatement, Conflict of Laws, sec. 360. However, even though the contract be deemed valid where made, it will not be enforced in New 

York if its enforcement will violate New York's public policy. Goodrich, Conflict of Laws, 2d Ed., sec. 8. 

§ 191a. What Law Governs-Subsequent Statutes. 

Generally, laws in force at the time a contract is made govern the obligations incurred under the contract. The legislature, however, has the power to pass legislation which may be retroactive in effect without violating state and federal constitutional provisions against taking property without due process of law. See New York Constitution, Article

I, section 6; United States Constitution, Fourteenth Amendment, section 1; Weiler v. Dry Dock Savings Institution, 258 

App. Div. 581, 17 N. Y. S. (2d) 192. The state may establish regulations reasonably necessary to secure the general welfare, although freedom of contract may be abridged, 173

§ 191a  

when great public needs so require. People ex rel. Durham 

Realty Corp. v. La Fetra, 230 N. Y. 429, 130 N. E. 601, 16

A. L. R. 152. Performance of contracts valid under existing laws may be made illegal by subsequent legislation. Thus, in 1933 Congress declared agreements in contracts which required payment in gold to be against public policy. The 

1933 Act was sustained on appeal to the Supreme Court with the result that persons who, by contract, had been promised payment in gold, were compelled to accept other tender in full payment. Norman v. Baltimore & Ohio R. R. Co., 294

U. S. 240, 79 Law. Ed. 885, 55 Sup. Ct. Rep. 407. Con versely, in certain circumstances, contracts which are invalid under laws as they exist at the time the contracts are made, may become enforceable if statutes removing the disability are adopted prior to the time stipulated for performance. Thus, a contract for the sale of wine entered into while the National Prohibition Act was in effect, but made in anticipation of repeal of the Eighteenth Amendment, was held binding after repeal. Farber v. John Aquino Sons, Inc., 253 App. Div. 600, 3 N. Y. S. (2d) 236. See, also, Lido

Capital Corp. v. Eskelsen, 162 Misc. 323, 295 N. Y. S. 163. 

Similarly, where a transaction was in its inception usurious, the defense of usury, nevertheless, was held not to be available after the repeal of the statute authorizing usury to be interposed as a defense. Curtis v. Leavitt, 15 N. Y. 9. 174

CHAPTER XI CONSIDERATION § 192. Consideration Defined. 

Every contract, as a rule, must have a consideration. 

Without it there can be no enforceable promise. It lies at the base of all contractual obligations. The promisee-the plaintiff must furnish a consideration for the promise of the defendant, who, in an action based on the contract, is the promisor. A mere gratuitous promise is not binding.

There must be a quid pro quo—something for something. 

Therefore, consideration is something done or something promised to be done by the promisee in return for a promise. In determining whether or not a consideration exists, the test is: Did the promisee (plaintiff) do something or promise to do something, concerning which he was not already obligated, in return for the promisor's (defendant's) promise? If so, there is a consideration; otherwise, there is not. Street v. Galt, 136 App. Div. 724, 121 N. Y. S. 514, aff'd 202 N. Y. 575, 96 N. E. 1133. Consideration has also been defined as some right, interest, profit, or benefit accruing to the promisor, or some forbearance, detriment, loss, responsibility, act, labor, or service given, suffered, or undertaken by the promisee. Ward v. Erie R. R. Co., 87 

Misc. 365, 149 N. Y. S. 717, aff'd 215 N. Y. 629, 109 N. E. 

1095. But this definition is too broad and does not conform to the test given above. Various definitions of consideration will be found in Hamer v. Sidway, 124 N. Y. 538, 27 N. E. 256, 12 L. R. A. 463. The act of the promisee may be one of forbearance, or it may consist of the surrender of some legal right, and even though this does not involve financial loss to the promisee or gain to the promisor, the forbearance to do something, or the doing of something he was not legally bound to do, is a sufficient consideration. 

Even if the forbearance of the right be beneficial to the party forbearing, it is nevertheless a sufficient consideration. Thus, the giving up of the right to use tobacco, to 175

§§ 192, 193  

swear, to play cards, or to abstain from the use of intoxicating liquor, is sufficient consideration, even though it is quite impossible to discover any trace of pecuniary loss to the promisee and despite the fact that the forbearance may have been highly beneficial to him. Hamer v. Sidway, supra. Also, where A gave his note to B for $10,000, in consideration that B name his child after A, the Court, holding the naming of the child to be a sufficient consideration, said: "Where a party contracts for the performance of an act which will afford him pleasure, gratify his ambition, please his fancy, or express his appreciation of a service another has done him, his estimate of value should be left undisturbed, unless, indeed, there is evidence of fraud." Wolford v. Powers, 85 Ind. 294, 44 Am. Rep. 16.

So, too, the privilege of naming a child was held to be a valid consideration for a promise to pay money. In Gardner v. Denison, 217 Mass. 492, 105 N. E. 359, 51 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1108, the Court said: "He [the child] loses the opportunity of receiving a more advantageous name, and is compelled to bear whatever detriment may flow from the name imposed upon him." Likewise, a contract to pay the plaintiff a certain amount each week as long as he abstained from the sale of a specified newspaper was held to be not without consideration. Rague v. New York Evening Journal Publishing Co., 164 App. Div. 126, 149 N. Y. S. 668.

In an English case, dated 1661, a woman, after the death of her husband, said to one of his creditors: "If you prove that he owed you 20 pounds, I will pay it." will pay it." Proof of such claim was declared to be sufficient consideration to support an action against the woman on her promise. Traverse v. 

Meres, T. Raymond 32, 83 Eng. Rep. 18. 

§ 193. 

Necessity of Consideration.

Aside from a few well-defined exceptions, consideration is necessary to the validity of every contract, and this is true irrespective of its form. At common law, a sealed instrument was conclusively presumed to have a consideration, and it was upon this presumption that a gratuitous prom- 176

CONSIDERATION §§ 193-195 

9 ise under the "hand and seal" of the promisor was enforceable. The common law rule has been changed in many jurisdictions by statute, some states having completely abolished the distinction between sealed and unsealed contracts. Williston on Contracts, Rev. Ed., Vol. I, sec. 218. 

See Perkins and Van Hecke, "Statutory Modification of Consideration," 9 North Carolina Law Review 196. In New

York, the law provides that "A seal upon a written instrument hereafter executed shall not be received as conclusive or presumptive evidence of consideration." Civil Practice

Act, sec. 342. For further discussion of the nature and effects of the seal, see sections 226 to 235, infra. 

§ 194. 

Consideration and Motive.

While the consideration stated in a contract may be the legal motive for a promise, a motive for a promise may exist and yet may not amount to a consideration. If a motive alone were equivalent to a consideration, every promise made free from fraud and duress would be enforceable without a consideration. The promisor has a motive in keeping every promise which he makes; namely, the maintaining of his reputation for keeping his promises. A promise to distribute articles for advertising purposes may be impelled by purely selfish and mercenary motives, but such motives are not a substitute for consideration. Fink v. 

Cox, 18 Johns. (N.Y.) 145, 9 Am. Dec. 191. 

§ 195. Exception to the Rule Requiring Consideration. 

To the rule that a consideration is essential to the validity of every contract there are the following exceptions: 

1. Holder in due course of negotiable instrument. Want of consideration is no defense in an action by a holder in due course of a negotiable instrument. As to such a holder there is a conclusive presumption of a consideration. Negotiable Instruments Law, secs. 54 and 96; Dougherty v. 

Salt, 227 N. Y. 200, 125 N. E. 94. 2. 

Validity of certain agreements made without consideration. From time immemorial, an agreement between at 177

§ 

debtor and a creditor whereby the creditor extends to a fixed future the time of payment of a matured debt has been held by the New York courts to be invalid because of want of consideration. But this rule, so long sustained by 

Kellogg v. Olmsted, 25 N. Y. 189, and Olmstead v. Latimer, 158 N. Y. 313, 53 N. E. 5, 43 L. R. A. 685, has been completely nullified by statute. In 1934, the New York Legislature, by statutory enactment, validated such an agreement, although it was without consideration, provided the extension agreement was in writing and signed by both parties thereto. Effective April 6, 1936, the law was further amended, so that, by statute: "An agreement hereafter made to change or modify, or to discharge in whole or in part, any contract, obligation, or lease, or any mortgage or other security interest in personal or real property, shall not be invalid because of the absence of consideration, provided that the agreement changing, modifying or discharging such contract, obligation, lease, mortgage or security interest, shall be in writing and signed by the party against whom it is sought to enforce the change, modification or discharge." 

Personal Property Law, sec. 33, subd. 2; Real Property Law, sec. 279. Prior to these statutory amendments, any agreement to change, modify, or discharge contractual obligations was invalid if not supported by consideration. The old rule is still unchanged insofar as oral agreements of the type specified are concerned. Thus, to render such agreements valid, there must be either consideration or a writing signed by the party against whom enforcement is sought. The New 

York statute now also provides that "A written instrument, hereafter executed, which purports to be a total or partial release of all claims, debts, demands or obligations, or a total or partial release of any particular claim, debt, demand or obligation, or a release or discharge in whole or in part of a mortgage, lien or charge upon personal or real property, shall not be invalid because of the absence of consideration or of a seal." Debtor and Creditor Law, sec. 243. 

3. Performance of gratuitous service. A promise to perform a gratuitous service, although not enforceable as 178

CONSIDERATION 

§ 195 a promise, imposes a duty to use due care and skill in its performance if one enters upon the undertaking. Thorne v. Deas, 4 Johns. (N.Y.) 84. Thus, if one gratuitously agrees to take charge of another's horse as a favor to the other, he is under an obligation to water and feed the horse, although there would be no liability for his failure to enter upon a performance of his gratuitous promise. 

Glanzer v. Shepard, 233 N. Y. 236, 135 N. E. 275, 23 A. 

L. R. 1425; Carr v. Maine Central R. R., 78 N. H. 502, 102 Atl. 532, L. R. A. 1918E 389. 4. 

Obligations created by statute. A statutory obligation on a bond or other obligation made valid and authorized by positive law needs no consideration to support it. 

The legislature may dispense entirely with the necessity of consideration, or it may limit its operation. Mittnacht v. 

Kellermann, 105 N. Y. 461, 12 N. E. 28; Carpenter v. Taylor, 164 N. Y. 171, 58 N. E. 53. еб 5. Promises enforceable by doctrine of estoppel_Promises may be enforced on the ground of estoppel rather than on the basis of a consideration where the promisee, relying on the promise, has assumed obligations or changed his position so that he must suffer loss if the promise is not fulfilled. Ricketts v. Scothorn, 57 Neb. 51, 77 N. W. 365, 42 L. R. A. 794. The exact limits of the doctrine of promissory estoppel have never been clearly defined. It is comparatively modern and it is interesting to note that the leading authority on the question, the Ricketts case, supra, has never been cited with approval. The doctrine finds some apparent support in De Cicco v. Schweizer, 221 N. Y. 431, 117 

N. E. 807, L. R. A. 1918E 1004, and in certain charitable subscription cases. See section 215, infra. There is another type of estoppel, where, as a matter of public policy, persons who hold themselves forth as committed to certain obligations are estopped from denying that such obligations exist. 

Thus, where a fictitious note was delivered to a bank, with the intention that it become part of the bank's apparent assets, though not intended to be enforceable, the maker of the note was estopped from denying his liability. It was held 179

§§ 195  

immaterial whether or not the note was based on a sufficient consideration, since the stability of banks and their proper operation is a matter of public concern, and "Public policy requires that a person who, for the accommodation of the bank executes an instrument which is in form a binding obligation, should be estopped from thereafter asserting that simultaneously the parties agreed that the instrument should not be enforced." Mount Vernon Trust Co. v. Bergoff, 272 

N. Y. 192, 5 N. E. (2d) 196. See, also, Bay Parkway National Bank v. Shalom, 270 N. Y. 172, 200 N. E. 686. 

§ 196. Unreal Consideration. 

Various types of unreal consideration may be classified as follows: 

Promise based upon past consideration or moral obli- gation; 

2. Promise based upon performance of legal obligation; 

3. Promise to do the impossible; 

4. Promise that is vague and uncertain; 

5. Promise based upon "good" as distinguished from "valuable" consideration. 

§ 197. 

Past Consideration-Moral Obligation. 

A consideration as to time may be executed or executory, but it cannot be past. A past consideration is not a consideration in a legal sense but is merely an act or forbearance whereby a benefit has been received by a person who at the time he received such benefit was under no legal obligation to pay for it. If he subsequently promises to pay for the favor, we say the promise is based on a past consideration, as it relates to acts in the past; the promisee does nothing, nor does he promise to do anything in return for the promise made to him. While either an executed or an executory consideration is sufficient to support a promise, a past consideration is not. Under past considerations may be classified many so-called moral obligations. For example, a person may make certain promises because he feels in 180

CONSIDERATION §§ 197, 198 

honor bound to do so on account of past favors of which he has been the recipient; or he may be prompted by motives of conscience, honor, or friendship; but such promises are unenforceable because the consideration is past, even though there may be a moral obligation resting upon the promisor to confer a benefit on the promisee. When one promises to pay for past services or for past hospitality, he gains nothing by making the promise, nor does the promisee do anything because of the promise; therefore, there is no consideration to support the promise. Mills v. 

Wyman, 3 Pick. (Mass.) 207; Dearborn v. Bowman, 3 Metc. (Mass.) 155; Myers v. Dean, 11 Misc. 368, 32 N. Y. S. 

237; Pershall v. Elliott, 249 N. Y. 183, 163 N. E. 554. See, also, notes 53 L. R. A. 353, 26 L. R. A. (N.S.) 520. 

§ 198. Past Consideration-Exceptions. 

To the rule that a past consideration is not a sufficient consideration to support a promise, there are certain exceptions. All, or nearly all, of such exceptions are cases wherein there was at one time a legal liability which may now be avoided by the promisor taking advantage of some defense given to him by law. The promisor may waive, or decline to take advantage of, the defense thus given to him by law. A renewal of the promise would constitute a waiver. The promisor then would be in the same position as he was in before his release by law. The new promise is binding even though it is without any new consideration. 

The well-recognized exceptions are: 

1. New promise after bar by the statute of limitations.

A debt barred by the statute of limitations is revived by a subsequent promise to pay it. In New York and many other jurisdictions, the promise must be in writing, except where a new promise is implied from a part payment of interest or principal. Civil Practice Act, sec. 59; Harper v. 

Fairley, 53 N. Y. 442. 

2. New promise after discharge in bankruptcy. It is well settled that a new promise to pay a debt discharged in bankruptcy is enforceable without a new consideration to 181

§ 

support it. Fonville v. Wichita State Bank & Trust Co., 161 Ark. 93, 255 S. W. 561, 33 A. L. R. 125. 

In many states, an oral promise is sufficient for this purpose. 

Vachon v. Ditz, 114 Wash. 11, 194 Pac. 545. However, in many other states including New York, such promise is not enforceable unless reduced to writing. Personal Property 

Law, sec. 31. In New York the promise must be expressed, since no enforceable promise to pay the balance can be implied from a part payment of a debt discharged in bankruptcy. Lawrence v. Harrington, 122 N. Y. 408, 25 N. E. 

406; Dusenbury v. Hoyt, 53 N. Y. 521, 13 Am. Rep. 543.

A conflict of opinion exists as to whether or not a new promise to pay a debt discharged by bankruptcy proceedings is enforceable without any new consideration to support it, when there has been a composition of the creditors assented to and accepted by the one seeking to enforce such new promise. Many courts have held that the new promise is unenforceable, on the theory that the discharge in this instance is by act of the parties and not by operation of law. Shepard v. Rhodes, 7 R. I. 470, 84 Am. Dec. 

573. But in New York such a promise is enforceable. 

Herrington v. Davitt, 220 N. Y. 162, 115 N. E. 476, 1 A. 

L. R. 1700 and note. 

3. New promise after majority. A promise by an adult to perform a contract made during his infancy needs no new consideration. A person who has reached the age of twenty-one is supposed to have acquired the power of deciding for himself whether in equity and in conscience he ought to discharge the debt, even though the law would not compel him to do so. He may waive the privilege of infancy, and if he chooses to do so, the new promise will operate upon the preceding consideration and will constitute a ratification of the original contract. Henry v. Root, 33 N. Y. 

526. An interesting case in this connection is Parsons v. 

Teller, 188 N. Y. 318, 80 N. E. 930, wherein an agreement made during infancy was without consideration, and therefore a new promise based thereon was held to be unenforceable, though made after the infant had attained majority. 1. 182

CONSIDERATION 

$$ 198, 199 4. Waiver by indorser of right to notice of dishonor. An indorser of a note who has been discharged from liability for want of due notice of dishonor may waive his discharge. 

This well-settled rule is now embraced in the Negotiable Instruments Law, section 180, which provides, as follows: "Notice of dishonor may be waived, either before the time of giving notice has arrived, or after the omission to give due notice, and the waiver may be express or implied." No new consideration is necessary to make such waiver effective. Ross v. Hurd, 71 N. Y. 14, 27 Am. Rep. 1.

The principle on which the foregoing exceptions to the rule that a past consideration or moral obligation is no consideration rest, is "that where the consideration was originally beneficial to the party promising, yet if he be protected from liability by some provision of the statute or common law, meant for his advantage, he may renounce the benefit of that law; and if he promises to pay the debt, which is only what an honest man ought to do, he is then bound by the law to perform it." Earle v. Oliver, 2 Exch. 

71, 154 Eng. Rep. 410. Shepard v. Rhodes, supra. 

5. Act performed at the request of the promisor. An exception to the rule that a past consideration is insufficient to support a promise is said to exist where the act was done at the request of the promisor. But this exception must be confined to cases where the request is made under such circumstances that the request implies a promise to pay. It does not include instances where an act has been done as a mere favor. In such cases, the subsequent promise is unenforceable for want of consideration. Hicks v. Burhans, 10 

Johns. (N.Y.) 243; Moore v. Elmer, 180 Mass. 15, 61 N. E. 

259; Blanshan v. Russell, 32 App. Div. 103, 52 N. Y. S. 

963, aff'd 161 N. Y. 629, 55 N. E. 1093; Edson v. Poppe, 24 S. D. 466, 124 N. W. 441, 26 L. R. A. (N.S.) 534. 

§ 199. 

"Volunteer" Not Favored by Law.

It is no longer the rule that, where one person voluntarily performs the legal obligation of another person and the latter afterwards promises to pay him therefor, the 183

§§ 199, 200  

promise is supported by a sufficient consideration. "Such an individual" (volunteer), said Surrogate Wingate in Matter of Welton, 141 Misc. 674, 253 N. Y. S. 128, "is permitted to suffer the usual legal fate of an intermeddler and is denied any rights whatsoever against any one, as a result of the transaction. The usual characteristics of human nature naturally tend to make such a situation of rare occurrence." McNamee v. Zimmett, 207 App. Div. 

60, 202 N. Y. S. 372, aff'd 239 N. Y. 602, 147 N. E. 213.

To the same effect is Massachusetts Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Green, 185 Mass. 306, 70 N. E. 202. 

§ 200. Unreal Consideration-Promise Based on Performance of Prior Legal Obligation or Contract. 

It has been noted that by statutory provision, no consideration is necessary to make valid the modification or discharge of a contractual obligation if the new promise is in writing and signed by the person whom it is sought to hold responsible. Personal Property Law, sec. 33, subd. 2; Real

Property Law, sec. 279; Debtor and Creditor Law, sec. 243. 

Except for such statutory provisions as eliminate the need for consideration, doing or promising to do that which one is already legally bound to do is an insufficient consideration to support a promise, for the promisee gets nothing more than he is already entitled to. The new promise creates no new legal obligation on the part of the promisor. Lingenfelder v. Wainwright Brewing Co., 103 Mo. 578, 15 S. W. 

844; Robinson v. Jewett, 116 N. Y. 40, 22 N. E. 224. The rule is well stated in McDevitt v. Stokes, 174 Ky. 515, 192 

S. W. 681, L. R. A. 1917D 1100, as follows: "A promise to do what the promisor is already bound to do cannot be a consideration, for if a person gets nothing in return for his promise but that to which he is already legally entitled, the consideration is unreal. This legal obligation may arise from: (1) the law independent of contract, or it may arise from (2) a subsisting contract. *** Where a party is under duty created or imposed by law to do what he does, or promises to do, his act or promise is clearly of 184 

EL 

TE

CONSIDERATION §§ 200, 201 

no value and is not a sufficient consideration for a promise given in return."

The preexisting legal obligation which the promisor promises to perform may have been created either (a) by contract, or (b) by law, independent of contract.

Suppose A, in the performance of an excavation contract with B, discovers that the earth which he had supposed to be sand is solid rock, in consequence of which he refuses performance of his contract unless B promises to pay him an additional $25,000, and B so promises. Is B liable? Weston v. State of New York, 262 N. Y. 46, 186 

N. E. 197.

There are, however, a few most exceptional cases holding that unforeseen difficulties and burdens may be of such a character as to support a promise for additional compensation. United Steel Co. v. Casey, 262 Fed. 889; Meech v. City of Buffalo, 29 N. Y. 198; Linz v. Schuck, 106 Md. 

220, 67 Atl. 286, 11 L. R. A. (N.S.) 789. Perhaps the better theory in this connection, and one which has been resorted to by some of the authorities in order to render the promise to pay the additional sum enforceable, is that the new agreement is a substitution for the old agreement, whether the latter is abandoned while wholly executory or after partial performance. Morecraft v. Allen, 78 N. J. L. 

729, 75 Atl. 920, L. R. A. 1915B 1 and note. See Schwartzreich v. Bauman-Basch, Inc., 231 N. Y. 196, 131 N. E. 887. 

§ 201. Promise Made by a Third Person To Induce Performance of Existing Contract. 

While the rule is quite generally recognized that a promise made to induce the other party to perform his contract with the promisor is without consideration, a serious conflict exists in the cases as to whether the performance of an existing contract between two parties, or a promise to perform it, is a sufficient consideration to support a promise made by a third person to induce said performance. 

Thus, if A is under contract with B, is a promise by Y to A to induce him to perform his contract with B enforce- 185

§  

able? In England and Massachusetts this question is answered affirmatively. Shadwell v. Shadwell, 9 C. B. (N.S.) 159, 142 Eng. Rep. 62; Abbott v. Doane, 163 Mass. 

433, 40 N. E. 197, 34 L. R. A. 33 and note. In New York and other jurisdictions, the question is answered negatively. In De Cicco v. Schweizer, 221 N. Y. 431, 117 N. E. 

807, L. R. A. 1918E 1004, Judge Cardozo, in delivering the opinion of the Court, said: "The courts of this state are committed to the view that a promise by A to B to induce him not to break his contract with C is void." To the same effect is Arend v. Smith, 151 N. Y. 502, 45 N. E. 

872. However, in New York, prior to the decision in De

Cicco v. Schweizer, supra, it had been held by the Appellate 

Division of the Supreme Court, Second Department, that, if the promisor has an interest in the performance of the contract which the promisee has made with a third party, such interest is sufficient consideration to make the promise enforceable. Manetti v. Doege, 48 App. Div. 567, 62

N. Y. S. 918. This position has been upheld in other jurisdictions. Abbott v. Doane, supra; Bradley v. Glenmary 

Co., 64 N. J. Eq. 77, 53 Atl. 49. 

So, too, in De Cicco v. Schweizer, supra, the fulfillment of a marriage contract was held to be a sufficient consideration for an agreement by the bride's father with the intended groom to pay an annuity to the bride. In the instant case, the daughter of the defendant was engaged to be married. The defendant thereafter promised the daughter's intended husband that, if the marriage took place, he would pay his daughter an annuity. They were married, and the assignee of the husband and wife sued on the promise. The Court held that he could recover. The basis of the holding is that, although each was bound to the other, together they were not bound to a third person; they performed an act which no third person had a legal right to call upon them to do, and which, therefore, was a valid consideration. Although the case arrives at a conclusion contrary to the weight of authority, it cannot be regarded as in direct conflict therewith, because of the rather 186

CONSIDERATION §§ 201, 202 

novel circumstances of the case. Judge Crane, in concurring with Judge Cardozo, held that the contract could be supported as a marriage settlement. Note that since the 

De Cicco v. Schweizer decision, the 1936 amendment to the

Civil Practice Act, section 61-b, abolishing the right to recover damages for breach of contract to marry, has been adopted. The Court of Appeals decision must be re-evaluated in the light of this amendment. 

§ 202. 

Part Payment in Satisfaction of Debt. 

The payment of a smaller sum in satisfaction of a larger matured and liquidated amount is not a sufficient consideration to support a promise to release the debtor from liability as to the entire debt. To have this effect, there must be something else given or some additional benefit or legal possibility of benefit to the creditor. For example, Foakes v. 

Beer, 9 App. Cas. (Eng.) 605 (1884): A owes B $500, which is past due; the latter promises to discharge the debt if the former will pay $300. The payment of $300 by A does not discharge him. B may subsequently sue A for the balance, $200. But the gift of a horse, a hawk or a robe, in satisfaction is good. "For it shall be intended that a horse, hawk, or robe, etc., might be more beneficial to the plaintiff than the money, in respect of some circumstance, or otherwise the plaintiff would not have accepted of it in satisfaction." Pinnel's Case, 5 Coke 117a, 77 Eng. Rep. 237. 

Ludington v. Bell, 77 N. Y. 138; Restatement, Contracts, sec. 76. "A negotiable security may operate, if so given and taken, in satisfaction of a debt of a greater amount; the circumstance of negotiability making it in fact a different thing and more advantageous than the original debt which was not negotiable." Jaffray v. Davis, 124 N. Y. 164, 26 

N. E. 351, 11 L. R. A. 710. Restatement, Contracts, sec. 76.

In Jaffray v. Davis, supra, it was held, that where one, indebted on an open book account, gave to his creditor his promissory notes, secured by a chattel mortgage, for onehalf of the debt under an agreement with the creditor that he would accept the same in full satisfaction and discharge 187

§ 

of the debt, and the debtor paid the notes as they became due, and the creditor satisfied the mortgage, the new agreement was valid and supported by a sufficient consideration. 

A case apparently in conflict with this rule is Shanley v. 

Koehler, 80 App. Div. 566, 80 N. Y. S. 679, aff'd 178 

N. Y. 556, 70 N. E. 1109. This was an action for the specific performance of a contract. The defendant, plaintiff's judgment creditor for $226.29, agreed with the plaintiff that, upon receiving $50 in cash and plaintiff's negotiable note for $50, if the note was paid at maturity, he would accept the same in full settlement of the judgment of record. The note was paid when due, but the defendant refused to keep his part of the agreement. The Court held that the promise was without consideration. The decision is based on the ground that the receipt of the note did not put the creditor in a better position than he was before such note was given.

To the rule that the payment of a smaller sum in satisfaction of a larger one is not a sufficient consideration for discharging the entire debt, certain exceptions are recognized, as follows: 

1. Where payment is made in conformity with the creditor's written agreement to accept a lesser amount (Personal 

Property Law, section 33, subdivision 2 and section 33-a; 

Real Property Law, sections 279 and 280) or where the creditor executes a written instrument "which purports to be a total or partial release of all claims, debts, demands or obligations, or a total or partial release of any particular claim, debt, demand or obligation ***." Debtor and Creditor Law, sec. 243. These statutory provisions eliminate the need either of consideration or of a seal when there is a modification or discharge made in writing of an existing contractual obligation. The one who signs such an agreement is held to the promise he thus makes. Even the obligation of a sealed instrument may be discharged wholly or in part by an unsealed written agreement signed by the creditor. 

2. The creditor may make a gift of the debt to the debtor. 188

CONSIDERATION §§ 202, 203 

1 No formal words or expressions are required to constitute a gift. It is a question of intent, and the inquiry is as to what was intended by that which was said and done. The gift may be accomplished by a delivery of any evidence of indebtedness. The delivery of a note is evidence tending to show an intent to give. McKenzie v. Harrison, 120 N. Y. 

260, 24 N. E. 458, 8 L. R. A. 257; Liebreich v. Tyler State 

Bank & Trust Co., 100 S. W. (2d) (Tex.) 152. Thus, on a mortgage given to a father by his son, the father indorsed a receipt of a sum of money, which he had not received, to be applied on the bond and mortgage, intending it to be a gift of the amount. It was held to be a valid gift and not merely a promise to give without consideration. Carpenter v. Soule, 88 N. Y. 251, 42 Am. Rep. 248. 

3. Under the common law, the rule that the payment of a lesser sum will not discharge a greater applies only when the amount is past due and is definite and certain. Where the amount due is in dispute or unliquidated, the payment of a sum less than is claimed is sufficient consideration to support a promise to release the debtor from all further liability. The doctrine of accord and satisfaction applies in such cases. This subject is treated in Chapter XXII, infra. 

Fuller v. Kemp, 138 N. Y. 231, 33 N. E. 1034, 20 L. R. A. 

785 and note; Nassoiy v. Tomlinson, 148 N. Y. 326, 42 N. E. 715. 

4. Payment by a third person of a lesser sum than the amount due is sufficient consideration to support a discharge of the entire debt. This is sound in theory for the reason that the debtor alone is legally bound. Clearly, any payment by a third person is the doing of something which he is not legally bound to do. Sigler v. Sigler, 98 Kan. 524, 158 Pac. 864, L. R. A. 1917A 725; Marshall v. Bullard, 114 

Iowa 462, 87 N. W. 427, 54 L. R. A. 862. 

§ 203. 

Performance of Public Duty Imposed by Law. 

Public policy and sound morals, alike, forbid that a public officer should demand or receive, for services performed by him in the discharge of official duty, any other or further 189

§§ 203, 204  

remuneration or reward than that prescribed and allowed by law. Therefore, a promise to pay a peace officer, whose duty it is to make an arrest, or a promise to pay a witness, whose fees are fixed by law, extra compensation is without consideration. Duty imposed by law stands upon the same footing as duty imposed by contract. Smith v. Whildin, 10 

Pa. 39; Hatch v. Mann, 15 Wend. (N.Y.) 44. A public officer has no right to claim a reward for making an arrest which it was his duty to make. Atwood v. Armstrong, 102 

App. Div. 601, 92 N. Y. S. 596; Somerset Bank v. Edmund, 76 Ohio St. 396, 81 N. E. 641, 11 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1170.

However, if more is done than the legal duty requires, there is a sufficient consideration. Thus, in Reif v. Paige, 55 Wis. 496, 13 N. W. 473, 42 Am. Rep. 731, the defendant's wife was in a burning hotel, whereupon the defendant said: "I will give $5,000 to any person who will bring the body of my wife dead, or alive, out of that building."

The plaintiff was a paid officer of the fire department. 

With a view to claiming the reward he rescued the body. 

In an action for the reward, the Court held: "that inasmuch as the plaintiff could not rescue the body of Mrs. Paige from the burning building without imminent peril of losing his own life, and inasmuch as it was not his duty as a paid officer and member of the fire department to do so, he is in a position to claim the reward alleged to have been offered by the defendant for such rescue." To the same effect is Hartley v. Granville, 216 Mass. 38, 102 N. E. 942, 48 L. R. A. (N.S.) 392. 

§ 204. 

Performance of a Private Duty Imposed by Law. 

A promise to perform a domestic or private duty is without consideration. A promise by a husband to pay his wife for her services as housekeeper is void. Coleman v. 

Burr, 93 N. Y. 17, 45 Am. Rep. 160. See, also, Blaechinska v. Howard Mission & Home for Little Wanderers, 130 N. Y. 

497, 29 N. E. 755; Matter of Callister, 153 N. Y. 294, 47 

N. E. 268. Likewise, the promise of a blind woman to bequeath property to the man to whom she had already be- 190

CONSIDERATION §§ 204-206 

come engaged to marry is not enforceable where the sole consideration received is the promise of the husband to nurse and support her after marriage, duties which would devolve upon him in any event. Ryan v. Dockery, 134 Wis. 

431, 114 N. W. 820, 15 L. R. A. (N.S.) 491. So, too, a promise to surrender goods wrongfully withheld is not sufficient consideration to support a promise made by the owner of such goods to obtain their possession. Tolhurst v. Powers, 133 N. Y. 460, 31 N. E. 326. 

§ 205. 

Vague and Uncertain Promise as Consideration. 

A promise which is the consideration for another promise may be too vague and uncertain for enforcement. 

If so, the agreement is void. Where, for instance, in an action on a note given by a son to his father, the son pleaded a promise made by his father to discharge him from liability on the note, in consideration of his ceasing to make certain complaints to the effect that he had not enjoyed as many advantages as the other children, it was held that the son's promise was no more than a promise "not to bore his father," and was too vague to constitute a consideration for the father's promise. "A man," said the Court, "might complain that another person used the public highway more than he ought to do, and that other might say, 'do not complain and I will give you five pounds.' It is ridiculous to suppose that such promises could be binding." White v. Bluett, 2 C. L. R. 301; Hart v. Georgia R. R. Co., 101 

Ga. 188, 28 S. E. 637. In other words, the requirement that the consideration be certain is merely a part of the general requirement that all of the terms of an agreement must be definite in order to be enforceable. See section 18, supra. 

§ 206. Good Consideration-Term Defined. 

The term "good consideration" as distinguished from "valuable consideration" has no place in the law of contracts, but belongs in the field of conveyances in connection with such subjects as fraudulent conveyances, resulting 191

§§ 206, 207  

trusts, and covenants to stand seised to uses. In some instances, the terms good consideration and valuable consideration are used interchangeably. Aden v. City of Vallejo, 139 Cal. 165, 72 Pac. 905. Good consideration has been defined as a consideration founded upon natural love and affection between near relatives. Groves v. Groves, 65 Ohio 

St. 442, 62 N. E. 1044. Such a consideration, standing alone, is insufficient to render enforceable a promise based thereon. For example, the general duty of a husband to support his wife is not a sufficient consideration to validate a promissory note given by a husband to his wife without further consideration. Whitaker v. Whitaker, 52 N. Y. 

368, 11 Am. Rep. 711. A promise induced by gratitude and affection, to make provision for a friend is unenforceable as a contract. Parsons v. Teller, 188 N. Y. 318, 80 

N. E. 930. Likewise, a promissory note given by the maker to the payee in consideration of love and affection is unenforceable as between the immediate parties thereto. Sullivan v. Sullivan, 122 Ky. 707, 92 S. W. 966, 7 L. R. A. (N.S.) 156 and note. A few decisions to the contrary must be regarded as against the great weight of authority. 

Gooch v. Gooch, 178 Iowa 902, 160 N. W. 333, L. R. A. 1917C 582. 

A distinction should be noted between the gift of the donor's own note or check and the assignment by him, without consideration, of the note or check of another. The former, being executory in character is revocable because of the lack of consideration as to the donee, but in the case of the assignment of the note or check of a third party, there has been an executed gift which cannot be recalled by the donor. See Richardson v. Richardson, 148 Ill. 563, 36 

N. E. 608, 26 L. R. A. 305 and note; Woods v. Sturges, 116 Miss. 412, 77 So. 186, L. R. A. 1918C 338 and note. 

§ 207. 

Gifts-Engagement Ring-Engagement Presents. 

While an executed gift is irrevocable, an executory promise to confer on the promisee a benefit or a gift because of affection is wholly gratuitous and, therefore, unenforceable. 192

CONSIDERATION 

§ 207 If the purpose is to obtain a quid pro quo the promise is not gratuitous; it creates a legal obligation. Bibb v. Freeman, 59 

Ala. 612; Walton Water Co. v. Village of Walton, 238 N. Y. 

46, 143 N. E. 786. It is often difficult to discern the line which separates promises creating legal obligations from mere gratuitous promises which do not create contractual liabilities. Bibb v. Freeman, supra. Moreover, a gift may be conditional, not to become absolute until the performance of some future act.

The right of a rejected suitor to recover an engagement ring which he has given to a woman has been the subject of much litigation in the United States as well as in England. The controversy arises over the questions: What is the consideration for the gift? Did the suitor part with all "dominion over the property"? Is title to the ring vested in the woman? The weight of authority holds that, irrespective of the lapse of time between the mutual promises to marry and the gift of the ring, the gift is conditional and not absolute and title to the ring vests in the woman only upon a consummation of the marriage. However, if the man is in default without legal justification or is guilty of fraud, the gift becomes absolute. If the woman without legal justification breaks off the engagement, she must return the ring. Beck v. Cohen, 237 App. Div. 729, 262 N. Y. S. 716. Upon mutual rescission of the engagement, the ring is to be restored to the donor. Wilson v. 

Riggs, 243 App. Div. 33, 276 N. Y. S. 232, aff'd 267 N. Y. 

570, 196 N. E. 584. Frequently, many articles are exchanged between affianced persons in a sentimental spirit of giving, without any thought of attaching conditions to the gifts. 

In such instances, the claim of the jilted one for a return of the gifts is sympathetically regarded by the courts. Antaramian v. Ourakian, 118 Misc. 558, 194 N. Y. S. 100. 

These cases must be distinguished from cases in which gifts are made to influence the friendship or the favor of another, or as tokens of appreciation of another's kindness and hospitality. Such gifts are unconditional-the title vests in the donee hence, the donor cannot later, because of the ingrati- 193

§§ 207  

tude of the donee, recover the gifts. Editorial, New York 

Law Journal, July 21, 1932. 

§ 208. Forbearance To Exercise a Legal Right. 

A promise not to do what otherwise one has a legal right to do is a sufficient consideration. This may relate to a right of action or to personal conduct in general. Hamer v. 

Sidway, 124 N. Y. 538, 27 N. E. 256, 12 L. R. A. 463. Refraining from bidding at a sale is a sufficient consideration to support a promise. White v. McMath & Johnson, 127 

Tenn. 713, 156 S. W. 470, 44 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1115; Rauch v. Donovan, 126 App. Div. 52, 110 N. Y. S. 690. 

§ 209. 

Forbearance To Sue.

A promise not to sue on a claim may constitute a sufficient consideration for the forbearance of such a right. The test in determining whether forbearance to sue on a claim is sufficient consideration is: Was the dispute honest or fraudulent? If honest, it affords the basis for an accord between the parties, which the law favors. Post v. Thomas, 212 N. Y. 264, 106 N. E. 69; Blount v. Dillaway, 199 Mass. 

330, 85 N. E. 477, 17 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1036. But where the claim is not even doubtful, colorable, or plausible, in that there is no reason for an honest belief that it has some foundation in law or equity, forbearance to sue is without consideration. Di Iorio v. Di Biazio, 21 R. I. 208, 42 Atl. 

1114; Springstead v. Nees, 125 App. Div. 230, 109 N. Y. S. 

148. Forbearance to sue on an illegal claim, for example, a note given for a gambling debt, is an insufficient consideration to support a promise. This is true for the reason that such new contract might be an indirect affirmance of the prior illegal one. Union Collection Co. v. Buckman, 150 Cal. 159, 88 Pac. 708, 9 L. R. A. (N.S.) 568. It is well settled that an agreement to forbear to sue, in consideration of a promise by a third person to pay the debt of another, constitutes a valid contract. Traders' National 

Bank v. Parker, 130 N. Y. 415, 29 N. E. 1094. 194

CONSIDERATION §§ 210, 211 § 210. 

Agreement To Forbear. 

The agreement to forbear may be either expressed or implied. The time need not be fixed and definite. A reasonable time, which is a mixed question of law and fact, is presumed, in the absence of an express agreement as to length of time of forbearance. Strong v. Sheffield, 144 N. Y. 392, 39 N. E. 330; Traders' National Bank v. Parker, 130 N. Y. 

415, 29 N. E. 1094; Scherer v. East Side National Bank, 263 N. Y. 190, 188 N. E. 645. It has been held that mere forbearance or indulgence at the request of another is insufficient and that there must be an agreement to forbear and actual forbearance under such agreement. Queal & Co. v. Peterson, 138 Iowa 514, 116 N. W. 593, 19 L. R. A. (N.S.) 842. But such decisions are sound only when the offer called for a promise to forbear rather than a mere act of forbearance. Strong v. Sheffield, supra. 

§ 211. Compromise of Claim. 

Analogous to the relinquishment of a right to sue is the compromise of a disputed claim whereby the claim is extinguished. Whenever there is a dispute as to a doubtful or unliquidated claim, the parties may adjust their differences by a compromise, the mutual release of their respective rights being a sufficient consideration for the compromise. 

After a compromise has been agreed upon in good faith, the merits of the original controversy cannot be called into question; and this is true, though it should afterwards appear that one has thereby received property to which he was not legally entitled. But if the claimant knows his demand to be wholly groundless and forces the other party to a settlement by threats of suit, the compromise will be void. "It is not necessary to uphold a promise based upon the surrender or composition and compromise of a claim that it was a valid claim, one that could be enforced at law. 

A promise made upon a settlement of disputes, and to prevent litigation, is made upon a good consideration. The settlement of a doubtful claim will uphold a promise to pay a stipulated sum, or do any other lawful act." White v. 195

§§ 211, 212  

merican 

Hoyt, 73 N. Y. 505, 514; Sears v. Grand Lodge, Ancient 

Order of United Workmen, 163 N. Y. 374, 57 N. E. 618, 50 L. R. A. 204; Post v. Thomas, 212 N. Y. 264, 106 N. E. 

69; Schnell v. Perlmon, 238 N. Y. 362, 144 N. E. 641, 34

A. L. R. 1023. 

In Post v. Thomas, supra, the Court declared: "It is the settled law of this state that if a debt or claim be disputed or contingent at the time of payment the payment when accepted of a part of the whole debt is a good satisfaction, and it matters not that there was no solid foundation for the dispute. The test in such cases is, was the dispute honest or fraudulent? If honest it affords the basis for an accord between the parties, which the law favors, the execution of which is the satisfaction." But if the claim is not doubtful or uncertain, the new promise is without consideration.

The settlement of claims, illegal or void by statute or public policy, affords no consideration to support a promise. 

For an exhaustive note on whether void, invalid, or unfounded claims may be the subject of a valid compromise, see Armijo v. Henry, 14 N. M. 181, 89 Pac. 305, 25 L. R. A. (N.S.) 275. 

The compromise of a doubtful claim; i.e., doubtful as to whether there is any claim or as to the amount of the claim, is to be distinguished from a forbearance to sue upon a claim of a definite amount. In forbearance, the one forbearing simply postpones his suit; he does not agree to accept a smaller sum in full payment. 

§ 212. Composition with Creditors. 

The agreement to pay a lesser sum in satisfaction of a greater, in the case of a debtor's composition deed with his creditors, may appear, at first sight, to be an infraction of the rule that the payment of a part of a debt is an insufficient consideration to support a promise to discharge the whole of the debt, but it is not an exception to the rule. 

Such an agreement, if in writing, and properly signed, needs no consideration to support it. Personal Property Law, sec. 196

CONSIDERATION §§ 212, 213 

33, subd. 2; Real Property Law, sec. 279. But, regardless of these statutory provisions, consideration in a composition with creditors is found in the substitution of a new agreement, with new parties, and a new consideration. The consideration is the mutual forbearance of all the creditors to insist upon their claims. The rights and interests of others become involved in the new arrangement, and, hence, there is no violation of the rule that the payment of a lesser sum will not discharge a greater. A composition deed is an agreement between the creditors themselves as well as between them and their debtor. White v. Kuntz, 107 N. Y. 

518, 14 N. E. 423; Perkins v. Lockwood, 100 Mass. 249, 1 

Am. Rep. 103. 

§ 213. 

Mutual Promises. 

When a promise is given for a promise, a bilateral contract is formed, each promise being the consideration for the other, and therefore a sufficient consideration to support the contract resulting from the mutual promises, at least where there are promises to do what the parties are not otherwise legally bound to do. Thus, in the exchange of promissory notes, the note of each finds consideration in the note of the other. Each is bound to do some act in the future. Tradesmen's National Bank v. Curtis, 167 N. Y. 

194, 60 N. E. 429, 52 L. R. A. 430. Mutual promises of merchants to refrain from engaging in business after a certain hour of the evening each day give rise to a sufficient loss or detriment in the way of financial transactions, or to a sufficient gain and advantage from a social or healthful standpoint to support a contract to close their places of business at that hour. Stovall v. McCutchen & Co., 107 

Ky. 577, 54 S. W. 969, 47 L. R. A. 287.

The promises need not necessarily be expressed. One may be expressed and the other implied from the particular circumstances of the case. Wood v. Lucy, Lady Duff-Gordon, 222 N. Y. 88, 118 N. E. 214; Grossman v. Schenker, 206 

N. Y. 466, 100 N. E. 39.

But in Sorrentino v. Bouchet, 161 N. Y. S. 262, where a 197

§§ 213, 214  

contract of employment failed to specify that the employee would work for the time mentioned, it was held to be merely unilateral, the Court refusing to imply a promise to work for the time specified.

A close case illustrating a unilateral contract in form, and therefore not binding on the promisor, is Levin v. 

Dietz, 194 N. Y. 376, 87 N. E. 454, 20 L. R. A. (N.S.) 251.

In this case, the defendant, the owner of land, wrote a letter to the plaintiff saying that, if the latter would appear at a certain time and place with the price in cash, he would receive a deed to the property. The plaintiff appeared at the time and place with the money, prepared to demand the deed, but the defendant was not present. The Court held that since the plaintiff was not bound to buy, the contract was lacking in mutuality. See, also, Cohen & Sons v. 

Lurie Woolen Co., 232 N. Y. 112, 133 N. E. 370; Sun Printing & Publishing Assn. v. Remington Paper & Power Co., 235 N. Y. 338, 139 N. E. 470; Nassau Supply Co., Inc. v. Ice 

Service Co., Inc., 252 N. Y. 277, 169 N. E. 383. 

§ 214. Contingent Promises. 

When the consideration for a promise is a promise or an act, the whole contract may be contingent and may never come into effect, save at the will of one of the parties.
