A says to B, "If you will furnish goods to C, I will pay for them." B is not bound to furnish the goods, but if he does, he may recover on A's promise to pay for them. Willetts v. 

Sun Mutual Insurance Co., 45 N. Y. 45, 6 Am. Rep. 31.

Again, if A promises to supply, at a certain price, all the goods of a certain kind that B may need in his established business, and B promises to buy such goods, the promises are mutual and binding. Should B need no goods, he is not bound to pay anything, but in the event of the contingency of his needing them, he is bound to buy them of A. Wells v. 

Alexandre, 130 N. Y. 642, 29 N. E. 142, 15 L. R. A. 218

Other instances of a similar character are agreements to buy all, or a certain part of all, the goods of a certain kind that one may produce in a given time. McCall Co. v. Icks, 198

CONSIDERATION §§ 214, 215 

107 Wis. 232, 83 N. W. 300; Burgess Sulphite Fibre Co. v. 

Broomfield, 180 Mass. 283, 62 N. E. 367. In Jenkins & Co. v. Anaheim Sugar Co., 247 Fed. 958, L. R. A. 1918E 293, the mutual promises of the parties, one to supply and the other to buy from him exclusively all sugar required in the purchaser's established business during a specified period, were held to create an enforceable agreement. 

§ 215. Charitable Subscriptions-Doctrine of Promissory 

Estoppel.

Subscriptions for benevolent, educational, and religious purposes have furnished very fruitful sources of litigation. 

The question most frequently presented is as to the consideration therefor. As a general rule, a mere promise to donate money for a charitable purpose is gratuitous and, therefore, unenforceable. Matter of Taylor, 251 N. Y. 257, 167 N. E. 434.

In Presbyterian Church of Albany v. Cooper, 112 N. Y. 

517, 20 N. E. 352, 3 L. R. A. 468, which is a leading case in this State, the deceased, among others, had promised to pay the sum of $5,500 in order to wipe out a church mortgage in consideration of $1 nominally in hand paid to him "and the agreement of each other in this contract contained." A condition was that the sum of $45,000 be subscribed. On compliance with the condition, the trustees of the church brought suit against the decedent's estate for the sum of $3,500, being the balance due on his $5,500 subscription. The Court of Appeals refused to allow a recovery for four reasons: (1) the dollar was not in hand paid; (2) the promises of the other subscribers did not constitute consideration so far as the church was concerned; (3) the efforts of the church trustees to raise money were not at the decedent's request, express or implied-at least the Court so found; (4) the trustees in any case were under a duty to act on behalf of the church. For these reasons the 

Court held that decedent's promise was purely gratuitous and was unenforceable.

However, later cases hold that the subscriber is liable, 199

$

upon the theory that when the subscription carries with it an implied or express request to the promisee to render services or incur liabilities, after such expenditures or obligations are undertaken, the donor should be estopped to plead want of consideration. In Keuka College v. Ray, 167 N. Y. 96, 60 N. E. 325, the Court purported to find such an implied request on the part of the subscriber to the Trustees of Keuka College to perform services in order to raise funds for the College and, therefore, the performance of the services was the consideration for the subscriber's promise. 

This same principle was applied to a subscription to a charitable institution to aid it in carrying on its work. The subscription, it was held, constituted an offer of a unilateral contract. The offer was accepted by the charitable institution when it incurred liabilities in order to carry on its. work. This action in reliance on the promise was an acceptance of the donor's offer and created a binding obligation. I. & I. Holding Corp. v. Gainsburg, 276 N. Y. 427, 12 

N. E. (2d) 532, 115 A. L. R. 582; Restatement, Contracts, sec. 45. Charitable subscriptions may also be held enforceable on the ground that a bilateral contract has been created, expressly or by implication. Thus, where a college assumed the obligation of perpetuating the name of a donor, this implied promise constituted adequate consideration to sustain recovery of the amount pledged. Allegheny College v.

The National Chautauqua County Bank of Jamestown, 246 

N. Y. 369, 159 N. E. 173, 57 A. L. R. 980. In this case, the

Court referred to the doctrine of "promissory estoppel" as a modification of the basic concept of consideration without, however, passing upon its application in New York. In a subsequent case, it was stated: "That doctrine need not be applied to save a subscription where a request or invitation that the promisee go on with its work can be implied from the subscription agreement. It is only when a request or an invitation to carry on cannot be implied in fact that it is necessary to invoke that doctrine." I. & I. Holding Corp. v. Gainsburg, supra. For a discussion of promises to pay 200

CONSIDERATION §§ 215, 216 

charitable subscriptions "when convenient," see Editorial, New York Law Journal, September 30, 1939. 

From a moral and equitable standpoint, promises of this nature should be held binding where the beneficiary has gone ahead relying upon the agreement. Though the promise is without consideration in its inception, it is submitted that a consideration was supplied from the moment there was affirmative action by the donee on the strength of the promise.

Mutual subscriptions for a business purpose may be distinguished from subscriptions for a charitable purpose, in that, in such cases, there is usually some valuable consideration moving to the subscriber; namely, a promise to deliver to him certain shares of stock. Martin v. Meles, 179 Mass. 

114, 60 N. E. 397. 

§ 216. 

Adequacy of Consideration-In Law.

As a rule, the adequacy of the consideration is immaterial. Courts of law will not make bargains for the parties to a suit, and, if a man gets what he has contracted for, will not inquire whether it was equivalent to the promise which he gave in return. Hamer v. Sidway, 124 N. Y. 538, 27 N. E. 256, 12 L. R. A. 463. If courts were compelled to pass upon the adequacy of the consideration, their time. would be consumed in making contracts for the parties. 

This would violate the fundamental principle that there should be the utmost liberty and freedom in the making of contracts, and in refraining from making them if the individual so desires. "The value of all things contracted for," said Hobbes, "is measured by the appetite of the contractors, and therefore the just value is that which they be contented to give." Cited in Pollock on Contracts, sec. 172. "A valuable consideration," said the Court, in Lawrence v. McCalmont, 2 How. (U.S.) 426, 11 Law. Ed. 326, "how ever small or nominal, if given or stipulated for in good faith, is, in the absence of fraud, sufficient to support an action on any parol contract. * ** A stipulation in con- 201

§§ 216, 217  

sideration of one dollar is just as effectual and valuable a consideration as a larger sum stipulated for or paid." 

If an article be of the slightest value to either vendor or vendee, or of no value whatsoever, it will suffice, by way of consideration, for a promise to pay the agreed price, however disproportionate to the real value. Thus, where one purchased mulberry trees which turned out to be of no value by reason of being decayed and almost lifeless, it was held that, as there was neither fraud nor warranty, the inadequacy of the consideration was no defense to an action on the note given for the purchase price. Johnson v. Titus, 2 Hill (N.Y.) 606. So, also, the following have been held sufficient to constitute consideration: allowing the promisor to weigh certain boilers; surrendering a worthless document; and transferring worthless stock in a corporation. 

Bainbridge v. Firmstone, 8 Ad. & E. 743, 112 Eng. Rep. 

1019; Wilton v. Eaton, 127 Mass. 174; State Bank v. Gates, 114 Iowa 323, 86 N. W. 311.

For the same reason, where a person traveled for his own pleasure and benefit at the request of another, it was held that this was sufficient to support a promise by the latter to reimburse him for his expenses. Devecmon v. 

Shaw, 69 Md. 199, 14 Atl. 464. 

§ 217. When Value Is Fixed. 

The doctrine of inadequacy of consideration does not apply where the agreement is for the mere exchange of sums of money or coin whose value is fixed by law, and the element of time is of no importance. Thus, a promise to pay one dollar is no consideration for a promise to pay one thousand dollars. Shepard v. Rhodes, 7 R. I. 470, 84 Am. 

Dec. 573. In Schnell v. Nell, 17 Ind. 29, 79 Am. Dec. 453, the Court said: "It is true, that as a general proposition, inadequacy of consideration will not vitiate an agreement. 

But this doctrine does not apply to a mere exchange of sums of money, of coin, whose value is exactly fixed, but to the exchange of something of, in itself, indeterminate value, for money, or, perhaps, for some other thing of in- 202

CONSIDERATION §§ 217, 218 

determinate value. In this case, had the one cent mentioned, been some particular one cent, a family piece, or ancient, remarkable coin, possessing an indeterminate value, extrinsic from its simple money value, a different view might be taken. As it is, the mere promise to pay $600 for one cent, even had the portion of that cent due from the plaintiff been tendered, is an unconscionable contract, void, at first blush, upon its face, if it be regarded as an earnest one."

The practice which has grown up, in certain sections of the country, of reciting "in consideration of one dollar each to the other paid" appears to involve a mere exchange of sums of money of equal value and has, therefore, been condemned as insufficient to constitute consideration. Hall v. Allfree, 51 Ind. App. 387, 99 N. E. 813; Velie Motor Car 

Co. v. Kopmeier Motor Car Co., 194 Fed. 324. 

§ 218. Adequacy of Consideration—In Equity. 

As a rule, inadequacy is not sufficient ground for relief in equity, the rule in this respect being the same in equity as at law. Thus, in Parmelee v. Cameron, 41 N. Y. 392, the Court said: "There is no general rule of equity which relieves a party from hard and unreasonable bargains after they become executed, merely because they are such. On the contrary, mere inadequacy is not a sufficient ground for avoiding a sale, unless the inadequacy is gross as to afford presumptive evidence of actual fraud * * * or is attended with actual fraud, surprise, ignorance, mistake, delusion, or imbecility of mind." 

It should be noted also that, in rare instances, a court of equity will enforce agreements where there is, seemingly, no consideration. Thus, in Freeman v. Freeman, 43 N. Y. 

34, 3 Am. Rep. 657, the court of equity decreed specific performance of a promise by a father to make a gift of a farm to his son, where the son, relying upon such promise, had made substantial improvements upon the premises. The 

Court said: "It is further insisted, that an executory promise, not founded upon any valuable consideration, is a 203

§§ 218  

mere nude pact, furnishing no grounds for an action at law, and that performance of such a promise will not be enforced in equity. This is true so long as the promise has no consideration. *** Expenditures made upon permanent improvements upon land with the knowledge of the owner, induced by his promise, made to the party making the expenditure, to give the land to such party, constitute in equity a consideration for the promise." 

§ 219. Failure of Consideration. 

Strictly speaking, there is no such thing as a failure of consideration. The promisor either receives the consideration he has bargained for, or he does not. If he does not receive the consideration, there is no contract, for there is no consideration; and if there is consideration, there is no failure of it. If there be consideration at the outset, how can it afterwards fail? But it must be admitted that the terms "want" and "failure" of consideration are frequently used as synonymous terms. Hurlburt v. Kephart, 50 Colo. 

353, 115 Pac. 521; Ward v. Textile Commission Co., 139 

App. Div. 109, 123 N. Y. S. 918. 

§ 220. Nominal Consideration. 

The term nominal consideration is often applied to a cent, a dollar, a farthing, or other small sum recited as consideration in a contract. On principle and, by the weight of authority, such sum is sufficient consideration to support a contract. Thus, in Ross v. Parks, 93 Ala. 153, 8 So. 

368, 11 L. R. A. 148, fifty cents was held to be sufficient consideration to support a contract to grant an option for the purchase of a tract of land for $200. However, in certain jurisdictions, a nominal consideration has been held insufficient to support a contract. Thus, in Rude v. Levy, 43 Colo. 482, 96 Pac. 560, 24 L. R. A. (N.S.) 91, the payment of one dollar was held not to be a proper and fair consideration to support an option to purchase real estate worth nearly $1,000. 27 Michigan Law Review 314, note. 204

CONSIDERATION 

§ 221 

§ 221. 

Presumption of Consideration. 

The rule is, clearly, that there is no presumption of consideration in an action on a simple or an informal contract. 

The promisee must both allege and prove consideration as an essential part of his case. Richardson on Evidence, 5th

Ed., sec. 186. The presumption of consideration is now controlled by statute in the following specific instances: 

1. A negotiable instrument in the possession of the payee raises a presumption of consideration.

The pre- sumption, however, is not conclusive. But when such an instrument is in the possession of a third party who is a holder in due course, the presumption of consideration becomes conclusive or irrebuttable. Negotiable Instruments 

Law, secs. 50 and 54; Dougherty v. Salt, 227 N. Y. 200, 125 

N. E. 94; Richardson on Bills and Notes, 4th Ed., sec. 10. 

2. An agreement, signed by the party whom it is sought to hold liable, to change, modify, or discharge any contract, obligation, or lease, or any mortgage or security interest in personal or real property, or to release totally or partially any claim, debt, demand, or obligation, needs no consideration for its validity. Personal Property Law, sec. 33, subd. 

2; Real Property Law, sec. 279; Debtor and Creditor Law, sec. 243. 205

CHAPTER XII FORM OF CONTRACT § 222. 

Classification of Contracts. 

Contracts are either (1) informal, or (2) formal. Informal contracts are those which require no particular form to make them effective. All other contracts are formal and are either: a. 

Contracts of record; b. Contracts under seal; or

C. Contracts required to be in writing. 

§ 223. Contracts of Record. 

Contracts of record are of two main classes: 

1. Judgments; 

2. Recognizances. 

§ 224. 

Judgments.

A judgment is an obligation created by a judicial act. 

It is clearly not a contract because it lacks the element of agreement. But since a judgment was enforced in an action of assumpsit under the common law at a time when all civil actions were regarded either as actions ex contractu or actions ex delicto, it has, for certain purposes, been classified as a contract. O'Brien v. Young, 95 N. Y. 428, 47 Am. Rep. 64, aff'd 146 U. S. 162, 36 Law. Ed. 925, 13 

Sup. Ct. Rep. 54; Wyman v. Mitchell, 1 Cowen (N.Y.) 316.

It is well settled that a provision for interest in a judgment, based on a non-interest bearing obligation, is not a contract within the meaning of the United States Constitution, Article I, section 10, forbidding a state to pass any law impairing the obligation of contracts. Morley v. Lake Shore & 

Michigan Southern Ry. Co., 146 U. S. 162, 36 Law. Ed. 925, 13 Sup. Ct. Rep. 54; Missouri & Arkansas Lumber & Mining Co. v. Greenwood District of Sebastian County, 249 

U. S. 170, 63 Law. Ed. 538, 39 Sup. Ct. Rep. 202. However, the term "contract" is sometimes construed to include judg ments. Thus, in Gutta Percha & Rubber Manufacturing Co. 

206

FORM OF CONTRACT §§ 224-226 

v. Houston, 108 N. Y. 276, 15 N. E. 402, a judgment was held to be within the provision of the Civil Practice Act, section 902, which permits a warrant of attachment to be obtained in an action for a "breach of contract, express or implied." 

§ 225. Recognizances. 

A recognizance is an acknowledgment of a debt of record. It has many of the attributes of a judgment. The term is commonly applied to the various forms of security for the appearance of the accused in criminal proceedings and is often used synonymously with the term "bond.” 

Wright v. State, 22 Tex. App. 670, 3 S. W. 346; National Surety Co. v. Nazzaro, 233 Mass. 74, 123 N. E. 346. A 

recognizance is not a contract between individuals; hence, the word "contracts" as used in a statute has been held not to include recognizances. State v. Weatherwax, 12 

Kan. 463. The common law objection to treating a recognizance as a contract was that the recognizance had been made with the sovereign with whom a subject could not contract. 

§ 226. 

Contracts under Seal. 

A contract under seal is sometimes called a deed, a bond, or a specialty, and is the only formal contract known to the common law. A sealed instrument derives its validity neither from the fact of the agreement nor from the consideration, but from the form in which it is expressed. 

The act of sealing an instrument is deemed to be done with great deliberation and solemnity, implying great caution and the fullness of consent. It has been said that: "The solemnity which for centuries has been attached to instruments under seal still remains anchored as something tangible and of substance, although in this practical age we are apt to think it rests upon a fiction." Williams v. 

Magee, 76 App. Div. 512, 78 N. Y. S. 550, aff'd 177 N. Y. 

534, 69 N. E. 1133. See, also, Crowley v. Lewis, 239 N. Y. 

264, 146 N. E. 374. 207

§ 

 § 227. 

How Seal May Be Made. 

At common law, in order that a seal might exist in legal contemplation, there must have been an impression upon wax or other adhesive substance. A mere scrawl with a pen was insufficient to constitute a seal, though, in some states, it was established at an early date that a mere flourish of the pen was sufficient to constitute a seal. Warren v. Lynch, 5 Johns. (N.Y.) 239. The General Construction Law, section 44, now provides that: "The private seal of a person, other than a corporation, to any instrument or writing shall consist of a wafer, wax or other similar adhesive substance affixed thereto, or of paper or other similar substance affixed thereto, by mucilage or other adhesive substance, or of the word 'seal,' or the letters 

'L.S.,' opposite the signature." General Construction Law, section 43, provides: "A seal of a court, public officer or corporation may be impressed directly upon the instrument or writing to be sealed, or upon wafer, wax or other adhesive substance affixed thereto, or upon paper, or other similar substance affixed thereto by mucilage or other adhesive substance."

However, in New York and in certain other states, the mere attaching of a seal after the signature is not in and of itself sufficient to make the writing an instrument under seal. In addition to the seal, there must be an acknowledgment or recognition of the seal in the body of the instrument by some such phrase as "witness my hand and seal" or "signed and sealed"; or there must be proof that it was the intention of the parties to make the writing a sealed instrument. "This proof may refer to words spoken or to circumstances of execution leading to the inference that the parties intended to use the seal." Transbel Investment Co., Inc. v. Venetos, 279 N. Y. 207, 18 N. E. (2d) 129. See, also, Matter of Pirie, 198 N. Y. 209, 91 N. E. 587.

An expression of an intention in a document to make it a sealed instrument is of itself insufficient to make it one. 

Thus, the recital "signed, sealed, and delivered" or "whereunto we have affixed our seals" is not sufficient to constitute 208

FORM OF CONTRACT 

§§ 227-230 a sealed instrument if in fact nothing which the law recognizes as a seal is affixed to the signatures. Empire Trust 

Co. v. Heinze, 242 N. Y. 475, 152 N. E. 266. 

§ 228. Adopting Seal Previously Affixed. 

If the impression or mark upon the paper is of such a nature as to be recognized in law as a seal, as where the seal is printed on a blank form, the obligor may adopt it as his own seal. Furthermore, the same seal may be adopted by more than one party to the instrument when two or more sign. Rusling v. Union Pipe & Construction Co., 5 App. 

Div. 448, 39 N. Y. S. 216, aff'd 158 N. Y. 737, 53 N. E. 

1131; Ryan v. Cooke, 172 Ill. 302, 50 N. E. 213.

EFFECT OF SEAL § 229. 

Estoppel-Sealed Instruments. 

The general rule is that the truth of the statements in a sealed instrument cannot be disputed by the party who made them. He is estopped from denying them. Metropolitan Life Insurance Co. v. Bender, 124 N. Y. 47, 26 

N. E. 345, 11 L. R. A. 708; Watkins v. Robertson, 105 Va. 

269, 54 S. E. 33, 5 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1194. However, the modern tendency is to apply the same rule in this connection to both sealed instruments and simple contracts. Hastings v. Lovejoy, 140 Mass. 261, 2 N. E. 776, 54 Am. Rep. 462. 

§ 230. 

Consideration-Sealed Instruments. 

By virtue of the 1935 and 1936 amendments to the Civil Practice Act, section 342, the common law rule making the seal conclusive evidence of consideration has, for instruments made on or after September 1, 1935, been completely abolished in New York. The section, as amended, provides that: "A seal upon a written instrument hereafter executed shall not be received as conclusive or presumptive evidence of consideration." 

For many years prior to September 1, 1935, the effective 209

§§ 230  

date of the 1935 amendment to the Civil Practice Act, section 342, the New York rule was that a seal upon an executory contract created a presumption of consideration which could be rebutted, while a seal on an executed contract was conclusive evidence of consideration. Stiebel v. Grosberg, 202 N. Y. 266, 95 N. E. 692, 36 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1147. This rule is still applicable to contracts made prior to September 

1, 1935. However, a seal on a contract made on or after that date creates no presumption of consideration whatsoever. But see Cochran v. Taylor, 273 N. Y. 172, 7 N. E. (2d) 89. 

§ 231. Merger-Sealed Instruments. 

A sealed instrument is of a higher nature than an informal written contract or one not under seal. Therefore, if, after making an informal contract, the parties enter into another contract under seal relating to the same subject matter, the former is merged in the latter. Clifton v. Jackson Iron Co., 74 Mich. 183, 41 N. W. 891; Butler & Vosburgh v. Miller, 1 Denio (N.Y.) 407; Schoonmaker v. Hoyt, 148 N. Y. 425, 42 N. E. 1059; Restatement, Contracts, sec. 446. 

§ 232. Statute of Limitations. 

Wherever the common law prevails and private seals have not been abolished, the period of the statute of limitations within which an action may be brought to enforce a contract under seal is generally much longer than in the case of contracts not under seal. Clarke v. Pierce, 215 

Mass. 552, 102 N. E. 1094. Thus, in New York, a right. of action on informal contracts is barred if it is not brought within six years after such action accrues. A longer period, twenty years, prevails with sealed instruments, except where the action is on a bond secured by a mortgage on real property. Civil Practice Act, secs. 47, 47-a, and 48. 210

FORM OF CONTRACT §§ 233-235 § 233. Delivery-Sealed Instruments. 

To render a sealed instrument operative, it must be delivered and accepted. What becomes of it after delivery is immaterial; it may even be redelivered to the person who executed it. King v. Fragley, 19 Cal. App. 735, 127 Pac. 

813. However, possession by the obligee is prima facie evidence of delivery and acceptance. The mere formal execution and retention of a deed by the grantor is insufficient to pass title to the grantee, and no delivery thereof to the grantee could be presumed from these facts alone. But the recording of a deed is presumptive evidence of delivery. 

Sweetland v. Buell, 164 N. Y. 541, 58 N. E. 663. 

§ 234. Delivery in Escrow. 

A deed or other instrument may be delivered in escrow.

This occurs when the obligor delivers the deed to a third party, to be held by him until some contingency occurs or some condition is complied with, upon the happening of which the instrument is delivered to the obligee and becomes of full force and effect. If the depositary delivers the deed to the grantee before the conditions are complied with, such delivery has no validity as between the grantor and grantee. Hogueland v. Arts, 113 Iowa 634, 85 N. W. 818. 

§ 235. Necessity of Seal. 

Although the seal is uniformly used on all important contracts, deeds, and the like, yet its absence does not invalidate the instrument. Contracts of corporations and deeds of land are valid even though not executed under seal. 

Leinkauf v. Calman, 110 N. Y. 50, 17 N. E. 389; Leask v. 

Horton, 39 Misc. 144, 79 N. Y. S. 148. In some jurisdictions, a seal is made necessary in the case of particular contracts. Paine v. The Guardians of the Poor of the 

Strand Union, 8 Q. B. Div. 326, 115 Eng. Rep. 899. But there is no statute in New York requiring contracts of any nature whatsoever to be under seal. 211

CHAPTER XIII  § 236. At Common Law. 

The common law made no provision requiring that any contract, irrespective of its purpose or object, should be in any prescribed form. An oral or implied contract was as effective, under the common law, as a written contract, except where the written contract was under seal. The common law gave to written instruments under seal certain benefits not enjoyed by any other form of contract. 

§ 237. By Statute. 

The principal contracts which are required to be in writing are those which fall within the provisions of the old English Statute of Frauds. But there is constantly an increasing number of contracts which do not fall within the Statute of Frauds that are required by statute in New York to be in writing. These contracts are not fully developed but are only noted as preliminary to a consideration of those within the Statute of Frauds and are: 

1. The acceptance of a bill of exchange. Negotiable 

Instruments Law, sec. 220. 

2. A promise to pay a debt when its recovery is barred by the statute of limitations. Civil Practice Act, sec. 59. 

3. A promise to pay a debt discharged in bankruptcy. 

Personal Property Law, sec. 31, subd. 5. 

4. An agreement to change, modify, or discharge in whole or in part an existing contract where the change is made without new consideration. Personal Property Law, sec. 33, subd. 2; Real Property Law, sec. 279. See section 

195, supra. 

5. The conveyance or assignment of a trust in personal property. A contract to establish a trust. Also a contract to bequeath property or make a testamentary provision of any kind. 

Personal Property Law, sec. 31, subds. 4, 7, and 8. 212

§§ 237, 238 

6. 

Marriage contracts wherein the parties become husband and wife without having the marriage solemnized by a clergyman or a public officer. Domestic Relations Law, sec. 11, subd. 4. 

7. Special agreements of innkeepers as to liability for the loss of property of guests. General Business Law, sec. 

200. 

8. An assignment or other transfer of an interest in the estate of a deceased person. Personal Property Law, sec. 32. 

9. The authorization of an agent to convey or create an interest in real property or to lease real property for a term of more than one year. Real Property Law, sec. 242. 

See section 253, infra. 

10. The authorization of an agent to execute a contract for the sale of real property, or an interest therein, or for the leasing of real property for a period of more than one year. Real Property Law, sec. 259. See section 253, infra.

Similar provisions are common in other jurisdictions. 

Under federal statutes, the assignment of a copyright or patent right must be in writing. 17 United States Code 

Annotated, sec. 42.

STATUTE OF FRAUDS § 238. Origin and History. 

One of the most important statutes ever promulgated, either in England or in this country, is the celebrated 

Statute of Frauds. The statute was enacted in England in 1676 (29 Charles II, Chap. 3). The act was entitled "An Act for Prevention of Frauds and Perjuries." The object of the statute is to prevent certain fraudulent practices which are commonly endeavored to be upheld by perjury. 

The fourth and seventeenth sections of the statute have been reenacted by most of the states, though with slight deviations. In all the states, contracts for the sale of real property are required to be in writing, but this is not true in reference to contracts for the sale of personal property. 213

§§ 238  

The Statute of Frauds is in force in this country only where, and to the extent to which, it has been adopted by the legislature. It is not a part of the common law which was received and adopted by the states irrespective of legislation. McKennon v. Winn, 1 Okla. 327, 33 Pac. 582, 22

L. R. A. 501. In the state of Maryland, however, by virtue of its Declaration of Rights, Article V, which declares in force in Maryland all English statutes enacted prior to

July 4, 1776, and applicable to local circumstances, the Statute of Frauds became a part of the law of Maryland. 

Lewis v. Tapman, 90 Md. 294, 45 Atl. 459, 47 L. R. A. 385.

A similar situation exists in other states. See note in 22 

L. R. A. 508. 

§ 239. 

English Statute of Frauds-Section 4. 

The English Statute of Frauds, section 4, provides that: "No action shall be brought whereby to charge any executor or administrator, upon any special promise, to answer damages out of his own estate; or whereby to charge the defendant upon any special promise to answer for the debt, default, or miscarriages of another person; or to charge any person upon any agreement made upon consideration of marriage; or upon any contract or sale of lands, tenements, or hereditaments, or any interest in or concerning them; or upon any agreement that is not to be performed within the space of one year from the making thereof; unless the agreement upon which such action shall be brought, or some memorandum or note thereof, shall be in writing, and signed by the party to be charged therewith, or some other person thereunto by him lawfully authorized." 

§ 240. English Statute of Frauds-Section 17. 

The English Statute of Frauds, section 17, follows: 

"No contract for the sale of any goods, wares and merchandises, for the price of ten pounds sterling or upwards, shall be allowed to be good; except the buyer shall accept part of the goods so sold, and actually receive the same, or 214

§§ 240, 241 

give something in earnest to bind the bargain, or in part of payment, or that some note or memorandum in writing of the said bargain be made, and signed by the parties to be charged by such contract or their agents thereunto lawfully authorized." 

§ 241. New York Statutes. 

The following statutory provisions in New York cor respond to the provisions contained in the original Statute of Frauds, sections 4 and 17. The fourth section of the original statute includes the first five of the following subdivisions; the seventeenth section includes the sixth subdivision. These are: 

1. A special promise by an executor or administrator to answer for the damages or to pay the debts of the testator or intestate out of his own estate. Decedent Estate 

Law, sec. 113. 

2. Any special promise to answer for the debt, default, or miscarriage of another person. Personal Property Law, sec. 31, subd. 2. 

3. Agreements made in consideration of marriage. 

Personal Property Law, sec. 31, subd. 3. 

4. Agreements not to be performed within one year from the making thereof, or the performance of which is not to be completed before the end of a lifetime. Personal 

Property Law, sec. 31, subd. 1. The latter part of this subdivision was not included within the provisions of the original Statute of Frauds. It was added to the New York statute by the legislature in 1933. 

5. Contracts for the sale of any real property or any interest therein, or a lease for more than one year. Real 

Property Law, sec. 259. Also a contract to establish a trust or to make a devise of real property. Real Property 

Law, sec. 259-a. 

6. Contracts for the sale of goods or choses in action of the value of fifty dollars or upward, unless a. The buyer accepts and actually receives a part of the goods; 215

§§ 241  

b. Pays part of the purchase price; c. The sale is by auction. The auctioneer's memorandum is sufficient. Personal Property Law, secs. 

85 and 31, subd. 6. 

§ 242. 

Special Promise by Executor or Administrator. 

"No executor or administrator shall be chargeable upon any special promise to answer damages, or to pay the debts of the testator or intestate, out of his own estate, unless the agreement for that purpose, or some memorandum or note thereof, be in writing, and signed by such executor or administrator, or by some other person by him thereunto specially authorized." Decedent Estate Law, sec. 113.

The phrase "or pay debts of the testator or intestate" was inserted in the New York statute. This provision does not apply where the executor or administrator promises to pay. a debt which is not a debt of the estate. Mackin v. Dwyer, 205 Mass. 472, 91 N. E. 893. Even though the claim against the estate did not arise until after the death of the deceased, the executor is not personally liable on his oral promise to pay the claim if it is a debt of the estate. Dillaby v. Wilcox, 60 Conn. 71, 22 Atl. 491, 13 L. R. A. 643. However, if the executor makes a personal oral promise to pay a debt which is not a binding obligation on him as executor and which arose after the death of the testator, such promise is not within the statute. Fehlinger v. Wood, 134 Pa. 

517, 19 Atl. 746. Moreover, if the promise by the executor is made to subserve some purpose of his own, the statute does not apply. It is then regarded as an original and independent promise. Bellows v. Sowles, 57 Vt. 164, 52 Am. 

Rep. 118; Wales v. Stout, 115 N. Y. 638, 21 N. E. 1027. 

§ 243. Special Promise To Answer for the Debt, Default, or Miscarriage of Another Person. 

"Every agreement, promise or undertaking is void, unless it or some note or memorandum thereof be in writing, and subscribed by the party to be charged therewith, or by his lawful agent, if such agreement, promise or undertak- 216

§ ing is a special promise to answer for the debt, default or miscarriage of another person." Personal Property Law, sec. 31, subd. 2. Despite the use of the word "void" in the statute, it is well established that such agreement, promise, or undertaking is merely unenforceable. Crane v. 

Powell, 139 N. Y. 379, 34 N. E. 911.

The term "debt" means a definite amount; while "default" refers to an indefinite amount of loss or damage resulting from a breach of a contract. "Miscarriage" is applied to tort cases, as where one agrees to answer for the wrongful act of another. Richardson v. Crandall, 48 N. Y. 

348; Kirkham v. Marter, 2 Barn. & Ald. 613, 106 Eng.

Rep. 490. The phrase "of another person" refers to one other than the promisor.

The promise of a third person to answer for another's debt, where the promise is made contemporaneously with the creation of the debt or subsequent thereto, may be either original or collateral, depending entirely upon the intentions of the parties. If the promise is direct and unconditional, it is considered an original undertaking and, therefore, not within the statute. But where it appears that credit is not given in the first instance to the third person who promises to pay for the goods sold or the credit extended to another, the undertaking is collateral and must be in writing. To bring the promise within the statute there must be three parties; namely, the debtor, the creditor, and the guarantor (third person). Thus, if property is delivered or services are rendered to one person, upon an oral promise of payment by another, the goods or services being charged only to the person to whom delivery was so made or for whom services were so rendered, the promise of such other person is collateral to that of the principal debtor and, if not in writing, void. Shay v. Cruxton, 116 N. Y. S. 

1123. In ascertaining to whom the credit was given, whether to the party sought to be charged or to the debtor, the intention of the parties, as gathered from all the circumstances of the transaction, will control and this, too, regardless of the language used in creating the obligation. 217

§§ 243, 244  Davis v. Patrick, 141 U. S. 479, 35 Law. Ed. 826, 12 Sup. 

Ct. Rep. 58; Richardson Press v. Albright, 224 N. Y. 497, 121 N. E. 362, 8 A. L. R. 1195. 

A promise "to see another paid" or "to pay if the debtor does not" is usually treated as collateral and within the 

Statute of Frauds. If A says to B: "Let X have what groceries he wants and I will pay for them," A's promise is original and not within the statute; but if A says to B, "If X does not pay I will," the undertaking is collateral. 

Mallory v. Gillett, 21 N. Y. 412; Brown v. Weber, 38 N. Y. 

187; Nugent v. Wolfe, 111 Pa. 471, 4 Atl. 15. 

§ 244. Promise To Answer for Debt or Default of Another Made for Benefit of Promisor. 

Where a promise is made for the benefit of a promisor and to subserve his own ends and purposes, the promise is original and not collateral and, therefore, not within the Statute of Frauds although it is in effect a promise to pay the antecedent debt of another. And this is true even though the nature of the promise is collateral in form and would be so construed but for the fact that it was made for the promisor's own benefit. This proposition of law finds frequent application in building contracts. For example: Where, after the abandonment of a building contract by the principal contractor, the owner of the building makes an oral agreement with a person to whom the principal contractor had sublet the iron work required by the contract, and who, prior to the abandonment, had furnished a portion of such iron work, that, if such sub-contractor would finish the said work, the owner would pay him for all the iron work, such promise is an original promise, founded upon a valid consideration moving to the owner, and entitles the sub-contractor to recover from the owner, not only for work done and materials furnished subsequent to the making of the new promise, but for that which had been done and furnished prior thereto. Raabe v. Squier, 148 

N. Y. 81, 42 N. E. 516; Reisler v. Silbermintz, 99 App. Div. 

131, 90 N. Y. S. 967. 218

§ 

In White v. Rintoul, 108 N. Y. 222, 227, 15 N. E. 318, the

Court said: "Where the primary debt subsists and was antecedently contracted, the promise to pay it is original when it is founded on a new consideration moving to the promisor and beneficial to him, and such that the promisor thereby comes under an independent duty of payment irrespective of the liability of the principal debtor." But the mere fact that the promisor may have an interest in the performance of a contract by another, or that he may derive some possible benefit thereby, cannot alone determine whether his promise is original or collateral, although it may be some evidence of his intention in the matter. In Richardson Press v. Albright, 224 N. Y. 497, 121 N. E. 362, 8

A. L. R. 1195, the Court held that the oral promise in question to pay the debt of another was within the Statute of Frauds, even though the new consideration for the promise was beneficial to the defendant. In addition to the beneficial nature of the consideration, the question still remains whether or not the promisor comes under an independent duty to make payment, irrespective of the liability of the principal debtor. Whether in any particular case there is an independent duty to make payment is a question concerning which there is an irreconcilable conflict in the authorities. This is especially noticeable in building contracts where the owner of a building in process of construction promises to see that a materialman or sub-contractor is paid if the latter will complete his contract which he has refused to do unless the owner promises to pay him. Cases holding that the interest of the owner is sufficient to constitute an original promise are considered in Voska, Foelsch & Sidlo v. Ruland, 172 App. Div. 616, 158 N. Y. S. 780; 

Howell v. Harvey, 65 W. Va. 310, 64 S. E. 249, 22 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1077 and note; Windsor Construction Co. v. Ruland, 173 App. Div. 94, 159 N. Y. S. 446. Cases holding that the interest of the promisor is not sufficient to constitute an original undertaking and, therefore, that the promise is within the Statute of Frauds are: Snyder v. Monroe Eckstein Brewing Co., 107 App. Div. 328, 95 N. Y. S. 144, aff'd 219

§§ 244, 245  

188 N. Y. 576, 80 N. E. 1120; Roscoe Lumber Co. v. Reynolds, 124 App. Div. 539, 108 N. Y. S. 1018. The interest of a stockholder of a corporation, even though the stockholder may be an officer of the corporation and receiving the direct and immediate benefit of the contract, is not of itself sufficient to constitute an original and independent promise. 

Thus, an oral promise of an officer and stockholder of a corporation to become individually responsible for a loan made or goods sold to the corporation is collateral and within the statute, the benefit accruing to the stockholder from such loan or sale being remote and indirect. Richardson Press v. 

Albright, supra; Witschard v. Brody & Sons, Inc., 257 N. Y. 

97, 177 N. E. 385.

The pivotal point, in all cases where it is a close question whether the promise is within or without the Statute of Frauds, rests on the intention of the parties, and evidence of the conditions and circumstances of the parties at the time the promise in question was made is admissible for the purpose of determining the intention of the promisor. His interest may be such as to clearly justify the conclusion that at the time he made the promise it was his intention to pay the debt. If so, his promise is original and not within the statute; otherwise it is collateral and, therefore, within the Statute of Frauds. The cases support the conclusion that it is the intention as evidenced by the interest that controls the application of the statute. Witschard v.

Brody & Sons, Inc., supra. For a collation of authorities and a thoroughly prepared study of when a promise to answer for the debt or default of another is within the Statute of Frauds, see 3 Brooklyn Law Review 270.

Indemnity Contract Not Promise To Answer for § 245. 

Debt or Default.

A promise by one person to indemnify another for becoming a surety for a third person is not within the Statute of Frauds and, therefore, need not be in writing. An oral promise by B to C that he will indemnify C for any loss he may sustain by becoming a surety for D is enforceable. A 220

§§ 245, 246 

promise by the payee of a note that, if another will sign the same as surety for the maker, he, the payee, will indemnify and save the surety harmless is not within the statute. The theory is that the statute is applicable only to promises made to persons to whom another is answerable, that is, the creditor. Posten v. Clem, 201 Ala. 529, 78 

So. 883, 1 A. L. R. 381, 383 and note; Assets Realization 

Co. v. Roth, 226 N. Y. 370, 123 N. E. 743. 

§ 246. Agreement Made in Consideration of Marriage. 

"Every agreement, promise or undertaking is void, unless it or some note or memorandum thereof be in writing, and subscribed by the party to be charged therewith, or by his lawful agent, if such agreement, promise or undertaking is made in consideration of marriage, except mutual promises to marry." Personal Property Law, sec. 31, subd. 

3. A similar provision exists in nearly all states. This provision of the statute embraces all antenuptial agreements to pay money or to settle property upon one of the parties if the marriage is consummated. Hence, an oral antenuptial agreement is void, and even after the marriage, the wife cannot enforce specific performance on the ground that she has performed her part of the agreement. Hunt v. Hunt, 171 N. Y. 396, 64 N. E. 159, 59 L. R. A. 306. See, also, Matter of Goldberg, 275 N. Y. 186, 9 N. E. (2d) 829.

But where the agreement has been fully carried out according to its terms, the Statute of Frauds no longer affects the transaction. Miller v. Sire, 224 Fed. 424, L. R. A. 1916D 

1211; Lyons v. The Maccabees, 192 App. Div. 109, 182

N. Y. S. 212. Furthermore, when there is a consideration for the contract other than the marriage of the parties, the promise is not within the statute. Thus, where a father promised to convey land to the promisee if the latter would marry the son of the promisor in order to settle a prosecution for seduction, the promise was held not to be within the statute. Bader v. Hiscox, 188 Iowa 986, 174 N. W. 565, 10 A. L. R. 316. 221

§  § 247. Agreement Not To Be Performed within One Year. 

"Every agreement, promise or undertaking is void, unless it or some note or memorandum thereof be in writing, and subscribed by the party to be charged therewith, or by his lawful agent, if such agreement, promise or undertaking by its terms is not to be performed within one year from the making thereof or the performance of which is not to be completed before the end of a lifetime." The latter part was added by the legislature in 1933. Personal 

Property Law, sec. 31, subd. 1. This provision requires contracts to be reduced to writing, if they cannot, by their terms, be performed within one year after they have been made.

In one case, the defendant claimed to be discharged as a surety because of a change in the time of payment on a bond and mortgage without his consent. It appeared that there was an oral agreement between the holder of the mortgage and the purchaser of the mortgaged premises, providing for payments on the bond and mortgage quarterly instead of monthly. It was held that the oral agreement was within the statute because it could not be performed within a year and was, therefore, unenforceable. 

Hence, the original mortgagor was not discharged. Though clearly legally sound, this seems to be a harsh ruling. Hermann v. Gressel, 148 Misc. 775, 266 N. Y. S. 263; David 

Taylor Co., Inc. v. Fansteel Products Co., Inc., 234 App. Div. 

548, 255 N. Y. S. 270, aff'd 261 N. Y. 514, 185 N. E. 718.

Is a promise to pay commissions to a realty broker provided the tenant renews a five-year lease within the statute? 

Allwin Realty Co. v. Barth, 161 App. Div. 568, 146 N. Y. S. 

960. Although the rule that agreements not to be performed within one year must be in writing is very definite and not difficult to understand when literally interpreted, yet by innumerable judicial decisions the construction placed upon it has become extremely artificial. In construing the rule, the courts have held the following oral contracts to be valid: 

1. If the contract be fully performed by one of the par- 222

§ ties within the year, although not required by the terms thereof, it need not be in writing. This is the prevailing rule in the United States and England. Great Western 

Turnpike Co. v. Shafer, 57 App. Div. 331, 68 N. Y. S. 5, aff'd 172 N. Y. 662, 65 N. E. 1121; Donellan v. Read, 3 

Barn. & Ald. 899, 110 Eng. Rep. 330. Thus, where, by its terms, a contract for the delivery of wood was not to be performed within one year, the contract was nevertheless held to be enforceable where the entire amount of wood was delivered within the year. Barkley v. Rensselaer & 

Saratoga R. R. Co., 71 N. Y. 205; Rague v. New York 

Evening Journal Publishing Co., 164 App. Div. 126, 149 

N. Y. S. 668. 

2. If, by any possibility, the contract may by its terms be performed within a year although the parties may have intended that its operation should extend through a longer period, the statute will not apply. A testator orally agreed to set aside $3,000 for Jones if Jones would give up his position and pass the entrance examination for Pratt Institute and enter the Institute for a two-year course of study. Jones passed the examination and entered the Institute. Jones fully performed his promise within a year. 

Since he did not undertake to complete the course in two years, the testator's oral promise was not within the statute. 

Robinson v. Hayes' Estate, 207 App. Div. 718, 202 N. Y. S. 

732, aff'd 239 N. Y. 512, 147 N. E. 175. A contract to build a house within fifteen months is not within the statute, since it may be performed within a year. So, too, a contract not to be performed within a year is taken out of the statute where either party may rightfully terminate it within a year. Thus, an oral contract for nine years which could be terminated by either party at the end of six months is not within the statute and is therefore valid. 

Standard Bitulithic Co. v. Curran, 256 Fed. 68; Gallagher v. Finch, Pruyn & Co., 211 App. Div. 635, 207 N. Y. S. 403. 

3. The interpretation of the old English Statute of 

Frauds was that the statute did not apply if the death of either party within a year would leave the purpose for which 223

§§ 247, 248  

the contract was made fully performed. This rule no longer applies in New York, because the New York statute was modified in 1933 by adding "or the performance of which is not to be completed before the end of a lifetime." Personal Property Law, sec. 31, subd. 1. Formerly, an agreement for a person's support during life was not within the statute, because death within a year would leave the purpose for which the contract was made fully performed. But since 

1933, such an agreement is within the statute because performance of the contract is not to be completed before the end of a lifetime. The former rule is supported by McCabe v. Green, 18 App. Div. 625, 45 N. Y. S. 723. See Terminello v. Bleeker, 155 Misc. 702, 280 N. Y. S. 326; Doyle v. Gleason, 244 App. Div. 52, 278 N. Y. S. 802; In re Douglas' Estate, 169 Misc. 716, 7 N. Y. S. (2d) 717.

As to whether agreements to refrain from engaging in business for a definite number of years are without the statute on the theory that the death of the promisor would leave the purpose of the contract fully performed, see Mc- 

Girr v. Campbell, 71 App. Div. 83, 75 N. Y. S. 571; Shapiro v. Balaban, 210 App. Div. 47, 205 N. Y. S. 208. 

§ 248. 

Employment Contracts for One Year or Longer. 

An oral employment contract for one year and one day or longer is clearly within the statute and unenforceable unless in writing. If the contract is for one year, the employment to commence on the day of making the contract, it is clearly not within the statute. But is a contract for one year, to commence on a day following the date of its making, a contract which by its terms is "not to be performed within one year from the making thereof"? For instance, is an oral contract of employment made March 31st for one year to commence April 1st, which, by its terms "is not to be performed within one year from the making thereof," within the statute? If within the statute, it is unenforceable; otherwise it is valid. The earlier cases held that a contract for one year to begin on the day following the making thereof was within the statute. Fanger v. Caspary, 87 App. www 224

§§ 248, 249 

Div. 418, 84 N. Y. S. 410; Billington v. Cahill, 51 Hun 132, 4 N. Y. S. 660; Jonap v. Preger, 59 Misc. 187, 110 N. Y. S. 483. 

But in Prokop v. Bedford Waist & Dress Co., 105 Misc. 

573, 173 N. Y. S. 792, aff'd 187 App. Div. 662, 176 N. Y. S. 

376, it was held that an oral contract of employment for one year, performance to commence the day following the making of the contract, is valid and enforceable. The same problem arose in Goon v. Fu Manchu's Restaurant, Inc., 253 

App. Div. 531, 2 N. Y. S. (2d) 879. The Court carefully considered all the cases and reiterated the rule of the Prokop case as sound on principle as well as authority. See, also,

Restatement, Contracts, sec. 198, comment d. 

§ 249. Effect of Part Performance of Employment Con tract.

While the decisions are in conflict, yet the weight of authority holds that part or even full performance on one side does not take an oral contract of employment for more than a year out of the statute.

The question becomes important whenever an employee sues for services already rendered under an oral contract that was not to be completed within a year. Although he cannot recover the contract price, he may recover on quantum meruit for the reasonable value of his services, which may be considerably less than that agreed upon. In 

Tyler v. Windels, 186 App. Div. 698, 174 N. Y. S. 762, aff'd 

227 N. Y. 589, 125 N. E. 926, the oral employment contract was for eighteen months. It provided for a stipulated compensation and an additional bonus. The plaintiff had fully performed his part of the contract and had been paid the agreed compensation for eighteen months and the bonus for the first twelve months. The Court held that the contract was not taken out of the statute and, therefore, that the plaintiff could not recover the unpaid promised bonus. 

To the same effect is Sophie v. Ford, 230 App. Div. 568, 245 N. Y. S. 470. 

In Galvin v. Prentice, 45 N. Y. 162, 6 Am, Rep. 58, the 225

§§ 249  

Court said: "Where payments are made *** upon a contract void by the statute of frauds and the party receiving the services or payments refuses to go on and complete the performance of the contract, the other party may recover back the amount of such payments." Lockwood v. Barnes, 3 Hill (N. Y.) 128. Cf. Kremer v. Kremer, 243 App. Div. 

66, 276 N. Y. S. 212. 

§ 250. Agreement To Marry Not within Statute. 

The Statute of Frauds has no application to an agreement to marry. Therefore, an agreement to marry at a time not "within one year from the making thereof" is not within the operation of the Statute of Frauds. To require such an agreement to be reduced to writing is said to be contrary to practice, usage, and human nature. Brick v. Gannar, 36 Hun 52. Contra, Ullman v. Meyer, 10 Fed. 241. 

The ruling in Brick v. Gannar, supra, has been approved in Nearing v. Van Fleet, 151 N. Y. 643, 45 N. E. 1133.

The authorities on this subject are collected in the note in L. R. A. 1915D 1190. The question is academic in New 

York since the Civil Practice Act, section 61-b, has abolished any right of action for the breach of an agreement to marry. 

§ 251. Lease of Real Property. 

In New York, it has been held that the statute relating to contracts not to be performed within one year has no application to contracts concerning or relating to land. Hence, a lease for one year to commence at some time in the future need not be in writing. Ward v. Hasbrouck, 169 N. Y. 

407, 62 N. E. 434.

The Real Property Law, section 259, was amended in May, 1934, in relation to the written authority of agents to contract. See sections 252 and 253, infra. 

§ 252. Contract for Sale of Real Property. 

"A contract for the leasing for a longer period than one year, or for the sale, of any real property, or an interest therein, is void, unless the contract, or some note or memo- 226

§§ 252, 253 

randum thereof, expressing the consideration, is in writing, subscribed by the lessor or grantor, or by his lawful agent thereunto authorized by writing." Real Property Law, sec. 

259. This provision was derived from section 4 of the original Statute of Frauds and is similar to provisions contained in the statutes of every state. 

§ 253. Authority of Agent To Deal in Real Property. 

Prior to the 1934 amendment to the Real Property Law, section 259, it was held that an agent could be orally authorized to execute a written contract for the sale of land or to make a written contract for a lease for more than one year. Worrall v. Munn, 5 N. Y. 229, 55 Am. Dec. 330; 

Brune v. Vom Lehn, 112 Misc. 342, 183 N. Y. S. 360, aff'd 

196 App. Div. 907, 187 N. Y. S. 928. By virtue of the

Real Property Law, section 242, however, the authorization of an agent to execute a deed or a lease for more than one year was then, as now, required to be in writing. The 

1934 amendment to section 259 requires the agent's authority to execute a contract for the sale of an interest in real property, or to execute a contract for a lease for more than one year, to be in writing. This amendment changes the rule as laid down in Worrall v. Munn, supra, and in 

Brune v. Vom Lehn, supra.

The effect of section 242 and the present section 259 upon the methods of creating an agent's authority may be summarized as follows: 

1. The authority of an agent to convey or create an interest in real property or to lease real property for more than one year must be in writing. Real Property Law, sec. 242. 

2. The authority of an agent to execute a contract for the sale of an interest in real property or to execute a contract for a lease for more than one year must be in writing. 

Real Property Law, sec. 259.

The statute expressly refers to an agent's authority to sell real property; it would seem that his authority to buy real property need not be in writing. 227

§§ 254, 255  § 254. 

Contract for Sale of Interest in Real Property. 

An interest in real property means some substantial interest in the land, an estate which may be conveyed or assigned, and not a remote or inappreciable interest. A contract for services in investigating the title to land and to find a purchaser therefor, does not give rise to an "interest in real property." Heyn v. Philips, 37 Cal. 529.

But a contract to give a mortgage is a contract for the sale of an "interest in real property" and is, therefore, within the statute. Sleeth v. Sampson, 237 N. Y. 69, 142 N. E. 

355, 30 A. L. R. 1400. An agreement between a first and a second mortgagee of the same real property disposing of the proceeds of rents collected by a receiver in foreclosure does not constitute a sale of an interest in real property. 

Monica Realty Corp. v. One Twenty-two Fifth Avenue Corp., 264 N. Y. 52, 189 N. E. 778. But an assignment of the purchaser's interest under a real property contract is within the statute. Flinner v. McVay, 37 Mont. 306, 96 Pac. 340, 19 L. R. A. (N.S.) 879 and note. 

§ 255. Sale of Standing Timber. 

Contracts for the sale of standing timber and of bark on standing trees are for the sale of an interest in land. Hirth v. Graham, 50 Ohio St. 57, 33 N. E. 90, 19 L. R. A. 721; 

Green v. Armstrong, 1 Denio (N.Y.) 550; Thomson v. 

Poor, 57 Hun 285, 10 N. Y. S. 597. This has been held to be the rule even though the parties contemplate that the trees shall be immediately severed from the land by the purchaser. Hirth v. Graham, supra. However, in the latter case, there is authority to the effect that such a contract does not involve the sale of an interest in land and is, therefore, not within the provision of the Statute of Frauds in question. Leonard v. Medford, 85 Md. 666, 37 Atl. 365, 37 L. R. A. 449. See, also, Personal Property Law, section 

156, for a definition of goods. 228

§§ 256 § 256. 

Contract To Devise Real Property. 

An agreement to devise land involves the transfer of an interest in realty and is, therefore, within the statute. Pond v. Sheean, 132 Ill. 312, 23 N. E. 1018, 8 L. R. A. 414; Lasher v. McDermott, 173 App. Div. 79, 158 N. Y. S. 708, appeal dismissed 219 N. Y. 554, 114 N. E. 1070. For instance, an oral agreement to devise land to another in consideration of services to be rendered to the promisor is void. Ellis v. 

Cary, 74 Wis. 176, 42 N. W. 252, 4 L. R. A. 55. The Real 

Property Law, section 259-a, declares that a contract to establish a trust of real property or to make a devise is void unless the contract is in writing and subscribed by the party to be charged therewith. In re Wheeler's Estate, 164 Misc. 

441, 299 N. Y. S. 945. 

§ 257. 

Licenses and Easements.

A license is merely permission or authority to enter upon or pass over the land of another and is not intended to convey an interest in the land. Therefore, an agreement to give a license is not within the statute. Wiseman v. 

Lucksinger, 84 N. Y. 31, 38 Am. Rep. 479. But an easement in land is an interest therein and, therefore, an agreement to create an easement is within the statute and must be proved by a written instrument. Nellis v. Munson, 108 

N. Y. 453, 15 N. E. 739.

In some states, however, the statute is confined to estates in land and is held not to include agreements to create easements. Warner v. Texas & Pacific Ry. Co., 164 U. S. 418, 41 Law. Ed. 495, 17 Sup. Ct. Rep. 147. 

§ 258. Contract for Sale of Building To Be Removed. 

A contract for the sale of a building to be demolished and removed from the land of the vendor is not a sale of an interest in real property. The contract operates as a constructive severance of the building from the land, and the building is thereafter regarded as personal property. Melton v. Fullerton-Weaver Realty Co., 214 N. Y. 571, 108 

N. E. 849. In Long v. White, 42 Ohio St. 59, the Court 229

§§ 258  

said: "In applying the statute of frauds, buildings are not classed with forest trees, but with growing crops, nursery trees, and fixtures attached to realty." Cf. Eichhammer v. 

Parsons, 273 N. Y. 208, 7 N. E. (2d) 103, 110 A. L. R. 344. 

§ 259. Products of the Soil. 

It often becomes an important question whether the products of the soil are real property or personal property, since if they are treated as real property the contract is within the fourth section of the Statute of Frauds, while if considered as personal property the seventeenth section of the statute controls. All growing products of the soil are either: 

1. Fructus industriales, or 2. Fructus naturales. 

In determining whether growing crops are fructus industriales or fructus naturales, the intention of the parties may be taken into account as well as the nature of the transaction. Minneapolis Iron Stove Co. v. Branum, 36 

N. D. 355, 162 N. W. 543, L. R. A. 1917E 298. 

§ 260. Fructus Industriales. 

Annual crops, such as wheat, corn, oats, and potatoes raised by yearly labor and cultivation, are, although unsevered from the soil, to be regarded as personal property and this is true regardless of whether the crops are growing or have matured. Under no circumstances do they constitute an interest in land. The term "goods" as used in the 

Personal Property Law, Sales Act, section 156, subdivision 

1, expressly includes fructus industriales. Thus, a sale of a corn crop, although only a few inches high at the time. of making the contract, was held in Northern et al. v. State ex rel. Lathrop, 1 Ind. 113, to be a sale of personal property. 

Therefore, contracts for the sale of growing crops are controlled by the Statute of Frauds as embodied in the Personal 

Property Law, section 85. Green v. Armstrong, 1 Denio 

(N.Y.) 550; Sexton v. Breese, 135 N. Y. 387, 32 N. E. 133; 230

§§ 260, 261 

First National Bank v. Cazort & McGehee Co., 123 Ark. 

605, 186 S. W. 86, L. R. A. 1917C 7. 

§ 261. Fructus Naturales. 

Natural productions of the earth are a part of the land, and a contract for their sale constitutes a sale of an interest in land. Matter of Chamberlain, 140 N. Y. 390, 35 N. E. 

602. To this class belong growing trees, grass, and hops.

Fruit trees, such as apple and peach trees, do not require annual planting and cultivation. The same is true of grapes and hops. Although they are cultivated, yet cultivation is not necessary to their perennial production. Ross v. Cook, 71 Kan. 117, 80 Pac. 38. Hops were considered as personal property in Frank v. Harrington, 36 Barb. 415, on the ground that they are produced by annual cultivation and by the industry of the owner. But since annual cultivation, though quite advisable to obtain a large yield, is unnecessary to produce the crop, the correctness of the decision seems questionable unless all distinctions between fructus industriales and fructus naturales are to be disregarded. The hop roots when rooted in the ground are a part of the real estate. Webster v. Zielly, 52 Barb. 482.

Blackberry bushes are perennial and from one planting yield successive crops. They grow wild, but like every other kind of fruit or berry are improved by cultivation. The quantity and quality of the yield are largely dependent upon the amount of annual care expended upon them. Blackberries while growing on the bushes have been held not to be personal property. Sparrow v. Pond, 49 Minn. 412, 52 N. W. 36, 16 L. R. A. 103. Although the authorities are in conflict, the weight of authority is that fruit growing on trees constitutes personal property and not real property. Simmons v. Williford, 60 Fla. 359, 53 So. 452;

Sparrow v. Pond, supra. By analogy it would seem that if a building which is a part of the realty becomes personalty when sold to be removed by the vendee, fruit on trees or bushes when sold to be picked by the purchaser should also 231

§§ 261  

be treated as personal property. Melton v. Fullerton- 

Weaver Realty Co., 214 N. Y. 571, 108 N. E. 849. 

§ 262. 

Parol Reservation of Crops. 

A reservation of growing crops in favor of a stranger to a conveyance of land may be established by parol although there is no reservation clause in the deed, and this, too, notwithstanding the rule of the common law that growing crops pass by a conveyance of the realty as appurtenant to the land, while the natural products of the soil must be reserved by a written agreement. In New York, however, a parol reservation of growing crops in favor of the grantor cannot be shown as against the grantee. Austin v. 

Sawyer, 9 Cowen (N.Y.) 39; Beck v. McLane, 129 App. 

Div. 745, 114 N. Y. S. 44, aff'd 198 N. Y. 567, 92 N. E. 1078. 

§ 263. New York Statute of Frauds-Personal Property Law, Section 85. 

The provisions of the English Statute of Frauds, section 

17 (1676), are substantially reenacted in the Uniform Sales

Act, section 4, and in the Personal Property Law, Sales Act, section 85. The New York statute provides: 

"1. A contract to sell or a sale of any goods or choses in action of the value of fifty dollars or upwards shall not be enforceable by action unless the buyer shall accept part of the goods or choses in action so contracted to be sold or sold, and actually receive the same, or give something in earnest to bind the contract, or in part payment, or unless some note or memorandum in writing of the contract or sale be signed by the party to be charged or his agent in that behalf. "2. The provisions of this section apply to every such contract or sale, notwithstanding that the goods may be intended to be delivered at some future time or may not at the time of such contract or sale be actually made, procured, or provided, or fit or ready for delivery, or some act may be requisite for the making or completing thereof, or rendering the same fit for delivery; but if the goods are 232

§§ 263, 264 

to be manufactured by the seller especially for the buyer and are not suitable for sale to others in the ordinary course of the seller's business, the provisions of this section shall not apply. "3. There is an acceptance of goods within the meaning of this section when the buyer, either before or after delivery of the goods, expresses by words or conduct his assent to becoming the owner of those specific goods." 

The Personal Property Law, section 31, subdivision 6, provides that: "If goods be sold at public auction, and the auctioneer at the time of the sale, enters in a sale book, a memorandum specifying the nature and price of the property sold, the terms of the sale, the name of the purchaser, and the name of the person on whose account the sale was made, such memorandum is equivalent in effect to a note of the contract or sale, subscribed by the party to be charged therewith."

Therefore, in New York a contract for the sale of goods or choses in action of the value of fifty dollars or more must be in writing, unless the buyer: 

1. Accepts and receives a part of the goods; 2. 

3. The sale is by auction; or 

Pays part of the purchase price;

There is a proper note or memorandum evidencing the oral contract. 4. § 264.

Nature and Purpose of Statute of Frauds. 

The purpose of the Statute of Frauds is to guard against not merely the dishonesty of the parties and the perjury of witnesses but also the misunderstanding and mistakes of honest men. If the contract is reduced to writing, this object is effectually attained. Where this is omitted, the fact that the buyer has paid a part of the price, or the seller has delivered and the buyer has accepted a part or all of the property, furnishes unequivocal evidence of the existence of a contract of some kind between the buyer and the seller, although its terms and provisions must, after all, depend 233

§§ 264  

on the recollection of witnesses. Where the agreement is not in writing, the requirements of the statute can be satisfied only by something done, subsequent to the sale, indicating the agreement of the parties. Shindler v. Houston, 1 N. Y. 261, 49 Am. Dec. 316.

At common law, contracts for the sale of goods, regardless of their value, were not required to be in writing and this common law rule still prevails in many states. Odom v. Clark, 146 N. C. 544, 60 S. E. 513. § 265.

Goods Incapable of Present or Part Delivery. 

The statutory provision relating to a "contract to sell or a sale of goods" includes agreements to sell property to be acquired later as well as contracts to sell property which the seller has already acquired. The distinction between a contract to sell and a sale is that in the former no title passes to the buyer at the time of the making of the contract, while in the latter title does pass at such time. While a delivery of a part of the property sold is effective to relieve the contract from the operation of the statute, the courts have refused to hold that the statute applies only to goods of which a part may be delivered. For example, an animal is not susceptible of part delivery, yet the sale of a horse or a cow of the value of fifty dollars or upwards is within the statute. Tisdale v. Harris, 20 Pick. (Mass.) 

9. So, too, a contract for the sale of goods not in esse, or not owned by the seller, and, therefore, incapable of delivery at the time of sale, is within the operation of the statute. R. & L. Co. v. Metz, 175 App. Div. 276, 160 

N. Y. S. 145, aff'd 219 N. Y. 556, 114 N. E. 1082; Franklin v. Matoa Gold Mining Co., 158 Fed. 941, 16 L. R. A. (N.S.) 381. 

§ 266. Subject Matter of Sale. 

The English statute uses the phrase "goods, wares and merchandise." The controversy as to whether a chose in action is within the Statute of Frauds was settled by the enactment of the Personal Property Law, Sales Act, sec- 

234

§§ 266, 267 

tion 85, subdivision 1, the present law in New York, which employs the phrase "goods or choses in action." The term comprehends all kinds of corporeal and incorporeal movable property and, therefore, includes such property as bills, notes, checks, bonds, stocks, assignment of claims, etc. 

Tompkins v. Sheehan, 158 N. Y. 617, 53 N. E. 502. It in-. cludes also sales of foreign money when the same is treated as a commodity, as well as transfers of credit between different points by cable. Reisfeld v. Jacobs, 107 Misc. 1, 176 

N. Y. S. 223. 

§ 267. 

Contract for Work and Labor. 

A contract for work and labor is not within the operation of the Statute of Frauds. But where the contract is for the sale of goods not in existence at the time the agreement was made but to be thereafter manufactured or produced, it was long a controverted question whether the contract is for work and labor or for "goods, wares and merchandise." 

The controversy resulted in adopting three distinct rules, known as the English, the Massachusetts, and the New York rules, which are fully discussed in Cooke v. Millard, 65 N. Y. 352, 22 Am. Rep. 619.

The English rule is as follows: If the contract be such that it will result in the sale of a chattel, then it constitutes a sale; but if the work and labor be bestowed in such a manner that the result would not be anything which could properly be said to be the subject of sale, the action is for work and labor. Lee v. Griffin, 1 Best & Smith 272, 121 

Eng. Rep. 716. Under the English rule, a contract for the manufacture and sale of a set of false teeth, though made especially for the buyer, is within the statute, but a contract to supply labor and materials in repairing a carriage is not within the statute.

The New York rule prior to the Sales Act of 1911 was that where the substance of the contract is work and labor to be done in converting raw materials into a new and totally different article, although the transaction is to result in a sale, the contract is not within the statute; but 235

§§ 267, 268  

if at the time of the agreement the article sold substantially existed in its ultimate form, even though acts remained to be done to finish it, the agreement is a contract for a sale, and is within the statute. Under the former New York rule, the goods sold should exist in substantially their ultimate form at the time of making the contract; otherwise the contract is for work and labor. 

For example, a contract to manufacture paper or to make a tent was held to be one for work and labor. Parsons v. 

Loucks, 48 N. Y. 17, 8 Am. Rep. 517; Higgins v. Murray, 73 N. Y. 252. But a contract for the sale of specific lumber which is to be cut and dressed by the seller, or for the sale of furniture to be covered or finished in a particular way, was held to be within the statute because the articles are in existence as the subject matter of the sale at the time of the formation of the contract. Cooke v. Millard, supra; 

Flint v. Corbitt, 6 Daly (N.Y.) 429.

The Massachusetts rule is that a contract for the sale of articles then existing, or such as the vendor in the ordinary course of his business manufactures or procures for the general market, whether on hand at the time or not, is a contract for the sale of goods, to which the statute applies. 

Goddard v. Binney, 115 Mass. 450, 15 Am. Rep. 112.

Contract for Goods To Be Manufactured-New § 268. 

York Statute.

The Massachusetts rule as accepted in New York is embodied in the Personal Property Law, Sales Act, section 85, subdivision 2. The statute provides: "The provisions of this section apply to every such contract or sale, notwithstanding that the goods may be intended to be delivered at some future time or may not at the time of such contract or sale be actually made, procured, or provided, or fit or ready for delivery, or some act may be requisite for the making or completing thereof, or rendering the same fit for delivery; but if the goods are to be manufactured by the seller especially for the buyer and are not suitable for sale to others in the ordinary course of the seller's business, 236

§§ 268, 269 

the provisions of this section shall not apply." Under the present statute, a contract of sale is within the operation of the statute even though the articles sold are not in existence at the time of making the contract. If they are to be manufactured thereafter and are suitable for sale to others in the ordinary course of the seller's business, the contract is not for work and labor but is one of sale and, therefore, must be in writing. Goldowitz v. Henry 

Kupfer & Co., 80 Misc. 487, 141 N. Y. S. 531. Under the present statute, a contract to assemble and install a sodawater fountain, with counter, base, and superstructure of particular dimensions and special design is one for work and labor and not for the sale of goods. So, too, a contract for the sale of a suit of clothes to be made to measure out of cloth selected by the customer is not within the Statute of Frauds. The contract is not for the sale of cloth selected, but for a suit to be made especially for the customer according to his size, and which is to be suitable only for him. Davis v. Blanchard, 138 N. Y. S. 202; Schneider v. Lezinsky, 162 N. Y. S. 769. The distinction between the old and the new rule is discussed in Berman Stores Co., Inc. v. Hirsch, 240 N. Y. 209, 148 N. E. 212. A contract to sell goods to be manufactured especially by a third person is within the statute, and not within the exception contained at the end of the section, since the statutory exception is confined to cases where the goods are to be manufactured "by the seller." Eagle Paper Box Co. v. Gatti-McQuade 

Co., 99 Misc. 508, 164 N. Y. S. 201. $ 269. Amount or Value.

To bring the transaction within the provisions of the statute the value of the property must be "fifty dollars or upwards." It should be noted that while section 17 of the original statute used the word "price," the Sales Act uses the word "value." The exact significance of this change in the law is uncertain. Is the value to be determined by the amount which the buyer agrees to pay? If so, there is little or no distinction between the terms. 237

§§ 269, 270  

Where several articles are sold at the same time, though a separate price is placed on each article, the transaction is generally regarded as a single sale, and if the aggregate value is fifty dollars or more, the transaction is brought within the operation of the statute. Allard v. Greasert, 61 

N. Y. 1. But the circumstances may be such as to show an intent to make separate contracts. In Aldrich v. Pyatt, 64 Barb. 391, an agreement for the sale of apples and barley to be delivered at different times and at different prices was held to be composed of separate contracts. So, too, where separate shares of stock of several persons are sold to the same buyer, each sale is a separate contract. 

Tompkins v. Sheehan, 158 N. Y. 617, 53 N. E. 502. 

§ 270. 

Acceptance and Receipt. 

If the buyer accepts and receives a part of the goods, the contract is taken out of the statute and becomes enforceable even though it is not reduced to writing. 

Acceptance and receipt are distinct terms. 

There may be receipt without acceptance, or acceptance without receipt. In order to comply with the statute, the buyer must both accept and receive. But the acceptance may be expressed by words or by conduct and may either precede or follow delivery of the goods. Even if the goods tendered fully comply with the oral contract, the buyer is protected in his refusal to accept them. Personal Property Law, sec. 85; Spirt & Co. v. Prior, 93 Conn. 639, 107 Atl. 513; 

Tonkelson v. Malis, 119 Misc. 717, 197 N. Y. S. 309; Kieser & Son Co. v. Hallock, 201 App. Div. 186, 194 N. Y. S. 737.

Acceptance is defined as a manifestation by the purchaser to become the owner of the goods. This is in accord with the authorities both prior and subsequent to the Sales 

Act (1911). Personal Property Law, Sales Act, sec. 85, subd. 3; Flanigan v. Waterman, 117 Misc. 617, 191 N. Y. S. 646. 

It should be noted that where the goods are in the possession of a third party, there can be no delivery until the third party acknowledges that he holds the goods for the 238

§§ 270,271 

buyer.
