The Sales Act provides: "Where the goods at the time of sale are in the possession of a third person, the seller has not fulfilled his obligation to deliver to the buyer unless and until such third person acknowledges to the buyer that he holds the goods on the buyer's behalf." Personal Property Law, sec. 124, subd. 3. This principle is well illustrated in bailment cases. Thus, if the bailor, by oral contract, sells an article which is in the possession of a bailee, the purchaser is not protected until he receives notice from the bailee that he holds the article for him. Borenco 

Importing Corp. v. Sperber, 205 App. Div. 417, 199 N. Y. S. 

469; Flanigan v. Waterman, supra.

Delivery of goods subject to an examination is not sufficient to constitute an acceptance. The buyer must have an opportunity to make an examination and pronounce the goods satisfactory or to do some act showing his intention to accept them. Stone v. Browning, 68 N. Y. 598. The receipt by the buyer may be, and often is, for the express purpose of deciding whether he will accept or reject. Cooke v. Millard, 65 N. Y. 352, 368, 22 Am. Rep. 619; Cleveland 

Worsted Mills Co. v. J. C. Brownstone & Co., 190 N. Y. S. 

601. While the buyer may accept the goods or designate an agent to accept for him, the seller himself cannot accept as the agent of the buyer. Broom v. Joselson, 211 App. Div. 

157, 206 N. Y. S. 841; Restatement, Contracts, sec. 204. 

§ 271. Mutuality of Acceptance and Receipt. 

The design of the statute is that neither party can create the evidence which shall prove an oral contract as against the other. Both parties must participate in the acceptance and receipt which takes the contract out of the statute. Accordingly, where the property is already in the purchaser's possession, his acts indicative of the transfer of the title cannot alone constitute acceptance and receipt within the meaning of the statute; as where a person already in possession of law books, which he claims to have purchased, pastes his name labels thereon and assumes authority over them as his own. Young v. Ingalsbe, 208 N. Y. 503, 102 239

§§ 271  

N. E. 590; Maher v. Randolph, 275 N. Y. 80, 9 N. E. (2d) 

786, 111 A. L. R. 1309, noted 7 Brooklyn Law Review 254, 7 Fordham Law Review 122. Clearly, the return of the goods after a proper acceptance and receipt in no way affects the enforceability of the oral contract. Koster v. Koedding, 34 Misc. 765, 68 N. Y. S. 794. 

§ 272. Time of Acceptance and Receipt. 

A receipt and acceptance of any part of the goods which are the subject of an oral contract of sale, and within the Statute of Frauds, at any time subsequent to making the contract and prior to an action to enforce the contract take the contract out of the statute and render it enforceable to the same extent as if reduced to writing when made. Mc- 

Knight v. Dunlop, 5 N. Y. 537, 55 Am. Dec. 370; Jackson v. 

Tupper, 101 N. Y. 515, 5 N. E. 65.

The rule remains unchanged under the Sales Act. Gorden v. Witty, 198 App. Div. 333, 190 N. Y. S. 381. But acts done after the commencement of an action to enforce the contract are inadmissible to prove an acceptance. 

Drake Hardware Co. v. Dewitt, 142 App. Div. 189, 126 

N. Y. S. 868. 

§ 273. 

Constructive or Symbolic Acceptance and Receipt. 

The receipt and acceptance may be evidenced by a symbolic act, such as delivery and acceptance of a key, a bill of lading, or a warehouse receipt. Rodgers v. Phillips, 40 

N. Y. 519; Whitlock v. Hay, 58 N. Y. 484. So, too, with bulky and ponderous articles, it is not required that these should be manually delivered by the seller. Bowe v. Ellis, 3 Misc. 92, 22 N. Y. S. 369.

An acceptance of samples will satisfy the requirements of the statute where the samples are included in the bulk of sale. But if the samples are not treated by both parties as a part of the goods sold, the statute is not satisfied. 

Richardson v. Smith, 101 Md. 15, 60 Atl. 612, 70 L. R. A. 

321 and note; Duncan v. Wohl, South & Co., 201 App. Div. 

737, 195 N. Y. S. 381. 240

§§ 274 § 274. Delivery to Common Carrier. 

Delivery to a common carrier is not sufficient to take the contract out of the statute, even though the vendee designates the carrier, if the vendee gives to the carrier no other authority than to transport the goods. Rodgers v. Phillips, 40 N. Y. 519; Pierson v. Crooks, 115 N. Y. 539, 22 N. E. 

349; Dolan Mercantile Co. v. Marcus, 276 Pa. 404, 120 Atl. 

396. But the vendee may give authority to the carrier to both "accept and receive," in which case acceptance and receipt by the carrier satisfy the statute. Cross v. O'Donnell, 44 N. Y. 661, 4 Am. Rep. 721; Wilcox Silver Plate Co. v. Green, 72 N. Y. 17. 

§ 275. When Part Payment May Be Made. 

Prior to the Sales Act, the statute required part payment to be made "at the time" of the making of the contract of sale. The Sales Act changed the law by omitting the words "at the time." Therefore, a part payment made at any time before an action is brought to enforce the contract is sufficient to take it out of the statute. Jackson v. 

Tupper, 101 N. Y. 515, 5 N. E. 65; Widmeyer v. Crane, 121 

Misc. 309, 200 N. Y. S. 875. 

§ 276. Payment in Money, Property, or Commercial 

Paper.

It is not necessary that the part payment be made in money. Such part payment may be made in property other than money or in the mere use of property. So, too, the statute applies to barter and exchange as well as to contracts strictly for the sale of goods. Personal Property 

Law, sec. 90, subd. 2; Herrick v. Carter, 56 Barb. 41; W. R. 

Grace & Co. v. Nagle, 275 Fed. 343; Weir v. Hudnut, 115 

Ind. 525, 18 N. E. 24.

Considerable controversy has arisen over cases wherein part payment has been made by means of a negotiable instrument; for example, where the buyer has given to the seller, his, the buyer's, own promissory note in part pay- 241

§§ 276  

ment. Such payment has been held insufficient to satisfy the statute for the following reason: "It would be but an agreement that a promise of future part payment shall operate as part payment, while the statute requires something more than words; it requires part payment, and not a promise of part payment to be received as payment." 

Krohn v. Bantz, 68 Ind. 277. Accordingly, the fact that the seller has merely received the note of the buyer is not sufficient to constitute part payment. Combs v. Bateman, 10 

Barb. 573. The same is true of the receipt by the seller of the check of the buyer, especially where payment of the check was stopped. Hessberg v. Welsh, 147 N. Y. S. 44.

Even though payment is not stopped, if the payee subsequently returns the check, the statute is not satisfied. Bates v. Dwinnell, 101 Neb. 712, 164 N. W. 722, L. R. A. 1918B 

900. However, it is recognized that the acceptance of the note of a third party in absolute payment is sufficient to satisfy the statute. Combs v. Bateman, supra. So, also, authority is not wanting to the effect that the check of the buyer himself may be received in absolute payment if such is the agreement of the parties; in which case the statute is satisfied. Bates v. Dwinnell, supra; Groomer v. McMillan, 143 Mo. App. 612, 128 S. W. 285. But the burden of proof rests upon the buyer to show clearly that the seller agreed to receive the check in absolute payment. Groomer v. McMillan, supra. 

§ 277. 

Giving Something in Earnest.

The giving of something "in earnest" which is derived from the civil law means merely a part payment of the purchase price. De Waal v. Jamison, 176 App. Div. 756, 163 

N. Y. S. 1045, aff'd 226 N. Y. 644, 123 N. E. 862; Wenger v. Grummel, 136 Md. 80, 110 Atl. 206. 

§ 278. 

Auction Sales.

The usual memorandum made in a sales book by the auctioneer, or under his direction, of goods sold at public auction is equivalent to a memorandum of a contract or sale 242

§§ 278, 279 

subscribed by the party to be charged therewith. The sales book entry must disclose: (a) the nature of the property, (b) the price, (c) the terms of sale, (d) the name of the purchaser, and (e) the name of the seller. Personal Property Law, sec. 31, subd. 6; Meyer v. Redmond, 205 N. Y. 

478, 98 N. E. 906, 41 L. R. A. (N.S.) 675.

NOTE OR MEMORANDUM § 279. 

Form Required. 

No particular form of note or memorandum is required to satisfy the Statute of Frauds. Moreover, the memorandum may consist of several writings. Telegrams, or letters and telegrams, are sufficient if, when taken together, they disclose all the necessary elements of the contract. 

Trevor v. Wood, 36 N. Y. 307, 93 Am. Dec. 511; Peck v. 

Vandermark, 99 N. Y. 30, 1 N. E. 41; Brewer v. Horst- 

Lachmund Co., 127 Cal. 643, 60 Pac. 418, 50 L. R. A. 240 and note. A case illustrating the insufficiency of letters to constitute a contract within the statute is Poel v. Brunswick-Balke-Collender Co., 216 N. Y. 310, 110 N. E. 619.

Where the contract consists of several writings, telegrams, letters, etc., they must not only contain the substance of a complete agreement but must also refer to each other. 

Parol evidence is not admissible to connect the various writings or to supply any of the material terms of the contract. 

Thus, the contents of an unsigned paper must, in order that it may become a part of the memorandum, be referred to by a paper which is signed by the party sought to be charged therewith. Furthermore, the writings must show on their face the nature of the entire agreement. 

If a material term of the agreement is omitted or left for future determination as, for instance, the amount to be paid, the memorandum is insufficient. Ansorge v. Kane, 244 N. Y. 395, 155 N. E. 683; Berman Stores Co., Inc. v. 

Hirsch, 240 N. Y. 209, 148 N. E. 212; Brauer v. Oceanic 

Steam Navigation Co., 178 N. Y. 339, 70 N. E. 863; Wilson v. Lewiston Mill Co., 150 N. Y. 314, 44 N. E. 959; Marks v. 

Cowdin, 226 N. Y. 138, 123 N. E. 139. 243

$ 

 § 280. 

The memorandum must contain: 

Sufficiency of Memorandum. 

1. A description of the subject matter of the contract so that, if not specifically named, it may be identified. Tallman v. Franklin, 14 N. Y. 584; Evans v. Pelta, 146 App. 

Div. 749, 131 N. Y. S. 411. A memorandum for the sale of "all the paper in rolls now stored at the warehouse at 405 East Tenth Street, New York City" is sufficient description of the property sold. Northeastern Paper Co. v. Concord 

Paper Co., 214 App. Div. 537, 212 N. Y. S. 318, aff'd 242 

N. Y. 562, 152 N. E. 428. So, too, evidence of a trade name or usage is admissible to identify the subject matter. 

Franklin Sugar Refining Co. v. Lipowicz, 247 N. Y. 465, 160 N. E. 916; Brewer v. Horst-Lachmund Co., 127 Cal. 

643, 60 Pac. 418, 50 L. R. A. 240. 

2. The names of the parties or a description of them. 

Mentz v. Newwitter, 122 N. Y. 491, 25 N. E. 1044, 11 L. R. 

A. 97; Irvmor Corp. v. Rodewald, 253 N. Y. 472, 171 N. E. 

747, 70 A. L. R. 192. 

3. A statement of the consideration. The note or memorandum of the agreement must expressly, or by implication, state the consideration. It should be observed that it is the plaintiff who furnishes the consideration, and, therefore, what the plaintiff did, or what he promised to do, in return for the defendant's promise must appear in the memorandum. Seymour v. Warren, 179 N. Y. 1, 71 N. E. 

260; Standard Oil Co. of New York v. Koch, 260 N. Y. 150, 183 N. E. 278. The Real Property Law, sections 258 and 

259, requires that the consideration must be expressed. 

4. The signature required by statute. In contracts involving the sale of personal property of the value of fifty dollars or more, the statute requires the memorandum to be signed by the party to be charged. It follows, therefore, that when the memorandum is signed by only one of the parties it is not enforceable against the one who did not sign. Thus, an action cannot be maintained on a contract by the seller against the purchaser of goods where the seller signed the memorandum but the purchaser did not. Levitt 244

§ v. Sliman, 137 N. Y. S. 904; Houser v. Hobart, 22 Idaho 

735, 127 Pac. 997, 43 L. R. A. (N.S.) 410. Since the memorandum is not the contract but only evidence of it, the signing of the memorandum by only one of the parties is not evidence of lack of mutuality. There may be mutuality of agreement without mutuality as to the remedy or evidence of the agreement. The statute presupposes a contract by parol.

In contracts involving the leasing of real property for more than one year, or the sale of real property, the New York statute expressly requires merely a subscribing by the grantor or lessor, or by his agent. There is no requirement that the buyer or lessee must sign. Real Property Law, sec. 259; Brune v. Vom Lehn, 112 Misc. 342, 183 N. Y. S. 

360, aff'd 196 App. Div. 907, 187 N. Y. S. 928. Although the statute requires the note or memorandum to be subscribed only by the grantor or lessor, or by his agent, the defense of non-compliance with the Statute of Frauds is available to both parties. And, although the note or memorandum is subscribed by the grantor or lessor, or by his agent, it becomes enforceable only when the grantee or lessee has in some manner accepted it as evidence of a binding agreement. Three Hundred West End Avenue Corp. v. Warner, 250 N. Y. 221, 165 N. E. 271. So, also, in Kittel v. Stueve, 10 Misc. 696, 31 N. Y. S. 821, aff'd without opinion 146 N. Y. 380, 41 N. E. 89, it is held to be unnecessary. to the validity of a contract for the sale of real estate that the buyer sign.

Contracts within the English Statute of Frauds, section 4, now embodied in the Personal Property Law, section 31, and Real Property Law, section 259, must be "subscribed."

Contracts within the Decedent Estate Law, section 113, and the English Statute of Frauds, section 17, now contained in the Personal Property Law, Sales Act, section 85, may be "signed" instead of "subscribed." To subscribe a writing, the signature must be written at the physical end thereof. 

James v. Patten, 6 N. Y. 9, 55 Am. Dec. 376; Coe v. Tough, 116 N. Y. 273, 22 N. E. 550. In "signing" an instrument 245

§ 

the signature may be placed "either at the top, in the middle, or at the bottom," provided it is placed thereon by the party himself or by his agent for the purpose of authenticating the instrument. Clason v. Bailey, 14 Johns. (N.Y.) 484; 

Doughty v. Manhattan Brass Co., 101 N. Y. 644, 4 N. E. 

747. The "signing" may be done by the adoption of a printed signature. Goldowitz v. Henry Kupfer & Co., 80 

Misc. 487, 141 N. Y. S. 531. But a printed signature is insufficient to constitute a subscription. Subscribing means a manual writing of one's name. Vielie v. Osgood, 8 Barb. 130.

Whether a signature on any part of a memorandum other than when the signature is subscribed thereto is sufficient to constitute a signing has been a controversial question with the courts from early times. In Dorian Holding & 

Trading Corp. v. Brunswick Terminal & Railway Securities 

Co., 230 App. Div. 514, 245 N. Y. S. 410, aff'd 256 N. Y. 674, 177 N. E. 188, the plaintiff, who was the president of the defendant corporation, sued the defendant for breach of an alleged contract whereby the defendant agreed to sell to the plaintiff a certain number of shares of the defendant's capital stock. The question litigated was whether a letter written and signed by the plaintiff and addressed to the defendant constituted a memorandum sufficient to satisfy the Statute of Frauds. Nowhere in the letter, except in the address, did the defendant's name appear; and the defendant in no way adopted the address in the letter as its signature. The Court held that the letter lacked the signature of the defendant, the party sought to be charged, and, hence, that the letter was insufficient to meet the requirements of the Statute of Frauds. 

For an exhaustive study of the sufficiency of the signature under the Statute of Frauds, see Editorial, New York 

Law Journal, April 24, 1930; also 9 New York University 

Law Quarterly Review 87. 246

§§ 281 

§ 281. Illustration of Brevity of Form. 

The following instrument was held to be sufficient to satisfy the Statute of Frauds: 

"Setauket, N. Y., Feb. 14, 1901. "Received from Thomas N. Bayles two hundred and twenty-five dollars for locust at Oakwood. 

Selah B. Strong." 

Bayles v. Strong, 104 App. Div. 153, 93 N. Y. S. 346, aff'd 

185 N. Y. 582, 78 N. E. 1099. 

§ 282. 

When Agreement May Be Signed. 

The agreement may be signed at any time after the contract is concluded, provided that it is signed before an action to enforce it is brought. Mentz v. Newwitter, 122 

N. Y. 491, 25 N. E. 1044, 11 L. R. A. 97; Bird v. Munroe, 66 Me. 337, 22 Am. Rep. 571. 

§ 283. 

Effect of Non-Compliance with Statute. 

A failure to comply with the statute does not render the contract void, but merely unenforceable. The statute enacts a rule of evidence and requires that the contract, if it is within the statute, be proved by a writing. Witnesses cannot take the place of the note or memorandum. Warth v. Kastriner, 120 App. Div. 480, 104 N. Y. S. 1056; Bird v. Munroe, 66 Me. 337, 22 Am. Rep. 571. 

As a rule, where a statute expressly declares a contract to be "void," it is void for all intents and purposes. But since our statutes requiring certain contracts to be reduced to writing were taken from the English Statute of Frauds which provides that certain oral contracts are merely unenforceable, our courts now hold that notwithstanding the express declarations of our statutes rendering certain oral contracts void they are merely unenforceable. 

Whether an oral agreement which is within the Statute of Frauds is merely "unenforceable" or "void" was a question of much controversy in the earlier cases in New York. 

In Dung v. Parker, 52 N. Y. 494, the Court said: "A con- 247

§§ 283, 284  

tract void by the statute is void for all purposes. It confers no right and creates no obligation as between the parties to it; and no claim can be founded upon it as against third persons." But in Crane v. Powell, 139 N. Y. 379, 34 N. E. 

911, the Court held that verbal contracts within the Statute of Frauds may be enforced if the defendant does not spe cifically plead the statute. The Court said: "The Statute of Frauds does not prohibit the making of any agreement in any way that the parties may see fit nor render them illegal or immoral if not made in some particular way. It simply requires that certain agreements must be proved by a writing. It introduced a new rule of evidence in certain cases without condemning as illegal any contract that was legal before." See, also, Bean v. Flint, 204 N. Y. 153, 162, 97 N. E. 490. Likewise, in Banta v. Banta, 84 App. 

Div. 138, 82 N. Y. S. 113, it was held to be error, where the

Statute of Frauds was not pleaded as a defense, to dismiss the complaint on the ground that the contract was unenforceable by reason of the statute. Therefore, it may be said that, notwithstanding the use of the term "void" in the statute, an oral contract within the statute is not void but is merely unenforceable at the election of the party against whom the plaintiff seeks to enforce it. Sanger v. 

French, 157 N. Y. 213, 51 N. E. 979. 

§ 284. Pleading Statute as Defense. 

Where one seeks to enforce a contract which is within the Statute of Frauds, he need not allege in his complaint a compliance with the statute, but if the defendant desires to avail himself of the benefit of the statute, he must plead it as a defense. Where the complaint sets out a contract void under the Statute of Frauds, the defendant may either demur or answer. If he answers, expressly pleading the statute, he may move for judgment on the pleadings at the opening of the trial. Seamans v. Barentsen, 180 N. Y. 

333, 73 N. E. 42. But where the complaint does not disclose whether the contract is oral or written, the defendant must plead the statute in order to avail himself of the 248

§§ 284, 285 

objection. If the objection is not taken by demurrer or answer, the defendant will be deemed to have waived it. 

Crane v. Powell, 139 N. Y. 379, 34 N. E. 911; Matthews v. Matthews, 154 N. Y. 288, 48 N. E. 531. Under the Civil 

Practice Act, section 277, the demurrer is abolished. Where the invalidity or illegality of a contract sued upon appears upon the face of the complaint, a defense of the Statute of Frauds may now be raised by motion under the Civil Practice Act, section 476, for judgment on the pleadings. Harmon v. Peats Co., 216 App. Div. 368, 214 N. Y. S. 353.

Subject to this limitation, the law as stated in this paragraph remains unchanged. $285.

Part Performance of Contracts Relating to Land. 

A part performance of an oral contract for the conveyance of land, void by the Statute of Frauds, may be sufficient to enable the party who has so performed to obtain relief in a court of equity by a decree of specific performance. But payment of the purchase money is not such performance as will enable the buyer to obtain specific performance in equity, since a recovery of the money so paid in an action at law will fully indemnify the party by whom the purchase price was paid. Where, however, the purchaser, relying upon the oral contract, takes possession and makes improvements of a permanent and substantial nature, any relief obtainable in a court of law is deemed inadequate, and he is entitled to enforce the contract in equity. McKinley v. Hessen, 202 N. Y. 24, 95 N. E. 32; 

Milholland v. Payne, 169 App. Div. 712, 155 N. Y. S. 773, aff'd 218 N. Y. 675, 113 N. E. 1061; Burns v. McCormick, 233 N. Y. 230, 135 N. E. 273. 249

CHAPTER XIV  § 286. 

Limits of Contractual Relation. 

The limits of the contractual relation embrace three points for consideration: 

1. The power to impose liabilities or to confer rights upon persons not parties to the contract; 

2. The assignment of rights and liabilities arising under a contract; 

3. The operation and effect of joint and several contractual obligations.

IMPOSING LIABILITIES UPON THIRD PERSONS § 287. 

General Rule. 

As one of the essential elements of a contract is consent, it necessarily follows that contractual obligations cannot be imposed upon a person without his consent. For example, A cannot make B his debtor, by paying B's debts without his consent. Nor will the delivery of goods or the rendering of services by a third party impose upon the party benefited thereby an obligation to pay for them, where there was no intention to enter into legal relations with the party furnishing them. Boston Ice Co. v. Potter, 123 Mass. 

28, 25 Am. Rep. 9; Noble v. Williams, 150 Ky. 439, 150 

S. W. 507, 42 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1177; Bartholomew v. Jackson, 20 Johns. (N.Y.) 28, 11 Am. Dec. 237. 

§ 288. 

Malicious Interference with Contractual Obligation. 

While a contract cannot impose a contractual obligation upon third persons to perform the contract, yet it does impose a duty upon third persons not to interfere with its performance. This duty is imposed upon all the world. 

Therefore, as a rule, where one maliciously interferes with a contract between two parties and induces one of them to break his contract to the injury of the other, an action- 250

§§ 288, 289 

able wrong is committed. This doctrine is supported by the English authorities and by the weight of American authority. Lumley v. Gye, 2 El. & Bl. 216, 118 Eng. Rep. 

749; Wheeler-Stenzel Co. v. American Window Glass Co., 202 Mass. 471, 89 N. E. 28, L. R. A. 1915F 1076 and note; 

Westinghouse Electric & Manufacturing Co. v. Diamond 

State Fibre Co., 268 Fed. 121. For a discussion of the remedy in tort for interference with contract relations, see 

33 Michigan Law Review 420. § 289. Rule.

Malicious Interference with Contract-New York 

The New York rule formerly was that an action would not lie against one who caused a breach of contract, unless he acted fraudulently or was guilty of misrepresentations, and these false representations induced the party in default to act against his will or contrary to his purpose through coercion or deception. Ashley v. Dixon, 48 N. Y. 430, 8 Am. 

Rep. 559; Rice v. Manley, 66 N. Y. 82, 23 Am. Rep. 30.

However, the New York rule was brought in line with the great weight of authority by the Court of Appeals in Lamb v. S. Cheney & Son, 227 N. Y. 418, 125 N. E. 817, wherein the Court approved the rule that if one maliciously interferes with a contract between two parties and induces one of them to break that contract to the injury of the other, the party so injured can maintain an action against the wrongdoer. This doctrine is not limited to contracts relating to services but is a general rule applicable to all contracts except contracts to marry. Campbell v. Gates, 236 N. Y. 457, 141 N. E. 914. The word "malicious" as used in this connection does not necessarily mean actual malice or ill will. A person is guilty of maliciously inducing a breach of contract where, having knowledge of an existing contract between others, he intentionally, knowingly, and without justification or excuse induces one of the parties to the contract to breach it to the damage of the other. Lamb v. S. Cheney & Son, supra; Hornstein v. Podwitz, 254 N. Y. 443, 173 N. E. 674. Where A and B entered 251

§§ 289  

into a contract whereby A was to supply B with certain news items, which contract required A to deceive C as well as to frustrate an existing contract between C and D, it was held that A's contract with B was illegal as a wrong to others, and A was denied recovery thereon. Reiner v. North American Newspaper Alliance, 259 N. Y. 250, 181 N. E. 561, 83 

A. L. R. 23. 

§ 290. 

Inducing Breach of Marriage Contract. 

There is no liability on the part of a third person for inducing the breach of a contract to marry, even though the act was done maliciously and wrongfully and accomplished by means of threats and false representations. For example, a man has no right of action against a third person for maliciously debauching and seducing his fiancee, alienating her affections, and interfering with the contract to marry. The modern doctrine of malicious interference with contractual relations has not been extended to this class of cases. Davis v. Condit, 124 Minn. 365, 144 N. W. 

1089, 50 L. R. A. (N.S.) 142; Homan v. Hall, 102 Neb. 

70, 165 N. W. 881, L. R. A. 1918C 1195; Ryther v. Lefferts, 232 App. Div. 552, 250 N. Y. S. 699. Note Civil Practice 

Act, section 61-b. 

§ 291. 

Inducing Breach of Employment Contracts. 

By the weight of authority, an action will lie against one who maliciously causes a breach of an employment contract. 

Hitchman Coal & Coke Co. v. Mitchell, 245 U. S. 229, 62

Law. Ed. 260, 38 Sup. Ct. Rep. 65; Old Dominion S. S. Co. 

v. McKenna, 30 Fed. 48, 18 Abb. N. C. 262. Contra, Bourlier v. Macauley, 91 Ky. 135, 15 S. W. 60, 11 L. R. A. 550.

A person who wrongfully induces an attorney's client to breach his contract with the attorney is liable for damages to the attorney. Lurie v. New Amsterdam Casualty Co., 

270 N. Y. 379, 1 N. E. (2d) 472. Formerly in New York, to constitute a cause of action in this class of cases, it was requisite that the defendant act not only maliciously but also that he use fraud or duress in bringing about the breach 252

· §§ 291 

of contract. 

National Protective Assn. v. Cumming, 170 

N. Y. 315, 63 N. E. 369, 58 L. R. A. 135; Park & Sons Co. v. 

National Wholesale Druggists' Assn., 175 N. Y. 1, 67 N. E. 

136, 62 L. R. A. 632, 644; De Jong v. B. G. Behrman Co., 148 App. Div. 37, 131 N. Y. S. 1083. But the rule in New

York is now in harmony with the weight of authority. 

Posner Co. v. Jackson, 223 N. Y. 325, 119 N. E. 573. These principles, it should be noted, do not necessarily apply in cases involving labor union activities in labor disputes. Under a concept of public policy favoring collective bargaining, interference with another's contract may be held permissible in certain circumstances. See Exchange Bakery & 

Restaurant, Inc. v. Rifkin, 245 N. Y. 260, 157 N. E. 130.

CONFERRING RIGHTS ON THIRD PERSONS § 292. 

General Rule.

As a general rule a person cannot acquire rights under a contract to which he is not a party. This rule is strictly followed in England. Dunlop Pneumatic Tyre Co., Ltd. v. 

Selfridge & Co., Ltd., App. Cas. (Eng.) 847 (1915). 

§ 293. 

American Doctrine. 

In most states, a third person, under certain circumstances, may acquire rights under a contract to which he is not a party. Seaver v. Ransom, 224 N. Y. 233, 120 

N. E. 639, 2 A. L. R. 1187; Hamilton v. Wheeling Public 

Service Co., 88 W. Va. 573, 107 S. E. 401, 21 A. L. R. 433;

Rhymer v. Federal Life Insurance Co., 13 Fed. Supp. 181. 

§ 294. Massachusetts Rule.

Massachusetts follows the English rule. In Exchange 

Bank v. Rice, 107 Mass. 37, 9 Am. Rep. 1, the Court said: "The general rule of law is, that a person who is not a party to a simple contract, and from whom no consideration moves, cannot sue on the contract, and consequently that a promise made by one person to another, for the benefit of a third person who is a stranger to the consideration, 253

§§ 294, 295  

will not support an action by the latter." Borden v. Boardman, 157 Mass. 410, 32 N. E. 469; Codman v. Deland, 231 

Mass. 344, 121 N. E. 14.

The same doctrine, with some difference as to the exceptions, prevails in Connecticut, Georgia, Michigan, New 

Hampshire, North Carolina, and Pennsylvania. National 

Bank v. Grand Lodge, 98 U. S. 123, 25 Law. Ed. 75; Adams v. Kuehn, 119 Pa. 76, 13 Atl. 184. In some states, the matter is controlled by statute. American Soda Fountain Co. v. 

Hogue, 17 N. D. 375, 116 N. W. 339, 17 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1113. $ 295. New York Rule.

The New York rule, which is followed by most of the states, is that a third person not a party to a contract made by others for his benefit may enforce the contract subject to the following limitations: 

1. The contracting parties must have intended that the third person should receive the benefit of the contract. 

2. The promisee, at the time the contract was made, must have been under an obligation to the person for whose benefit the promise was made.

The leading case on the right of a third person to sue on a contract made for his benefit is Lawrence v. Fox, 20 

N. Y. 268. In this case, one Holly lent Fox $300, stating at the time that he (Holly) owed Lawrence that same amount for money borrowed of him (Lawrence), and had agreed to pay it to him (Lawrence) the next day. Fox accordingly promised Holly to pay his (Holly's) debt to Lawrence at that time. Lawrence, who did not know of the agreement when made, upon learning the facts, sued Fox for the money, the original debtor, Holly, having failed. 

The defendant, Fox, moved for a non-suit on the ground, among others, that Lawrence was not a party to the contract; that his only obligation was to Holly; and that there was no privity between the plaintiff and the defendant. 

The Court ruled that Lawrence could maintain the action 254

§ and held, subject to the two limitations above named, that neither privity of contract between the promisor and the party claiming the benefit nor consideration moving from the third person is essential to support an action. But a mere stranger to a contract between others, in which one. of the parties promises to do something for the benefit of such stranger, there being no consideration moving from the stranger and no duty or obligation owing to him on the part of the promisee, cannot recover upon it. Thus, a mere direction by A to B to deliver to C money or property belonging to A in B's possession gives no right of action to C. Lawrence v. Fox, supra. 

The right in New York of a third party beneficiary to recover from the promisor is discussed in Vrooman v. 

Turner, 69 N. Y. 280, 25 Am. Rep. 195, as follows: "To give a third party who may derive a benefit from the performance of the promise, an action, there must be, first, an intent by the promissee to secure some benefit to the third party, and second, some privity between the two, the promissee and the party to be benefited, and some obligation or duty owing from the former to the latter which would give him a legal or equitable claim to the benefit of the promise, or an equivalent from him personally. "It is true there need be no privity between the promissor and the party claiming the benefit of the undertaking, neither is it necessary that the latter should be privy to the consideration of the promise, but it does not follow that a mere volunteer can avail himself of it. A legal obligation or duty of the promissee to him, will so connect him with the transaction as to be a substitute for any privity. with the promissor, or the consideration of the promise, the obligation of the promissee furnishing an evidence of the intent of the latter to benefit him, and creating a privity by substitution with the promissor. A mere stranger cannot intervene, and claim by action the benefit of a contract between other parties. There must be either a new consideration or some prior right or claim against one of the contracting parties, by which he has a legal interest in the 255

§§ 295, 296  

performance of the agreement." For a consideration of 

New York cases, see 15 Boston Law Review 133. See, also, Hendrix Mill Co. v. Meador, 288 Ky. 844, 16 S. W. (2d) 

482; In re Gubelman, 13 Fed. (2d) 730; Restatement, Contracts, sec. 133, subd. (1). 

§ 296. Necessity of Intent To Benefit Third Party. 

To give a third party a right of action, it must appear that the contract was made for his benefit. "It is not sufficient that the performance of the covenant may benefit a third person. It must have been entered into for his benefit, or at least such benefit must be the direct result of performance and so within the contemplation of the parties." Durnherr v. Rau, 135 N. Y. 219, 32 N. E. 49.

The right of a property owner to maintain an action against a water company for failure to supply sufficient water for fire purposes, as required by its contract with the municipality, is denied by the great weight of authority. 

The interest of the property owner is regarded as indirect. 

The contract of the water company is an assumption of duty to the city and not to its inhabitants. Intention is not shown that the water company is to be answerable to individual members of the community for loss ensuing from failure to maintain adequate water pressure in city hydrants. German

Alliance Insurance Co. v. Home Water Supply Co., 226 U. S. 

220, 57 Law. Ed. 195, 33 Sup. Ct. Rep. 32; Moch Co. v. Rensselaer Water Co., 247 N. Y. 160, 159 N. E. 896.

Although a contract between a municipal corporation and a water company for an adequate water supply for the extinguishment of fires is not for the direct benefit of the property owner, yet a contract between such parties, fixing the rate at which consumers shall be supplied, is intended to benefit the individual consumer, and he may sue to enjoin the collection of a higher rate than that stipulated in the contract between the company and the city. Pond v. 

New Rochelle Water Co., 183 N. Y. 330, 76 N. E. 211; Matter of International Ry. Co. v. Rann, 224 N. Y. 83, 120 N. E. 

153; Smyth v. City of New York, 203 N. Y. 106, 96 N. E. 256

§§ 296 409. Moreover, the individual consumer may compel the water company to furnish the water at the contract rate by means of mandamus proceedings. Baker v. New York Interurban Water Co., 113 Misc. 459, 184 N. Y. S. 833. 

§ 297. Necessity of Duty Owing by Promisee to Third 

Party.

The second limitation placed on the rule giving a third party a right to enforce a contract made for his benefit is that the relation of debtor and creditor must exist between the promisee and the third party, or there must be some other duty owing by the promisee to such third party. 

Durnherr v. Rau, 135 N. Y. 219, 32 N. E. 49; Wait v. Wilson, 86 App. Div. 485, 83 N. Y. S. 834.

The difficulty in applying this limitation arises where there is apparently no legal obligation but only a duty owing by the promisee to the beneficiary. What must be the nature of this duty? Is a moral duty sufficient to satisfy the requirements of the New York rule? Undoubtedly, certain family relationships may be relied upon as furnishing the duty or obligation which must exist between the promisee and the beneficiary. For example, a father's duty to his illegitimate child, Todd v. Weber, 95 

N. Y. 181, 47 Am. Rep. 20; a husband's moral duty for the future support of his wife, Buchanan v. Tilden, 158 N. Y. 

109, 52 N. E. 724, 44 L. R. A. 170; a mother's duty toward her child, Weinberger v. Van Hessen, 260 N. Y. 294, 183 

N. E. 429; an aunt's "constraining power of conscience" toward a "dependent or faithful niece," Seaver v. Ransom, 224 N. Y. 233, 120 N. E. 639, 2 A. L. R. 1187; a fiance's duty toward his affianced wife, De Cicco v. Schweizer, 221 

N. Y. 431, 117 N. E. 807, L. R. A. 1918E 1004. § 298.

Recapitulation-Right of Beneficiary To Sue. 

The right of a beneficiary to sue on contracts made for his benefit is confined, according to the case of Seaver v. 

Ransom, 224 N. Y. 233, 120 N. E. 639, 2 A. L. R. 1187; 257

§§ 298, 299  

1. To cases where there is a pecuniary obligation running from the promisee to the beneficiary; 

2. To cases where the contract is made for the benefit of the wife, affianced wife, or child of a party to the contract; 

3. To public contract cases where the municipality seeks to protect its inhabitants by covenants for their benefit; 

4. To cases where, at the request of a party to the contract, the promise runs directly to the beneficiary although he does not furnish the consideration. For examples of this last class, see Rector, etc., St. Mark's Church v. Teed, 120 

N. Y. 583, 24 N. E. 1014; First National Bank of Sing Sing v. Chalmers, 144 N. Y. 432, 39 N. E. 331.

This doctrine is illustrative of the progress of the ideals of equity in modifying the rigid rules of the common law as promulgated in the earlier cases. But that the question is still a matter of considerable uncertainty is evident from the fact that the Court, in Seaver v. Ransom, supra, after apparently confining the doctrine in question to the above four classes of cases, quoted without disapproval the following declaration of the Court in Wright v. Glen Telephone Co., 48 Misc. 192, 95 N. Y. S. 101, aff'd 112 App. 

Div. 745, 99 N. Y. S. 85: "The right of a third person to recover upon a contract made by other parties for his benefit must rest upon the peculiar circumstances of each case rather than upon the law of some other case.” 

§ 299. Applications of the Rule. 

Mortgages. The rule finds frequent application in cases where a landowner, who is liable to a mortgagee of the land for the mortgage debt, sells the land, and the grantee assumes, and agrees to pay, the mortgage. It is held that the mortgagee may sue the new owner on the promise made for his benefit. Burr v. Beers, 24 N. Y. 178, 80 Am. Dec. 

327; Wager v. Link, 134 N. Y. 122, 31 N. E. 213. But if the landowner is not personally liable to the mortgagee, the 258

$ grantee is not liable on his promise, because the grantor, in such a case, does not owe the mortgagee any duty or obligation. Vrooman v. Turner, 69 N. Y. 280, 25 Am. Rep. 195. 

Partnership. Where an incoming partner promises an outgoing partner, whose interest he has purchased, that he will assume the existing partnership debts, the creditors of the old firm may sue on the promise. Claflin v. Ostrom, 54 

N. Y. 581; Hannigan v. Allen, 127 N. Y. 639, 27 N. E. 402.

But a promise to pay one-half, or other fractional part, of the debts cannot be enforced, because no single creditor can show that it was made for his benefit. Wheat v. Rice, 97 N. Y. 296; Serviss v. McDonnell, 107 N. Y. 260, 14 

N. E. 314.

Surety bond. In a number of cases, laborers or materialmen have been permitted to bring suit to enforce surety bonds given to the employer or owner, on the theory that such laborer or materialman is a third party beneficiary entitled to sue. Forburger Stone Co. v. Lion Bonding & 

Surety Co., 103 Neb. 202, 170 N. W. 897; Knight & Jillson 

Co. v. Castle, 172 Ind. 97, 87 N. E. 976, 27 L. R. A. (N.S.) 

573 and note. In Strong v. American Fence Construction 

Co., 245 N. Y. 48, 156 N. E. 92, where a contractor failed to fulfill his promise to give a bond required by a federal statute, it was held that the laborer or materialman could maintain an action to recover damages for the breach, in an amount not exceeding the pro rata share that would have been his had the bond been given. However, in Fosmire v. 

National Surety Co., 229 N. Y. 44, 127 N. E. 472, where a surety company furnished for a highway contractor a bond to the people of the state guaranteeing the payment of the wages of the laborers employed by the contractor, it was held that such a laborer could not sue the surety on such undertaking. In this case, the plaintiff was not the sole beneficiary and the primary purpose of the bond was the protection of the state, not the plaintiff. The Court observed that the conclusion might have been different had the state or municipality been plaintiff. "What the defendant's liability would be if the action were prosecuted by the 259

§§ 299, 300  

People, we need not now determine." Fosmire v. National 

Surety Co., supra. The question expressly reserved by the 

Court was answered in Johnson Service Co. v. E. H. Monin, Inc., 253 N. Y. 417, 171 N. E. 692, 77 A. L. R. 214, where it was held that a municipality, the promisee, may sue in equity to enforce a surety bond, although the proceeds of the judgment will be held in trust for the benefit of the materialman. The promisee, however, cannot be compelled to maintain a suit for the benefit of the sub-contractor or materialman. William S. Van Clief & Sons, Inc. v. City of New 

York and Others, 141 Misc. 216, 252 N. Y. S. 402.

Agent of undisclosed principal. Contracts made by an agent for an undisclosed principal are to be distinguished from contracts made for the benefit of a third person. An agent is the representative of the principal, and the promise of the other party, in reality, is made to him. Carter v. 

Southern Ry. Co., 111 Ga. 38, 36 S. E. 308, 50 L. R. A. 354.

Assignments. The rule that one who is not a party to a contract may maintain a suit thereon only when it is made for his benefit does not apply to prevent suit by the assignee of an assignable contract. Merritt v. Booklover's 

Library, 89 App. Div. 454, 85 N. Y. S. 797. In such cases, the assignee asserts that the right to sue which resided in the assignor has been transferred to him. A third party beneficiary asserts a right in his own favor arising at the inception of the contract. See Chapter XV, infra. 

§ 300. Sealed Instruments. 

In many jurisdictions, a third party beneficiary can sue on a simple contract but not on a contract under seal. See cases collected in 25 L. R. A. 273. However, in New York and in many other states, the right of a third party to sue on a contract made for his benefit applies to promises under seal, as well as to simple contracts. Henricus v. 

Englert, 137 N. Y. 488, 33 N. E. 550; Jefferson v. Asch, 53 Minn. 446, 55 N. W. 604, 25 L. R. A. 257 and note. 260

$$ 301 

§ 301. Rescission by Parties to Contract. 

After the third party beneficiary has adopted or has acted in some manner upon the contract made for his benefit, the promisee and promisor cannot rescind the contract to the prejudice of the rights of the third party beneficiary. Gifford v. Corrigan, 117 N. Y. 257, 22 N. E. 756, 6 L. R. A. 610. But it may be avoided at any time before the third party elects to look exclusively to the promisor. 

Wood v. Moriarity, 15 R. I. 518, 9 Atl. 427. 

§ 302. 

Right of Promisee against Promisor. 

In mortgage cases, the promisee has been denied the right to sue on the promise made to him for the benefit of another. Thus, where the mortgagor sold real property and the grantee assumed and agreed to pay the mortgage thereon, it was held that the promisee could not sue on the promise made to him for the benefit of the mortgagee. 

Ayers v. Dixon, 78 N. Y. 318; Williston on Contracts, Rev.

Ed., Vol. II, sec. 390. However, in comparatively late cases not involving mortgages, the Court has upheld the right of the promisee to sue for specific performance of a third party beneficiary contract. Croker v. New York Trust Co., 245 

N. Y. 17, 156 N. E. 81; Johnson Service Co. v. E. H. Monin, Inc., 253 N. Y. 417, 171 N. E. 692, 77 A. L. R. 214; William

S. Van Clief & Sons, Inc. v. City of New York and Others, 

141 Misc. 216, 252 N. Y. S. 402. 

§ 303. Right of Beneficiary against Promisor. 

Where the beneficiary possesses the right to sue on a contract made for his benefit, he is not restricted to an action at law. He may, in a proper case, sue in equity for specific performance of the contract. Weinberger v. Van 

Hessen, 260 N. Y. 294, 183 N. E. 429. 261

CHAPTER XV ASSIGNMENT ASSIGNMENT BY ACT OF THE PARTIES § 304. 

Assignment of Choses in Action. 

It was an established rule of the common law that choses in action could not be assigned, except in the case of those governed by the law merchant. Their assignment was prohibited because it was a rule of the early common law that a man could not assign that which he did not have in his possession, and, further, because it was believed that to allow a man to make over to a stranger his right of action would enable the rich and more powerful to work oppression upon the lower classes, and thus violate the rules of champerty and maintenance. Thallhimer v. 

Brinckerhoff, 3 Cowen (N.Y.) 623, 15 Am. Dec. 308. But the early limitation of the common law against the assignability of choses in action was soon modified by the common law courts themselves and still more extensively by the equity courts, until the principle became established that practically all rights arising ex contractu, including rights of action for breaches of contract and contracts to pay money, were assignable unless there was something in the terms or nature of the contract itself which manifested a contrary intention. The change is due in part to statutes which may be divided into two classes: those statutes which expressly provide that choses in action may be transferred; and those statutes which provide that the action to enforce an obligation must be brought in the name of the real party in interest. The American Bonding & Trust Co. v. Baltimore & Ohio Southwestern Ry. Co., 124 Fed. 866; Atlantic & North Carolina R. R. Co. v. Atlantic & North Carolina 

Co., 147 N. C. 368, 61 S. E. 185, 23 L. R. A. (N.S.) 223.

Where the parties to the contract expressly stipulate that the contract shall not be assigned, such stipulation will always be recognized and enforced by the courts, and a statute which, in general terms, makes contracts or other 262

ASSIGNMENT §§ 304-306 

demands assignable does not have the effect of nullifying stipulations which the parties themselves may make on the subject. Lockerby v. Amon, 64 Wash. 24, 116 Pac. 463, 35 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1064 and note; LaRue v. Groezinger, 84 Cal. 281, 24 Pac. 42. 

§ 305. When Assignee May Sue. 

name.

At common law, an assignee could not sue in his own He was compelled to sue, if at all, in the name of his assignor to his own use. Jessel v. Williamsburgh Insurance Co., 3 Hill (N.Y.) 88; Carter & Moore v. United 

Insurance Co. of New York, 1 Johns. Ch. (N.Y.) 403. But now, by statute in New York and in most states, an assignee may sue in his own name. The Personal Property Law, section 41, subdivision 3, provides: "Where a claim or demand can be transferred, the transfer thereof passes an interest, which the transferee may enforce by an action or special proceeding, or interpose as a defense or counter-claim, in his own name, as the transferrer might have done; subject to any defense or counter-claim, existing against the transferrer, before notice of the transfer, or against the transferee. But this section does not apply, where the rights or liabilities of a party to a claim or demand, which is transferred, are regulated by special provision of law; nor does it vary the rights or liabilities of a party to a negotiable instrument, which is transferred." Seibert v. Dunn, 216 N. Y. 

237, 110 N. E. 447.

What Claims or Demands May Be Assigned-By 

$306. Statute.

The Personal Property Law, section 41, subdivision 1, provides that any claim or demand may be transferred, except in the following cases: "1. Where it is to recover damages for a personal injury, or for a breach of promise to marry. [The cause of action for breach of contract to marry has been abolished. 

Civil Practice Act, sec. 61-b.] 

"2.

Where it is founded upon a grant, which is made 263

§§ 306, 307  

void by a statute of the state; or upon a claim to or interest in real property, a grant of which, by the transferrer, would be void by such a statute. "3. Where a transfer thereof is expressly forbidden by a statute of the state, or of the United States, or would contravene public policy."

It provides, also, that "a judgment for a sum of money, or directing the payment of a sum of money, recovered upon any cause of action, may be transferred; but if it is vacated or reversed, the transfer thereof does not transfer the cause of action unless the latter was transferable before the judgment was recovered." The statutory provisions contemplate the assignment of contracts for the payment of money and not contracts for the performance of some personal act or individual duty. 

§ 307. 

Assignment of Choses in Action for Tort. 

Mere personal torts which die with the person and do not survive to his personal representatives are not capable of passing by assignment. The term "personal injury” includes libel, slander, criminal conversation, seduction, malicious prosecution, assault, battery, false imprisonment, or other actionable injury to the person either of the plaintiff, or of another. General Construction Law, sec. 37-a; 

Hunt v. Conrad, 47 Minn. 557, 50 N. W. 614, 14 L. R. A. 

512 and note; North Chicago Street Ry. Co. v. Ackley, 171 

Ill. 100, 49 N. E. 222, 44 L. R. A. 177 and note; Welch v. 

Verduin, 121 Misc. 545, 201 N. Y. S. 324. Claims arising from injuries strictly personal, whether arising from tort or from contract, are not assignable, but a right of action for injuries affecting the estate, rather than the person, is assignable. McKee v. Judd, 12 N. Y. 622, 64 Am. Dec. 515.

Examples of claims which may be assigned are those which are for services rendered; for prize money; for bounty; for money lost at play or betting; for open or running accounts; for injuries affecting property rather than persons; for property destroyed by a railroad, company; for conversion and other claims against common carriers. 264

ASSIGNMENT 

$$ 307, 308

Wolff v. Rausch, 22 Misc. 108, 48 N. Y. S. 716. An action for fraud is not for a personal injury and, therefore, is assignable. Keeler v. Dunham, 114 App. Div. 94, 99 

N. Y. S. 669. 

§ 308. 

Assignment of Salaries. 

It is generally held that the salary or fees of public officers cannot be assigned before they are earned. The assignment of such funds before they are due is forbidden by public policy because of the danger of impairing the efficiency of public service. This limitation upon the assignment of salaries applies to all public officers. Bowery National Bank v. Wilson, 122 N. Y. 478, 25 N. E. 855, 9 

L. R. A. 706; Walker v. City of New York, 72 Misc. 97, 129 N. Y. S. 1059. The prohibition against the assignment of salaries to be earned in the future has been held not to apply to private employments. Field v. City of New 

York, 6 N. Y. 179, 57 Am. Dec. 435. This rule, however, was modified by the enactment of the Personal Property Law, section 46, which provides that the assignment of future salaries, securing or relating to any indebtedness of less than one thousand dollars, shall be void unless: (a) the assignment is in a separate written instrument which identifies specifically the transaction to which it relates; (b) no other assignment exists in connection with the same transaction; (c) the salary of the employee is at least twelve dollars a week; (d) if the assignment is executed for the. assignor by another, under power of attorney, such power of attorney is in a separate instrument. Only ten per centum of the assignor's salary may be collected under such assignment, and this only if prescribed statutory requirements are met. For a consideration of stipulations in employment contracts against the assignment of salary, see 

Editorial, New York Law Journal, September 26, 1932.

Assignments of future wages are declared invalid by statute if made to employers by mechanics, workingmen, laborers, or other specified types of employees. Labor Law, sec. 197. 265

§§ 309, 310  § 309. 

Equities and Defenses between Original Parties. 

The assignee of a chose in action takes it subject to all defenses against it which the debtor might have interposed against the assignor not only at the time of assignment but also up to the time notice thereof is given to the debtor. 

But he does not take the assignment subject to equities in favor of the debtor arising subsequent to such notice. Civil 

Practice Act, sec. 267. The assignee acquires the same but no greater rights than his assignor had. Personal Property Law, sec. 41; French v. Stevenson, 57 Hun 585, 10 

N. Y. S. 386; Merchants' Bank v. Weill, 163 N. Y. 486, 57

N. E. 749. An unmatured debt existing at the time of the assignment cannot be set off as a counterclaim. Roberts v. 

Carter, 38 N. Y. 107.

The general question of counterclaims is regulated by the Civil Practice Act, section 266, et seq. 

§ 310. 

Notice of Assignment. 

The assignment is complete as between the assignor and the assignee without notice to the debtor or person liable, but it will not operate as against the debtor until he has received notice of it. If, for example, a mortgage is assigned by the mortgagee, and the mortgagor, subsequent to the assignment and without notice, settles a part of or the whole debt by making payment to the mortgagee, the payment is good as against a subsequent claim by the assignee. The recording of an assignment of a mortgage is not in itself a notice of such assignment to a mortgagor, his heirs, or his personal representatives, or to a grantee of the mortgagor where the grantee's deed is recorded prior to the recording of the assignment, so as to invalidate a payment made by one of them to the mortgagee. Real 

Property Law, sec. 324; Muir v. Schenck, 3 Hill (N.Y.) 

228, 38 Am. Dec. 633; Chittick v. Thompson Hill Development Corp., 230 App. Div. 410, 245 N. Y. S. 71, aff'd 259 

N. Y. 223, 181 N. E. 458.

Generally, no special form of notice of an assignment is 266

ASSIGNMENT 

§§ 310, 311 required. Continental Purchasing Co., Inc. v. Van Raalte 

Co., Inc., 251 App. Div. 151, 295 N. Y. S. 867. A demand by the assignee for payment is a sufficient notice, and a delay in making such a demand is immaterial if no equity arose between the debtor and the assignor before notice was given. But in order to protect his rights under an assignment, the assignee should give notice to the debtor as soon as the assignment is made. Danvers v. Lugar, 30 

Misc. 98, 61 N. Y. S. 778; Crosby v. Kropf, 33 App. Div. 

446, 54 N. Y. S. 76. However, the assignment of a checking account in a bank must be in writing, signed by the depositor, and filed with the bank, or the bank will not be bound by the assignment. Gibraltar Realty Co. v. Mt. Vernon Trust 

Co., 276 N. Y. 353, 12 N. E. (2d) 438, 115 A. L. R. 322.

The consent of the debtor is not essential to the creation of an obligation on his part to the assignee if the claim is, in its nature, assignable. Hinkle Iron Co. v. Kohn, 229 

N. Y. 179, 128 N. E. 113. If the rule were otherwise it would be a practical denial of the assignability of choses in action, because the right to assign would depend entirely upon the will of the debtor. § 311.

Notice of Assignment of Salary. 

The Personal Property Law, section 42, provides that any person who shall make to an employee a loan on account of wages due or to be earned in the future on an assignment or note covering such a loan shall not collect or attach the same while it is in the possession or control of the employer, unless within three days after "the execution of such assignment or notes and the making of such loan or loans" a copy of "such agreement or assignment or notes under which the claim is made" is filed with the employer. Thompson v. 

Gimbel Bros., 207 N. Y. 659, 100 N. E. 794; Vilbig v. Kyle &

Purdy, 182 N. Y. S. 905. In this connection, see the Personal 

Property Law, section 46, and also, section 308, supra. 267

§§ 312  § 312. Notice of Assignment of Non-Negotiable and 

Negotiable Paper.

The requirements as to notice to the debtor by the assignee apply to the assignment of non-negotiable paper. A failure to give notice is at the peril of the assignee either by reason of a new defense which may arise between the assignor and the debtor (maker) or by a payment by the debtor to the assignor. Moreover, if a negotiable instrument is assigned instead of negotiated, there arises the same necessity of giving notice as in the case of nonnegotiable instruments. The transfer of a negotiable note drawn to the order of the payee without the payee's indorsement constitutes a mere assignment thereof and not a negotiation. Hence, in such cases, the assignee must notify the debtor of the assignment in order to protect himself as against the assignor. Shaw v. Railroad Co., 101 

U. S. 557, 25 Law. Ed. 892; Goshen National Bank v. Bingham, 118 N. Y. 349, 23 N. E. 180, 7 L. R. A. 595. 

§ 313. Necessity of Consideration. 

No consideration is necessary to support an assignment as against the debtor. Therefore, in an action brought by the assignee against the debtor, the defendant cannot show that the assignment was made without consideration, since an assignment may be made as a gratuity. Richardson v. 

Mead, 27 Barb. 178; Guy v. Craighead, 6 App. Div. 463, 39 N. Y. S. 688; In re Farley's Estate, 91 Misc. 185, 155 

N. Y. S. 63. 

§ 314. 

Liability of Assignor to Assignee. 

The assignor makes no implied warranty that the debt will be paid and incurs no greater liability than the seller of chattels generally. Giffert v. West, 33 Wis. 617. In some states, however, the assignor is deemed to be virtually a guarantor of collection, and, therefore, impliedly warrants that the debt will be paid at maturity by the debtor. Welsh v. Ebersole, 75 Va. 651. 268

ASSIGNMENT §§ 315, 316 § 315. Priority of Title as between Assignees. 

As between successive assignees, the rule in New York and a number of other states is that the one which is prior in time will be protected. "As between different assignees of a chose in action by express assignment from the same person, the one prior in point of time will be protected, although he has given no notice of such assignment to either the subsequent assignee or the debtor." Fortunato v. Patten, 147 N. Y. 277, 41 N. E. 572. See, also, Salem 

Trust Co. v. Manufacturers' Finance Co., 264 U. S. 182, 68 

Law. Ed. 628, 44 Sup. Ct. Rep. 266; Superior Brassiere Co. v. Zimetbaum, 214 App. Div. 525, 212 N. Y. S. 473. But the rule is otherwise in many states. See note in 31 A. L. R. 876. 

§ 316. Assignment of Whole Fund. 

The common law rule that the creditor shall not be permitted to split his cause of action into many actions without the assent of his debtor does not find favor in New York. In this State the debt or obligation may be divided and assigned to various persons. The objection formerly urged to the splitting of a cause of action; namely, that it might subject the debtor to several actions to enforce a single obligation, has little force under our system of practice, which requires all parties in interest to be joined as parties to the action. Civil Practice Act, sec. 211, et seq.; 

Risley v. Phenix Bank of New York, 83 N. Y. 318, aff'd 

111 U. S. 125, 28 Law. Ed. 374, 4 Sup. Ct. Rep. 322; Whittemore v. Judd Linseed & Sperm Oil Co., 124 N. Y. 565, 27 

N. E. 244; Porter v. Lane Construction Corp., 212 App. Div. 

528, 209 N. Y. S. 54, aff'd 244 N. Y. 523, 155 N. E. 881.

A check drawn in the ordinary form for the payment of money does not constitute a common law assignment, in whole or in part, of the fund against which it is drawn, but is governed by the law merchant. Glennan v. Rochester 

Trust & Safe Deposit Co., 209 N. Y. 12, 102 N. E. 537, 52

L. R. A. (N.S.) 302. 

269

§§ 317  § 317. Form of Assignment. 

In New York, usually, no formality is required to perfect the assignment. It may rest in parol, or it may be in writing. Risley v. Phenix Bank of New York, 83 N. Y. 318, aff'd 

111 U. S. 125, 28 Law. Ed. 374, 4 Sup. Ct. Rep. 322; Riker v. Curtis, 17 Misc. 134, 39 N. Y. S. 340. Where there is no written assignment, it is merely a question of fact whether or not there was a transfer, and this may be established by oral evidence. Riker v. Curtis, supra. For the assignment of bank deposits, see Gibraltar Realty Co. v. 

Mt. Vernon Trust Co., 276 N. Y. 353, 12 N. E. (2d) 438, 115 A. L. R. 322; and section 310, supra. 

§ 318. 

Recording-Filing Assignments. 

In the absence of a specific statutory requirement, an assignment of a chose in action need not be recorded in order to be valid. Bates v. Salt Spring National Bank, 157 

N. Y. 322, 51 N. E. 1033. But under the requirements of the Lien Law of New York, section 15, an assignment of a contract for labor and materials for the improvement of real property must be filed in the office of the county clerk within ten days after the assignment. If not so filed, the assignment is void as against subsequent assignees in good faith and for a valuable consideration. Edison Electric Il-. luminating Co. v. Frick Co., 221 N. Y. 1, 116 N. E. 369,

L. R. A. 1917F 1123; Williams Engineering & Contracting 

Co. v. City of New York, 222 N. Y. 1, 117 N. E. 944. 

§ 319. Assignment of Liabilities. 

A person cannot assign his liabilities under a contract. A debtor cannot drop out and substitute another so as to escape his personal liability to his own creditor. Thus, it was held that a contract for the sale and delivery of lead ore by a mining company to persons conducting a smelter, the ore to be assayed after delivery and the price to be ascertained and subsequently paid according to the result of the assay, was not assignable by the vendees to a smelting company, since the mining company could not be 270

ASSIGNMENT §§ 319, 320 

compelled to accept the liability of a smelting company as a substitute for the liability of those with whom it had contracted. Arkansas Valley Smelting Co. v. Belden Min ing Co., 127 U. S. 379, 32 Law. Ed. 246, 8 Sup. Ct. Rep. 

1308; Davidson v. Madison Corp., 257 N. Y. 120, 177 N. E. 

393. But by novation, a party may absolve himself from liability. This, in reality, is a rescission of the old contract by agreement and a substitution of a new one in which the same acts are to be performed by different parties with the approval and consent of the creditor. Heaton v. Angier, 7 N. H. 397, 28 Am. Dec. 353. 

§ 320. Assignability of Executory Contracts. 

The general rule as to the assignability of executory contracts, as stated by the New York Court of Appeals, is that "an executory contract not necessarily personal in its character, which can, consistent with the rights and interests of the adverse party, be sufficiently executed by the assignee, is assignable in the absence of agreement in the contract." Quinn v. Whitney, 204 N. Y. 363, 97 N. E. 724. 

See, also, Paige v. Faure, 229 N. Y. 114, 127 N. E. 898, 10

A. L. R. 649. In Rosenthal Paper Co. v. National Folding 

Box & Paper Co., 226 N. Y. 313, 123 N. E. 766, the Court said: "The general rule now prevailing (as the successor of the archaic view that a contract created strictly personal obligations between the parties and non-assignability was a logical attribute), that any property right, not necessarily personal, is assignable, is overcome only by agreement of the contracting parties or a principle of law or public policy." 

If a contract, however, involves a personal liability-a relation of personal confidence-or calls for the personal skill or experience of one of the parties, it is not assignable. The real question to be considered, therefore, is whether a given contract comes within the principles thus stated. This is not always an easy matter to determine. Cases within the rule that contracts involving personal confidence cannot be assigned are New York Bank Note Co. v. Hamilton Bank 

Note Engraving & Printing Co., 180 N. Y. 280, 73 N. E. 271

§§ 320, 321  

48, and Arkansas Valley Smelting Co. v. Belden Mining 

Co., 127 U. S. 379, 32 Law. Ed. 246, 8 Sup. Ct. Rep. 1308. 

§ 321. 

Assignment of Contracts for Sale of Goods. 

Contracts to sell and deliver goods for a fixed price, payable in cash on delivery, in which the owner would receive the price at the time of parting with his property, are generally assignable. In such cases, the rights of the seller could not be affected by the question whether or not the price was paid by the person with whom he originally contracted or by his assignee. So, too, a seller may assign his right to receive payment for the goods sold and delivered. 

In New York, even in certain cases wherein goods were sold on credit, it has been held that the purchaser may assign his right of purchase under the contract. The purchaser's right to assign was recognized because of his continued contractual liability to the seller after the assignment, and hence the seller's rights were held to be in no way jeopardized by the assignment. Rochester Lantern Co. v. 

Stiles & Parker Press Co., 135 N. Y. 209, 31 N. E. 1018.

In Liberty Wall Paper Co. v. Stoner Wall Paper Manufac turing Co., 59 App. Div. 353, 69 N. Y. S. 355, aff'd 170 N. Y. 

582, 63 N. E. 1119, the contract provided for the sale of paper hangings on thirty days' credit. The Court, holding the contract to be assignable by the purchaser, said: "The contract is not a personal one in the sense that Stoner [the purchaser] was bound to perform in person. Stoner had a right to assign the contract, or in case of his death his executors or administrators would have succeeded to his rights and liabilities under the contract. The obligations of Stoner under the contract could have been discharged by any one. If the assignment was made without the consent of the plaintiff, the obligations of the contract would still have rested upon Stoner, and resort could have been had to him for the fulfillment of the contract if the same had not been carried out and discharged by his assignee." 

The seller, however, could not in all such cases assign a contract and thereby impose upon the buyer the duty to 272

ASSIGNMENT §§ 321, 322 

accept paper hangings manufactured by one other than the seller. In such cases the contract would, quite commonly, be classified as a personal contract. Where the seller is to manufacture the goods, there is usually the personal or human element involved, and this personal equation may be of the utmost importance to the parties; but the personal element is wholly lacking on the part of the buyer where his only duty is to make payment for the goods purchased. 

In Schlessinger v. Forest Products Co., 78 N. J. L. 637, 76 Atl. 1024, 30 L. R. A. (N.S.) 347, the Court said: "The injustice of permitting an assignment of a contract for personal services, for the painting of a picture, for a partnership, is obvious. A contract for the sale of goods to be manufactured stands on similar grounds where the vendee relies upon the skill and experience of the manufacturer, as well as upon the implied warranty of quality. No man who has employed a tailor to make a suit of clothes ought to be compelled to accept a suit made by the tailor's assignee." 

§ 322. Contracts Not Involving Personal Confidence. 

Executory contracts to perform a particular task and not involving the element of personal confidence, such as a contract to dig a trench, or to make an excavation for a cellar, or to cut cordwood, are assignable. Thus, in Janvey v. 

Loketz, 122 App. Div. 411, 106 N. Y. S. 690, it was held that a contract for painting, decorating, whitewashing, and wall papering involved no personal confidential relation, and no exceptional personal skill or knowledge, and was, therefore, assignable. The same is true in the case of a contract for street cleaning. Devlin v. New York, 63 

N. Y. 8, 50 How. Pr. 1.

One of the most common types of rights subject to assignment is that for the payment of money. Lee v. William 

Bailey Corp., 267 N. Y. 161, 196 N. E. 9; Williston on Contracts, Rev. Ed., Vol. II, sec. 412. One who is subject to a duty may delegate its performance, provided the duty does not involve personal confidence. Thus, there may be an assignment of the obligation to pay money, though the as- 273

§§ 322  

signor cannot, by the assignment, absolve himself from his obligations to the creditor. If the assignee does not pay, the assignor still remains fully liable. Rochester Lantern Co. v. Stiles & Parker Press Co., 135 N. Y. 209, 31 N. E. 1018.

Payment of money is not construed as involving the element of personal confidence, even though the debt might have arisen out of the performance of purely personal services. 

Thus, the act of paying or receiving money is fully assignable, since it can be discharged by any one. Where by the terms of a contract, money is due, but there is a promise by the creditor not to assign his right, assignment by the creditor in violation of the promise does not necessarily relieve the debtor of his obligation to pay the assignee. State 

Street Furniture Co. v. Armour, 345 Ill. 160, 177 N. E. 702; 

Williston on Contracts, Rev. Ed., Vol. II, sec. 422. 

§ 323. Assignment of Contracts in Restraint of Trade. 

There is no rule of public policy which makes a contract in restraint of trade non-assignable. A contract whereby a vendor agrees not to engage in a certain business within designated territory for a certain period of time is assignable by the vendee and when the business is sold by the vendee, the purchaser may enforce the obligation against the original vendor. Francisco v. Smith, 143 N. Y. 488, 38 N. E. 980. 

§ 324. Assignment of Building Contracts. 

While there are authorities which support the view that building contracts are assignable, the weight of authority is against the right of the contractor to assign. This class of contracts would seem to involve the element of personal confidence. The knowledge, experience, or pecuniary ability of the contractor is usually the inducement to, and the essence of, the contract. Johnson v. Vickers, 139 Wis. 145, 120 N. W. 837, 21 L. R. A. (N.S.) 359 and note. But it is generally understood that the contractor cannot do all the work in person. Hence, in the absence of a stipulation to the contrary, the contractor may perform the contract 274

ASSIGNMENT 

§§ 324-327 through workmen over whom he places a superintendent, and need not himself perform any of the actual labor. 

Council v. Teal, 122 Ga. 61, 49 S. E. 806. 

§ 325. Agreement Not To Assign. 

The parties may, in all cases, prohibit the assignment of a contract by express agreement and this, too, irrespective of the nature of the contract. In Devlin v. New York, 63 

N. Y. 8, 50 How. Pr. 1, the Court said: "Parties, may, in terms, prohibit the assignment of any contract and declare that neither personal representatives nor assignees shall succeed to any rights in virtue of it, or be bound by its obligations." A right which is contracted away is no longer a right to be asserted by the contractor. In other words, the party who has agreed that the contract shall be non-assignable cannot object because he is held to his bargain. Bar ringer v. Bes Line Construction Co., 23 Okla. 131, 99 Pac. 

775, 21 L. R. A. (N.S.) 597. 

§ 326. Recapitulation of Non-Assignable Contracts. 

Generally speaking, all contracts are assignable, except those within the three following classes: (1) personal contracts; (2) contracts containing a provision against assignment; (3) contracts forbidden by statute to be assigned. 

ASSIGNMENT BY OPERATION OF LAW § 327. In General. 

Rules of law may operate to transfer rights and liabilities arising under a contract from one person to another. Common law rules operate to transfer these rights and liabilities in the following cases: 

1. Upon conveyance of real estate; 

2. Upon marriage; 

3. Upon death. 275

§ 

§ 328. Assignment of Obligations upon Transfer of Interests in Land-Covenants Affecting Leasehold Interests. 

All covenants which touch or concern land which has been conveyed from one person to another are said to run with the land. They are binding upon the assignee of the covenantor and may be enforced by the assignee of the covenantee, even though not expressly made to run to "his assigns." Such covenants relate to the enjoyment, or title, or possession of the land, and so may be said to be made for the land, or to its advantage. The law, therefore, requires that there shall exist between the contracting parties and between those as to whom the question arises a "privity of estate," a "mutual or successive relationship to the same rights of property." Belonging to this class are covenants to pay rent; to repair; not to carry on a particular trade on the premises; to renew; to pay taxes; to surrender at the end of the term with improvements; of title (until breach); and of quiet enjoyment. Stewart v. Long Island R. R. Co., 102 N. Y. 601, 8 N. E. 200. The mere intention of the parties to the covenant that it shall run with the land, as shown by the use of the word "assigns," or otherwise, will not cause it to do so if it does not, in its nature, touch or concern the land. Sebald v. Mulholland, 155 N. Y. 455, 50 N. E. 260; Crawford v. Krollpfeiffer, 195 N. Y. 185, 88 N. E. 29. Even though a covenant touch or concern the land, if it concerns likewise a thing which is not in existence at the time of the covenantor's demise, but which is to be built or created thereafter, the covenant will not bind the assignees unless they are expressly mentioned. Spencer's 

Case, 5 Coke 16, 77 Eng. Rep. 72; Tallman v. Coffin, 4 N. Y. 

134; Coffin v. Talman, 8 N. Y. 465. 

All covenants that do not touch or concern the land are. said to be personal or collateral and do not pass to the assignee. Among this class are covenants to pay a certain sum to a stranger; that the lessor will not build or keep any house for the sale of intoxicating liquor within a certain distance of the premises; and that the lessor will not maintain a competing business within a certain distance 276

ASSIGNMENT §§ 328-330 

of the premises. Dolph v. White, 12 N. Y. 296;

Merly 

Realty Corp. v. Wallack, 134 Misc. 96, 234 N. Y. S. 491. 

§ 329. 

Covenants Affecting Freehold Estates. 

In deeds of conveyance of real property, the covenants which touch or concern the land involved; i.e., relate to its enjoyment, or title, or possession, are ordinarily entered into by parties in privity with each other, since one succeeds to the interests of the other; and these covenants, when not broken at the time of the transfer, uniformly run with the land and are binding upon and inure to the benefit of the assignees. Belonging to this class are covenants against nuisances; against encumbrances; for further assurance; for quiet enjoyment; and of warranty. Geiszler v. De Graaf, 166 N. Y. 339, 59 N. E. 993; Olmstead v. Rawson, 188 N. Y. 517, 81 N. E. 456. Covenants of seisin and of the right to convey are broken, if at all, at the time when the deed is delivered to the grantee. They become choses in action as soon as broken; therefore, they do not run with the land, and hence, do not pass to subsequent grantees. Mygatt v. Coe, 124 N. Y. 212, 26 N. E. 611, 11 

L. R. A. 646; Hilliker v. Rueger, 228 N. Y. 11, 126 N. E. 266. 

§ 330. Assignment of Contractual Obligations upon Mar riage.

At common law, a wife's rights and liabilities were assigned by marriage to the husband. See Robins v. Mc- 

Clure, 100 N. Y. 328, 3 N. E. 663. But by statute, in New

York and many other states, no assignment is thus effected by the marriage. No rights to the wife's property are acquired by marriage, and the husband becomes liable for his wife's debts incurred before marriage only when he acquires property of his wife by antenuptial contract, and then he is liable for her debts contracted before marriage only to the extent of the property thus acquired. Domestic 

Relations Law, secs. 50 and 54. 277

§ 

 § 331. 

Assignment of Contractual Obligations by Death. 

Death passes to the executor or administrator of the deceased all rights of action affecting the personal estate of the deceased as well as all liabilities chargeable against it. 

This does not include: 

1. Contracts depending upon the personal skill or services of the deceased; 

2. Contracts, the breach of which involves a purely personal loss.

Covenants which are attached to leasehold estates pass, as to benefit and liability, with the personalty to the personal representatives; covenants affecting freehold estates, however, such as covenants of title in a conveyance of freehold property, pass to the devisee of the realty or to the heir (designated as distributee since September 1, 1930).

Where one contracts to build upon his own land a house for another and dies before the building is completed, his executor is bound to perform the contract. If the land descends to the heirs, the covenants still remain in force. 

Chamberlain v. Dunlop, 126 N. Y. 45, 26 N. E. 966.

A contract of guaranty survives the death of the guarantor. Kernochan v. Murray, 111 N. Y. 306, 18 N. E. 868, 2 L. R. A. 183; Assets Realization Co. v. Roth, 226 N. Y. 

370, 123 N. E. 743. Contracts for personal service expire with the death of either party to the contract. Thus, an apprenticeship contract is terminated by the death of the master, and no claim to the services of the apprentice survives to the executor. Lacy v. Getman, 119 N. Y. 109, 23 

N. E. 452, 6 L. R. A. 728. In Sargent v. McLeod, 209 N. Y. 

360, 103 N. E. 164, 52 L. R. A. (N.S.) 380, the Court said: "Contracts for personal services requiring skill, which can only be performed by the person named, are held not of absolute obligation, under all circumstances, but subject to the implied condition that the person designated shall be able to perform. If a lawyer dies before he has prosecuted to a judgment or settlement a litigation which he has undertaken to conduct for a certain compensation, his contract 278

ASSIGNMENT § 331 

is at an end, * * * and the compensation agreed upon cannot be recovered by his representative under the agreement because he did not and his representatives cannot carry out his agreement. The extent of the recovery permitted his representative is the full reasonable value of the services rendered under the contract, not exceeding, however, the sum or the rate fixed by it." 279

CHAPTER XVI JOINT AND SEVERAL OBLIGATIONS § 332. 

Classification. 

With relation to the number of persons involved, contracts may be divided into those wherein the persons involved are: 

1. One promisor and one promisee; 

2. Two or more promisors and one promisee; 

3. One promisor and two or more promisees; or
