4. Two or more promisors and two or more promisees. 

§ 333. One Promisor and One Promisee. 

Contracts having but one promisor and one promisee need no special consideration. This is the normal case and presents no difficulties in this connection, for it involves no inter se relation. 

CO-PROMISORS § 334. 

Several Promisors.

If a promise in a contract is made by two or more several promisors, the promise is treated as if the writing contained the separate promise of each promisor. There are, however, two types of several promisors: (1) Where two or more promisors make separate promises to render separate performances to the same promisee; and (2) Where two or more promisors make separate promises to render the same performance to the same promisee. The following illustrations will serve to clarify the distinctions made: 

1. Two or more promisors sign a subscription which provides: "We promise to pay the amount set opposite our respective names." Each promisor is severally liable, but the liability of each is to pay only the amount set opposite his name. The liability of each promisor is completely independent of the liability of the other promisors. Thus, payment or default in payment by one of the promisors of 280

JOINT AND SEVERAL OBLIGATIONS §§ 334, 335 

his particular debt in no way affects the liability of the other promisors. Restatement, Contracts, sec. 113. 

2. Two or more parties sign a writing which provides: "Each of us promises to repay X the $100 he this day lent to Y." The promisors are severally liable, but they are severally liable on the same obligation. Payment by any one of the promisors will relieve all of the promisors from any further liability to X. Restatement, Contracts, sec. 113. 

The effect of the release of one debtor of this type on the remaining several debtors is considered in section 338, infra. 

Under the Debtor and Creditor Law, sections 231 to 240, the phrase "several obligors" or "several promisors" means only parties severally liable for the same performance. This is the type of several liability set forth in illustration 2, above. 

§ 335. 

Several Promisors-Procedure. 

Where two or more parties are severally liable, their liability, as has been pointed out, is separate; and they cannot, therefore, be joined as party defendants in the absence of some statutory provision to the contrary. The New York rules governing the joinder of defendants is contained in the Civil Practice Act which, in part, provides: "Joinder as defendants of persons severally liable. Two or more persons severally liable upon the same written instrument, including the parties to a bill of exchange or a promissory note, whether the action is brought upon the instrument, or by a party thereto to recover against other parties liable over to him, may all or any of them be included as defendants in the same action at the option of the plaintiff." Civil Practice Act, sec. 216. "Joinder of defendants generally. All persons may be joined as defendants against whom the right to any relief is alleged to exist, whether jointly, severally or in the alternative; and judgment may be given according to their respective liabilities, against such one or more of the defendants as may be found to be liable upon all of the evidence, 281

885, 836 

without regard to the party by whom it has been introduced." Civil Practice Act, sec. 211. 

§ 336. Joint Promisors. 

It is a general presumption of law that when two or more persons undertake an obligation they undertake it jointly, unless words indicating a contrary intention are used. The fact that the interests of the obligors in a contract are diverse does not prevent the obligation from being joint. 

United States Printing & Lithograph Co. v. Powers, 233 

N. Y. 143, 135 N. E. 225.

At common law, the liability of co-partners in respect to partnership contracts was joint; while in equity it was several. It is now provided by statute that the partners are liable jointly for all debts and obligations of the partnership, exclusive of torts and breaches of trust, and for these, they are jointly and severally liable. Any partner, however, may enter into a separate obligation to perform a partnership contract. Partnership Law, sec. 26.

Both at common law and under the statute, joint promisors cannot be sued separately over the objection of one of them. All must be joined as defendants.

By statute in New York, it is now provided that in an action against two or more defendants jointly indebted on a contract, or jointly liable in tort, the plaintiff may proceed to final judgment without having served all of the defendants. Such judgment must be taken against all of the defendants. Civil Practice Act, sec. 1197. Such judgment is conclusive against such defendants as were served personally or who entered their appearance in the action, but, against the others, is evidence only of the extent of the plaintiff's demand. Civil Practice Act, sec. 1198. An execution upon such judgment may be levied only upon the sole property of the defendants served personally, and upon the joint property of all the defendants. Civil Practice Act, sec. 

1199. The sole property of the joint debtors who were not served in such action may be charged in a subsequent action 282

JOINT AND SEVERAL OBLIGATIONS §§ 336-338 

against them. Civil Practice Act, secs. 1185 and 1186.

Similar statutes have been enacted in other states. 

§ 337. Joint Promisors-Survivorship. 

At common law, upon the death of one joint promisor, the entire liability devolved upon the survivors, and no action at law could be maintained against the estate of the deceased promisor. In equity, unless he was a guarantor or surety without benefit or advantage, his estate could be charged upon proof that the survivors were insolvent and unable to pay. But the estate of the last deceased promisor remained liable. Davis v. Van Buren, 72 N. Y. 587; Richardson v. Draper, 87 N. Y. 337; Gere v. Clarke, 6 Hill (N.Y.) 350; see note, 67 A. L. R. 609, 614.

In New York, the rule has been modified by statute. The estate of a joint promisor is not now discharged by his death. His personal representatives may be substituted in his place, but only when the surviving debtors are insol vent. Civil Practice Act, sec. 85; Potts v. Dounce, 173 

N. Y. 335, 66 N. E. 4. However, under the Debtor and

Creditor Law, section 236, which did not specifically repeal the Civil Practice Act, section 85, the estate of a joint obligor upon his death becomes bound jointly and severally with the surviving obligors. It seems, therefore, that it is no longer necessary to allege the insolvency of the surviving joint obligor in order to proceed directly against the estate of the deceased joint obligor. Sisto v. Bambara, 228 App. 

Div. 456, 240 N. Y. S. 121. 

§ 338. Joint Promisors-Release. 

At common law, a voluntary release of one joint promisor is a release of all. The reason is that the release is conclusive that the releasor has been fully satisfied, and after satisfaction, although the release absolved only one, no foundation remains for an action against the coobligor. Hillas v. Fuller, 143 N. Y. S. 15. But where a release of one of two joint debtors contains an express provision or reservation that it shall not affect or impair the 283

§ 

claim of the creditor against the other debtor, the latter is not discharged. A covenant not to sue does not operate as a release, and a release containing such a reservation is construed to be merely a covenant not to sue and not a technical release. Hale v. Spaulding, 145 Mass. 482, 14 N. E. 534; 

Whittemore v. Judd Linseed & Sperm Oil Co., 124 N. Y. 

565, 27 N. E. 244.

In New York, the effect of the release of one co-debtor on the liability of the remaining co-debtors is governed by the Debtor and Creditor Law, sections 231 to 240.

Under this statute, the effect of the release of one codebtor on the liability of the remaining co-debtors is the same whether the co-debtors are bound jointly, severally, or jointly and severally. To ascertain the precise effect of the release on the liability of the remaining co-debtors, it is necessary first to determine whether the release contained an express reservation by the creditor of his rights against the remaining co-debtors. If the creditor did expressly reserve his rights against the remaining co-debtors, the latter are discharged only to the extent of the consideration received by the creditor for the release. Debtor and Creditor 

Law, secs. 233 and 234.

However, if the creditor in releasing one or more of the co-debtors fails to reserve his rights against the remaining co-debtors, the latter are discharged only to the extent provided by the Debtor and Creditor Law, section 235, which reads as follows: 

"(a) If an obligee releasing or discharging an obligor without express reservation of rights against a co-obligor, then knows or has reason to know that the obligor released or discharged did not pay so much of the claim as he was bound by his contract or relation with that co-obligor to pay, the obligee's claim against that co-obligor shall be satisfied to the amount which the obligee knew or had reason to know that the released or discharged obligor was bound to such co-obligor to pay. "(b) If an obligee so releasing or discharging an obligor has not then such knowledge or reason to know, the obligee's 284

JOINT AND SEVERAL OBLIGATIONS §§ 338, 338a claim against the co-obligor shall be satisfied to the extent of the lesser of two amounts, namely (1) the amount of the fractional share of the obligor released or discharged, or (2) the amount that such obligor was bound by his contract or relation with the co-obligor to pay." 

To illustrate: A, B, and C are jointly liable to X in the sum of $300. The co-debtors had agreed among themselves that A was to be liable for $150, B for $100, and C for $50. X releases C for $25. What is the effect of the release upon the liability of A and B? There are three possibilities: (1) If the release given to C expressly reserved X's rights against A and B, the latter are discharged only to the extent of $25, the consideration received for the release. A and B are still liable to X for $275. Debtor and Creditor Law, secs. 232 and 233. (2) If X did not reserve his rights against A and B, and X knew or had reason to know the terms of the inter se agreement, A and B are discharged to the extent of $50, the amount which C was bound to pay under the inter se agree- A and B are still liable to X for $250. Debtor and 

Creditor Law, sec. 235, subd. a. (3) If X did not reserve his rights against A and B, and X neither knew nor had reason to know of the inter se agreement, A and B are discharged to the extent of $50, which sum is the lesser of two amounts: (a) the fractional, i.e., pro rata, share of C which is $100-one-third of $300; (b) the inter se share of C which is $50. Thus, in this illustration, A and B are still liable to X for $250. Debtor and 

Creditor Law, sec. 235, subd. b.

The problem used in the above illustrations involved joint debtors, but the same principles are applicable where the debtors are severally liable (i.e., on the same debt) or jointly and severally liable. 

§ 338a. 

Joint Promisors-Release by Partner. 

A partner, who is jointly liable for contract obligations and jointly and severally liable for torts and breaches of trust, cannot make a separate composition with a firm 285

§§ 338a  

creditor until the partnership has been dissolved. Partnership Law, sec. 26. Therefore, a release of one of the partners before dissolution will discharge all the partners, even though the release contains a reservation by the creditor of his rights against the co-partners. Debtor and Creditor 

Law, sec. 237. After dissolution, the release of one partner will operate to discharge his co-partners as though they were merely co-obligors. 

§ 339. Joint Promisors-Payment. 

Payment by one joint debtor of a debt owing jointly by two or more persons extinguishes the debt so far as the creditor is concerned. Storz v. Boyce, 34 Misc. 279, 69 

N. Y. S. 612, note 68 L. R. A. 514. But merely taking the note of one joint debtor does not operate to release co-joint debtors unless there is an agreement to that effect. The reason for this is that a note is presumed to be conditional and not absolute payment. So, too, the check of one of the co-debtors is presumptively regarded as only a conditional payment. Bates v. Rosekrans, 37 N. Y. 409; Leschen & 

Sons Rope Co. v. Mayflower Gold Mining & Reduction Co., 173 Fed. 855, 35 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1 and note, 30, 61; Watson v. First State Bank of Dallas, 237 S. W. (Tex.) 1106. 

§ 340. 

Joint Promisors—Contribution. 

Where one joint promisor pays more than his pro rata share, he is entitled to contribution from his co-promisors unless he has in some manner precluded himself from such right. Morgan v. Smith, 70 N. Y. 537; Jeffries v. Ferguson, 87 Mo. 244. However, in United States Printing & Lithograph Co. v. Powers, 233 N. Y. 143, 157, 135 N. E. 225, the Court of Appeals said: "It is the rule that a plaintiff who enters judgment against one of several joint obligors causes a merger of his entire claim against all of the obligors in the judgment which he has thus entered and thenceforth cannot prosecute those who have been omitted from the judgment, and they would not be liable for contribution." See, also, Peters v. Sanford & Read, 1 Denio 286

JOINT AND SEVERAL OBLIGATIONS §§ 340-341a 

(N.Y.) 224. This rule has been modified by the Debtor and 

Creditor Law, section 232, which provides that the entry of judgment against one or more joint obligors shall not discharge a co-obligor who was not a party to the proceeding in which the judgment was entered. Where all are joined as defendants and some have not been served, the judgment must be entered against all the joint obligors as provided by the Civil Practice Act, section 1197. If judgment is entered only against those who have been served, the others are discharged not only from the debt but also from their obligation to contribute to the joint coobligor who paid the debt. United States Printing & Lithograph Co. v. Powers, supra. 

§ 341. 

Joint and Several Promisors Where two parties to a contract jointly promise to perform, and each party also separately promises to render the same performance, the parties are jointly and severally liable. Thus, if two promisors sign a writing which provides: "We, and each of us, promise, etc.," they are bound jointly and severally. Episcopal Church of St. Peter v. 

Varian, 28 Barb. 644; Restatement, Contracts, sec. 114.

It is also the rule that where the words "I promise" are expressed in a writing signed by two or more persons, the signers are jointly and severally liable in the absence of other words in the writing clearly showing the contrary. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, sec. 36, subd. 7; National 

Surety Co. v. Seaich, 171 App. Div. 414, 157 N. Y. S. 422; 

Restatement, Contracts, sec. 115. 

§ 341a. Joint and Several Promisors-Procedure. 

At common law, where the promisors are jointly and severally liable, all the promisors may be joined in one action, or they may be sued separately, at the election of the promisee. Schilling v. Black, 49 Kan. 552, 31 Pac. 143; 

Cummings v. People, 50 Ill. 132. But, in the absence of a statutory provision to the contrary, all must be sued jointly, or each severally. An action cannot be maintained against 287

§§ 341a  

an intermediate number. Cummings v. People, supra; 

Bangor Bank v. Treat, 6 Me. 207, 19 Am. Dec. 210.

In New York, by virtue of the Civil Practice Act, sections 211 and 216, promisors who are jointly and severally liable may be joined in one action.

It is also provided by statute in New York that in an action against two or more defendants severally or jointly and severally liable, the plaintiff may proceed to final judgment against those who have been served as if they were the only defendants. Upon the entry of the judgment, the action is severed and may be continued against the remaining defendants. Civil Practice Act, sec. 475. Compare this procedure with that required where the action is against joint debtors. Section 336, supra. 

§ 342. Joint and Several Promisors-Survivorship. 

The doctrine of survivorship does not apply to several contracts, and, therefore, has no application to joint and several obligations. Erie County v. Baltz, 125 App. Div. 

144, 109 N. Y. S. 304. 

§ 343. 

Joint and Several Promisors-Release. 

At common law, a release of one of a number of joint and several promisors operates to release all. But a covenant not to sue one will not release the others. Whittemore v. 

Judd Linseed & Sperm Oil Co., 124 N. Y. 565, 27 N. E. 244; 

Morgan v. Smith, 70 N. Y. 537.

The language of the Debtor and Creditor Law, sections 

232, 233, and 234 (sections 338 and 340, supra), specifically includes joint, several, and joint and several obligors. Section 235 of the Debtor and Creditor Law (section 338, supra) does not enumerate three classes of obligors, but it undoubtedly applies to all three. Thus, the effect of the release of one of a number of joint and several obligors is the same as though he were one of a number of joint obligors. See section 338, supra. 288

JOINT AND SEVERAL OBLIGATIONS § 344 § 344. Joint Tort-Feasors-Release. 

Although a release or discharge of one of a number of joint tort-feasors, prior to obtaining judgment against them, operates as a release of all, if such release contains a reservation of rights against the others, it will be construed to be simply a covenant not to sue the one nominally released, and the liability of the others will not be extinguished thereby. Gilbert v. Finch, 173 N. Y. 455, 66 N. E. 

133, 61 L. R. A. 807; Walsh v. New York Central & Hudson 

River R. R. Co., 204 N. Y. 58, 97 N. E. 408, 37 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1137; Berry v. Pullman Co., 249 Fed. 816, L. R. A. 

1918F 358 and note, 363. For a collation of the New York cases, see German-American Coffee Co. v. O'Neil, 102 Misc. 

165, 169 N. Y. S. 421. The rule is the same under the

Debtor and Creditor Law, section 234. 

The Debtor and Creditor Law, section 235, does not apply unless there is a duty between and among the joint tortfeasors to contribute to the joint debt. There is no such duty until a joint judgment has been rendered against the joint tort-feasors. Therefore, a release of one of the joint tort-feasors, executed prior to obtaining a judgment against them jointly, will discharge them all if the release contains no reservation of rights against the others. Gavin v. Malherbe, 240 App. Div. 779, 266 N. Y. S. 897, aff'd 264 N. Y. 

403, 191 N. E. 486.

Under the Civil Practice Act, section 211-a, contribution may be enforced between and among those joint tort-feasors whom the plaintiff has voluntarily joined as co-defendants, but only after a joint judgment has been rendered against them. Fox v. Western New York Motor Lines, Inc., 257 

N. Y. 305, 178 N. E. 289, 78 A. L. R. 578. When the obligation to contribute has thus arisen, the Debtor and Creditor Law, section 235, applies, and the release of one joint tort-feasor from the joint judgment without reservation of rights against the others releases the others only to the extent as provided by that section. See section 338, supra. 289

88 345 

 CO-PROMISEES § 345. 

Joint Promisees.

Where the obligation of a contract runs to two or more joint promisees, all must unite in bringing an action for its enforcement. Ehle v. Purdy, 6 Wend. (N.Y.) 629.

But if the consent of any one who should be joined as a plaintiff cannot be obtained, he may be made a defendant, the reason therefor being stated in the complaint. Civil 

Practice Act, sec. 194. 

§ 346. Joint Promisees-Survivorship. 

If one of several joint promisees dies, the right of action devolves on the survivors. Birdsall v. Delaware & Hudson 

Co., 216 Fed. 717. The personal representative of the deceased promisee need not be joined as party plaintiff. Upon the death of the last survivor, the right of action devolves on his representative. Murray v. Mumford, 6 Cowen (N.Y.) 441; Supreme Lodge Knights of Honor v. Portingall, 167 Ill. 291, 47 N. E. 203. 

§ 347. Joint Promisees-Release. 

A release by one joint promisee is, in the absence of fraud, a bar to an action by the others, and payment of the debt to one discharges the debt. An accord and satisfaction of the debt made with one or more joint promisees is a discharge of the obligation to all promisees. Osborn v. Martha's Vineyard R. R. Co., 140 Mass. 549, 5 N. E. 486; 

People ex rel. Eagle v. Keyser, 28 N. Y. 226, 228, 84 Am. 

Dec. 338. § 348. 

Joint or Several Promisees.

The promisees must be either joint or several; they cannot be both joint and several. Willoughby v. Willoughby, 5 N. H. 244. 

Thus, in Eveleth v. Sawyer, 96 Me. 227, 52 Atl. 639, the 

Court said: "One and the same contract cannot be so 290

JOINT AND SEVERAL OBLIGATIONS §§ 348, 348a framed, as to give the promisees the right to sue upon it both jointly and separately. They must be entitled under it either jointly only, or separately only, and must sue accordingly." However, it has been held that under the Negotiable Instruments Law, a negotiable instrument may be made payable to the order of A, B, and C, or either of them. 

Richardson on Bills and Notes, 4th Ed., sec. 39. § 348a. Several Promisees.

The general rule is that if one party severally binds himself to each of several persons, their right to enforce the promise is several, and each must sue separately. Emmeluth v. Home Benefit Assn., 122 N. Y. 130, 25 N. E. 234, 9 L. R. A. 704; Yates v. Foot, 12 Johns. (N.Y.) 1. However, the Civil Practice Act provides: "All persons may be joined in one action as plaintiffs, in whom any right to relief in respect of or arising out of the same transaction or series of transactions is alleged to exist whether jointly, severally or in the alternative, where if such persons brought separate actions any common question of law or fact would arise." Civil Practice Act, sec. 209. 291

CHAPTER XVII  § 349. Purpose of Construction. 

The primary purpose of construction in the law of contracts is to ascertain the intention of the parties and to give effect to that intention if it can be done consistently with legal principles. "The ascertainment of the substantial intent of the parties is the fundamental rule in the construction of all agreements." People ex rel. New York 

Central & Hudson River R. R. Co. v. Walsh, 211 N. Y. 90, 105 N. E. 136. This intention in express contracts is embodied, in the first instance, in the words which the parties have used and is to be deduced therefrom. This rule applies to oral contracts as well as to contracts in writing. It must not be supposed, however, that the courts will attempt to ascertain the actual mental processes of the parties to a particular contract. The law presumes that the parties understood the import of their contract and that it expresses their intention. In the absence of ambiguity and uncertainty, the parties will not be permitted to prove that they intended something different from that which is expressed in the agreement. Dwight v. Germania 

Life Insurance Co., 103 N. Y. 341, 8 N. E. 654. The purpose of construing the contract, therefore, is to ascertain the intention of the parties as disclosed by the contract itself, and not the intention of the parties independent of the contract. Where, by their words, the parties have left no reasonable ground for doubt, there is no defensible excuse for construction. Gans v. Aetna Life Insurance Co., 214 N. Y. 326, 108 N. E. 443. A court cannot, by implication, add to or change a contract which is clear and complete in itself. South Utah Mines & Smelters v. Utah Leasing Co., 260 Fed. 149. 

In Lynch v. Houston, 138 Mo. App. 167, 119 S. W. 994, the Court held that: "Aside from some technical rules adopted for the general good there is, ordinarily, no hard 292

§§ 349, 350 

and fast rule to govern the interpretation of an intention. 

In conversation it is shown by a look, a gesture, an emphasis or tone of voice. In writing, a mere word, phrase or sentence will put a meaning on a contract which may control implications of law and which will give it a direction and application contrary to what it otherwise would have received." 

§ 350. Interpretation and Construction Defined. 

Interpretation consists in determining the meaning of the words as used by the parties; while construction embraces the thought or intention of the parties as gathered from, not only the words employed, but the whole transaction, including subjects that lie beyond the direct expres sions of the contract. Construction includes interpreta tion. Therefore, a contract must be interpreted before it can be construed. However, the rules of interpretation and construction are necessarily more or less blended, since in determining the meaning of a word, it is sometimes necessary to appeal to the context, just as in determining the meaning of the context, the court finds it necessary to place itself in the position of the author when he used the particular words. Hull Coal & Coke Co. v. Empire Coal & 

Coke Co., 113 Fed. 256; Ullman v. Chicago & Northwestern 

Ry. Co., 112 Wis. 150, 88 N. W. 41, 56 L. R. A. 246. In 

Bloomer v. Todd, 3 Wash. T. 599, 19 Pac. 135, 1 L. R. A. 111, the Court said: "Interpretation differs from construction in this: that it is used for the purpose of ascertaining the true sense of any form of words; while construction involves the drawing of conclusions regarding subjects that are not always included in the direct expression."

Does a clause in a lease which provides that the lessee "is given first privilege to buy said property" give to the lessee an absolute option to purchase the property or merely the right to buy prior to any one else, if the owner sells? 

R. I. Realty Co. v. Terrell, 254 N. Y. 121, 172 N. E. 262. 293

§§  



351, 352 

§ 351. Parol Evidence Rule. 

The parol evidence rule excludes evidence of all previous and contemporaneous negotiations which are offered for the purpose of adding to or varying the terms of a written contract which appears to be complete. But this rule does not operate to exclude parol evidence when such evidence is offered for the purpose of showing a non-contractual relationship because the contract has had no legal inception. 

For instance, parol evidence is admissible to show (a) want of consideration; (b) fraud; (c) want of consent, by reason of mistake as to the nature of the contract, the person with whom the contract was made, or the subject matter of the contract; (d) illegality; (e) material alteration; or (f) to show that the enforcement of the contract was dependent upon a condition precedent, which condition has not been fulfilled. Richardson on Evidence, 5th Ed., sec. 426; 

Grannis v. Stevens, 216 N. Y. 583, 111 N. E. 263; Thomas v. 

Scutt, 127 N. Y. 133, 27 N. E. 961; Reynolds v. Robinson, 110 N. Y. 654, 18 N. E. 127. Nor does the rule exclude parol evidence when the evidence is offered solely to aid in the interpretation and construction of the contract. Richardson on Evidence, 5th Ed., sec. 442, et seq. 

§ 352. 

Meaning of Words. 

Words are to be construed in their grammatical and ordinary sense, unless such construction would lead to some manifest absurdity or to some inconsistency or would defeat the purpose of the agreement. While the courts cannot substitute a contract different from the one actually made, yet they will not allow the purpose of a contract to be defeated where, by the application of certain legal principles of construction, they can ascertain the intention of the parties as expressed in the contract. Manson v. Curtis, 223 N. Y. 313, 119 N. E. 559. "Unless, therefore, the terms of a written instrument have generally, in respect to the subject-matter, as, by the known usage of trade, or the like, acquired a peculiar sense, 294

§§ 352, 353 

distinct from the popular sense of the same words; or unless the context points out that, in the particular instance, and in order to effectuate the immediate intention of the parties, they should be understood in a peculiar sense, they are to be understood in their plain, ordinary and popular sense." Pohalski v. Mutual Life Insurance Co., 45 How. 

Pr. 504, aff'd 56 N. Y. 640. If the word in question has two well-recognized meanings, one inclusive and the other exclusive, the meaning intended must be determined by the context. Chicago Great Western R. R. Co. v. Kansas City, 

North Western R. R. Co., 75 Kan. 167, 88 Pac. 1085. 

§ 353. Preliminary Negotiations. 

While the parol evidence rule excludes all preliminary and concurrent negotiations as a part of the contract, yet these may be resorted to for the purpose of explaining certain terms of a contract where such terms are susceptible of various interpretations. United States v. Bethlehem 

Steel Co., 205 U. S. 105, 51 Law. Ed. 731, 27 Sup. Ct. Rep. 

450. For instance, where a reaper was sold by a written contract, the buyer, who alleged a breach of warranty, was permitted to testify what the plaintiff said before the sale was consummated, for the purpose of explaining the meaning of the word "team" as used in the contract. Ganson v. Madigan, 15 Wis. 144, 82 Am. Dec. 659. So, too, where goods were sold under a contract to pay "ruling market rates," and there were two rates, one for goods bought of importers, and another for goods bought of jobbers, evidence of the conversation of the parties and the surrounding circumstances is admissible for the purpose of showing which rate was intended by the parties. Manchester 

Paper Co. v. Moore, 104 N. Y. 680, 10 N. E. 861. But such oral statements or declarations are not admissible for the purpose of showing an intention not therein expressed. Tuttle v. Burgett's Administrator, 53 Ohio St. 

498, 42 N. E. 427, 30 L. R. A. 214. 295

$$ 354, 354a

 § 354. 

Surrounding Circumstances. 

When the language used in a contract is susceptible of more than one interpretation, the court will look not only to the language of the contract and the subject matter but also to the surrounding circumstances and the object to be accomplished. It is a cardinal rule of construction that, in order to arrive at the intention of the parties, the contract itself must be read in the light of the circumstances under which it was executed; and parol evidence of extrinsic circumstances is admissible not for the purpose of varying the terms of the contract but to show the sense and understanding in which the language was used. Fleischman v. Furgueson, 223 N. Y. 235, 119 N. E. 400; Smith v. 

Webb, 176 Ala. 596, 58 So. 913, 40 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1191. "Contracts are not to be interpreted by giving a strict and rigid meaning to general words or expressions without regard to the surrounding circumstances or the apparent purpose which the parties sought to accomplish." Robertson v. Ongley Electric Co., 146 N. Y. 20, 40 N. E. 390. Thus, where a marine insurance policy provided that the risk was "to be suspended while the vessel is at Baker's Island loading," the question arose as to whether the parties intended this clause to mean while the vessel was at Baker's Island for the purpose of loading, or while it was at the island actually loading. The Court ruled that evidence was admissible to show the dangerous condition of the water surrounding the island and that it was during the entire time that the vessel was in these dangerous waters for the purpose of loading that the parties intended the risk to be suspended and not merely while loading actually was going on. Reed v. Merchants' Mutual Insurance Co., 95 U. S. 23, 24 Law. Ed. 348. 

§ 354a. Subsequent Conduct of the Parties. 

Where the terms of a contract are ambiguous or susceptible of varying interpretations, the subsequent acts of the parties under the contract will be considered as some evidence of what they intended the contract to mean. "There 296

§§ 354a, 355 

is no better way of ascertaining the meaning and construction of a written contract than to look at the acts and conduct of the parties under it." Seymour v. Warren, 179 

N. Y. 1, 71 N. E. 260. See, also, Irving Trust Co. v. Natica, Lady Lister-Kaye, 157 Misc. 32, 284 N. Y. S. 343, and cases therein cited; Restatement, Contracts, sec. 235, subd. (e). 

§ 355. 

Meaning of Words Controlled by Usage. 

Usages, such as those of a trade, are admissible as evidence to show the special meaning of words. Thus, parol evidence is admissible to show the meaning of "on approval" in the diamond trade; "order" in a contract of agency for the sale of books; "for the season" in a contract. of employment; “mill culls" and "shipping culls" in a contract for the sale thereof; "factor" in a contract for the disposition of goods. Smith v. Clews, 114 N. Y. 190, 21 

N. E. 160, 4 L. R. A. 392; Newhall v. Appleton, 114 N. Y. 

140, 21 N. E. 105, 3 L. R. A. 859; Shoyer v. Edmund 

Wright-Ginsberg Co., 240 N. Y. 223, 148 N. E. 328. In like manner, it is competent to prove the meaning of the terms in contracts by telegraph when a cipher code is used. 

Thus, the meaning of "Buy Three May" may be explained to mean 3,000 bushels of May wheat. Without such evidence, a contract in cipher could have no meaning. Carland v. Western Union Telegraph Co., 118 Mich. 369, 76 

N. W. 762, 43 L. R. A. 280; Weld v. Postal Telegraph-Cable 

Co., 199 N. Y. 88, 92 N. E. 415. See, also, Seaver v. Lindsay 

Light Co., 233 N. Y. 273, 135 N. E. 329.

In arriving at the "net profits" of a business, the customary procedure in any given business must be followed in arriving at the amount of such profits. Bolles v. Scheer, 225 

N. Y. 118, 121 N. E. 771. Likewise, in a contract for the delivery of paper of a certain quality, evidence is admissible to show that there was a trade usage as to variation and thickness of the paper. Oswego Falls Pulp & Paper Co. v. 

Stecher Lithograph Co., 215 N. Y. 98, 109 N. E. 92, L. R. A. 

1916B 1257. The Personal Property Law, section 125, sub division 4, expressly provides that, in reference to delivery 297

§§ 355  

of the proper quantity of goods, the statutory provisions contained in such section "are subject to usage of trade, * * * between the parties." Mutual Chemical Co. v. Marden, Orth & Hastings Co., 235 N. Y. 145, 139 N. E. 221. 

§ 356. 

Custom and Usage as Part of Contract. 

With certain well-defined exceptions which are not within the scope of this work, the parol evidence rule excludes proof of prior and contemporaneous negotiations as affecting a written contract. St. Regis Paper Co. v. Hubbs & 

Hastings Paper Co., 235 N. Y. 30, 138 N. E. 495. However, parol evidence of a general custom and usage is admissible. 

This is based upon the theory that such custom is a part of the law of the land, and the law is a part of the contract, as much so as if incorporated therein. Therefore, the existing statutes and the settled common law principles in force at the time and place where the contract is made become a part of the contract and must be read into it. Walls v. 

Bailey, 49 N. Y. 464, 10 Am. Rep. 407; Pennell v. Delta 

Transportation Co., 94 Mich. 247, 53 N. W. 1049; People v. Metropolitan Surety Co., 211 N. Y. 107, 105 N. E. 99;

Armour Packing Co. v. United States, 209 U. S. 56, 52 Law. 

Ed. 681, 28 Sup. Ct. Rep. 428. 

§ 357. 

Distinction between General and Local Custom. 

Where the contract involves a local usage or the usage of a particular trade or profession, the defendant may show that he had no knowledge of the existence of such usage, and thus defeat the presumption that he contracted with reference to it. The authorities on this question are collated and discussed in Walls v. Bailey, 49 N. Y. 464, 10 Am. Rep. 

407; Miller v. Fischer, 142 App. Div. 172, 126 N. Y. S. 996. 

§ 358. Requisites of Custom and Usage. 

The common law itself is based upon customs established by long usage. A universal custom becomes a part of the common law. But to establish and give validity to a cus- 298

§§ 358 

tom as part of a contract, it must be: 

General and uniform; 1. 

2. Not inconsistent with the terms of the contract; 

3. Not opposed to any statute or principle of the common law; 

4. Reasonable. 

Wadley v. Davis, 63 Barb. 500; Hatch v. Luckman, 64 

Misc. 508, 118 N. Y. S. 689, aff'd 155 App. Div. 765, 140 

N. Y. S. 1123.

To illustrate: an alleged custom of stenographers to charge twenty cents per folio for copies of their minutes is not established by evidence that "the custom is to pay from fifteen to twenty cents a folio," since such evidence negatives the requirement of uniformity. Cavanagh v. O'Neill, 20 Misc. 233, 45 N. Y. S. 789. Also, where a contract provides for the delivery of 8,000 barrels of cement, each barrel to weigh 396 pounds gross, evidence of a usage to deliver less than this for a barrel is inadmissible, because it is inconsistent with the terms of the contract. Richard v. Haebler, 36 App. Div. 94, 55 N. Y. S. 583. Likewise, the custom of sawmill owners to keep the culls, clippings, and slabs of their customers' logs which they saw into lumber is unreasonable and will not be sanctioned by the courts, although this custom has been uniformly acquiesced in from time immemorial. Wadley v. Davis, supra. 

§ 359. Matter in Letterheads and Billheads. 

The printed matter in letterheads and billheads, or on the margin of paper on which a contract is written, is not a part of the contract unless referred to in the agreement. 

Sturtevant Co. v. Fireproof Film Co., 216 N. Y. 199, 110 

N. E. 440. 

§ 360. Written and Printed Matter. 

Where part of a contract is written and part is printed, the words in writing will control the construction of the contract. The reason for this rule is that the written words are the immediate language and terms selected by the parties themselves for the expression of their meaning, while 299

§§ 360  

the printed form is intended for general use without reference to particular objects and aims. Hill v. Miller, 76 N. Y. 

32; Heyn v. New York Life Insurance Co., 192 N. Y. 1, 84 N. E. 725; Restatement, Contracts, sec. 236, subd. (e). 

§ 361. 

Grammar.

It is a legal maxim that bad grammar does not vitiate a contract. Therefore, grammatical inaccuracies are immaterial, provided the intention of the parties is clearly indicated by the contract. Castelli v. Burns, 156 App. Div. 200, 140 N. Y. S. 1057. To give effect to the intention of the parties, the word "and" may be read "or." Jackson v. 

Topping, 1 Wend. (N.Y.) 388, 19 Am. Dec. 515. The converse is equally true. The word "or" may be read "and." 

Bettman v. Harness, 42 W. Va. 433, 26 S. E. 271, 36 L. R. A. 

566. In Potthoff v. Safety Armorite Conduit Co., 143 App. 

Div. 161, 127 N. Y. S. 994, the Court said: "To carry out the intention of a contract words may be transposed, rejected or supplied, if necessary, to make its meaning more clear." 

§ 362. 

Punctuation.

The want of proper punctuation will not vitiate a contract. It may be considered when the meaning is doubtful, but it cannot control if the meaning otherwise plainly appears. Kinkele v. Wilson, 151 N. Y. 269, 45 N. E. 869; 

Holmes v. Phoenix Insurance Co., 98 Fed. 240, 47 L. R. A. 308.

In the case of In re Brooklyn Elevated R. R. Co., 125 

N. Y. 434, 26 N. E. 474, the Court transposed a comma in construing a statute, but declared that "in so doing we violate no canon of construction as we think the legislative sense requires it, and such liberty with punctuation is frequently taken by the courts." 

§ 363. 

Construction To Uphold Contract. 

Where a contract is open to two constructions, one legal and the other illegal, the courts will adopt that construc- 300

$$ 363 tion which will impute to the parties a legal intention. 

Powers v. Clarke, 127 N. Y. 417, 28 N. E. 402. "The written undertakings of parties are always to be construed so as to be upheld and enforced, instead of rendering them nugatory, if it can be done without violence to the terms employed." Auburn City Bank v. Leonard, 40 Barb. 119. $364.

Construction When Party Uses Ambiguous Words. 

Ambiguous words will be construed against the party using them. Liverpool & London & Globe Insurance Co. v. 

Kearney, 180 U. S. 132, 45 Law. Ed. 460, 21 Sup. Ct. Rep. 

326. "The reason of the rule that the language of an instrument is to be construed against the person who proposes it rather than against the person who is invited to accept it, is that men are supposed to take care of themselves, and that he who chooses the words by which a right is given, ought to be held to the strict interpretation of them, rather than he who only accepts them." Gillet v. Bank of 

America, 160 N. Y. 549, 55 N. E. 292. See, also, Taylor v. 

United States Casualty Co., 269 N. Y. 360, 199 N. E. 620, 115

A. L. R. 822; Restatement, Contracts, sec. 236, subd. (d). 

§ 365. In General-Time of Performance. 

The time within which a contract is to be performed may or may not be of the essence of the contract. Where the parties have expressly stipulated that time is to be regarded as of the essence of the contract, there is no room for doubt. 

The question becomes important when there is a failure to perform the contract within the agreed time. 

Where time is of the essence of the contract, the failure by one party to perform within the prescribed time discharges the contractual obligation of the other party. But where time is neither expressly nor by implication of the essence of the contract, the failure or inability of one party to perform within the time designated gives the other no right to rescind or abandon the contract but only the right to recover damages in consequence of the delay. As to whether the date set for the closing of the title in the sale of real 301

§§ 365, 366  

property is of the essence of the contract, see Ballen v. 

Potter, 251 N. Y. 224, 167 N. E. 424, and Editorial, New

York Law Journal, September 10, 1937. It is a well-settled rule that the breach of an independent covenant which does not go to the whole consideration of the contract, but is subordinate and incidental to its main purpose, does not entitle the injured party to rescind the contract. The remedy of the injured party in such a case is merely an action for damages sustained by delay in performance. A few hours' delay in the performance of a contract may be most vital; while on the other hand it may be of little or no consequence. For instance, a delay of two hours in making a delivery of programs for an entertainment might defeat the very purpose for which the programs were printed; while a few hours' delay in the delivery of other printed matter would result in no loss. Mercantile National Bank v. Heinze, 75 Misc. 551, 135 N. Y. S. 962; Taylor v. Goelet, 208 N. Y. 253, 101 N. E. 867. A frequently cited English case on this point is Bettini v. Gye, 1 Q. B. Div. 183 (1876).

Bettini entered into a contract with Gye, the director of Italian opera in London, for his services as a singer in operas. He agreed to be in London for rehearsals at least six days before the commencement of his engagement, but arrived only two days before his engagement commenced. 

Gye thereupon repudiated the contract. The Court held, in interpreting the contract, that the provision as to rehearsals was not vital to the contract, and, therefore, was not a condition, but merely a collateral undertaking, and its breach did not operate as a discharge of the contract but gave to Gye merely a cause of action for compensation in damages. 

Holt v. United Security Life Insurance & Trust Co., 76 

N. J. L. 585, 72 Atl. 301, 21 L. R. A. (N.S.) 691 and note. 

§ 366. 

Performance When Time Not Fixed by Contract. 

An implication of law is as much a part of a written contract as if embodied in the contract itself. Therefore, a contract, otherwise complete on its face but silent as to the time of performance, and hence, by implication of law 302

§§ 366 

to be performed within a reasonable time, cannot be varied by oral testimony showing that a definite time of performance was agreed upon by the parties. Stange v. Wilson, 17 

Mich. 342; Morowsky v. Rohrig, 4 Misc. 167, 23 N. Y. S. 

880; Richardson on Evidence, 5th Ed., sec. 423. 

§ 367. Employment Contracts at Will. 

Where an employment contract does not specify the time or duration of the contract of hiring but provides for weekly, monthly, or yearly payments, the New York rule is that the employment is at will and that the employer has the right to discharge, and the employee the right to leave, at any time. For instance, a contract to pay one $2,000 a year for services, or at a salary of $2,000 a year, is not a contract for a year, but a contract to pay at the rate of $2,000 a year for services actually rendered. Watson v. Gugino, 204 N. Y. 535, 98 N. E. 18, 39 L. R. A. (N.S.) 

1090; Cuppy v. Stollwerck Bros., 216 N. Y. 591, 111 N. E. 249. 

§ 368. 

Time Fixed by Extrinsic Act. 

The time of performance may be fixed with reference to the doing of some specified act. But the act is not necessarily a condition precedent to the time of performance. 

It has been held, as a matter of construction, in certain cases, that it merely fixes the time of performance, which is construed to be within a reasonable time. This principle has been applied to a promise to pay "on the sale" of certain property, or “as soon as the crops can be sold." Payment in such cases does not depend upon the happening of the contingency but is made absolute after the lapse of a reasonable time. Simon v. Etgen, 213 N. Y. 589, 107 N. E. 

1066. The weight of authority, however, supports the rule that, where a promise is made by a debtor to pay "as soon as he can," "when able," or "when in his power," the promise is conditional and does not become absolute merely upon the lapse of a reasonable time, and it is incumbent upon the creditor to show that the condition has been fulfilled; in 303

$$ 368

other words, that the debtor is now able to pay. Tebo v. 

Robinson, 100 N. Y. 27, 2 N. E. 383; Benton v. Benton, 78 

Kan. 366, 97 Pac. 378, 27 L. R. A. (N.S.) 300 and note; 49

Harvard Law Review 1000; Editorial, New York Law Jour nal, September 10, 1937. 

§ 369. Waiver of Time of Performance. 

The parties may waive the right to insist upon performance within the time fixed by the contract, and when so waived, the time fixed is no longer an essential element of the contract. Performance is then required only within a reasonable time. But time may be again made the essence by a reasonable notice demanding performance and stating that the contract will be rescinded if the notice is not complied with. Taylor v. Goelet, 208 N. Y. 253, 101 N. E. 867. 

§ 370. Effect of Notice When No Time Fixed for Per formance.

If the time of performance is left indefinite in the contract, neither party can thereafter rescind the contract on account of delay in performance without notice to the other party requiring performance within a reasonable time specified in the notice and stating that the contract will be abrogated in case of non-compliance with such notice. Taylor v. Goelet, 208 N. Y. 253, 101 N. E. 867. 

§ 371. When Time Is Not of Essence of Contract. 

In contracts for the construction of buildings and railroads, and other contracts involving work and labor, time is not generally of the essence of the contract. This is so, even though the contract fixes the time of performance. 

In such cases, the aggrieved party will not be justified in repudiating the entire contract because of delay in performance. His sole remedy is an action for damages for the delay. Thurston v. Arnold, 43 Iowa 43; Beck & Pauli 

Lithographing Co. v. Colorado Milling & Elevator Co., 52 

Fed. 700; Brady v. Oliver, 125 Tenn. 595, 147 S. W. 1135, 41 L. R. A. (N.S.) 60 and note. However, the duty of the 304

$$ 371 landowner to make payments agreed upon as the work progresses generally goes to the essence of the contract. 

The contractor can hardly be expected to finance the operation to completion without receiving the stipulated payments as the work progresses. "In such cases a substantial compliance as to advance payments is a condition precedent to the contractor's obligation to proceed." Guerini Stone 

Co. v. P. J. Carlin Construction Co., 248 U. S. 334, 63 Law. 

Ed. 275, 39 Sup. Ct. Rep. 102. 

§ 372. Time as Essence of Contract in Equity. 

In equity, as a general rule, time is not of the essence of the contract, but it may be made so by express stipulation or by circumstances indicating that the parties require prompt performance at the time stipulated. At law, as a rule, the promisee acquires no interest in the property under an executory contract until he performs or tenders performance; while in equity he acquires an interest in the property when the contract of sale is made. Hence, in the latter, there is less reason for insisting on punctual performance. Phillips & Colby Construction Co. v. Seymour, 91 U. S. 646, 23 Law. Ed. 341; King v. Connors, 222 Mass. 

261, 110 N. E. 289.

DAMAGES § 373. Liquidated Damages. 

When the parties have ascertained and assessed the damages and have embodied the provisions concerning them in the contract itself, the damages are said to be liquidated. “The meaning of the words 'liquidated damages' is of wellunderstood and recognized significance. These words are used in reference to the breach of a contract or the nonperformance of a duty as expressing a fixed sum which is agreed upon between the parties as the ascertained damage which the one is to receive and the other to pay because of the default." United States Fidelity & Guaranty Co. v. 

United States, 151 Fed. 534, aff'd 209 U. S. 306, 52 Law.

Ed. 804, 28 Sup. Ct. Rep. 537. 

305

§§ 374, 375  § 374. 

Unliquidated Damages. 

If the contract is silent as to the damages that shall be assessed in case of its breach, the damages are said to be unliquidated, because the right to ascertain them is not fixed by the parties to the contract but is left to be determined by the court and jury. "A demand is not liquidated even if it appears that something is due, unless it appears how much is due." Nassoiy v. Tomlinson, 148 N. Y. 326, 42 N. E. 715. 

§ 375. Penalty Distinguished from Liquidated Damages. 

The enforcement of a provision in a contract that the party in default shall pay or forfeit to the other party a certain sum of money depends upon whether the sum named is a penalty or liquidated damages. If it is in the nature of a penalty, the courts will not enforce it. The party aggrieved will be entitled to actual damages only. 

But, if the stipulated amount to be paid or forfeited is liquidated damages, it may be recovered. The authorities are in hopeless confusion on the question as to whether a stipulated amount shall be treated as a penalty or as liquidated damages. They furnish "conspicuous examples of judicial efforts to make for parties wiser and more prudent contracts than they had made for themselves." Kemp v. 

Knickerbocker Ice Co., 69 N. Y. 45. See, also, Mosler Safe 

Co. v. Maiden Lane Safe Deposit Co., 199 N. Y. 479, 93 N. E. 

81, 37 L. R. A. (N.S.) 363. 

In Lenco, Inc. v. Hirschfeld, 247 N. Y. 44, 159 N. E. 718, the Court held that security deposited by a tenant for the faithful performance of his lease, which contained a clause providing for the forfeiture of the security as liquidated damages for the breach of any covenant, irrespective of any loss that the landlord might sustain, was a penalty and unenforceable notwithstanding it was denominated by the parties as liquidated damages. 306

§ 

§ 376. 

Rules To Determine Nature of Provision. 

The following rules are resorted to by the courts to assist them in determining whether the sum named is a penalty or liquidated damages: 

1. It is wholly immaterial whether the sum fixed upon is stated in the contract to be a "penalty" or "liquidated damages." If it be designated "liquidated damages" and it is, in fact, a penalty, it will be so regarded notwithstanding its designation. On the other hand, if the parties designate a sum a "penalty," it may be treated as liquidated damages where there is a manifest difficulty in ascertaining the actual damage sustained. Pastor v. Solomon, 26 

Misc. 125, 55 N. Y. S. 956. If the rule were otherwise, the prohibition against a penalty could be readily avoided. 

Ayers v. Houston, 193 App. Div. 145, 183 N. Y. S. 808. 

2. The courts will consider in each case: a. The subject matter; b. The nature of the agreement; and C. 

The intention of the parties.

The intention of the parties is to be gathered from the language used in making the contract, read in the light of circumstances at the time, and if the measure of damages, as agreed upon, is not so excessive as to shock the moral sense of man, the courts will hold the parties to their agreement. Curtis v. Van Berg, 161 N. Y. 47, 55 N. E. 398; 

Ward v. Hudson River Building Co., 125 N. Y. 230, 26 

N. E. 256.

The intention of the parties is the most important criterion. The court is not to be wiser than the parties. As a rule, therefore, when the parties say that a sum is to be payable as liquidated damages, they will be deemed to mean what they say. Guerin v. Stacy, 175 Mass. 595, 56 N. E. 

892. And a disproportion between the sum fixed and the loss is not enough to induce the court to ignore the agreement by which the parties fixed their own estimate of damages. Sun Printing & Publishing Assn. v. Moore, 183 

U. S. 642, 46 Law. Ed. 366, 22 Sup. Ct. Rep. 240; Fleisher 307

§ 

v. Friob, 97 Misc. 343, 161 N. Y. S. 940, aff'd 177 App. Div. 

921, 164 N. Y. S. 1092 

3. If the matter of the contract is of certain value, a sum greatly in excess of that value is likely to be held to be a penalty; if the matter is of uncertain value, the courts are inclined to treat the sum fixed as liquidated damages. "Where an amount, stipulated as liquidated damages, would be grossly in excess of the actual damages, they [the courts] have leaned to hold it a penalty. Where the actual damages were uncertain and difficult of ascertainment, they have leaned to hold the stipulated amount to have been intended as liquidated damages." Kemp v. Knickerbocker Ice Co., 69 N. Y. 45. See, also, Chicago, Burlington & Quincy R. R.

Co. v. Dockery, 195 Fed. 221. In accordance with this principle, where a greater sum of money is to be paid out upon the failure to pay a lesser sum with interest, it is construed to be a penalty. Legal interest on the money is all that the law allows the plaintiff to recover as damages. London v. 

Taxing District, 104 U. S. 771, 26 Law. Ed. 923; Niver v. 

Rossman, 18 Barb. 50. 

4. If some of the terms of a contract are of certain value and some are not, and a gross sum is to be paid in the event of a breach of any one of them, it will be regarded as a penalty. Niver v. Rossman, supra; Chicago, Burlington & Quincy R. R. Co. v. Dockery, supra; Seidlitz v. Auerbach, 230 N. Y. 167, 129 N. E. 461. 

5. When it is provided that a sum deposited in part performance of a contract is to be forfeited upon failure of the other party to complete it, if the sum is excessive, it is a penalty. Whether it be a penalty or liquidated damages is governed by the same general rules that control in other cases. Chaude v. Shepard, 122 N. Y. 397, 25 N. E. 358.

However, in the case of executory contracts for the sale of real or personal property, the buyer cannot recover a partial payment which he has made if the seller is not in default and the buyer fails to complete his payments. Chaude v. Shepard, supra; Beveridge v. West Side Construction 

Co., 130 App. Div. 139, 114 N. Y. S. 521. 308

$$ 376 6. A stipulation for the payment of a sum of money for the breach of a contract in restraint of trade is generally held to be liquidated damages, because the damage in such a case is wholly uncertain and incapable of being determined except by conjecture. Tode v. Gross, 127 N. Y. 480, 28 N. E. 469, 13 L. R. A. 652. "A more suitable case, for the liquidation of the damages by the parties themselves, can scarcely be imagined." Nobles v. Bates, 7 Cowen (N.Y.) 307. 

7. The damages for non-acceptance or refusal to deliver merchandise or personal property are, ordinarily, the difference between the contract price and the market price at the time and place of delivery. Therefore, if the parties agree that the buyer shall pay, as liquidated damages, a sum greatly in excess of such an amount, it will be regarded as a penalty. Tufts v. Bennett, 163 Mass. 398, 40 N. E. 172; 

Bridgford v. Crocker, 60 N. Y. 627. 

§ 377. Effect of Determination. 

Where the sum agreed upon for the breach of a contract is therein termed liquidated damages, and is such in reality, the plaintiff is limited to the stipulated amount, even though his actual loss is much greater. The stipulation is mutually obligatory. Winch v. Mutual Benefit Ice Co., 86 N. Y. 

618. On the other hand, if the sum stipulated is held to be a penalty, it is binding on neither party. In such cases, the plaintiff may recover more than the sum stipulated. “Parties are not released from performing their agreement by inserting a penalty for non-performance." Noyes v. Phillips, 60 N. Y. 408. 

§ 378. 

Damages-Personal Property Law. 

The measure of damages in an action involving a breach of contract to sell personal property is now regulated by statute. The Personal Property Law, section 144, provides: 

"1. Where, under a contract to sell or a sale, the property in the goods has passed to the buyer, and the buyer wrongfully neglects or refuses to pay for the goods accord- 309

§ 

ing to the terms of the contract or the sale, the seller may maintain an action against him for the price of the goods. "2. Where, under a contract to sell or a sale, the price is payable on a day certain, irrespective of delivery .or of transfer of title, and the buyer wrongfully neglects or refuses to pay such price, the seller may maintain an action for the price, although the property in the goods has not passed, and the goods have not been appropriated to the contract. But it shall be a defense to such an action that the seller at any time before judgment in such action has manifested an inability to perform the contract or the sale on his part or intention not to perform it. "3. Although the property in the goods has not passed, if they cannot readily be resold for a reasonable price, and if the provisions of subdivision four of section one hundred and forty-five are not applicable, the seller may offer to deliver the goods to the buyer, and if the buyer refuses to receive them, may notify the buyer that the goods are thereafter held by the seller as bailee for the buyer. Thereafter the seller may treat the goods as the buyer's and may maintain an action for the price."

The Personal Property Law, section 145, provides: 

"1. Where the buyer wrongfully neglects or refuses to accept and pay for the goods, the seller may maintain an action against him for damages for nonacceptance. "2. The measure of damages is the estimated loss directly and naturally resulting, in the ordinary course of events, from the buyer's breach of contract. "3. Where there is an available market for the goods in question, the measure of damages is, in the absence of special circumstances, showing proximate damage of a greater amount, the difference between the contract price and the market or current price at the time or times when the goods ought to have been accepted, or if no time was fixed for acceptance, then at the time of the refusal to accept. "4. If, while labor or expense of material amount are necessary on the part of the seller to enable him to fulfill 310

§§ 378, 379 

his obligations under the contract to sell or the sale, the buyer repudiates the contract or the sale, or notifies the seller to proceed no further therewith, the buyer shall be liable to the seller for no greater damages than the seller would have suffered if he did nothing toward carrying out the contract or the sale after receiving notice of the buyer's repudiation or countermand. The profit the seller would have made if the contract or the sale had been fully performed shall be considered in estimating such damages." 

The Personal Property Law, section 148, provides: 

"1. Where the property in the goods has not passed to the buyer, and the seller wrongfully neglects or refuses to deliver the goods, the buyer may maintain an action against the seller for damages for nondelivery. "2. The measure of damages is the loss directly and naturally resulting in the ordinary course of events from the seller's breach of contract. "3. Where there is an available market for the goods in question, the measure of damages, in the absence of special circumstances showing proximate damages of a greater amount, is the difference between the contract price and the market or current price of the goods at the time or times when they ought to have been delivered, or, if no time was fixed, then at the time of the refusal to deliver." § 379.

Damages-Real Property. 

There is a conflict of authority in this country as to the measure of damages in an action by a prospective buyer for the breach of a contract for the sale of real estate. The 

United States Supreme Court allows the same measure of damages in such an action as in actions involving the sale of personal property. Hopkins v. Lee, 6 Wheat. (U.S.) 

109, 5 Law. Ed. 218.

However, in New York, the rule is that if the seller has acted in good faith and is unable to give the buyer a good title to the land, owing to a defect in the title thereto, the buyer cannot recover substantial damages. Pumpelly v. 311

§ 

Phelps, 40 N. Y. 59, 100 Am. Dec. 463; Margraf v. Muir, 57 N. Y. 155.

In case of breach by the buyer, where the seller is able and willing to give the buyer a good title to the real estate, the measure of damages is the same as in actions involving personal property; namely, the difference between the contract price and the market value of the land. Stevenson v. 

Maxwell, 2 N. Y. 408. The seller cannot recover the full purchase price. Bensinger v. Erhardt, 74 App. Div. 169, 77 N. Y. S. 577. If the defaulting vendee had paid part of the purchase price prior to his breach of the contract, the sum paid must be deducted from the amount of damages awarded to the vendor. Hayden v. Pinchot, 172 App. Div. 

102, 158 N. Y. S. 215. 312

CHAPTER XVIII DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY OPERATION OF LAW § 380. 

Modes of Discharge.

The term "discharge of contract" relates to the modes by which the contractual tie may be severed and the parties wholly freed from their obligations. The term also includes the method of discharging a right of action resulting from a breach of a contract. The various modes of discharging a contract may be classified as follows: 

1. By operation of law; 

2. By agreement; 

3. By performance; 

4. By breach. 

§ 381. By Operation of Law. 

The term "discharge by operation of law" includes the following methods of discharge: 

1. By impossibility of performance; 

2. By merger; 

3. By alteration of instrument; 

4. By bankruptcy. 

§ 382. By Impossibility of Performance. 

When a person seeks to avoid a contract on the ground that its performance is impossible, it becomes necessary to distinguish between an impossibility of performance existing at the time the contract was made and an impossibility arising subsequent to the making thereof. 

Impossibility of performance existing at the time of making the contract may be divided into: 1. 

Objective impossibility: Physical; a. b. Legal. 

2. Subjective impossibility. 

313

§§ 383, 384  § 383. Existing Impossibility-Objective-Physical. 

Where the performance of a promise is impossible because of facts existing at the time the promise was made and of which neither party had knowledge, performance is excused. Thus, a contract for the sale of an article which, unknown to either party, has ceased to exist creates no duty of performance. A contract made under such circumstances is, of course, also void on the ground of mistake preventing consent. Uniform Sales Act, sec. 7; Personal

Property Law, sec. 88; Duncan v. New York Mutual Insurance Co., 138 N. Y. 88, 33 N. E. 730, 20 L. R. A. 386.

Where the defendant agreed to mine, within a certain tract of land, not less than 1,500 tons of ore annually and to pay therefor thirty five cents per ton, and it was later found that there was not so much ore in the land, the Court held that, since the parties were mutually mistaken as to the quantity of ore in the land, the contractual obligation ceased because of an impossibility of performance that existed at the time the contract was made. Muhlenberg v. 

Henning, 116 Pa. 138, 9 Atl. 144. 

Similarly, in Lord Clifford v. Watts, 5 C. P. (Eng.) 577 (1870), where the defendant, a lessee, covenanted to dig from the land in question not less than 1,000 tons of potter's clay annually, it was held that the fact that there was not so much as 1,000 tons of clay in the land was a good defense to an action for breach of the covenant. Likewise, where the performance of a contract for the excavation of a canal was rendered impossible by the condition of the subsoil, the contractors were excused from performance. 

Kinser Construction Co. v. State of New York, 204 N. Y. 

381, 97 N. E. 871; Virginia Iron Coal & Coke Co. v. Graham, 124 Va. 692, 98 S. E. 659. See, also, Clarksville Land Co. v. Harriman, 68 N. H. 374, 44 Atl. 527. 

§ 384. Existing Impossibility—Objective-Legal. 

A promise to do something which the law forbids cannot be enforced. However, it is well settled that an unmarried woman may recover damages from a married man for 314

DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY OPERATION OF LAW §§ 384-386 

1 breach of a contract to marry, provided she had no knowledge of the promisor's existing marriage at the time of the alleged engagement. One theory of liability in such a case is that the defendant impliedly warrants that he has the legal capacity to make the contract in question. Waddell v. 

Wallace, 32 Okla. 140, 121 Pac. 245. The position has also been taken that the basis of liability in such cases is the fraud practiced on the plaintiff, giving rise to liability in tort. Cammerer v. Muller, 60 Hun 578, 14 N. Y. S. 511, aff'd 133 N. Y. 623, 30 N. E. 1147. It should be noted that in New York, as well as some other states, the right of action to recover damages for breach of contract to marry has been abolished by statute. Civil Practice Act, sec. 61-b. § 385.

Subjective Impossibility. 

A subjective impossibility is one that is wholly due to the inability of the individual promisor. Performance is impossible for him, but not for others. It neither prevents the formation of a contract nor excuses performance thereunder. An application of this rule arises where a debtor promises to pay a sum of money which he neither has nor can procure. Money exists everywhere, and it is merely the present situation of the debtor that prevents him from procuring it. His individual inability is no excuse for nonpayment. Similarly, that the lessee has met with financial reverses is no excuse for not paying rent. This must be deemed to result from his own improvidence. Taylor v. 

Syme, 162 N. Y. 513, 57 N. E. 83. 

§ 386. Subsequent Impossibility-Common Law Rule. 

Impossibility of performance which arises subsequent to the formation of a contract does not, as a rule, excuse its performance. "When a party by his own contract creates a duty or charge upon himself, he is bound to a possible performance of it, because he promised it, and did not shield himself by proper conditions or qualifications." 

Cameron-Hawn Realty Co. v. City of Albany, 207 N. Y. 377, 101 N. E. 162, 49 L. R. A. (N.S.) 922; Strochak v. Glass 315

§ 

 Paper Making Supplies Co., 239 App. Div. 312, 267 N. Y. S. 

282. 

It is necessary to distinguish between cases in which a duty is imposed by law and those in which a duty is imposed by contract. An act of God will excuse non-performance of a duty imposed by law, but not one imposed by contract; for where a party, by his own contract, creates a duty or charge upon himself, he is bound to make it good, notwithstanding any accident or delay by an act of God, as provision for such contingency might have been embodied in the contract. Anderson v. May, 50 Minn. 280, 52 N. W. 530, 17 L. R. A. 555; Tompkins v. Dudley, 25 

N. Y. 272, 82 Am. Dec. 349; Runyan v. Culver, 168 Ky. 

45, 181 S. W. 640, L. R. A. 1916F 3 and note; Maidment v. 

Kraise Milling Co., 225 App. Div. 492, 233 N. Y. S. 621.

The following may serve as illustrations of this doctrine: 

A person who agreed to ship three hundred barrels of apples at a certain time was not excused from a performance of his contract because the harbor froze and thus prevented the sailing of the ship. Kribbs v. Jones, 44 Md. 396.

Performance of a contract to build a house for another within a certain time was not excused by the fact that there was a latent defect in the soil, in consequence of which the walls sank and cracked and had to be taken down and rebuilt on artificial foundations. Dermott v. Jones, 2 Wall. 

(U.S.) 1, 17 Law. Ed. 762. 

Where one agreed to build a schoolhouse by a certain time, he was held not to be relieved from liability on the contract because of the fact that, when nearly completed, the building was destroyed by lightning. School District 

No. 1 v. Dauchy, 25 Conn. 530, 68 Am. Dec. 371.

Failure to perform a contract to dig a well was held not to be excused by accidental injury to the tools of the contractor. Janes v. Scott, 59 Pa. 178, 98 Am. Dec. 328.

An agreement to carry goods from B to X within twentysix days was not discharged by the fact that the canal upon which they were to be transported was made impassable 316

DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY OPERATION OF LAW §§ 386-388 

by a freshet. Harmony v. Bingham, 12 N. Y. 99, 62 Am. 

Dec. 142. 

§ 387. Subsequent Impossibility-Implied Conditions or 

Exceptions.

The modern decisions reveal a tendency to extend the "exceptions"-not as "exceptions" but as implied conditions of the contract—to the rule that a subsequent impossibility will not discharge a contract. In New York, the courts invariably refer to the "exceptions" as implied conditions.

Thus, where performance of a contract is rendered legally impossible by a change in domestic law, the promisor is excused from his duty of performance on the ground that there was an implied condition in the contract that such change in the law should discharge the parties from further liability. Dexter v. Norton, 47 N. Y. 62; Cameron-Hawn 

Realty Co. v. City of Albany, 207 N. Y. 377, 101 N. E. 162, 49 L. R. A. (N.S.) 922.

An implied condition is a legal fiction based upon purely equitable principles; it does not rest upon the actual intent of the parties. The danger inherent in too great a relaxation of the harsh common law rule was noted by the Court in Retail Merchant's Business Expansion Co. v. Randall, 103 Vt. 268, 153 Atl. 357, wherein it was said: "While it is undoubtedly true that the tendency of the law is toward the enlargement of the defense of impossibility, such process should be regarded with great caution since there is danger that courts, in their desire to relieve parties in hard cases, may go too far. The province of courts is to construe and enforce contracts, not to make or modify them. 

Nor will it be assumed that the people of the present generation are less competent to make their own contracts than were the people of former generations." § 388.

Classification of Implied Conditions. 

Implied conditions may be classified as follows: 

1. Continued existence of the law governing the con- 317

§§ 388, 389  

tract and in force at the time the contract was made: a. Of our own state or country; or b. Of a foreign country. 

2. Continued existence of the specific thing or condition in reference to which the contract is made. 

3. Continued health or life on the part of one who has contracted to render some personal service.

In a well-considered opinion the New York Court of 

Claims in Kinser Construction Co. v. State of New York, 

125 N. Y. S. 46, aff'd 204 N. Y. 381, 97 N. E. 871, makes a fourth classification as follows: "Where conditions essential to performance do not exist." The facts in this case show that the impossibility of performance existed at the time of making the contract, and the case should have been determined upon the principle of law governing such cases and not on the ground of an exception to the subsequent impossibility rule. 

§ 389. Continued Existence of the Laws of Our Own State or Country. 

One of the implied conditions in a contract is that the promisor shall not be compelled to perform if performance is subsequently prohibited by law or governmental authority. Therefore, a legal impossibility, arising from a change of the law of our own state or country or from the exercise of governmental authority wholly terminates the contract. American Mercantile Exchange v. Blunt, 102 Me. 

128, 66 Atl. 212, 10 L. R. A. (N.S.) 414 and note; Panto v. 

Kentucky Distilleries & Warehouse Co., 215 App. Div. 511, 214 N. Y. S. 19; People v. Globe Mutual Life Insurance Co., 

91 N. Y. 174; 6 L. R. A. (N.S.) 809, note. For instance, a contract between a school teacher and the school directors is discharged by an order of the board of health directing the closing of the school. Likewise, a city ordinance prohibiting the erection of wooden buildings will discharge the promisor from his obligations to erect such buildings. 

School District No. 16 v. Howard, 5 Neb. 340, 98 N. W. 666; 318

DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY OPERATION OF LAW §§ 389, 390 

Bown Bros., Inc. v. Merchants' Bank of Rochester, 243 N. Y. 

366, 153 N. E. 493.

The effect of a prohibitory law on a lease for a saloon is subject to controversy. In Heart v. East Tennessee 

Brewing Co., 121 Tenn. 69, 113 S. W. 364, 19 L. R. A. (N.S.) 964 and note, the Court held that the enactment during the term of the lease of a law forbidding the sale of intoxicating liquors, terminated the lease. The weight of authority is apparently to the contrary. Goodrum Tobacco

Co. v. Potts-Thompson Liquor Co., 133 Ga. 776, 66 S. E. 

1081, 26 L. R. A. (N.S.) 498; L. R. A. 1916F 66, note.

Much depends on the wording of the lease. If the lease allows the premises to be used for saloon purposes only, there is reason for holding that the lease is terminated with the enactment of prohibitory laws. The Stratford, Inc. v. 

Seattle Brewing & Malting Co., 94 Wash. 125, 162 Pac. 

31, L. R. A. 1917C 931 and note; Palombi v. Volpe, 249 

N. Y. 194, 163 N. E. 607. 

§ 390. 

Performance Affected by Imprisonment.

Where the contractual obligation is one which in its nature continues, notwithstanding death or illness, the imprisonment of one of the parties is no excuse for nonperformance. But where the contract is for personal service, the imprisonment of one of the parties has been held to discharge the contractual obligation. But the better rule, supported by reason and the weight of authority, is that the imprisonment of one guilty of an offense will not excuse performance of such a contract. His imprisonment effects an abandonment or repudiation of the contract on his part. Jinnings v. Amend, 101 Kan. 130, 165 Pac. 845, L. R. A. 1917F 626 and note. However, as a matter of construction, an employee is not guilty of quitting his employment "voluntarily" where his absence from work is due to his arrest and imprisonment. Kowalski v. McAdoo, 93 N. J. L. 340, 107 Atl. 477, aff'd 96 N. J. L. 293, 114 Atl. 927. 319

$$ 391, 392

 § 391. Continued Existence of the Laws of a Foreign State or Country. 

When performance of a contract becomes impossible because of a change in the law of another state or country, the seemingly harsh rule has been laid down that the promisor is not excused on that account. Furness, Withy & Co. 

v. Rederiaktiegolabet Banco, 2 K. B. Div. (Eng.) 873 

(1917). Thus, in Tweedie Trading Co. v. James P. McDonald Co., 114 Fed. 985, the libelant entered into a contract in the United States to make four trips with its steamship from Barbadoes to Colon to transport laborers; but after two trips had been made a regulation of the colonial government of Barbadoes was promulgated forbidding the future embarkation of laborers. By reason of this, the defendant was unable to furnish further transportation, and it was held that such embargo did not constitute a defense to an action to recover the hire of the ship for the remaining voyages at the contract price. So, too, impossibility of performance due to a foreign war does not excuse non-per formance of a contract. Richards & Co., Inc. v. Wreschner, 

174 App. Div. 484, 156 N. Y. S. 1054. 

§ 392. Continued Existence of Specific Thing or Con dition.

Where, by the terms of the contract, the continued existence of a specific thing or condition is essential to the performance of the contract, the destruction or non-existence of the thing or condition, without fault of either party, operates as a discharge of the contractual obligation. This rule of law is based upon the theory that the parties impliedly agreed that in case the specific thing should perish, they would be excused from the performance of their contract. Runyan v. Culver, 168 Ky. 45, 181 S. W. 640, L. R. 

A. 1916F 3 and note; Matousek v. Galligan, 104 Neb. 731, 178 N. W. 510, 12 A. L. R. 1270 and note.

A leading case on this subject is one in which the defendant had agreed to let the plaintiff have the use of a music hall for the purpose of giving concerts upon certain 320

DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY OPERATION OF LAW 

§ 392 days. Before performance of the contract the hall was destroyed by fire. It was held that the defendant was not liable for breach of contract. Taylor v. Coldwell, 3 Best & Smith 826, 122 Eng. Rep. 309. So, too, where the contract is for the sale of specific goods, which, without the seller's fault, perish before the day of delivery, the seller is excused from his obligation to deliver, and the buyer from his obligation to pay. Uniform Sales Act, sec. 7; Personal 

Property Law, sec. 88; Dexter v. Norton, 47 N. Y. 62; 

Stewart v. Stone, 127 N. Y. 500, 28 N. E. 595, 14 L. R. A. 215. 

The following are illustrations of the application of the same principle:

A breach of a contract to supply a hotel with water from a certain spring is excused by the drying up of the spring. 

Ward v. Vance, 93 Pa. 499.

A contract to sell two hundred tons of potatoes to be grown on the promisor's land, and not two hundred tons of potatoes generally, becomes unenforceable if the crop fails by reason of a blight. In this case, the Court said: "There was an implied term in the contract that each party should be free if the crop perished." Howell v. Coupland, 9 Q. B. Div. (Eng.) 462 (1774). 

Performance of a contract for the sale and delivery of wood to be cut from trees on a particular tract of land is excused when, without fault of the vendor, the trees are destroyed by fire. International Paper Co. v. Rockefeller, 161 App. Div. 180, 146 N. Y. S. 371.

A contract for a term of years to conduct a particular line of business in a building which was occupied by a department store was held to be terminated by the destruction of the building.
