The promisee, therefore, had no right to an allotment of space in the new building to which the department store was moved. Martin Emerich Outfitting 

Co. v. Siegel, Cooper & Co., 237 Ill. 610, 86 N. E. 1104, 20 

L. R. A. (N.S.) 1114.

Where a contract is entered into on the assumption by both parties that a particular event or state of things will 321

§ 

occur, the non-occurrence of the event or state of things, without the fault of either party, discharges the contractual liability. This proposition of law finds application in contracts for renting window or other space from which to view a procession, race, or contest which is abandoned by reason of circumstances over which the parties have no control. Alfred Marks Realty Co. v. Hotel Hermitage Co., 

170 App. Div. 484, 156 N. Y. S. 179; Chandler v. Webster, 1 K. B. Div. (Eng.) 493 (1904). 

If a party agrees to deliver a certain number of bales of cotton to another, and the parties contemplate that the cotton shall be that raised on a certain farm, the farmer may excuse himself from liability if a drought occurs and no cotton is produced in consequence thereof. But whether or not the contract involves the production on a particular tract of land is often a close question of construction. Davis v. Bishop, 139 Ark. 273, 213 S. W. 744. If an agreement for the sale of milk is not made with reference to the milk produced on a certain farm, it is no defense for the seller to show that his cows were quarantined and killed. 

Whitman v. Anglum, 92 Conn. 392, 103 Atl. 114.

One court has gone so far as to hold that "a thing is impossible in legal contemplation when it is not practicable; and a thing is impracticable when it can only be done at an excessive and unreasonable cost." Mineral Park 

Land Co. v. Howard, 172 Cal. 289, 156 Pac. 458, L. R. A. 

1916F 1. See, also, Bliss Co. Claim (Matter of Casualty 

Co.), 250 N. Y. 410, 165 N. E. 829.

Eight months after the entrance of the United States in the World War, the defendant contracted with the United States government to erect a post office building. Thereafter, the activities of the government in conscripting men, taking control of transportation, etc., made performance of the contract difficult or impossible. Was the defendant thereby discharged from liability under the contract? Maxwell v. United States, 3 Fed. (2d) 906, aff'd 271 U. S. 647, 70 Law. Ed. 1130, 46 Sup. Ct. Rep. 487. 322

DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY OPERATION OF LAW 

§ 393 

§ 393. Covenant To Repair Building or Chattel. 

One who contracts to erect a building upon the land of another assumes the risk of the accidental destruction of the building before its completion, in the absence of a provision in the contract providing for such a contingency. Moreover, the accidental destruction of the building does not discharge the contractor's liability, for even an act of God will not excuse the performance of a contractual obligation. 

Tompkins v. Dudley, 25 N. Y. 272, 82 Am. Dec. 349; Butterfield v. Byron, 153 Mass. 517, 27 N. E. 667, 12 L. R. A. 

571. In such case the contractor not only cannot recover for the work and materials furnished but is liable for damages for his failure to perform. Tompkins v. Dudley, supra; 

Beechwood Gun Club, Inc. v. City of Beacon, 153 Misc. 358, 275 N. Y. S. 249, aff'd 242 App. Div. 761, 275 N. Y. S. 219.

However, where one agrees to make repairs or do any other work on a building already constructed and owned by another, and performance of the contract becomes impossible because of the destruction of the building without fault on his part, a conflict of authority exists as to whether or not he may recover in quasi-contract for that part of the work which was completed. The general rule in this country is that the contractor may recover because the owner is under an implied obligation to keep the building in readiness to receive the labor and material. Carroll v. Bowersock, 100 

Kan. 270, 164 Pac. 143, L. R. A. 1917D 1006 and note; 

Angus v. Scully, 176 Mass. 357, 57 N. E. 674, 49 L. R. A. 

562. See, also, Editorial, New York Law Journal, November 18, 1931. However, in England and a number of states, a recovery has been denied in such cases. Appleby v. 

Meyers, 2 C. P. (Eng.) 651 (1867); Krause v. Crothersville, 162 Ind. 278, 70 N. E. 264, 65 L. R. A. 111; Taulbee v. 

McCarty, 144 Ky. 199, 137 S. W. 1045, 36 L. R. A. (N.S.) 

43. In New York, recovery has been allowed on the contract for work completed, and in quasi-contract for the work partly completed. Whelan v. Ansonia Clock Co., 97 N. Y. 293. 323

§§ 394, 395  § 394. 

Continued Health or Life of Party. 

When the performance of a contract depends upon the continued health or life of a particular person, his death or such illness as makes performance by him impossible effects a discharge of his contractual liability. Thus, a contract which has for its object the rendering of personal service is discharged by the death or incapacitating illness of the promisor. In contracts for personal service, there is an implied condition that, should one of the parties become disabled through sickness or death, the obligation to perform is extinguished. This rule is applied to skilled, as well as to unskilled, labor. Spalding v. Rossa, 71 N. Y. 

40, 27 Am. Rep. 7; Lacy v. Getman, 119 N. Y. 109, 23 

N. E. 452, 6 L. R. A. 728; Mendenhall v. Davis, 52 Wash. 

169, 100 Pac. 336, 21 L. R. A. (N.S.) 914 and note; L. R. A. 

1916F 79, note.

An interesting case involving this principle is Brearton v. De Witt, 252 N. Y. 495, 170 N. E. 119, where the plaintiff agreed to isolate herself and subject herself to certain experimental treatments by defendant's testator, a physician, and the testator promised to pay the plaintiff $1,000 monthly during her life. In an action on the contract against the testator's estate, the Court of Appeals, reversing the Appellate Division, held that the performance of the promise to make monthly payments did not depend upon the continued existence of the testator and, hence, that the contract survived his death. 

§ 395. 

Illness of Employee-Right of Employer To Terminate Contract. 

When the employee's illness is of more than a temporary nature, the employer may rescind the contract; if he does not do so, but takes the employee back, it has been held that he can make no deduction from the wages of the employee for the lost time. Rubin v. International Film Co., 122 Misc. 413, 204 N. Y. S. 81. 

In Gaynor v. Jonas, 104 App. Div. 35, 93 N. Y. S. 287, it was held that illness, to justify the dismissal of an em- 324

DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY OPERATION OF LAW §§ 395-397 

ployee, must be more than a mere temporary malady; it must be a serious sickness, lasting or likely to last so long as to interfere substantially with the interests of the employer. It was specifically decided that, under a contract period for three months, illness of the employee for a day and a half did not entitle the employer to discharge him and that to do so rendered the master liable to an action for breach of the contract. The rule is that an employer is not bound to wait an unreasonable time for the recovery of a servant incapacitated by illness, before terminating the contract. What is an unreasonable time is generally a question of fact. The authorities are collated in Rubin v. 

International Film Co., supra. § 396. 

Dissolution of Corporation.

Where a corporation was a party to a contract, the question has arisen as to whether the dissolution of the corporation is to be treated in the same way as the death of a natural person. The rule that where the performance of a contract depends upon the continued existence of a person the death of the person terminates the obligation has been applied in the case of the involuntary dissolution of a corporation. Lorillard v. Clyde, 142 N. Y. 456, 37 N. E. 489, 24 L. R. A. 113. However, the tendency of modern authority is to hold the corporation for future obligations, notwithstanding its dissolution, and to give the other party a right to damages for the failure of the corporation to perform its part of the contract. In re Mullings Clothing 

Co., 238 Fed. 58, L. R. A. 1918A 539; Huber v. Martin, 127 Wis. 412, 105 N. W. 1031, 3 L. R. A. (N.S.) 653. § 397. 

By Merger.

The term "merger" refers to the absorption of a thing of lesser importance by a greater, whereby the lesser ceases to exist, but the greater is not increased. Thus, where an obligation arising under a contract is reduced to judgment, or a simple or oral contract is put in the form of a contract under seal or a written contract, respectively, the 325

§§ 397, 398  

prior obligation is merged in the new one. For example, a simple contract for the sale of real property is merged in a subsequent deed executed and delivered in full performance of the contract, and such deed supersedes the contract. 

Schoonmaker v. Hoyt, 148 N. Y. 425, 42 N. E. 1059. Likewise, a written contract merges all prior preliminary negotiations. Societa Italiana Di Beneficenza v. Sulzer, 138 

N. Y. 468, 34 N. E. 193. Whether or not there has been a merger may be determined from the intention of the parties. 

Van Vliet v. Jones, 20 N. J. L. 340, 43 Am. Dec. 633. 

§ 398. By Alteration. 

An instrument is altered when its meaning or language is changed, either in a material or immaterial particular. 

A material alteration changes the legal effect of the instrument; an immaterial one does not. The term alteration without any qualifications generally means, however, a ma terial alteration. Bull Remedy Co. v. Clark, 109 Minn. 396, 

124 N. W. 20, 32 L. R. A. (N.S.) 519; Ryan v. First National Bank, 148 Ill. 349, 35 N. E. 1120.

The following are material alterations: a change in the parties to the instrument; the addition or mutilation of a seal where the same is of some legal effect; a change in the date of an instrument; a change in the amount of money called for by the instrument, even though the amount be decreased; a change in the rate of interest, even though the amount be diminished; and a change in the place of performance. Farmers' Loan & Trust Co. v. Siefke, 144 

N. Y. 354, 39 N. E. 358; Wood v. Steele, 6 Wall. (U.S.) 80, 18 Law. Ed. 725. The following are immaterial altera tions: inserting the rate of interest which the law allows; a change in the Christian name of the party; and an alteration in printed figures which does not affect the obligation. First National Bank v. Carson, 60 Mich. 432, 27 N. W. 589; Lombardo v. Lombardini, 57 Wash. 352, 106 

Pac. 907, 32 L. R. A. (N.S.) 515.

If a deed or contract in writing is materially altered by 326

DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY OPERATION OF LAW §§ 398, 399 

addition or erasure, it is discharged, provided the alteration is made: 

1. By a party to the contract or by a stranger with the party's consent. Alteration by a stranger without authority is a mere spoliation and does not discharge the obligation. Gleason v. Hamilton, 138 N. Y. 353, 34 N. E. 283, 21 

L. R. A. 210; Martin v. Tradesmen's Insurance Co., 101 

N. Y. 498, 5 N. E. 338. 

2. Intentionally. Alteration by mistake or accident has no effect. Van Brunt v. Eoff, 35 Barb. 501; 32 L. R. A. (N.S.) 517, note. 

3. Without consent of the other party. Booth v. Powers, 56 N. Y. 22. 

§ 399. 

Effect of Material Alteration. 

A material alteration, innocently or fraudulently made, destroys the instrument. If fraudulently made, it discharges the original debt as well; if, however, the material alteration is innocently made, a recovery can be had on the original debt or obligation. 

Thus, where a note which is secured by a mortgage is fraudulently altered, the mortgage as well as the note is discharged. Walton Plow Co. v. Campbell, 35 Neb. 173, 52 N. W. 883, 16 L. R. A. 468. One reason for the rule is that the instrument as altered has lost its identity and is no longer the same. To allow one who has fraudulently altered a contract to recover upon it is said to be a departure from the just principle that no one shall be permitted to take the chance of committing a fraud, without incurring the risk of losing by the act when it is detected. Meyer v. Huneke, 55 N. Y. 412; Booth v. Powers, 56 N. Y. 22. But if the alteration is without fraudulent intent, an action will lie upon the original consideration. Edington v. McLeod, 87 

Kan. 426, 124 Pac. 163, 41 L. R. A. (N.S.) 230 and note.

A different rule applies in the case of a material alteration on a negotiable instrument where the instrument, as altered, has passed into the hands of a holder in due course. 

The Negotiable Instruments Law, section 205, provides 327

§§ 399  

that, notwithstanding the alteration, the holder in due course may recover according to the original tenor of the instrument. 

§ 400. Lost Instrument. 

The loss of an instrument affects the parties only in so far as it occasions difficulty of proof. In the case of negotiable instruments, before the owner of lost paper can recover, he may be required to give a bond of indemnity which will secure the maker against loss. Civil Practice 

Act, sec. 333; Church v. Stevens, 56 Misc. 572, 107 N. Y. S. 

310. However, there are instances wherein such provisions appear to be ineffective. Thus, where an agent is authorized to pay interest coupons only upon their surrender, it has been held to be a breach of agency to pay upon proof that the coupons were lost and upon the giving of an indemnity bond. Erb v. Banco di Napoli, 243 N. Y. 45, 152 N. E. 

460, 50 A. L. R. 1009. 

§ 401. By Bankruptcy. 

*. 

The Bankruptcy Act, section 17 (11 United States Code Annotated, section 35), provides as follows: "A 

discharge in bankruptcy shall release a bankrupt from all of his provable debts, except such as (first) are due as a tax ***; (second) are liabilities for obtaining property by false pretenses or false representations, or for willful and malicious injuries to the person or property of another, or for alimony due or to become due, or for maintenance or support of wife or child, or for seduction of an unmarried female, or for breach of promise of marriage accompanied by seduction, or for criminal conversation; (third) have not been duly scheduled in time for proof and allowance, with the name of the creditor, if known to the bankrupt, unless such creditor had notice or actual knowledge of the proceedings in bankruptcy; or (fourth) were created by his fraud, embezzlement, misappropriation, or defalcation while acting as an officer or in any fiduciary capacity; or (fifth) are for wages due to workmen, clerks, *** sales- 328

DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY OPERATION OF LAW § 401 

men, or servants, which have been earned within three months before the date of commencement of the proceedings in bankruptcy; or (sixth) are due for moneys of an employee received or retained by his employer to secure the faithful performance*** of a contract of employment." 329

CHAPTER XIX DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY AGREEMENT 

$ 402.

Forms of Discharge by Agreement. 

The discharge of a contract by agreement will assume one of three forms: Rescission-abandonment; 1. 

2. Substituted contract-novation; or 

3. Discharge in accordance with a provision in the original contract. 

§ 403. 

Rescission-Abandonment. 

A bilateral contract may be discharged or rescinded by mutual consent so long as each party thereto remains subject to some duty to the other. The consideration to support the promise to abandon the contract is the mutual promise of each to waive or surrender his rights against the other. The agreement to abandon or discharge the contract need not be expressed; it may be inferred from the conduct of the parties and the surrounding circumstances. Schwartzreich v. Bauman-Basch, Inc., 231 N. Y. 196, 131 N. E. 887; 

Coletti v. Knox Hat Co., 252 N. Y. 468, 169 N. E. 648; 

Williston on Contracts, Rev. Ed., Vol. VI, sec. 1826; Restatement, Contracts, sec. 408.

When a partially performed bilateral contract is rescinded, the question often arises as to the right of restitution or compensation for prior performance or for expenses. 

In New York, such claims are usually denied unless expressly or impliedly reserved in the agreement of rescission. 

In McCreery v. Day, 119 N. Y. 1, 23 N. E. 198, 6 L. R. A. 

503, the Court held that the duty to reimburse the plaintiff for expenses was discharged by the rescission agreement. 

If the contract is fully executed by one party, the other cannot be released from his obligation without giving some consideration to the one who has performed. An agreement for rescission must have all the elements necessary 330

DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY AGREEMENT §§ 403, 404 

for the formation of a new contract. But in New York, a right or claim may be discharged or extinguished by a writing without consideration provided the writing is signed by the party against whom it is sought to be enforced. 

Debtor and Creditor Law, sec. 243; Personal Property Law, sec. 33, subd. 2; Real Property Law, sec. 279.

It should be noted also that the promisee may make a gift to the debtor or promisor of the outstanding obligation. A mere promise to give is executory and is without legal effect and should be distinguished from a gift which implies that the gift is executed or completed. The intention to make a gift must be clearly established. The surrender of a bill or a note or the delivery of a receipt is evidence of a gift. But a mere oral statement by a creditor to his debtor, such as "I am giving you this debt," "I release you," or "I discharge you," does not necessarily operate to discharge the debtor. Gray v. Barton, 55 N. Y. 68, 14 Am. Rep. 181; McKenzie v. Harrison, 120 N. Y. 260, 24 N. E. 458, 8 L. R. A. 257. 

§ 404. Discharge of Negotiable Instrument by Renuncia-. 

tion.

The Negotiable Instruments Law, section 203, provides: 

"The holder [of a negotiable instrument] may expressly renounce his rights against any party to the instrument, before, at or after its maturity. An absolute and unconditional renunciation of his rights against the principal debtor made at or after the maturity of the instrument, discharges the instrument. A renunciation must be in writing, unless the instrument is delivered up to the person primarily liable thereon." The renunciation is not required to be in writing if a consideration is given therefor. 

Bank of United States v. Manheim, 264 N. Y. 45, 189 N. E. 776. 

B made his note payable to C. After C's death, the note was found enclosed in an envelope together with a writing signed by C, the payee, addressed to his executor, as follows: "In the case of my death, B's note herewith enclosed 331

§§ 404, 405  

is to become null and void." Is B liable to C's executor on the note? 

§ 405. 

Substituted Contract. 

Parties who make a contract may unmake it and substitute in whole or in part therefor another contract. Therefore, a contract may be abandoned by mutual agreement of the parties, and in its place the parties may substitute a new and different contract, or they may waive or modify their rights under it and ingraft new terms upon it. As is the case with all other contracts, however, in order to be valid, a new agreement by the parties to an older one, altering, canceling, supplementing, or supplanting their former contract, requires a new consideration to support it. The new obligations assumed in the substituted contract, annulling the previous one between the parties, are sufficient consideration to support the new agreement. McCreery v. 

Day, 119 N. Y. 1, 23 N. E. 198, 6 L. R. A. 503; Colvin v. 

Post Mortgage & Land Co., 225 N. Y. 510, 122 N. E. 454.

At any time prior to the breach of an executory contract, the time fixed for the performance of the contract may be extended, or any other obligation in the contract may be modified, without any consideration other than the mutual consent of the contracting parties. Thus, it is competent for the parties in an executory contract of sale and purchase of personal property to agree upon another purchase price, time of payment, or place of delivery than that fixed in the original contract. Green-Shrier Co. v. State Realty & Mortgage Co., 199 N. Y. 65, 92 N. E. 98; De Klyn v. 

Silver Lake Ice Co., 12 N. Y. S. 917, aff'd 128 N. Y. 582, 28 N. E. 250.

It must again be noted that in New York by statute an agreement to change, modify, or discharge a contract is valid without consideration provided the agreement is in writing and is signed by the party against whom the change, modification, or discharge is sought to be enforced. 

Debtor and Creditor Law, sec. 243; Personal Property Law, sec. 33, subd. 2; Real Property Law, sec. 279. 332

DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY AGREEMENT §§ 406, 407 

§ 406. 

Substituted Contract-Novation. 

A contract may be modified by the substitution of a new party for one of the original parties. This can be done only by the consent of all. This amounts to a substitution of debtors, and is known as "novation." Thus, where A owed 

B a sum of money, and C owed A a like sum, it was agreed among all parties that C should give his note to B for the amount and be substituted in the place of A as B's debtor. 

C was insolvent at the time, but this was unknown. It was held that A's liability to B was extinguished by the novation. Cadens v. Teasdale, 53 Vt. 460, 38 Am. Rep. 697; 

Millerd v. Thorn, 56 N. Y. 402. Cf. Klinkoosten v. Mundt, 36 S. D. 595, 156 N. W. 85, L. R. A. 1918B 111.

The term "novation" is derived from the civil law. While the earlier usage of the common law confines it to cases where there is a substitution of parties, the term is now quite commonly used in a more comprehensive sense, as including all forms of substituted agreements, whether there is a substitution of parties or of subject matter. Hence, in its broader sense, the term novation is properly applied to all cases wherein a new obligation is substituted for an old one. Griggs v. Day, 136 N. Y. 152, 32 N. E. 612, 18 

L. R. A. 120; Bandman v. Finn, 185 N. Y. 508, 78 N. E. 

175, 12 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1134. 

§ 407. Substituted Contract-Account Stated. 

The doctrine of account stated had its origin in the transactions of merchants. Freeland v. Heran, Lenox & Co., 7 Cranch (U.S.) 147, 3 Law. Ed. 297. But it has since been extended to embrace transactions between other persons. Stenton v. Jerome, 54 N. Y. 480. Briefly defined, an account stated is a contract, express or implied, between the parties regarding the amount due on a past transaction or transactions. Rodkinson v. Haecker, 248 N. Y. 480, 162 

N. E. 493; Corr v. Hoffman, 256 N. Y. 254, 176 N. E. 383.

The elements are a mutual examination of the claims and an implied or expressed agreement as to the allowance or disallowance and as to the balance due, Carlisle v. Norris, 333

§§ 407, 408 

144 App. Div. 690, 129 N. Y. S. 585. The promise must be founded on previous transactions of a monetary character creating the relation of debtor and creditor. There must be an antecedent enforceable debt. An unenforceable debt is an insufficient basis for an account stated. Tucker v. Columbian National Life Insurance Co., 232 Mass. 224, 122 N. E. 

285; Parsons v. Batchelor, 233 App. Div. 517, 253 N. Y. S. 728. 

The mere rendering of a bill or statement of account does not constitute an account stated. If the debtor, however, upon receipt of the bill or statement of account, by silence or otherwise, admits its correctness, the bill or statement of account becomes conclusive as an account stated. Failure to object within a reasonable time shows assent. Schmid v. Du Val, 200 App. Div. 514, 193 N. Y. S. 278; Lockwood v. Thorne, 11 N. Y. 170, 62 Am. Dec. 81; Davison v. Klaess, 280 N. Y. 252, 20 N. E. (2d) 744.

An account stated can be opened only by showing fraud, mistake, or manifest error. Murphy v. Ross, 118 N. Y. 

676, 23 N. E. 1146. Where a confidential relationship exists, the fiduciary must show that the account stated was fairly brought about. Rodkinson v. Haecker, supra. § 408.

Form of Modified Contracts-Sealed Instruments. 

At common law, a contract under seal could not be modified by an unsealed executory agreement. McKenzie v. 

Harrison, 120 N. Y. 260, 24 N. E. 458, 8 L. R. A. 257; 

Harris v. Shorall, 230 N. Y. 343, 130 N. E. 572. In New 

York, however, the Civil Practice Act, section 342, as amended in 1935 and 1936, expressly provides that a contract under seal may be changed, modified, or discharged by an unsealed executory agreement provided the agreement is in writing and signed by the party against whom the change, modification, or discharge is sought to be enforced. 

A sealed instrument so changed or modified continues to be construed as an instrument under seal. 

A wholly executory oral modification of a contract under seal still remains unenforceable in New York. However, 334

DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY AGREEMENT §§ 408, 409 

a sealed contract may be modified by a subsequent oral agreement if the oral agreement has been executed by both parties, or if it has been so acted upon by one of the parties that it would be inequitable to enforce the original agreement. McKenzie v. Harrison, supra; National Cash Register Co. v. Remington Arms Co., 242 N. Y. 99, 151 N. E. 

144; McCreery v. Day, 119 N. Y. 1, 23 N. E. 198, 6 L. R. A. 

503; note 55 A. L. R. 685. 

§ 409. 

Form of Modified Contract-Statute of Frauds. 

The general rule is frequently stated to be that where the law requires a contract to be in writing, any modification thereof must be in writing. Kerzner v. Chanin, 98 N. J. L. 

38, 118 Atl. 693; 20 Columbia Law Review 497. However, in New York and in a number of other jurisdictions, a contract within the Statute of Frauds may be modified by a subsequent oral agreement where the contract as modified is not within the Statute of Frauds. Thus, a written contract originally within the statute because it was not to be performed within a year may be modified orally at a time when performance within the year is possible. "The validity of the substituted contract must be determined, like that of any other, in the light of the situation existing at the hour of its making." Lieberman v. Templar Motor Co., 236 N. Y. 139, 140 N. E. 222, 29 A. L. R. 1089 and note. 

See, also, Restatement, Contracts, section 223, subdivision 1.

An oral modification, which, because of the Statute of Frauds, was unenforceable when made, may be held binding where one party has thereby been induced so to act upon the modification that it would be inequitable to permit the other party to enforce the original agreement. Lieberman v. 

Templar Motor Co., supra; Imperator Realty Co. v. Tull, 228 N. Y. 447, 127 N. E. 263; Harris v. Shorall, 230 N. Y. 

343, 130 N. E. 572.

Unless the Statute of Frauds or the rule governing sealed instruments requires a writing, a written agreement may be modified by a subsequent oral agreement, even though the written agreement provides that it cannot be thus modified. 335

§§ 409, 410  

For instance, the provisions of a contract that the contractor shall make no claim for extra work unless the same shall be done in pursuance of a written order, may be waived, and an oral order or agreement for extra work made subsequent thereto will be regarded as a waiver of the written stipulation forbidding a recovery for such extra work without a written agreement. Solomon v. Vallette, 152 N. Y. 147, 46

N. E. 324; General Electric Co. v. National Contracting Co., 

178 N. Y. 369, 70 N. E. 928; Headley v. Cavileer, 82 N. J. L. 

635, 82 Atl. 908, 48 L. R. A. (N.S.) 564 and note. "Whenever two men contract, no limitation self-imposed can destroy their power to contract again." Beatty v. Guggenheim Exploration Co., 225 N. Y. 380, 122 N. E. 378. 

§ 410. Provision for Discharge Contained in Contract. 

A contract may contain within itself stipulations providing for its discharge upon the occurrence of certain events, or at the option of one of the parties. Cf. New York 

Telephone Co. v. Jamestown Telephone Corp., 282 N. Y. 365, 26 N. E. (2d) 295. These provisions are, in their nature, conditions subsequent. They may become operative upon : 

1. The non-fulfillment of some term of the contract; 

2. The occurrence of a particular event; or 

3. The exercise by one of the parties of an option to terminate the contract, the option being given: a. By express provisions in the contract; or b. By a custom or usage forming part of the contract. 

Moore v. Phoenix Insurance Co., 62 N. H. 240, 13 Am. 

St. Rep. 556; Gray v. Gardner, 17 Mass. 188.

Illustrations: The ordinary clause of defeasance in a chattel mortgage discharges the contract and conveyance upon payment of the mortgage debt. The usual excepted risks in a charter-party also illustrate conditions subsequent. Likewise, where a servant is engaged to carry on his master's business "to the master's satisfaction," the master has the absolute right to discharge the servant whenever the master becomes dissatisfied, and the master 336

DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY AGREEMENT §§ 410, 411 

is the sole judge as to whether or not the servant is satisfactory.

Where a contract of employment gives to either party a right to terminate it upon notice, the giving of the notice operates to discharge the contract. Graves v. The Calvin S. 

Edwards, 50 Fed. 477; Ginsberg v. Friedman, 146 App. 

Div. 779, 131 N. Y. S. 517; Crawford v. Mail & Express 

Publishing Co., 163 N. Y. 404, 57 N. E. 616; Mackenzie v. 

Minis, 132 Ga. 323, 63 S. E. 900, 23 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1003 and note. 

§ 411. 

Sales on Approval-Provisions for Discharge.

Where a person buys goods on approval or with the privilege of returning them if unsatisfactory, the Personal Property Law, section 100, subdivision 3, confers an option upon the buyer to either accept or reject the goods. If the goods are rejected and returned, the contract is discharged and not broken. If the goods, though unsatisfactory, are not returned within a reasonable time or within the time agreed upon, the purchaser will be obligated to pay the full contract price. Brown & Lowe Co. v. Potolski, 221 

App. Div. 299, 223 N. Y. S. 71; Kelley v. Thomas G. Plant 

Co., 274 Mass. 102, 174 N. E. 224.

Where goods are sold under a contract which contains a warranty and the goods do not comply with the warranty, the buyer is given a choice of remedies, one of which is to rescind the contract. It must be noted that while the buyer, under such circumstances, has an option to discharge the contract, yet the sale is not an optional sale such as has been discussed above. Personal Property Law, sec. 150. 337

CHAPTER XX  § 412. In General. 

The most natural mode of discharging a contract is by performance. What must be done in order to comply with the terms of the contract is in each case primarily a question of construction. Whether the promisee has received all that he has a right to demand often involves interesting and difficult questions. The rights of the parties must be determined by the contract construed in the light of surrounding circumstances. The functions of both the court and the jury may be required in answering the question: 

Has the promisee received all that he has a right to demand? For instance, where one has agreed to drill a well for another, when is the contract performed? Sweezy v. 

O'Rourke, 226 N. Y. 378, 123 N. E. 752. When are contracts for permanent employment or employment for life discharged by performance? Cox v. Baltimore & Ohio 

Southwestern R. R. Co., 180 Ind. 495, 103 N. E. 337, 50 

L. R. A. (N.S.) 453 and note. 

§ 413. 

Payment-Place. 

In the absence of an agreed place of payment, it is the duty of the debtor to seek his creditor and make payment. 

The debtor, however, need not go out of his own state to make payment on a contract which is made within his state. Where the creditor is a non-resident, he should provide for a place of payment within the state of his debtor. 

But if the contract was made out of the state, the debtor should make payment at the place where the contract was made. Weyand v. Park Terrace Co., 202 N. Y. 231, 95 

N. E. 723, 36 L. R. A. (N.S.) 308 and note.

Special rules apply to a negotiable instrument and to the payment of rent under a lease of real property. A negotiable instrument silent as to the place for payment is presumed to be payable at the place where made. Richardson 338

§§ 413 

on Bills and Notes, 4th Ed., sec. 30. Where a lease for the payment of rent for the use of real property is silent as to the place of payment, the law fixes the place of payment upon the leased premises. House v. Lewis, 108 Neb. 257, 187 N. W. 784, 23 A. L. R. 877 and note. 

§ 414. 

Payment-Money. 

If the contract requires the payment of money, such payment extinguishes the debt and discharges the contract. 

Lancey v. Clark, 64 N. Y. 209, 21 Am. Rep. 604.

The transmission of money by mail by a debtor to his. creditor is at the hazard of the debtor. If, however, a debtor sends the money as directed by his creditor, it is at the latter's risk, and the debt is discharged, even though the remittance is lost. Palmer v. Phoenix Mutual Life Insurance Co., 84 N. Y. 63; Masterson v. Union Bank & Trust 

Co., 86 Wash. 560, 150 Pac. 1126, L. R. A. 1918A 531 and note; Mitchell-Henry v. Norwich Union Life Insurance 

Society, Ltd., 2 K. B. Div. (Eng.) 67 (1918); 2 A. L. R. 

1644 and note. 

§ 415. Payment-Note of Debtor. 

Whether the payment of a debt by a note of the debtor acts as a discharge of the debt is a question of intention. 

If the note is accepted expressly as absolute payment, it will discharge the debt. If litigation is necessary, the payee must bring his action on the note. But in the absence of such an agreement, the note is, at most, a conditional, and not an absolute, payment. The law presumes that the note was not accepted as absolute payment. Hence, as a rule, if the note is not paid at maturity, an action may be maintained on the original consideration. This presumption prevails, whether the debt is a preexisting one or is concurrent with the giving of the note. Winsted Bank v. 

Webb, 39 N. Y. 325, 100 Am. Dec. 435; St. Albans Beef Co. v. Aldridge, 112 App. Div. 803, 99 N. Y. S. 398; Leschen & 

Sons Rope Co. v. Mayflower Gold Mining & Reduction Co., 173 Fed. 855, 35 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1 and note; State v. 339

§§ 415, 416  

Royal Indemnity Co., 44 N. D. 550, 175 N. W. 625. In a minority of states, the rule is that the negotiable note of a debtor given to his creditor is prima facie a satisfaction of a prior simple contract debt. See note in 35 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1. It should be noted that there is a third possibility in addition to the acceptance of the note as absolute or conditional payment; namely, that it may be accepted merely as collateral security, in which case the right of the creditor to sue on the original contract is not suspended and exists even prior to the maturity of the note. See 

Weymouth v. Sanborn, 43 N. H. 171, 80 Am. Dec. 144; 

Smith v. Ryan, 66 N. Y. 352, 23 Am. Rep. 60. 

§ 416. 

Payment-Check of Debtor. 

In the absence of an agreement to take the debtor's check in absolute payment or satisfaction of a debt, the law presumes that it is received as conditional payment only, the same as in the case of the receipt of the debtor's note. 

If the check is paid, the debt will be discharged; if the check is not paid, the debt will not be discharged. Delivery of a certified check though the certification by the bank was procured by the debtor (drawer) at the request of the creditor (payee) does not operate to extinguish the debt, unless the check is duly paid. Randolph National 

Bank v. Hornblower, 160 Mass. 401, 35 N. E. 850; Davenport v. Palmer, 152 App. Div. 761, 137 N. Y. S. 796, reversed on other grounds in 211 N. Y. 596, 105 N. E. 800.

Where, however, the creditor procures a certification of his debtor's check, it will constitute a payment to the amount of the check. St. Regis Paper Co. v. Tonawanda 

Board & Paper Co., 107 App. Div. 90, 94 N. Y. S. 946, aff'd 186 N. Y. 563, 79 N. E. 1115. A creditor's failure to use due diligence in presenting a check for payment operates as payment, but only to the extent that the debtor is damaged thereby. Carroll v. Sweet, 128 N. Y. 19, 27 N. E. 

763, 13 L. R. A. 43. 340

§ 

§ 417. 

Payment-Note, Check, or Draft of Third Person. 

The acceptance of a note, check, or draft of a third person by the creditor from his debtor does not operate as payment of an antecedent debt, unless the parties so agree. 

But the transfer of a note, check, or draft of a third person, upon a purchase of property or for an indebtedness contracted at the time of transfer, is presumably a payment pro tanto, and the burden of showing the contrary rests on the person receiving the paper. Noel v. Murray, 13 N. Y. 

167; Dille v. White, 132 Iowa 327, 109 N. W. 909, 10 

L. R. A. (N.S.) 510 and note; Hamilton v. Dickson & Co., 85 Fed. (2d) 107. 

In Hall v. Stevens, 116 N. Y. 201, 22 N. E. 374, 5 L. R. A. 

802, an action was brought for the purchase price of cattle sold by the plaintiff to the defendants. At the time of the sale, the plaintiff accepted from the defendants a draft for $1,311.25 drawn by the First National Bank of Buffalo upon the Fourth National Bank of New York. The Buf falo bank was insolvent, unknown to both plaintiff and defendants. The drawee refused to pay the draft. The 

Court said: "The plaintiff having proved the sale and delivery of the cattle and the acceptance by him of a draft growing out of the transaction itself, but neither signed nor indorsed by the defendants, who was required to show whether the draft was taken as payment or as security? 

The answer to this question depends upon whether the draft was taken for a present or a precedent debt. If it was for the former, the presumption is that it was agreed to be taken in payment, and the burden of proving the contrary rested upon the plaintiff; while if it was for the latter, the presumption is that it was not taken as payment, and the onus of establishing that it was so taken rested upon the defendants." For an exhaustive analysis of the authorities, see note to Leschen & Sons Rope Co. v. Mayflower Gold 

Mining & Reduction Co., 173 Fed. 855, in 35 L. R. A. 

(N.S.) 1. 

It is generally conceded that there is no implied warranty that the drawer or maker is solvent by one selling, without 341

§§ 417, 418  

indorsing, the negotiable instrument of another. Hecht v. 

Batcheller, 147 Mass. 335, 17 N. E. 651, 9 Am. St. Rep. 

708; People's Bank v. Bogart, 81 N. Y. 101, 37 Am. Rep. 

481. But where a note, given in payment of an antecedent indebtedness, is worthless because of the insolvency of the maker, the debt is not discharged, and a recovery may be had on the original debt. Roberts v. Fisher, 43 N. Y. 159, 3 Am. Rep. 680. There is an obvious distinction between a sale of worthless paper in the open market and the payment of an antecedent debt with such paper. Hecht v. 

Batcheller, supra; note to Dille v. White, supra. 

§ 418. 

Application of Payment-By the Parties. 

Where a person owes several debts or items to another, he has a right to direct to which item any payment which he may make shall be applied. Goodman v. Snow, 81 Hun 

225, 30 N. Y. S. 672. The reason for the rule which confers upon the debtor the right primarily to direct the application of a payment voluntarily made by him is apparent. Until the money is actually paid over, it belongs to him, and he may do with it as he sees fit. If he makes a specific direction, the creditor must observe it or refuse to accept the payment. New York & Brooklyn Brewing Co. v. 

Angelo, 144 App. Div. 655, 129 N. Y. S. 713. In the absence of such direction, the creditor may apply the payment to whichever debt he pleases. For example, a creditor having secured and unsecured claims may apply an undirected payment to an unsecured claim. Bank of California v. 

Webb, 94 N. Y. 467; Wanamaker v. Powers, 102 App. Div. 

485, 93 N. Y. S. 19, aff'd 186 N. Y. 562, 79 N. E. 1118; 

Wilkes v. Kitchen, 187 Ky. 211, 218 S. W. 718. The creditor, however, cannot, without the debtor's consent, apply payments to an illegal claim, such as a debt for liquor sold in violation of law or a claim for usurious interest. Huffstater v. Hayes, 64 Barb. 573; Anderson v. Griffith, 51 

Ore. 116, 93 Pac. 934. The payment must be applied to a claim on which the payor is liable. Sherman v. Harley, 178 Cal. 584, 174 Pac. 901, 7 A. L. R. 950. It has also been 342

418, 419 held that the creditor cannot apply the payment to an unmatured debt if there is a matured debt to which the payment might be applied. Williston on Contracts, Rev. Ed.,

Vol. VI, sec. 1797; Restatement, Contracts, sec. 389, subd. (a). A conflict of authority exists as to the right of the creditor to apportion the payment among several obliga tions. According to Sanborn v. Cole, 63 Vt. 590, 22 Atl. 

716, 14 L. R. A. 208, he cannot. But Young v. Alford, 118 N. C. 215, 23 S. E. 973, holds that he may do so. 

§ 419. Application of Payment-By Law. 

When neither the debtor nor the creditor applies the payment to any particular debt or item, the law will make the application. In re Milligan's Estate, 112 App. Div. 373, 98 N. Y. S. 480.

The courts have met with many difficult questions in making the application. The common law rule requires the application to be made by law in the way most beneficial to the creditor. McCurdy v. Middleton, 82 Ala. 131, 2

So. 721. The civil law rule requires it to be applied in the way most beneficial to the debtor. McLaughlin v. 

Green, 48 Miss. 175. The common law rule, that it is not inequitable to extinguish first those debts less burdensome and for which the security is more precarious, is illustrated by Field v. Holland, 6 Cranch (U.S.) 8, 3 Law. Ed. 136, wherein the United States Supreme Court applied a payment to other demands rather than to a judgment debt, on the ground that the former were not so well secured. To the same effect is Coons & Braine v. Tome, 9 Fed. 532. 

While the decisions in New York are not free from conflict, they reveal a preference for the civil law rule. Thus, in Pattison v. Hull, 9 Cowen (N.Y.) 747, the Court applied the payment to a mortgage and judgment debt in preference to an account because the former would bear more heavily on the debtor. So, too, in Dows v. Morewood, 10 Barb. 

183, the Court applied the payment to a secured debt in preference to an unsecured debt. And in Griswold v. 

Onondaga County Savings Bank, 93 N. Y. 301, the Court of 343

§ 

Appeals, commenting on the civil law rule that the payment should be applied to the debts which lie heaviest on the debtor, said by way of dictum: "We have found no contrary doctrine asserted in this court, after an examination intended to be careful and accurate." For cases indicating a contrary tendency, see Thomas v. Kelsey, 30 Barb. 268, and note, 97 A. L. R. 345.

The New York courts have also enunciated the principle that where payments are made on a running account, the court will apply the payments to the discharge of the oldest obligations. Carson v. Federal Reserve Bank, 245 N. Y. 

218, 172 N. E. 475, 70 A. L. R. 435. See, also, Restatement, Contracts, section 394, subdivision (1). Where all of the obligations are secured or all are unsecured, there is no conflict between this principle and the rule of application which favors the debtor.

But where some of the obligations are secured and some are unsecured, it is not yet clear whether the rule of application favoring the debtor or the rule applying the payment to the oldest obligation applies. Cf. Truscott v. King, 6 N. Y. 147; note, 97 A. L. R. 345, 356.

It has been recognized by the courts that the payment in each instance should be applied to accomplish the ends of justice, and that, therefore, no rigid formula can be devised to fit all cases. Armstrong v. McLean, 153 N. Y. 490, 47 

N. E. 912; Korbly v. Springfield Institution for Savings, 245 

U. S. 330, 86 Sup. Ct. Rep. 88, 62 Law. Ed. 326. In a discussion of the rule that payments should be applied first to the obligations first contracted when neither party makes direction therefor, the then Chief Judge Cardozo in Carson v. Federal Reserve Bank, supra, stated: "We have no thought to suggest that this or any other formula as to the application of payments to the items of an account is of such inflexible validity as to admit of no exceptions. Whatever rule is framed will be subordinated to the broader principle that an application, usually appropriate, may be varied by the court when variance is necessary to promote the ends of justice. None the less, the formula is expressive 344

§§ 419, 420 of a rule that must prevail in the absence of persuasive reasons for qualification or exception."

TENDER § 420. What Is Tender-Tender of Goods or Services. 

A tender is an offer to do what a party has promised to do but is prevented from so doing by acts of the other party. People ex rel. Haas v. New York Motor Boat Club, 

70 Misc. 603, 129 N. Y. S. 365. Where it is applied to a performance of a promise to do something other than the payment of money, its effect is to extinguish the other party's rights to demand performance. In employment contracts, it is not necessary for the employee, upon a repudiation of the contract by the employer, thereafter to tender his services or to keep himself in readiness to perform. The employee's readiness to perform at the time stipulated constitutes a tender of his services, and the employer's refusal to accept the tendered services operates to discharge the employee's contractual obligation. Howard v. Daly, 61 N. Y. 362, 19 Am. Rep. 285.

The buyer's refusal to accept goods when tendered by the seller discharges the seller's duty of making further efforts to deliver, since the tender and refusal discharge the contract of sale. Jardine Matheson & Co. v. Huguet Silk Co., 203 N. Y. 273, 96 N. E. 449. In this connection, the Sales

Act, which is merely declaratory of the common law, provides: "When the seller is ready and willing to deliver the goods, and requests the buyer to take delivery, and the buyer does not within a reasonable time after such request take delivery of the goods, he is liable to the seller for any loss occasioned by his neglect or refusal to take delivery, and also for a reasonable charge for the care and custody of the goods. If the neglect or refusal of the buyer to take delivery amounts to a repudiation, or breach of the entire contract, the seller shall have the rights against the goods and on the contract hereinafter provided in favor of the seller when the buyer is in default." Personal Property 345

§§ 420, 421  

Law, sec. 132. A tender of delivery and refusal to accept are not the same for all purposes as an actual delivery. Thus, a tender of delivery and refusal to accept before title has passed would not authorize a recovery of the purchase price; while an actual delivery would give the seller this right. Where there is a refusal to accept delivery, the seller's remedy is an action for damages only. Reichbart v. 

Smith-Eisemann Corp. of America, 199 App. Div. 571, 191 

N. Y. S. 803; Bready v. Wechsler Co., Inc., 200 App. Div. 

78, 192 N. Y. S. 660, aff'd 235 N. Y. 539, 139 N. E. 726. 

§ 421. 

Tender of Money.

Where the performance of the contract requires the payment of money, the tender must be for the full amount. 

The tender of any less sum is nugatory and ineffective as a tender. The tender must be absolute and unconditional; it must observe the terms of the contract as to time, place, and mode of payment; and the debtor must continue always ready and willing to pay the debt. Noyes v. Wyckoff, 114 N. Y. 204, 21 N. E. 158; Werner v. Tuch, 127 N. Y. 

217, 27 N. E. 845; Graves v. Burch, 26 Wyo. 192, 181 Pac. 

354, 5 A. L. R. 1216 and note. A conflict of opinion exists as to whether or not a trifling variation from the exact amount due will render the tender ineffective. In Woodworth v. Morris, 56 Barb. 97, failure to include a single day's interest was held to render the alleged tender ineffective. The rule that the tender must be kept good is not adhered to in a court of equity, as in a court of law. In equity, the necessity for doing so depends on the circumstances in each case. Thompson v. Crains, 294 Ill. 270, 128 

N. E. 508, 12 A. L. R. 931 and note; Zebley v. Farmers' 

Loan & Trust Co., 139 N. Y. 461, 34 N. E. 1067.

A defect in tender, whether it is a deficiency in amount or a defect in the time, place, manner, or medium of payment, may be waived by the creditor. Williston on Contracts, Rev. Ed., Vol. VI, sec. 1819. 346

§ 

§ 422. 

Effect of Tender of Money. 

The only effect of tender of money, if it be sufficient, is to stop interest and throw the cost of suit on the other party. 

Nelson v. Loder, 132 N. Y. 288, 30 N. E. 369.

A tender at any time before a sale on foreclosure of a debt secured by a mortgage extinguishes the lien, and as it leaves the debt unaffected, it is not necessary to keep the tender good. The leading case on this question is Kortright v. Cady, 21 N. Y. 343, 78 Am. Dec. 145. In Cresco Realty 

Co. v. Clark, 128 App. Div. 144, 112 N. Y. S. 550, it was held that a tender of interest prior to an action of foreclosure for its non-payment bars the action, and that such tender need not be kept good by payment into court to be available as a defense. Under the rule formerly prevailing in New York, a tender and payment into court admitted the debt or contract and transferred the title of the money to the creditor, even if it was not accepted. The money could not afterwards be withdrawn by the debtor, and this, too, even though the plaintiff was defeated in the action or recovered a verdict for less than the sum tendered. Mann v. Sprout, 185 N. Y. 109, 77 N. E. 1018, 5 L. R. A. (N.S.) 

561. However, the Civil Practice Act now provides: 

"Section 171. Tender after suit. Where the complaint demands judgment for a sum of money only, and the action is brought to recover a sum certain, or which may be reduced to certainty by calculation, or to recover damages for a casual or involuntary personal injury or a like injury to property, the defendant or his attorney, at any time before the trial, may tender to the plaintiff or his attorney such a sum of money as he conceives to be sufficient to make amends for the injury or to pay the plaintiff's demand, together with the costs of the action to that time. "Section 172. Amount to be paid into court. A tender made as prescribed in the last section does not avail the defendant, unless the money is accepted or is paid into court and notice thereof in writing served upon the plaintiff's attorney before the trial and within ten days after the tender. In making such a payment into court the de- 347

§ 

fendant shall file therewith a memorandum specifying the portion thereof deposited on account of costs. If, before the trial and within ten days after such notice, the plaintiff takes out the amount paid in, he accepts the tender. 

Effect of tender. 

Effect of tender. If the plaintiff accept 

"Section 173. 

a tender, it shall be deemed in satisfaction of the cause of action in respect of which the tender was made, and the defendant may move for a judgment dismissing the complaint, without costs. Where money constituting a tender shall have been paid into court and not accepted, "1. If the defendant recover judgment, the money shall be paid to him; "2. If the plaintiff recover judgment, the money shall be applied upon his judgment, and the surplus, if any, paid to the defendant; "3. If the plaintiff's judgment exclusive of costs is not in excess of the tender exclusive of the portion thereof deposited on account of costs, the plaintiff cannot recover costs or interest from the time of the tender, but must pay the defendant's costs from that time; "4. The plaintiff's right to costs is determined by the excess of his judgment exclusive of costs over the amount of the tender exclusive of the amount thereof deposited on account of costs. "Section 174. Tender on counterclaim. 

A tender may be made by the plaintiff in respect of a counterclaim, in the same cases, in the same manner, and with the same effect as to the counterclaim, as in case of a tender by a defendant on account of a demand for payment in a complaint. "Section 175. Offer to liquidate damages conditionally.

In an action to recover damages for breach of a contract, the defendant's attorney may serve, with the answer upon the plaintiff's attorney, a written offer that if the defendant fails in his defense the damages may be assessed at a specified sum. If the plaintiff serves notice, with or before the note of issue, that he accepts the offer, and damages are awarded to him on the trial, they must be assessed accordingly. 348

§§ 422, 423 "Section 176. Effect of refusal of offer. If the plain tiff does not accept the offer, he cannot prove it upon the trial. If the damages awarded to the plaintiff do not exceed the sum offered, the defendant is entitled to recover the expenses necessarily incurred by him in preparing for the trial of the question of damages. The expenses must be ascertained and the amount thereof determined by the judge, or the referee, before whom the cause is tried." 

For the application of the Civil Practice Act, section 172, see Hirsch v. Spitzer, 208 App. Div. 711, 202 N. Y. S. 338. 

§ 423. Duty To Make Change. 

The sum tendered should be such as not to require the creditor to make change. However, a duty is imposed upon carriers of passengers to make change for a reasonable amount. The question of the reasonableness of the rules of these companies as to the amount, where the facts are undisputed, is one of law for the courts. A rule of a street railway company that not more than two dollars will be accepted and change given in payment of a five cent fare is reasonable. And where a passenger tenders a five dollar bill, it may be refused and the passenger may be ejected, since the amount tendered is unreasonable. Barker v. Central Park, North & East River R. R. Co., 45 N. E. 550, 35 L. R. A. 489 and note. rett v. Market Street Ry. Co., 81 Cal. 296, 22 Pac. 859, 6 L. R. A. 336, it was held that it was not unreasonable for a passenger to tender a five dollar bill to a street car conductor in payment of his fare. And in Jones v. Louisville & Nashville R. R., 109 Miss. 655, 68 So. 924, where a passenger tendered a railroad conductor a hundred dollar bill, with the request that the conductor return the necessary change only when they reached their destination in a large city, the size of the bill was held to be immaterial to the question of the reasonableness of the amount tendered. 

151 N. Y. 237, However, in Bar The duty of a telegraph company to make change is in doubt. Apparently, the company may justly refuse to send 349

§§ 423  

a message unless the sender tenders the exact amount of the charges. Dale v. Western Union Telegraph Co., 181 

App. Div. 292, 168 N. Y. S. 783. 

§ 424. Legal Tender-Lawful Money. 

Since June 5, 1933, all coins and currencies are legal tender for any amount. Formerly there were certain restrictions as to fractional silver coins, coppers, nickels, silver certificates, and bank notes. An Act of the 73d Congress, Extra Session, provides: "All coins and currencies of the United States (including Federal Reserve notes and circulating notes of Federal Reserve banks and national banking associations) heretofore or hereafter coined or issued, shall be legal tender for all debts, public and private, public charges, taxes, duties and dues." 31 United States 

Code Annotated, sec. 462. 

By a joint resolution adopted in 1933, Congress has declared that clauses in a contract requiring payment in gold or payment in United States money measured by gold are in violation of public policy, and that all such contracts are payable in legal tender. 31 United States Code Annotated, sec. 463. See, also, Norman v. Baltimore & Ohio R. R. Co., 294 U. S. 240, 79 Law. Ed. 885, 55 Sup. Ct. Rep. 407. 

§ 425. Waiver of Legal Tender. 

While a legal tender can be made only in the money which has been made legal tender by law, the objection to the medium in which the tender is made may be waived by the creditor. Thus, if a check is tendered by a debtor, and the creditor refuses to receive it on some ground other than that it is not legal tender, the tender is valid. Gaunt v. 

Alabama Bound Oil & Gas Co., 281 Fed. 653, 23 A. L. R. 

1279 and note; Mitchell v. Vermont Copper Mining Co., 67 N. Y. 280. So, too, where a debtor was in the act of drawing his check for the amount due, he was interrupted by the creditor with an inquiry as to the amount of the check, and a dispute arose as to the amount due. The creditor finally declared that he would not accept the 350

§§ 425, 426 

amount offered.

It was held that there was a sufficient tender. Link v. Mack, 25 Misc. 615, 56 N. Y. S. 115. Fur thermore, it has been held that, in the absence of notice to the effect that property of large value must be paid for in cash, a buyer is entitled to time to secure the money from the bank on the following day if the seller refuses to accept the buyer's check after banking hours, at least, where, from previous dealings, the buyer had reason to believe that his check would be accepted. Farris v. Ferguson, 146 Tenn. 

498, 242 S. W. 873, 23 A. L. R. 624 and note.

SUBSTANTIAL PERFORMANCE § 426. Sufficiency of Performance. 

It is a general rule of law that a contract must be performed according to the terms of the agreement before a party can have any right of action thereon. A person cannot be compelled to accept one thing when he bargained for another. He has the right to have the thing contracted for in the form agreed upon. While these general principles were strictly enforced under the common law, yet the more modern decisions indicate a tendency to relax the rigors of the earlier rules. Harrild v. Spokane School District, 112 Wash. 266, 192 Pac. 1, 19 A. L. R. 811. The modern tendency is to look to the spirit of the contract and not to the letter of it, and to make the vital question, therefore, not whether a party has literally complied with the terms of the contract, but whether he has substantially done so. Although a plaintiff is not absolutely free from fault in every particular, the court will not always turn him away without any remedy if he has in good faith made substantial performance, but will accord to him certain rights on the one hand, and preserve the rights of the defendant on the other, by permitting a recoupment. "The courts never say that one who makes a contract fills the measure of his duty by less than full performance. 

They do say, however, that an omission, both trivial and innocent, will sometimes be atoned for by allowance of the resulting damage, and will not always be the breach of a 351

§ 

condition to be followed by a forfeiture." Jacob & Youngs 'v. Kent, 230 N. Y. 239, 129 N. E. 889, 23 A. L. R. 1429 and note. For example, it has been held that a contract to place and keep up fourteen hundred signs on an elevated railway is substantially performed where all except twenty of the signs were kept up, and these twenty were to have been placed in galleries leading to a bridge but were placed in temporary galleries while the other galleries were being reconstructed. Desmond Dunne Co. v. Friedman- 

Doscher Co., 162 N. Y. 486, 56 N. E. 995. So, also, where plumbing of the same quality but of a different brand from that called for in the specifications is placed in a building, there may be a recovery on the theory of substantial performance. Jacob & Youngs v. Kent, supra. But there can be no recovery under the theory of substantial performance unless the plaintiff acted in good faith. Gillespie Tool Co. v. Wilson, 123 Pa. 19, 16 Atl. 36. Thus, where A contracted to keep B's automobile in "good running condition," putting it in "fair condition" was not substantial performance. Kehoe v. Newman, 169 N. Y. S. 71.

A contract to build a monument is not substantially performed if the monument is of different dimensions from those specified in the contract. Cutler v. Dix, 67 Vt. 347, 31 Atl. 780; Blitz v. Toovey, 28 N. Y. St. Rep. 160, 9 N. Y. S. 

439. The doctrine of substantial performance has been recognized for many years in the courts of equity in actions for specific performance. A court of equity in such actions may give the plaintiff relief and compel the defendant to perform, although the plaintiff cannot tender all he contracted to deliver. Thus, a contract for the sale of a farm of two hundred acres may be enforced, even though the farm consists of but one hundred and ninety-five acres. The court in its discretion may grant the vendor specific performance against the vendee, with a deduction of a proportionate amount from the purchase price. But if the variation is great, equity will not compel the purchaser to perform. 

Wetmore v. Bruce, 118 N. Y. 319, 23 N. E. 303; King v. 

Bardeau, 6 Johns. Ch. (N.Y.) 38, 10 Am. Dec. 312. 352

$

§ 427. Substantial Performance of Building Contracts. 

The doctrine of substantial performance finds frequent application in building contracts. Jacob & Youngs v. Kent, 230 N. Y. 239, 129 N. E. 889, 23 A. L. R. 1429 and note.

Substantial performance entitles the builder to recover under a complaint for performance, with allowances to the owner for unsubstantial omissions. Nolan v. Whitney, 88 

N. Y. 648. This is now the accepted view in England. H. Da kin & Co., Ltd. v. Lee, 1 K. B. Div. (Eng.) 566 (1916). The deviations in the completion of or failure to complete the building must not have been wilful or intentional. There must be an honest attempt to perform the contract. If the contract is performed in a careless and negligent manner and defects appear throughout the entire building, there is not a substantial performance. Carpenter Co. v. Ellsworth, 151 App. Div. 532, 136 N. Y. S. 108; Easthampton 

Lumber & Coal Co. v. Worthington, 186 N. Y. 407, 79 N. E. 323. 

The principal reason for allowing a recovery in case of substantial performance is that the building so constructed is an additional value to the land and it is not deemed fair to allow to the owner such increased value without requiring compensation therefor. Approximate justice is arrived at in such cases by allowing the contractor to recover the contract price less a deduction for the damages caused by the omission or defects. But if there be structural defects, or defects in material, or in specifications as to size of rooms, windows, doors, etc., there can be no recovery even on quantum meruit, and this, too, though the building as completed may be substantial, habitable, and of much value. 

Spence v. Ham, 163 N. Y. 220, 57 N. E. 412, 51 L. R. A. 238;

Steel Storage & Elevator Construction Co. v. Stock, 225 

N. Y. 173, 121 N. E. 786.

Precisely the same principles that govern the right to recover for substantial performance of a building contract apply to other contracts where there has been substantial performance which has benefited the defendant. Dickinson v. Sheldon, 146 App. Div. 144, 130 N. Y. S. 889; Williston 353

§§ 427  

on Contracts, Rev. Ed., Vol. III, sec. 842. See, also, section 

426, supra.

CONTRACTS CONDITIONED UPON PERSONAL SATISFACTION § 428. Performance to the Satisfaction of Adverse Party. 

A contract in which one party agrees to perform his part to the "satisfaction" of the adverse party presents important questions as to what constitutes satisfaction. Contracts involving this question may be classified as follows: 

1. Contracts involving personal taste; 

Contracts not involving personal taste; 

3. Contracts for the sale of goods. 

§ 429. 

Contracts Involving Personal Taste. 

Where a contract stipulates that it is to be performed to the satisfaction of one of the parties and its performance involves personal taste, fancy, judgment, or individual preference, the party who is to be satisfied is the sole judge as to whether or not he has received all that he has a right to demand. There can be no recovery under such contracts unless the party who is to make compensation is satisfied. For example, where the publishers of a newspaper engaged the plaintiff to write for the paper for a certain period, provided his services were "satisfactory to the publishers," it was held that the publishers were the sole judges of the quality of the work done. The Court said: "An article printed in a paper or magazine may please one person and displease another. It may gratify the taste of one and become the subject of criticism and ridicule on the part of another. The plaintiff did not agree to satisfy a court or jury, but undertook to satisfy the publishers. It was their taste, their fancy, their interest and their judgment that was to be satisfied." Crawford v. Mail & Express Publishing Co., 163 N. Y. 404, 57 N. E. 616. See, also, Wolff v. Smith, 25 N. E. (2d) (Ill.) 399, discussed in 26 

Virginia Law Review 1064 (June, 1940). 

An interesting case involving a contract for personal 354

§§ 429, 430 

service in which the employee conceded his services were "special, unique, and extraordinary" is Applegate v. Mac- 

Fadden Newspaper Publishing Corp., 214 App. Div. 221, 

212 N. Y. S. 67. The Court held that when the employer becomes dissatisfied with the services of the employee he has a right to dismiss him and is not called upon to give reasons, subject to one limitation; i.e., that the employer's dissatisfaction must be real and genuine and not merely feigned or simulated. The employer may act unreasonably, but he must act in good faith. The question of performance to the satisfaction of a party is further illustrated in the following cases: Saxe v. Shubert Theatrical Co., 57 Misc. 

620, 108 N. Y. S. 683 (an actor); Adams Radiator & Boiler

Works v. Schnader, 155 Pa. 394, 26 Atl. 745 (steam heating apparatus); Gibson v. Cranage, 39 Mich. 49, 33 

Am. Rep. 351 (portrait); Zaleski v. Clark, 44 Conn. 218, 26 Am. Rep. 446 (bust); Brown v. Foster, 113 Mass. 136, 18 Am. Rep. 463 (suit of clothes); Haven v. Russell, 34 

N. Y. S. 292 (manuscript for play); Gray v. Alabama 

National Bank, 38 N. Y. St. Rep. 169, 14 N. Y. S. 155 (design for steel plate); Walker v. Edward Thompson Co., 37 

App. Div. 536, 56 N. Y. S. 326 (literary work). 

§ 430. Contracts Not Involving Personal Taste. 

Where, under a contract not involving personal taste, one agrees to perform to the satisfaction of a party, and the work and labor would be wholly lost to the party who rendered the services unless he is paid therefor, the courts hold it would be manifestly inequitable to allow a repudiation of the contract on the ground of dissatisfaction, and rule that what "the law will say a contracting party ought in reason to be satisfied with, that the law will say he is satisfied with." Therefore, if he ought to be satisfied, the law says he is satisfied. In such cases he cannot, arbitrarily and unreasonably, deny satisfaction. This rule of law has been applied in a contract for the repair of a boiler, to be paid for only when the defendant was "satisfied" that it was, as changed, a "success." The contract does not in- 355

§§ 430, 431  

volve a matter of personal taste. It is, at most, a promise to give reasonable satisfaction, subject to the judgment of judicial triers. Duplex Safety Boiler Co. v. Garden, 101 

N. Y. 387, 4 N. E. 749; Doll v. Noble, 116 N. Y. 230, 22

N. E. 406, 5 L. R. A. 554. So, also, an agreement of a life insurance company to reinstate a former policy holder on the production of satisfactory evidence of his insurability does not contemplate the exercise of the company's taste, fancy, or caprice. When it ought to be satisfied, it is re garded as satisfied. Thompson v. Postal Life Insurance 

Co., 226 N. Y. 363, 123 N. E. 750. 

A contract for a ventilating machine, guaranteed to operate to "the purchaser's satisfaction," involves no question of personal taste or individual preference. Hummel v. 

Stern, 21 App. Div. 544, 48 N. Y. S. 528, aff'd 164 N. Y. 

603, 58 N. E. 1088.

In doubtful cases the courts are strongly inclined to construe contracts of this class as agreements to perform or to do the thing in such a way as ought reasonably to satisfy the defendant. Thus, where a contract to furnish a system of heating for a building specifies that the system shall prove satisfactory, the satisfactoriness is to be determined by the mind of a reasonable man and not by the private taste or liking of the owner of the building. Hawkins v. Graham, 149 Mass. 284, 21 N. E. 312. The view in the latter case is expressly approved in Erikson v. Ward, 266 Ill. 259, 107 N. E. 593. To the same effect is Parlin & Orendorff Co. v. Greenville, 127 Fed. 55. Where a builder agreed to pay a plastering contractor a bonus for cooperating in hastening the work to the builder's satisfaction, the builder to be the sole judge thereof, the Court held that the contractor was entitled to the bonus if he did the work in proper and workmanlike manner, even though the builder was not satisfied with it. Duffy Bros. v. Bing & Bing, 217 

App. Div. 10, 215 N. Y. S. 755. 

§ 431. 

Contracts for the Sale of Goods. 

In the sale of goods under a contract that the goods may 356

§§ 431, 432 

be returned if not satisfactory to the vendee, the rule of personal taste or individual preference quite generally applies. McClure Bros. v. Briggs, 58 Vt. 82, 2 Atl. 583 (organ); McCarren v. McNulty, 7 Gray (Mass.) 139 (bookcase). Even though personal taste is not involved, if the contract makes the buyer the sole judge, he may return the goods at least, where the seller can be substantially placed in status quo. Singerly v. Thayer, 108 Pa. 291, 2 Atl. 230 (passenger elevator); Exhaust Ventilator Co. v. Chicago, Milwaukee & St. Paul R. R. Co., 66 Wis. 218, 28 N. W. 

343, 57 Am. Rep. 257 (exhaust fans); Campbell Printing- 

Press Co. v. Thorp, 36 Fed. 414, 1 L. R. A. 645 (printing press); Wood Machine Co. v. Smith, 50 Mich. 565, 15 N. W. 

906, 45 Am. Rep. 57 (harvesting machine); Aiken v. Hyde, 99 Mass. 183 (gas generator). Similarly, in Dvorak v. 

Prucha, 156 Ill. App. 514, it was held that if a seller contracts to furnish an article satisfactory to the purchaser, with the right to return the same within a specified time if not satisfactory, the purchaser has an absolute right to return the same within such time. See, also, Personal Property Law, section 100, rule 3, subdivision 2; Sabarsky v. 

Drew, 176 App. Div. 80, 162 N. Y. S. 505, aff'd 226 N. Y. 

557, 123 N. E. 887. 

§ 432. Approval by Third Person. 

Where the articles sold or the work to be done or performed is to be subject to the approval of, or satisfactory to, some third person, the approval of such person becomes a condition precedent to a recovery of the price. In the absence of fraud or bad faith, his judgment or determination is final and conclusive. Atkins v. Trowbridge, 162 App. 

Div. 629, 148 N. Y. S. 181. Thus, where the parties stipulate that the legality or conditions of a contract shall be first approved by the attorney of one of the parties as to its validity, his approval becomes a condition precedent to the inception of the contract. Wilhelm v. Wood, 151 App. Div. 

42, 135 N. Y. S. 930. And this is true even if the refusal to approve is wholly unreasonable. Van Iderstine Co. v. 357

§§ 432, 433  

Barnet Leather Co., 242 N. Y. 425, 152 N. E. 250, 46 

A. L. R. 858.

Where in a cost plus contract the contractor agrees to build houses for the owner, under the direction and to the satisfaction of the owner's superintendent, is the contractor entitled to compensation if the superintendent is honestly dissatisfied either with the work as completed or with the methods used by the contractor, provided he believes that such methods are extravagant and have led to an increase in the cost? Thompson-Starrett Co. v. La Belle Iron Works, 

17 Fed. (2d) 536. 

§ 433. Building Contract—Architect's Certificate. 

Where a building or construction contract provides that the contractor shall not be entitled to any payment until he has obtained a certificate from the architect or the engineer that the work has been done in strict accordance with the drawings and specifications and that the payment is properly due, the contractor cannot recover for work performed under the contract without obtaining such certificate, unless the provisions requiring it have been waived, or he is prevented from obtaining it by some wrongful act of the owner, or by the fraudulent, collusive, or unreasonable refusal of the architect. Such is the rule in New York and in many other states. Sweet v. Morrison, 116 N. Y. 19, 22 N. E. 276; Neidlinger v. Onward Construction Co., 107 

App. Div. 398, 95 N. Y. S. 1148, aff'd 188 N. Y. 572, 80 

N. E. 1114; Hamilton College v. Roberts, 223 N. Y. 56, 119

N. E. 97; Hennebique Construction Co. v. Boston Cold Stor age & Terminal Co., 230 Mass. 456, 119 N. E. 948, L. R. A. 

1918F 374 and note. However, there are authorities which hold that, unless the architect acts fraudulently, there can be no recovery by the builder, and that the fact that the architect acted unreasonably or unfairly does not dispense with the necessity of obtaining his certificate. See Second 

National Bank v. Pan-American Bridge Co., 183 Fed. 391; 

Tullis v. Jacson, 3 Ch. (Eng.) 441 (1892); Landstra v. 

Bunn, 81 N. J. L. 680, 80 Atl. 496. 358

CHAPTER XXI  § 434. Definition of Breach. 

A breach of a contract consists of an unjustified nonperformance of a contractual duty by a party to the contract. It may take place through a repudiation of the contract or by conduct inconsistent with the contract. Howard v. Daly, 61 N. Y. 362, 19 Am. Rep. 285. $435.

Effect of Breach. 

The effect of a breach of a contract is to create a new obligation; namely, the duty to compensate the party injured by the breach. The breach always gives to the injured party a right of action and frequently discharges him from further contractual obligations to the other party. 

It does not have this effect, however, where the breach relates merely to a subsidiary part of the contract. For instance, where there is substantial performance of a building contract, the failure of the contractor fully to perform does not discharge the owner from his obligation to make payment, although the owner may make proper deductions for damages. Nolan v. Whitney, 88 N. Y. 648; Roehm v. 

Horst, 178 U. S. 1, 44 Law. Ed. 953, 20 Sup. Ct. Rep. 780. § 436. 

Rights of Party Discharged. 

Where a party to a contract is discharged by the default of the other party, he may: 

1. Consider himself exonerated and resist an action brought against him for non-performance on his part, and sue on quantum meruit to recover the reasonable value of the part, if any, which he has performed. Kokomo Strawboard Co. v. Inman, 134 N. Y. 92, 31 N. E. 248; Henderson 

Tire & Rubber Co. v. Wilson & Son, 235 N. Y. 489, 499, 139 

N. E. 583; or 

2. Sue at once for such damages as he has sustained, without showing full performance on his part. He need 359

§§ 436 

not wait until the time fixed for performance. Canda v. 

Wick, 100 N. Y. 127, 2 N. E. 381; Henderson Tire & Rubber Co. v. Wilson & Son, supra. 

§ 437. Modes of Discharge by Breach. 

A breach of contract may occur in one of three ways: 

Either party may renounce the contract: 1. a. 

Before performance is due, or b. In the course of performance. 2. 

Either party may make it impossible for the other to perform: a. 

Before performance is due, or b. In the course of performance. 3. 

Either party may, without justification or excuse, totally or partially fail to perform that which he promised. § 438. 

Renunciation before Performance Is Due.

An unqualified and positive refusal to perform a contract before performance is due constitutes a complete breach of the contract, often called "an anticipatory breach." The parties have a right to rely upon the contractual relationship being maintained from the date of the making of the contract until the performance is due, and renunciation before this time gives the injured party a right to an immediate action. A contract is a contract from the time it is made and not from the time it is to be performed. For instance, on February 1, 1940, A employs B for one year, the employment to begin September 1, 1940. On March 

1, 1940, A writes B that he will not require his services. B may bring an action instantly for damages for the breach of the contract. Roehm v. Horst, 178 U. S. 1, 44 Law. Ed. 953, 20 Sup. Ct. Rep. 780; Petschke v. Rohdiek, 173 N. Y. S. 380.

The rule that an immediate right of action arises upon renunciation applies to only three classes of contracts: 

Contracts for personal service; 1. 

2. Contracts for the sale or manufacture of goods.

360

§ 

Kelly v. Security Mutual Life Insurance Co., 186 N. Y. 16, 

78 N. E. 584. 

3. Contracts to marry. (Right of recovery, however, completely abolished by statute in New York as well as in other states. Civil Practice Act, sec. 61-b.) 

The right to sue immediately upon a renunciation does not apply to contracts for the payment of money at a future time. In some jurisdictions the principle is disapproved unqualifiedly. Porter v. Supreme Council American Legion of Honor, 183 Mass. 326, 67 N. E. 238. An interesting and important case in this connection is Hall v. Nassau Consumers Ice Co., 260 N. Y. 417, 183 N. E. 903.

The party not in default is not compelled to act immediately upon the renunciation. He may treat the contract as still subsisting, and defer his action until the time for performance arrives. Ga Nun v. Palmer, 202 N. Y. 483, 96 

N. E. 99, 36 L. R. A. (N.S.) 922; De Forest Radio Telephone & Telegraph Co. v. Triangle Radio Supply Co., 243 

N. Y. 283, 153 N. E. 75. However, upon an anticipatory repudiation of the contract by the buyer, the seller may elect to consider his contractual obligation at an end and sue at once as for an anticipatory breach of the contract. But the seller may elect to waive the anticipatory breach and to treat the contract as alive, in which case the contract is kept alive for the benefit of both parties, so that the buyer has as much right to a performance on the seller's part as the seller has a right to a performance on the buyer's part. 

Lace Selling Co. v. Shapiro, 249 N. Y. 68, 162 N. E. 586.

If, after an election to treat the contract as still subsisting, the seller fails to perform according to the terms of the contract, the buyer may avail himself of any legal advantage which he may have by reason of the seller's failure to perform. "The buyer may now insist that the seller's misconduct shall be cast in the balance with its own." Rub ber Trading Co. v. Manhattan Rubber Manufacturing Co., 

221 N. Y. 120, 126, 116 N. E. 789. 361

§§ 439, 440  § 439. Anticipatory Breach-Essential Elements. 

To give the injured party an immediate right of action, the renunciation must be: 1. 

Unequivocal and absolute; 2. 

Regarded as renunciation by the other party; and 

3. Must terminate the entire contract.

There is authority for the principle that under certain circumstances an anticipatory inability to perform may amount, in legal contemplation, to an anticipatory repudia tion of the contract. Hall v. Nassau Consumers Ice Co., 260 

N. Y. 417, 183 N. E. 903; Restatement, Contracts, sec. 318. 

§ 440. 

Renunciation in Course of Performance.
