A repudiation of a contract in the course of its performance brings the contract to an end. The other party is not at liberty to disregard the renunciation. He cannot complete the contract, if, by so doing, the damages are increased. However, he is fully protected. Two remedies are available to him. He may elect to treat the contract as rescinded and recover, on quantum meruit, the reasonable value of that part of the contract which he has performed, or he may sue for damages for breach of contract and recover what the labor and material furnished are actually worth plus the loss of profits measured by legal principles applicable in the assessment of damages in the particular case. For instance, in building and construction contracts the loss of profits would be the difference between the contract price and the cost of performance, assuming the cost to be less than the contract price. Clark v. Marsiglia, 1 

Denio (N.Y.) 317, 43 Am. Dec. 670; Wright v. Reusens, 133 N. Y. 298, 305, 31 N. E. 215. See, also, Louvin Realty 

Corp. v. City of New York, 242 App. Div. 181, 272 N. Y. S. 

752. So, also, where there is a contract for advertising and the defendant notifies the publisher to discontinue further insertions of the advertisements contracted for, the publisher is entitled to recover the profits he would have made if the notice had not been given and he had fully performed. 362

§§ 440, 441 

J. K. Rishel Furniture Co. v. Stuyvesant Co., 123 Misc. 208, 204 N. Y. S. 659.

Where the action is based on quantum meruit, the contract price is important only in so far as it is evidence of the value of the labor and material furnished. The value may be, but is not necessarily, measured by the contract price. Connolly v. Sullivan, 173 Mass. 1, 53 N. E. 143.

Where an attorney, engaged under a contract for a definite purpose and not under a general retainer, is discharged without cause, he is entitled to recover on quantum meruit the fair and reasonable value of the services rendered. Matter of Montgomery, 272 N. Y. 323, 6 N. E. (2d) 

40, 109 A. L. R. 669. The client, however, is not compelled to pay damages for breach of contract, for he is exercising the right of discharge given to him by law. Martin v. 

Camp, 219 N. Y. 170, 114 N. E. 46, L. R. A. 1917F 402.

The repudiation by a client of a contract with an attorney whereby the attorney was to serve for a contingent fee entitles the lawyer to recover only for the value of services rendered and not for the profits that would result from a successful prosecution of the litigation. This is true for the reason that the client may discontinue the suit at any time. 

Andrewes v. Haas, 214 N. Y. 255, 108 N. E. 423, 3 A. L. R. 

458. However, an attorney who is discharged for sufficient cause has no right of recovery. Holmes v. Evans, 129 N. Y. 

140, 29 N. E. 233. For a discussion of what constitutes cause for an attorney's discharge, see Editorial, New York

Law Journal, January 14, 1939. 

§ 441. Impossibility Created by Either Party before Per formance Is Due.

Where the performance of a contract is made impossible by one of the parties, the effect is the same as repudiation. 

The party making the performance impossible does not discharge himself from liability. The injured party has an immediate right of action. Thus, if A promises to sell and deliver specified goods to B at a certain time, but before the time arrives, he sells and delivers the goods to C, A disables 363

§§ 441  

himself from performing his contract. B is discharged and may bring action at once. Where one thus disables himself from the performance of a contract, he waives performance by the other party. Woolner v. Hill, 93 N. Y. 

576; Editorial, New York Law Journal, December 24, 1938. 

§ 442. Impossibility Created by One Party in Course of Performance. 

The rule applicable to cases where performance is made impossible before the time of performance is due is applied to cases of impossibility created by one party during the course of performance. Where A agreed to furnish air propellers to remove the smoke from B's tempering room, B to furnish the power from a shaft in such room, and the machine to be subject to a thirty days' successful trial, it was held that the fact that the shaft broke and that B declined to furnish a new shaft, thus making impossible the stipulated test, rendered B liable to A for the contract price. 

Howard v. American Manufacturing Co., 162 N. Y. 347, 56

N. E. 986. Similarly, where a heating plant was sold under a contract by which the vendor was allowed a year in which to remedy any defects, the removal of the plant by the vendee before the expiration of the year made the vendee liable for the contract price. Lehmann v. Warren Webster & Co., 209 Ill. 264, 70 N. E. 600.

So, too, where the plaintiff sold to the defendant a monument upon which were to be inscribed four lines of verse to be furnished by the defendant, it was held that, if the defendant refused to furnish the lines of verse, the plaintiff could erect the monument without the verse and recover the contract price, less the cost of inscribing the verse. Eastern 

Granite Co. v. Heim, 89 Iowa 698, 57 N. W. 437.

FAILURE OF PERFORMANCE § 443. 

Partial or Total. 

Whether a partial or a total failure to perform a promise or covenant of a contract discharges the adverse party de- 364

§ pends upon the nature and character of the respective promises. The question concretely stated is this: Where A and B make mutual promises, must a performance by A precede a performance by B; or may B be liable to A even though A has not performed; or must both A and B be ready to perform at the same time? This can be answered only by a determination of the relation of the promises. 

There are three possible relations: 

1. The promises, although mutual, may be independent of each other; 

2. One promise may be dependent and the other independent; 

3. The promises may be mutually dependent, one upon the other. For historical bases of rules, see Pordage v. 

Cole, 1 Wms. Saund. 319, 85 Eng. Rep. 449. 

Where the promises are mutually independent of each other, either party to the contract may sue without showing performance. Where one promise is independent and the other dependent, the performance of the independent promise must precede the performance of the dependent promise. 

Where the promises are dependent or concurrent, the performance of both must be simultaneous-there must be a readiness or willingness to perform at the same time. 

Where the promises are thus concurrent, neither party can sue until he has put the other in default. Delaware Trust 

Co. v. Calm, 195 N. Y. 231, 88 N. E. 53.

In any particular case, whether the promises are dependent or independent, or dependent in part and independent in part is to be determined by the intention of the parties as ascertained from the contract. The general rule, however, is to consider all covenants dependent, in the absence of a contrary intention, for this is the way that most men make their bargains-neither party intends to perform unless the other at the same time performs on his part. The Personal Property Law, Sales Act, section 123, expressly provides that, unless otherwise agreed, delivery of the goods and payment of the price are concurrent conditions. A different construction would lead in many cases 365

§ 

to the greatest injustice, since a purchaser might have payment enforced against him without procuring the property for which he paid. Delaware Trust Co. v. Calm, supra. 

But where the contract clearly indicates that one promise is to be performed prior to the other, a suit may be had on the prior promise without showing a readiness to perform the promise dependent thereupon. To illustrate: A land contract contained the following stringent provisions:

The seller covenanted that "after the making of the payments and the full performance of the covenants to be made and performed by the purchaser," the vendor in consideration thereof, would convey by deed certain real property. The purchaser went into possession, made improvements, and paid all the instalments except the last, but tendered the last payment upon condition that the deed be delivered simultaneously. The vendor was not ready to tender a deed, but sued for the last payment without tender or offer to convey. The actual payment or tender of payment was held to be a condition precedent to the right to a conveyance. Eastern Oregon Land Co. v. Moody, 198 

Fed. 7.

Where a contract for the sale of land provides for partial or instalment payments of the purchase money prior to a delivery of the deed, the vendor may sue for each instalment when due without tendering a conveyance. But when the last instalment falls due, the payment of the unpaid purchase money and the conveyance or delivery of the deed ordinarily become dependent upon acts concurrent in point of time. The vendor cannot maintain an action to recover the purchase price without proving a readiness or offer, before suit, to convey the land to the vendee upon receipt of the purchase price. Ewing v. Wightman, 167 N. Y. 107, 60 

N. E. 322; Beecher v. Conradt, 13 N. Y. 108, 64 Am. Dec. 

535; Walker v. Hewitt, 109 Ore. 366, 220 Pac. 147, 35 

A. L. R. 100 and note.

Mutually independent promises give rise to mutual remedies in case of default by either promisor. For instance, where A gave B his non-negotiable note and B, in considera- 366

§ tion thereof, agreed to have satisfied, as of record, certain claims held by X which were liens on A's property, no time of performance being specified, it was held that the promises were independent of each other and that A should not succeed in an action for a cancelation of the note by reason of B's failure to perform his promise. A's remedy is to sue for damages for the breach of B's promise. Tronson v. 

Colby University, 9 N. D. 559, 84 N. W. 474. 

Independent promises are further illustrated in the case. of Waterhouse v. Kendall, 11 Cush. (Mass.) 128. In this case the action was upon a promissory note and the defendant pleaded the general issue, and relied upon a receipt for the note in which the plaintiff promised to procure and deliver a certain execution issued in favor of a third party against the defendant, which execution, he attempted to show, had never been so delivered up. Upon appeal from a verdict in favor of the plaintiff, the Court, by Shaw, C.J., overruling the defendant's exceptions, said: “This is a note unlimited in its terms for $105.37, payable to the plaintiff, or the same thing. Can the defendant set up the receipt in defense? What is the undertaking? That Springer will procure and deliver up the execution. That is a good consideration for the note. Is it a condition precedent to a recovery upon it? We think not. Here was one undertaking to pay money, another to deliver up the execution. Neither was precedent to the other. The stipulation to procure and deliver up the execution on receiving the note, is a mutual and independent stipulation, executory in its character, a failure to perform which gives a good ground of action against the party undertaking it. Suppose the defendant were sued on the judgment, and these facts could not be proved in defense. Then the other party would be responsible on the undertaking to procure and deliver up the execution, which is, in effect, a contract to indemnify. The contracts were to be fulfilled at different times, and were independent, though mutual."

A promise, the performance of which depends on prior performance of another promise, is illustrated by Northrup 367

§§ 443  

v. Northrup, 6 Cowen (N.Y.) 296, wherein A promised to pay to one X certain rent due and in arrears on a certain farm, and to pay all that should become due on March 25, 1825. B promised that on A's so paying the rent, he would give up and discharge a certain bond and mortgage. In a suit by B for the rent, A pleaded that on March 25, B did not give up and discharge the bond and mortgage. It was held that A's plea was bad, as the performance of his promise was a condition precedent to performance by B. § 444. 

Waiver of Condition Precedent. 

Performance of a condition precedent may be waived by the party for whose benefit it was introduced into the contract. Clark v. West, 193 N. Y. 349, 86 N. E. 1. "But although conditions precedent must be performed, and a partial performance is not sufficient, yet when a contract has been performed in a substantial part, and the other party has voluntarily accepted and received the benefit of the part performance, knowing that the contract was not being fully performed, the latter may thereby be precluded from relying upon the performance of the residue as a condition precedent to his liability to pay for what he has received, and may be compelled to rely upon his claim for damages in respect of the defective performance." Wiley v. Athol, 150 Mass. 426, 23 N. E. 311, 6 L. R. A. 342. See, also, Rosenthal Paper Co. v. National Folding Box & Paper 

Co., 226 N. Y. 313, 123 N. E. 766.

ENTIRE AND DIVISIBLE CONTRACTS § 445. Entire and Divisible Contracts Defined. 

An entire contract is one where full and complete performance on one side constitutes a consideration for the performance on the other. It is one bargain or unit, and it matters not whether there are one or several things to be done. Until the performance is completed, there is no obligation to pay. Full performance becomes a condition 368

§§ 445, 446 

precedent to payment. Ming v. Corbin, 142 N. Y. 334, 37 

N. E. 105; Gorse v. Lynch, 36 Misc. 150, 72 N. Y. S. 1054.

A divisible contract is one which in its nature and purposes is susceptible of division and apportionment. If the contract is divisible, a failure to perform one item does not entitle the other party to rescind the contract and to refuse to accept further performance, nor does it discharge him from the obligation to pay for the part which has been performed. Tipton v. Feitner, 20 N. Y. 423.

Whether a contract is entire or divisible is often a question of intention and not infrequently one of fact. Portfolio v. Rubin, 233 N. Y. 439, 445, 135 N. E. 843; Stearns 

Salt & Lumber Co. v. Dennis Lumber Co., 188 Mich. 700, 154 N. W. 91, 2 A. L. R. 638 and note. 

§ 446. General Rules for Determining Entirety and Divisibility of Contracts. 

Parsons lays down the following rule for determining whether a contract is entire or divisible: "If the part to be performed by one party consists of several distinct and separate items, and the price to be paid by the other is apportioned to each item to be performed, or is left to be implied by law, such a contract will generally be held to be severable. * *And if the consideration to be paid is single and entire, the contract must be held to be entire, although the subject of the contract may consist of several distinct and wholly independent items." Parsons on Contracts, 9th Ed., Vol. II, p. 673. This doctrine is narrower than the cases, as a whole, would seem to justify. 

The entirety of the contract does not necessarily depend on the nature of the items and the price apportioned to each or all thereof. If the seller was unwilling to sell one portion without selling the whole, and the buyer was unwilling to take a part unless he could have the whole, the contract is entire in its nature. Rugg v. Moore, 110 Pa. 236, 1 Atl. 

320. So, too, the contract is presumably entire where it is for the sale of a quantity of goods, although the price is based upon weight or measure, at a certain rate per unit; 369

§ 

or where the taking of the whole is essential for the purposes of the buyer; or where the contract, by its terms, nature, and purpose shows that all of its parts should be common each to the others, and that all were necessary to accomplish the purpose for which the contract was made, and this, too, even though the consideration was apportioned to the separate items. Hull v. Littauer, 8 App. Div. 

227, 40 N. Y. S. 338, aff'd 162 N. Y. 569, 57 N. E. 102; 

Seibert v. Dunn, 216 N. Y. 237, 110 N. E. 447; Manda v. 

Sullivan County Club, 16 Misc. 366, 38 N. Y. S. 55. See, also, the authorities collected in 2 A. L. R. 643; and Editorial, New York Law Journal, May 7, 1937. 

Illustrative cases:

The plaintiff contracted to sell to the defendant his stock of goods and, also, two stores and lots, all at the same time and embraced in the same contract. The contract treated the goods and the real property as distinct subjects of sale, the price of each being definitely fixed. It was held to be a divisible contract, and the avoidance of the contract by the plaintiff as to the land did not avoid the contract as to the stock of goods. Wooten v. Walters, 110 N. C. 251, 14 

S. E. 734.

A contract, by which the plaintiff agreed to deliver oak logs for $1 per thousand, and other logs for 75 cents per thousand, from certain points on a stream to a certain boom, and to deliver cross-ties at 3 cents each to a certain place, was held to be severable, and to entitle the plaintiff to recover at the specified rates for all logs and ties delivered at the specified points. Gill v. Johnstown Lumber 

Co., 151 Pa. 534, 25 Atl. 120.

Where a landscape architect submitted an estimate for labor and materials in laying out grounds, specifying the different items of labor, trees, vines, grass seed, etc., giving the price and sum of each item, and also recommended three tons, at $38 per ton, of sheep manure for fertilizing the ground, and his estimate, including the manure, was accepted, it was held that the contract was entire and that the gardener could not sue separately for the manure without 370

§§ 446, 447 

proof of the performance of the entire contract. Manda v. 

Sullivan County Club, supra.

A contract to cut, cure, and stack hay on a ranch, at so much per ton, which did not specify the number of tons, or the number of acres, or the work to be done, or the gross amount to be paid, was held to be a divisible contract and recovery could be had for each ton stacked. Hindrey v. 

Williams, 9 Colo. 371, 12 Pac. 436.

A contract to build a brick house, brick stable, and other buildings, each at a specified sum, was held to be divisible and the contractor entitled to recover for building the stable, although he abandoned the contract as to the other buildings. White v. Livingston, 69 App. Div. 361, 75 

N. Y. S. 466, aff'd 174 N. Y. 538, 66 N. E. 1118.

A contract to paint a house, barn, and windmill for a lump sum has been held to be entire, and the workman cannot recover for part, or unsubstantial, performance. 

Pearse v. Divver, 23 Misc. 691, 52 N. Y. S. 87. 

§ 447. 

Rescission of Entire and Divisible Contract. 

Where the contract is entire, the rescission must be as to the whole contract. A party cannot disaffirm the contract in part and enforce it in part. He must also place the other party in status quo. If this cannot be done, the contract cannot be rescinded. Where the contract is divisible, a failure by one of the parties to perform one of the terms does not authorize the other party to rescind the whole contract and refuse performance of the other terms by the party in default in the first instance. Morgan v. Mc- 

Kee, 77 Pa. 228; Rubin v. Sturtevant, 80 Fed. 930.

A contract for the sale of two piles of lumber for a single consideration is entire, and the purchaser cannot accept part of the lumber and reject the remainder on the ground that the part rejected does not meet the requirements of the contract. Levy v. Dettra & Co., 91 Misc. 41, 154 

N. Y. S. 176. 

Divisibility of the price does not of itself make what 371

§§ 447  

would otherwise be an entire contract divisible. But where in a contract for the sale of goods, the price is divisible, the parties may elect to treat the contract as divisible. This they did in Portfolio v. Rubin, 233 N. Y. 439, 135 N. E. 

843, where the defendant agreed to purchase four pieces of goods to be made into clothes. Upon delivery he accepted two only and rejected the other two as not in accordance with the order. The price of the goods accepted and those rejected could be easily ascertained by computation. It was held that the defendant had the right to accept part and reject the portion which did not comply with the contract. 

§ 448. Instalment Delivery and Payments. 

Where there is a contract for the sale of goods to be delivered in instalments which are to be paid for separately, authorities differ as to whether the contract is entire or divisible. If the seller makes defective deliveries in respect to one or more instalments, or the buyer neglects or refuses to accept deliveries or to pay for one or more instalments, is there a breach of the whole contract? Does a breach in respect to one instalment give the injured party a right to repudiate the entire contract? 

§ 449. Federal and New York Common Law Rule. 

Both the federal and New York courts have repudiated the doctrine that a sale and delivery of goods in instalments, to be paid for as each instalment is delivered, create a divisible contract. They have held that such contracts are entire, and that provisions for periodical deliveries and payments do not render the contract divisible. Norrington v. 

Wright, 115 U. S. 188, 29 Law. Ed. 366, 6 Sup. Ct. Rep. 12; 

De Vivo v. Gallerani, 105 Misc. 606, 174 N. Y. S. 13. In 

Wolfert v. Caledonia Springs Ice Co., 195 N. Y. 118, 88 

N. E. 24, 21 L. R. A. (N.S.) 864, it was held, in a contract for the sale of goods to be delivered in instalments, that the failure of the seller to deliver or the buyer to accept one instalment constituted such a breach of the contract 372

§§ 449, 450 

as to give to the party not in default the right to rescind the. entire contract. But in this case there was a waiver of the right to insist on full performance. Seibert v. Dunn, 216 

N. Y. 237, 110 N. E. 447. So, also, a contract for the sale of flour by the barrel, to be shipped in instalments, was held to be entire. Consumers Bread Co. v. Stafford County 

Flour Mills Co., 239 Fed. 693. Contra, Gerli v. Poidebard 

Silk Manufacturing Co., 57 N. J. L. 432, 31 Atl. 401, 30 

L. R. A. 61.

Default in an instalment payment also has been held to constitute a breach of the entire contract and to justify repudiation by the seller. Raabe v. Squier, 148 N. Y. 81, 42 

N. E. 516; Ferguson v. Chuck, 236 N. Y. 149, 140 N. E. 225. 

§ 450. 

Sales Act Affecting Instalment Sales. 

The common law rule that a default in an instalment delivery or payment operated as a breach of the entire contract has been modified by statute. The Personal Property 

Law, Sales Act, section 126, subdivision 2, provides: "Where there is a contract to sell goods to be delivered by stated installments, which are to be separately paid for, and the seller makes defective deliveries in respect of one or more installments, or the buyer neglects or refuses to take delivery of or pay for one or more installments, it depends in each case on the terms of the contract and the circumstances of the case whether the breach of contract is so material as to justify the injured party in refusing to proceed further and suing for damages for breach of the entire contract, or whether the breach is severable, giving rise to a claim for compensation, but not to a right to treat the whole contract as broken." The statute thus establishes a like test for vendor and vendee. A mere failure to make a proper delivery of an instalment, of itself, does not necessarily give to the injured party a right to repudiate the entire contract. Such failure, however, when coupled with evidence of an intention not to complete performance, may justify the injured party in refusing to proceed further. 

Bahnsen & Co. v. Gerst, 235 N. Y. 426, 139 N. E. 563. 373

§§ 450  

Deliveries which are defective in quantity or quality may give a right to rescind the contract as for a material breach. 

Thus, where ice is to be delivered in instalments and the quality of some of the ice delivered is defective and the seller admits he cannot furnish better ice, the buyer has been allowed to rescind the contract. Interboro Brewing 

Co. v. Independent Consumers Ice Co., 93 Misc. 24, 156 

N. Y. S. 410; Ellison, Son & Co. v. Flat Top Grocery Co., 69 W. Va. 380, 71 S. E. 391, 38 L. R. A. (N.S.) 539. 

§ 451. Failure To Pay Instalment When Due. 

A failure to pay an instalment when due does not necessarily effect a breach of the entire contract. In Helgar 

Corp. v. Warner's Features, 222 N. Y. 449, 119 N. E. 113, the Court said: "The vendor who fails to receive payment of an installment the very day that it is due, may sue at once for the price. But it does not follow that he may be equally precipitate in his election to declare the contract at an end. That depends upon the question whether the default is so substantial and important as in truth and in fairness to defeat the essential purpose of the parties. Whatever the rule may once have been, this is the test that is now prescribed by statute. The failure to make punctual payment may be material or trivial according to the circumstances." See, however, Bahnsen & Co. v. Gerst, 235 N. Y. 426, 139 N. E. 563; Ferguson v. Chuck, 236 N. Y. 149, 140 N. E. 225. 

$ 452. One Action for Breach of Instalment Contract. 

Since there is but one breach where the default in an instalment delivery or an instalment payment operates to discharge the entire contract, only one cause of action may be maintained thereon, and the injured party cannot sue successively for a breach of each remaining instalment. 

Pakas v. Hollingshead, 184 N. Y. 211, 77 N. E. 40, 3 

L. R. A. (N.S.) 1042 and note. See, also, Williston on Contracts, Rev. Ed., Vol. V, sec. 1292, footnote 10. 374

§§ 453, 454 

$ 453. 

Waiver of Default of Instalment, The failure to perform any instalment may be waived, and when waived the right to rescind is lost. However, where a purchaser of a quantity of gloves of different styles, at a certain price per dozen, claims that the gloves do not comply with the contract, he must reject them as a whole. 

If he accepts a part of the gloves, he becomes liable for the entire lot. Mason v. Smith, 130 N. Y. 474, 29 N. E. 

749. So, too, if the buyer with knowledge of the delivery of defective instalments, remains silent and permits the vendor to incur the expense of preparing and delivering further instalments, he will be estopped from claiming a release from his obligation to receive and pay for further deliveries on account of defects in the earlier deliveries. 

McDonald v. Kansas City Bolt & Nut Co., 149 Fed. 360, 8 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1110 and note; Wolfert v. Caledonia 

Springs Ice Co., 195 N. Y. 118, 88 N. E. 24, 21 L. R. A. (N.S.) 864. 

§ 454. No Date for Payment of Instalments. 

Where a contract is for the delivery of goods in instalments and is silent as to the time of payment, the contract is entire, and payment need not be made at the time of delivery, but only after delivery of all the instalments. But where the contract is divisible, payment is to be made for each instalment on its delivery. Baker v. Higgins, 21 N. Y. 

397; Tipton v. Feitner, 20 N. Y. 423. A contract for the sale of all the brick to be used in the construction of a building, which has no fixed time of payment, is entire; and a vendor would breach his entire contract by a refusal to deliver further instalments until he receives payment for those already delivered. Kelly Construction Co. v. 

Hackensack Brick Co., 91 N. J. L. 585, 103 Atl. 417, 2 

A. L. R. 685. 375

§§ 455 

 INCOMPLETE PERFORMANCE OF ENTIRE CONTRACT § 455. General Rule as to Recovery. 

Where the contract is entire, the plaintiff cannot recover in an action for breach of contract unless he proves: 

1. A complete performance of the contract; 

2. That a performance of the contract was waived by the defendant; 

3. That the defendant prevented a complete performance of the contract; or 

4. That complete performance of the contract was excused by operation of law. 

EXCEPTIONS TO GENERAL RULE § 456. Subsidiary Promises. 

There are cases where the incomplete performance of an entire contract affects only a subordinate part of the undertaking. Such subordinate parts are known as subsidiary promises, the breach of which will not operate to discharge the contract, because it does not defeat the object to be accomplished. Thus, a contract requires an opera singer, whose services are engaged for the season, to be on hand for rehearsal a few days before the season begins. His failure to do so will not justify his employer in rescinding the contract. Bettini v. Gye, 1 Q. B. Div. (Eng.) 183 (1876). § 457. Benefits Conferred.

Cases frequently occur where one party has not fully performed his contract, but, nevertheless, has conferred a benefit upon the other party, and although a complete performance is a condition precedent to payment, under certain conditions the plaintiff may have some redress without having fully performed his obligation. Kauffman v. Raeder, 108 Fed. 171, 54 L. R. A. 247; In re Morgantown Tin Plate

Co., 184 Fed. 109. The question most frequently arises in: 

1. Contracts where the rule as to substantial performance may be invoked; 376

§§ 457 

2. Contracts to repair buildings or to render service on the property of another; 

3. Contracts of employment; 

4. Contracts for the sale of goods. 

§ 458. Substantial Performance. 

For the doctrine of substantial performance, see section 

426, supra.

In building contracts the owner may waive his rights resulting from an unsubstantial performance by acquiescing in the deviations, but mere occupation or use of the building will not constitute a waiver. Notwithstanding the fact that the building as completed has greatly added to the value of the land, the builder cannot recover compensation for unsubstantial performance. Smith v Brady, 17 N. Y. 173, 72 Am. Dec. 442; Spence v. Ham, 163 N. Y. 

220, 57 N. E. 412, 51 L. R. A. 238. 

§ 459. Contracts To Repair Buildings or Chattels. 

The question as to who must bear the loss caused by the destruction of a building or chattel undergoing repair is discussed in section 393, supra.

Where the contract is for the repair of a building which is destroyed before the work is completed, the owner is liable for the work performed, as he is under an obligation to keep the building in readiness to receive the work. 

The same principle is applied to chattels which are destroyed before the completion of the work. The workman is allowed to recover at the price stipulated in the contract, or on quantum meruit if the contract price cannot be made applicable. Whelan v. Ansonia Clock Co., 97 N. Y. 293.

A recovery may be had for the value of the work done under a contract to move a building for a specified price, if the performance of the contract becomes impossible by reason of the destruction of the building through no fault of the contractor. Angus v. Scully, 176 Mass. 357, 57 N. E. 

674, 49 L. R. A. 562; L. R. A. 1917D 1012, note. A con- 377

§§ 459, 460  

trary holding prevails in a minority of jurisdictions. Taulbee v. McCarty, 144 Ky. 199, 137 S. W. 1045, 36 L. R. A. (N.S.) 43. 

§ 460. Contracts of Employment. 

Where a servant voluntarily abandons his contract of employment before the end of his term of service, there can be no recovery for what he has done. Where the contract is entire, complete performance by the servant is a condition precedent to payment. If A engages B to work for one year at a stipulated price, B must perform the entire year's work before he can collect anything from A. 

If A should engage B to work for a year, at a specified rate, payable monthly, B could sue and recover his salary at the end of each month. But if without just cause, B leaves A's employment before the end of the contract period, A may retain from B's wages such damages as were the result of the breach of the contract. The measure of damages is the difference between the contract price and the amount which the employer has to pay to procure the performance of the services by some other person. Triangle Waist Co. v. Todd, 223 N. Y. 27, 119 N. E. 85. See note to Timberlake v. Thayer, 71 Miss. 279, 14 So. 446, 24 L. R. A. 231.

Where an artist, who had contracted with a theatrical producer to stage a ballet for a play to the satisfaction of the producer, deliberately and intentionally breached the contract and walked out during a rehearsal prior to the dress rehearsal and before completion of his contract, it was held that there could be no recovery by the artist on the theory of contract or of quantum meruit. It was further held that the doctrine of substantial performance does not apply and that, therefore, plaintiff's judgment must be reversed and his complaint dismissed. Fokine v. Shubert, 210 App. Div. 468, 206 N. Y. S. 311.

If the completion of the contract is prevented by the employee's death or physical disability, his contractual obligation is discharged. Lacy v. Getman, 119 N. Y. 109, 23 N. E. 452, 6 L. R. A. 728; note to Runyan v. Culver, 168 378

§§ 460 

Ky. 45, 181 S. W. 640, in L. R. A. 1916F 3, at p. 79. A like effect results from the imprisonment of an employee. 

Hughes v. Wamsutta Mills, 11 Allen (Mass.) 201. Where the relation is not that of employer and employee, the imprisonment of one of the parties may operate as a breach and not as a discharge of the contract. Jinnings v. Amend, 101 Kan. 130, 165 Pac. 845, L. R. A. 1917F 626 and note.

Where hiring is for an indefinite period, it is terminable at the will of either party. See section 367, supra. So, too, a contract for "permanent employment" does not by itself justify a conclusion that employment for life was intended, and an employer is not obliged to continue the employee's services when in good faith there is no further use for such services. Arentz v. Morse Dry Dock & Repair Co., 249 

N. Y. 439, 164 N. E. 342, 62 A. L. R. 231; Skagenberg v. 

Blandin Paper Co., 266 N. W. (Minn.) 872. See note, 35 

Michigan Law Review 321. 

§ 461. Contracts of Employment-Recovery on Quantum 

Meruit-Statute of Frauds.

The right to recover on quantum meruit for services rendered under an oral contract which is within the Statute of Frauds is discussed in section 249, supra. 

§ 462. Employee Rightfully Discharged. 

If a servant is rightfully and lawfully discharged because of conduct which incapacitates or disqualifies him from fulfilling his contract, he forfeits his wages for the time he has served. The effect is the same as a voluntary abandonment of the contract without cause. But if the employment is for a year, and the contract provides for monthly payments during the year, the employee, although discharged for just cause, may recover any arrears of monthly salary, subject to recoupment for any damages suffered by the employer by reason of neglect, unskillfulness, or non-performance by the employee. If the servant is discharged during the month, and before the salary therefor becomes due and payable, no recovery can be had 379

§§ 462  

under the contract for the work done during that month, for the reason that the contract is for an entirety as to each month and a recovery for any portion thereof can be had only in an action for damages for breach. Tipton v. Feitner, 20 N. Y. 423; Huntingdon v. Claffin, 38 N. Y. 182; 

Walsh v. New York & Kentucky Co., 88 App. Div. 477, 85 N. Y. S. 83. 

Employee Wrongfully Discharged.

Where an employee is wrongfully discharged, that is, without just cause, he may choose one of three remedies: 

1. He may consider the contract rescinded and sue on quantum meruit for services rendered, less such sum as he has received. 

2. He may wait until the end of the term and then sue for the full amount, less such sum as the defendant has a right to recoup. 

3. He may bring action at once for a breach of the contract. Banta v. Banta, 84 App. Div. 138, 82 N. Y. S. 113; 

Howard v. Daly, 61 N. Y. 362, 19 Am. Rep. 285. 

§ 464. Measure of Damages-Wrongful Discharge. 

Where an employee is wrongfully discharged, the measure of damages, in an action for damages for the breach of the contract, is the amount of his salary under the contract, less any wages he has received or might reasonably have earned in a similar employment since his discharge. 

The burden of showing that the employee earned or might have earned something in another employment is upon the employer. Howard v. Daly, 61 N. Y. 362, 19 Am. Rep. 285; 

Banta v. Banta, 84 App. Div. 138, 82 N. Y. S. 113; Wooldridge v. Shea, 186 App. Div. 705, 175 N. Y. S. 130. Damages for injury to plaintiff's good name or character or for injury resulting in decreased ability to obtain employment in the future are not recoverable in an action for wrongful discharge. Sinclair v. Positype Corp. of America, 237 App. 

Div. 525, 261 N. Y. S. 900. 380

§ 

The discharged employee is under no obligation to accept an employment of a nature different from that for which he contracted, or in a different locality, nor is he under obligation to accept employment from a person against whom there is reasonable ground for objection. Levin v. 

Standard Fashion Co., 16 Daly 404, 11 N. Y. S. 706. See the authorities collected in 28 A. L. R. 736.

The employee may recover damages for the full contract period, although the contract period may not have ended at the time of the trial. Denniston v. Finnegan, 174

App. Div. 8, 160 N. Y. S. 5. Contra, Howay v. Going- 

Northrup Co., 24 Wash. 88, 64 Pac. 135, 6 L. R. A. (N.S.) 

49. Although the damages succeeding the trial may be largely conjectural or uncertain, the jury may approximately estimate the loss by taking into consideration all the facts, probabilities, certainties, and uncertainties. 

Wakeman v. Wheeler & Wilson Manufacturing Co., 101 

N. Y. 205, 4 N. E. 264; Davis v. Dodge, 126 App. Div. 469, 110 N. Y. S. 787; 32 A. L. R. 236, note.

Where the damages, however, are purely conjectural, they will not be allowed. In one case, the plaintiff was employed "for the purposes of promoting the club, acquiring members therefor, and developing and managing the club, or for any one or more of said purposes in the discretion of the owner" for a stated period of time at a rate of compensation equal to a certain percentage of payments made by club members for stock and for initiation fees. In an action to recover damages for wrongful discharge, it was held that the plaintiff could not recover, in addition to the agreed percentage on the amount paid by members before his discharge, a percentage on a sum fixed by the jury as the amount he would have earned if he had not been discharged. The latter is too speculative to constitute legal damages. Willis v. S. M. H. Corp., 259 N. Y. 144, 181 

N. E. 79. 381

§§ 465, 466  § 465. 

Offer To Reinstate Discharged Employee.

Where an employee is wrongfully discharged and thereafter is offered reinstatement by his former employer, it is a debatable question in many cases whether he must accept the offer and waive damages accruing after the offer of reemployment. The answer to the question seems to depend upon the circumstances attending the discharge, and the reasons, if any, for not accepting reemployment. If the employee is discharged after being wrongfully accused by his employer of theft or dishonesty, or if the employee's refusal to resume work is caused by fear of assault, he is justified in refusing reemployment. Horn v. Luntz, 125 N. Y. S. 

786; Levin v. Standard Fashion Co., 16 Daly 404, 11

N. Y. S. 706. Even in cases where the offer to reinstate the employee may be shown in mitigation of damages, the burden is on the employer to show the good faith of the offer. 

Bigelow v. American Forcite Powder Manufacturing Co., 39 Hun 599; Gray v. Pacific Suction Cleaner Co., 171 Cal. 

234, 155 Pac. 469. 

§ 466. Implied Renewal of Contract. 

Parties dealing with each other after the expiration of the period specified by a contract for its operation are ordinarily presumed to proceed upon the same terms as those specified in the contract; the original contract is presumed to have been renewed with all its terms and conditions. 

Huntingdon v. Claffin, 38 N. Y. 182; Douglas v. Merchants' 

Insurance Co., 118 N. Y. 484, 23 N. E. 806, 7 L. R. A. 822; 

Stewart Dry Goods Co. v. Hutchison, 177 Ky. 757, 198 S. W. 

17, L. R. A. 1918C 704 and note. However, there is no presumption of renewal of a written contract of employment which is for a longer period than one year. Schott v. La 

Compagnie Generale Trans-Atlantique, 52 Misc. 236, 102 

N. Y. S. 900; Carter v. Bradlee, 245 App. Div. 49, 280 

N. Y. S. 368, aff'd 269 N. Y. 664, 200 N. E. 48.

The rule that the terms of the original contract control, is also applied to leases where the period of leasing is for one year. Adams v. City of Cohoes, 127 N. Y. 175, 28 382

§ 

N. E. 25; Haynes v. Aldrich, 133 N. Y. 287, 31 N. E. 94, 28 Am. St. Rep. 636. But where the lease is for a longer period than a year, there seems to be some question as to whether a holding over renews the original lease for the full period of its duration or from year to year only. Suppose, for instance, that A leases to B a dwelling or other building for a term of two years. At the end of the second year, without any agreement or even a conference as to a renewal of the lease, B, with A's implied consent, continues in possession, fulfilling all the conditions of the lease, for another year. Six months before the expiration of the third year of tenancy, B gives A notice that he will vacate the premises at the end of the year. A contends that a holding over after the two-year period was, by implication of law, a renewal of the lease for two more years and, hence, seeks to hold B liable for another year's rent. B's contention is that a holding over under a two-year lease constituted a tenancy from year to year. Who is right, A or B? Kennedy v. City of New York, 196 N. Y. 19, 89 N. E. 360, 25 L. R. A. (N.S.) 847 and note. The presumption of renewal arising from continuance of contractual relations after the expiration of the original contract may be completely rebutted by proof that the parties continued their contractual relationship on a different basis. 

The Court of Appeals has thus summarized the law: "Where, after the expiration of a contract fixing reciprocal rights and obligations of the parties, they continue to do business together, the conduct of the parties may at times permit, or even constrain, a finding that the parties impliedly agree that their rights and obligations in connection with such business should continue to be measured as provided in the old contract. Even in such case, however, the reciprocal obligations arise from the new implied contract and, unless an intent to make such a new contract is expressed or may be fairly inferred from the conduct of the parties, the obligations of the parties are as matter of law not measured by the terms of the contract which has 383

§§ 466, 467  expired." New York Telephone Co. v. Jamestown Tele phone Corp., 282 N. Y. 365, 26 N. E. (2d) 295. 

§ 467. Sale of Goods-Defective Delivery-New York Common Law Rule. 

When the seller delivers only a part of the goods contracted for, he cannot recover for the part delivered, where the buyer received such part without knowledge that the balance would not be delivered. Champlin v. Rowley, 18 

Wend. (N.Y.) 187. In this case the contract called for the sale of 100 tons of hay, to be delivered between certain dates and at a certain price per ton, part to be paid for in advance and the residue when the whole should be delivered. 

The seller delivered only 52 tons, and after the expiration of the time fixed for the delivery of the whole, brought action to recover for the quantity delivered at the stipulated price. It was held that there could be no recovery, since the buyer had neither waived nor prevented a full performance. The ruling in this case has been followed in New 

York and in some other jurisdictions. Catlin v. Tobias, 26 

N. Y. 217, 84 Am. Dec. 183; Nightingale v. Eiseman, 121 

N. Y. 288, 24 N. E. 475. A limitation of the doctrine of

Champlin v. Rowley, supra, was introduced in Avery v. 

Willson, 81 N. Y. 341, 37 Am. Rep. 503. The contract involved in this case was for the delivery of 699 boxes of glass at one time, and the buyer accepted the delivery of a part, without knowledge that the rest was not to be delivered, but without any reservation. It was held that the seller could recover for the glass delivered. The case was distinguished from the earlier one on the ground that as the hay was capable of delivery in parcels, the buyer could not reject a partial delivery, and, hence, there was no waiver of the condition that the whole must be delivered; but that in the case at bar, the delivery of the whole being required to be made at one time, the buyer could decline to receive a partial delivery, and, consequently, acceptance of a partial delivery operated as a waiver of the condition. 384

§§ 467 

Brady v. Cassidy, 145 N. Y. 171, 39 N. E. 814; 2 A. L. R. 

663, note. 

§ 468. 

Defective Delivery-Statutory Rule. 

The common law rule that a seller of goods who has only partly performed cannot recover, even on quantum meruit, has been modified by statute. The Personal Property Law, Sales Act, section 125, provides that if "the buyer has used or disposed of the goods delivered before he knows that the seller is not going to perform his contract in full, the buyer shall not be liable for more than the fair value to him of the goods so received." This, apparently, makes the buyer liable for the "fair value to him" of the goods used, although it is not affirmatively so stated. Kirshman v. Crawford-Plummer Co., 165 App. Div. 259, 150 N. Y. S. 886; Dancey-Davis

Press, Inc. v. American Fashion Co., 120 Misc. 157, 197 

N. Y. S. 844. Where the buyer waives full performance he is liable at the contract rate. Weinberg v. Gash, 94 Misc. 

303, 158 N. Y. S. 179. The failure of the seller to make prompt delivery under one contract is no excuse for the buyer's rejection of a tender of goods under another contract made at the same time. Doxey v. Coates, Bennett & 

Reidenbach, Inc., 181 App. Div. 207, 168 N. Y. S. 76. $469.

Abandonment of Contract after Part Payment. 

A vendee who, after making a part payment, abandons or refuses to complete performance of a contract, cannot recover any part of the money paid; and this seems to be true, whether the abandonment be wilful or from necessity occasioned by poverty or illness. Lawrence v. Miller, 86 N. Y. 

131; Steinhardt v. Baker, 163 N. Y. 410, 57 N. E. 629; Hurley v. Anicker, 51 Okla. 97, 151 Pac. 593, L. R. A. 1918B 

538 and note. 

§ 470. Right To Recover Payments Made on Oral Con tract within Statute of Frauds. 

Where the vendor is unable or unwilling to convey land under a contract of sale which is unenforceable because 385

§§ 470  

of the Statute of Frauds, the vendee may recover payments made upon the contract; but where the vendor is willing to perform, the vendee cannot recover the payments made. 

Graham v. Healy, 154 App. Div. 76, 138 N. Y. S. 611; Cook v. Griffith, 76 W. Va. 799, 86 S. E. 879, L. R. A. 1916D 466 and note.

SPECIFIC PERFORMANCE AND INJUNCTION § 471. 

Specific Performance of Contract. 

A court of equity may enforce a promise to do a thing by a decree of specific performance; and, by the issuance of an injunction, it may enforce a promise not to do a particular act. These remedies may be had only when the common law remedy of an action for damages for breach of contract would be inadequate to repair the loss sustained and where the contract is valid, fair and mutual, its enforcement equitable, and it is susceptible of present performance. The fact that an action at law could not be based upon the contract does not prevent a suit for specific performance. 

Winne v. Winne, 166 N. Y. 263, 59 N. E. 832. While a contract must be mutual in its remedy to warrant specific performance, such mutuality need not exist at the inception of the contract. The requirement of mutuality is regarded as satisfied if there is mutuality of remedy at the time the relief is sought. Epstein v. Gluckin, 233 N. Y. 490, 135 

N. E. 861. 

§ 472. 

Contracts Relating to Real Property. 

Contracts for the sale of real property may be enforced specifically, because the land may have been selected by consideration of health, convenience, or neighborhood, and money damages cannot replace the same property. The remedy of specific performance is open to both vendor and vendee. Walter v. Hoffman, 267 N. Y. 365, 196 N. E. 

291, 101 A. L. R. 919; Reformed Protestant Dutch Church v. Madison Avenue Building Co., 214 N. Y. 268, 108 N. E. 

444, L. R. A. 1915F 651. 386

$ 

§ 473. 

Contracts Relating to Personal Property. 

If the contract is for the sale of goods and chattels which may be obtained readily from other dealers, the contract will not be enforced in equity, because the legal remedy for damages is adequate and enables the party recovering damages to buy the same goods or chattels from others with the money recovered; but, if the contract is for the sale of an article which no one but the vendor can supply, the vendee may obtain a decree for specific performance. "While it may be conceded that in general a court of equity will not take upon itself to make such decree where chattel property alone is concerned, its jurisdiction to do so is no longer to be doubted, and it is believed that no good reason exists against its exercise in any case where compensation in damages would not furnish a complete and satisfactory remedy." 

Johnson v. Brooks, 93 N. Y. 337; Menier v. Donald, 98 Misc. 

684, 165 N. Y. S. 50; Adams v. Messinger, 147 Mass. 185, 17 N. E. 491; Southern Iron & Equipment Co. v. Vaughan, 201 Ala. 356, 78 So. 212, L. R. A. 1918E 594 and note.

A contract for the purchase of stock freely sold in the market is not specifically enforceable, since there is an adequate legal remedy; but if the stock is unobtainable from any person other than the vendor, the contract may be specifically enforced. Waddle v. Cabana, 220 N. Y. 18, 114 

N. E. 1054; Rimes v. Rimes, 152 Ga. 721, 111 S. E. 34, 22 

A. L. R. 1030 and note.

On this question, the Personal Property Law, Sales Act, section 149, provides: "Where the seller has broken a contract to deliver specific or ascertained goods, a court having the powers of a court of equity may, if it thinks fit, on the application of the buyer, by its judgment or decree direct that the contract shall be performed specifically, without giving the seller the option of retaining the goods on payment of damages. The judgment or decree may be unconditional, or upon such terms and conditions as to damages, payment of the price and otherwise, as to the court may seem just." This section is probably declaratory of the 387

§§ 473, 474  

rules that already existed.

Godley, Outline of Equity, 2d Ed., sec. 130. 

§ 474. Contracts for Personal Services. 

Contracts for personal services never can be specifically enforced, although where a contract stipulates for special, unique, or extraordinary personal services or acts, as, for example, by an eminent actor, singer, artist, or the like, a court of equity will grant an injunction prohibiting the rendition of services for others while under contract with the plaintiff. An injunction thus restraining the breach of a contract is characterized as a negative specific enforcement of the contract. If, however, the services are not special or unique, an injunction will not be granted. Hammerstein v. 

Mann, 137 App. Div. 580, 122 N. Y. S. 276; Shubert Theatrical Co. v. Gallagher, 200 App. Div. 596, 193 N. Y. S. 

401, same case, 206 App. Div. 514, 201 N. Y. S. 577; 

Chambers v. Davis, 128 Miss. 613, 91 So. 346, 22 A. L. R. 

114 and note. 

Parties cannot contract that courts shall or shall not exercise their discretion against or in favor of either party. 

Dockstader v. Reed, 121 App. Div. 846, 106 N. Y. S. 795. 

Where there is no express negative covenant in a contract, there is some doubt as to whether the courts will imply one. 

Apparently they will in New York. Harry Rogers Theatrical Enterprises v. Comstock, 225 App. Div. 34, 232 

N. Y. S. 1.

An injunction will lie to restrain an employee whose position gives him possession of his employer's trade secrets from violating his contract not to enter the service of a rival concern within the duration of the contract. McCall Co. v. 

Wright, 198 N. Y. 143, 91 N. E. 516, 31 L. R. A. (N.S.) 249 and note; Clark Paper & Manufacturing Co. v. Stenacher, 236 N. Y. 312, 140 N. E. 708, 29 A. L. R. 1325; Kaumagraph Co. v. Stampagraph Co., 235 N. Y. 1, 138 N. E. 485;

Mutual Milk & Cream Co. v. Prigge, 112 App. Div. 652, 

98 N. Y. S. 458. See, also, 22 Cornell Law Quarterly 246. 388

CHAPTER XXII DISCHARGE OF RIGHT OF ACTION § 475. Three Modes of Discharge. 

The right of action arising from a breach of contract may be discharged in any one of three ways: 

1. By consent of the parties; 2. 

By judgment of a court of competent jurisdiction; or 

3. By lapse of time. 

§ 476. 

Discharge by Consent.

The discharge of a right of action by consent may be by: 

1. Release; or 2. 

Accord and satisfaction. 

§ 477. Release. 

A release is a written declaration discharging a right or cause of action. When comprehensive in its scope or effect, as it frequently is, it is familiarly known as a "general release." Under the common law, a release, like other instruments, must be supported by a consideration; but where the release is under seal, the consideration to support it cannot be questioned, since the seal raises a conclusive presumption that there was a sufficient consideration. Stiebel v. Grosberg, 202 N. Y. 266, 95 N. E. 692, 36 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1147; 

Orthey v. Bogan, 236 N. Y. 351, 140 N. E. 722.

Recent legislation has considerably modified the common law principles applicable to releases. Thus, it has been enacted that a written release or discharge of any debt or obligation "shall not be invalid because of the absence of consideration or a seal." Debtor and Creditor Law, sec. 

243. This statute does not set forth the conditions upon which a release shall be valid, but it does remove common law limitations on otherwise valid releases. Elsewhere, it is provided that an agreement to discharge any obligation in whole or in part shall not be invalid for lack of consideration if the agreement is in writing and signed by the party 389

§  

to whom the debt or obligation is owed. Personal Property 

Law, sec. 33, subd. 2; Real Property Law, sec. 279. Thus, a release is valid and enforceable to-day if it is agreed to orally and supported by consideration, or if it is in writing signed by the obligee, whether it is supported by consideration or not.

On releases under seal executed prior to September 1, 1935, the common law principle that the seal is conclusive of a consideration is still applicable. On such instruments parol evidence is not admissible for the purpose of establishing that the release is without any consideration to sustain it. The present statute which provides that a seal is no longer either conclusive or presumptive evidence of consideration places sealed releases on the same basis, in this regard, as unsealed written releases. See Civil Practice 

Act, sec. 342.

Though consideration is unnecessary to make a written release valid, a release may be given in return for a promised consideration, or subject to some condition. Under the common law affecting releases under seal, it might be shown by parol: 

1. That the agreed consideration has not been paid. 

2. That the release under seal was delivered under an agreement that it should not become operative until the happening of some condition precedent: Stiebel v. Grosberg, supra; Richardson on Evidence, 5th Ed., sec. 427.

Under the present statutes, these principles of the parol evidence rule would obviously apply in the same way to releases in writing, whether sealed or not.

A release may be employed also to discharge a right of action arising out of a tort. Where there are several tortfeasors, the release of one before judgment will discharge all, unless the release expressly reserves the right to sue any person not named therein. Gilbert v. Finch, 173 N. Y. 

455, 66 N. E. 133, 61 L. R. A. 807; Berry v. Pullman Co., 249 Fed. 816, L. R. A. 1918F 358. See section 339, supra.

A right of action arising upon a negotiable instrument may be discharged by a delivery of the instrument, or by 390

DISCHARGE OF RIGHT OF ACTION §§ 477, 478 

a gratuitous renunciation in writing. Negotiable Instruments Law, sec. 203. § 478. 

Discharge by Accord and Satisfaction.

An accord and satisfaction results from the performance of an agreement between two persons whereby a disputed claim is to be settled by one party paying or giving something to the other party in full satisfaction of the claim. 

When the new agreement is executed, it is called "accord and satisfaction." The "accord" is the agreement, and its performance is the "satisfaction." The terms are copulative. Kromer v. Heim, 75 N. Y. 574, 31 Am. Rep. 491.

Perhaps there is no technical expression in the law of contracts that has given rise to so much controversy as that of "accord and satisfaction." Various expressions, such as "compromise and settlement," "novation," and "substituted agreement," have been intermingled with the doctrine of "accord and satisfaction," until even the courts do not seem clear as to the extent to which the doctrine applies. Whatever uncertainty has heretofore existed as to what might constitute a "satisfaction" of the "accord" is now quite definitely settled by statutory provisions, provided the accord is in writing and signed by the person against whom it is to be enforced. The Personal Property Law, section 33-a, provides for an executory accord. This means that a signed promise in writing to accept something in the future in full satisfaction of the present disputed claim is binding and constitutes a complete "accord and satisfaction." Cf. Bandman v. Finn, 185 N. Y. 508, 78 N. E. 175, 12 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1134. The statute pertaining to executory accords also provides that the executory accord for settlement of a claim is not invalid because of a want of consideration provided the agreement is in writing and signed by the party to be charged. Personal Property Law, secs. 33, subd. 2, and 33-a; Real Property Law, secs. 279 and 280. The Personal Property Law, section 33-a, subdivision 3, provides further, that if an executory accord is not performed according to its terms by one party, the other party is entitled 391

$ 

to assert his rights under the accord or on the original claim.

One other method of offering to settle a claim should be noted. That is, a unilateral offer by the creditor to accept something in full satisfaction of the debt. The legal effect of the debtor's tender of the consideration formerly was to be evaluated by the principle laid down in Petterson v. 

Pattberg, 248 N. Y. 86, 161 N. E. 428. That is, only actual receipt of the consideration would discharge the obligation. 

However, modification of this principle has also been made by statute, which provides that an offer in writing signed by the offeror to accept performance in satisfaction of a claim "shall not be denied effect as a defense or as the basis of an action or counterclaim by reason of the fact that such tender was not accepted by the offeror.". Personal Property 

Law, sec. 33-b; Real Property Law, sec. 281.

If the executory accord is oral, it creates no binding obligation on the part of either party until there is a satisfaction. The issue has frequently arisen as to whether an agreement for future settlement is one of accord or of novation. For an interesting discussion of the question, read the opinion by the then Chief Judge Cardozo in Hudson v. Yonkers Fruit Co., 258 N. Y. 168, 179 N. E. 373, 80 

A. L. R. 1052; also note in 3 Brooklyn Law Review 301. An accord and satisfaction may result from the express agreement of the parties, or it may be implied from their conduct. Thus, where there is a bona fide dispute as to the amount due a creditor by a debtor and the debtor remits his check to the creditor stating that it is in full for the amount of the creditor's claim, the retention and appropriation of the check is an accord and satisfaction and, therefore, extinguishes the creditor's claim. Schuttinger v. 

Woodruff, 259 N. Y. 212, 181 N. E. 361; Fuller v. Kemp, 138 N. Y. 231, 33 N. E. 1034, 20 L. R. A. 785 and note; 

Nassoiy v. Tomlinson, 148 N. Y. 326, 42 N. E. 715. However, in Gaston & Co. v. Storch, 253 N. Y. 68, 170 N. E. 

496, the Court of Appeals, reversing the Appellate Division, First Department, held that the acceptance and reten- 392

DISCHARGE OF RIGHT OF ACTION §§ 478-481 

tion of a check marked in "full settlement" was not an accord and satisfaction where the debtor thereafter failed to perform a certain condition subsequent. For a distinction between account stated and accord and satisfaction, see 

Hopwood Plays, Inc. v. Kemper, 263 N. Y. 380, 189 N. E. 

461. Note that all cases dealing with an accord and satisfaction made prior to March 18, 1937, should be re-evaluated in terms of statutory changes. For a recent decision on accord and satisfaction, see Matter of Campbell, 256 App. 

Div. 693, 11 N. Y. S. (2d) 503, aff'd 281 N. Y. 685, 23 

N. E. (2d) 17. 

§ 479. 

Discharge of Right of Action by Judgment. 

A judgment by a court of competent jurisdiction discharges all remedies growing out of the same cause of action. If the judgment is in favor of the plaintiff, the cause of action is merged in the judgment; if the judgment is against the plaintiff, he is precluded from further proceedings. The judgment is also conclusive even as to matters which might have been presented and litigated, but were not. Reich v. Cochran, 151 N. Y. 122, 45 N. E. 367, 37 

L. R. A. 805; Pakas v. Hollingshead, 184 N. Y. 211, 77 

N. E. 40, 3 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1042 and note. 

§ 480. 

Discharge by Arbitration and Award. 

If parties submit their controversy to a board of arbitrators, as they may, the award, when judgment has been properly entered thereon, effectually discharges all rights of action and is a bar to an action on the original claim. “It may be enforced as if it had been rendered in an action in the court in which it is entered." Civil Practice Act, sec. 

1466; Young v. Crescent Development Co., 240 N. Y. 244, 148 N. E. 510. 

§ 481. Discharge by Lapse of Time. 

By virtue of the statute of limitations, a right of action is lost if a suit is not begun within a certain time after such 393

§  

right or cause of action arose. The statute of limitations is embraced in the Civil Practice Act, sections 10 to 61, inclusive, but particular reference is made to sections 47, 48, and 56, because such sections regulate the time within which actions based upon contractual rights must be commenced. 394
